# SDD runtime: running and operations

The runtime is available through `baked workflow` and `/workflow` in the TUI. It is on by default in every project. Bun is required; SQLite and the existing BAKED providers are used. No separate server or daemon is needed; `baked serve` optionally exposes the same commands to local clients ([Local API](#local-api-baked-serve)).

Runs freeze existing project inputs at startup. Approved edits to `package.json`, `bun.lock`, `bun.lockb`, `package-lock.json`, `pnpm-lock.yaml`, `tsconfig.json` and `biome.json` can continue through checks, review, acceptance and resume when the run's applied or reconciled file-patch journal proves the before/after hash chain. The original hashes stay in the input history. A private worktree edit authorizes no change to the original project file; publication there needs its own journaled patch. Outside edits, deletions and unconfirmed effects still stop the run with `input_changed`. Instructions, the constitution, skills, profiles, workflow definitions and gates retain their existing revision rules. The TUI, headless CLI and desktop API use this same core check.

Workflow edits journal their preparation as `tool.phase` events, including `match`, `analyze`, `pre_diagnostics` and `cas` before `write`, followed by formatting and diagnostic phases. This identifies the last step reached if a driver stops. Pre-edit language-server work runs through the intelligence worker with a bounded wait, and fuzzy matching yields so tool deadlines and lease renewals can run.

After a supervisor takeover or ordinary crash recovery, an interrupted attempt keeps the tokens persisted by its completed model steps. The run budget and usage totals include this lower-bound estimate. Its `attempt.finished` event has `usageComplete: false` and, when tokens are known, `usageEstimated: true`. The run report and usage Markdown show "Tokens of interrupted attempts" separately. The unfinished model step may have spent more than the recorded amount. The database's `usage_complete` flag releases a reservation when a non-null estimate has been charged; it does not claim that the provider supplied a final total.

No step or task stays open after the work it stands for ended. When a stage throws (a worker crash, a provider stop, an inconclusive review), the task, review, critic and other child steps opened under it end with the stage step, `failed` or `interrupted`; a transition step whose gate threw ends the same way. A resume or the next drive ends any step still `running` from the previous driver as `interrupted` and invalidates its running tasks. A stage that ended `waiting` for a decision is marked `completed` when the next drive starts, so a completed run never shows its approve stage as waiting. A run that is cancelled or fails ends its remaining open steps as `interrupted` and its running tasks as `cancelled` or `failed`. A task whose own check fails after its attempt, or that is blocked in any other way, ends its step as `failed` before its corrective starts, so a run that repairs it shows no open step meanwhile (#1805). A task step still `running` when the run completes ends with it: `completed` when its task is `verified`, `interrupted` otherwise.

## Enabling

Workflows need no setup: without a `workflows` block a run uses the default policy below with no checks, and `enabled` defaults to `true`. Select a configured BAKED model; `defaultModel: "none"` does not allow starting a workflow. To change the policy, add `workflows` to `.baked/config.json` (or the global config) and keep the other settings; to turn workflows off, set `"enabled": false`. The full example is below; `policy` is required in a `workflows` block and unknown fields are rejected.

Default checks are not written for you. A check is a command that runs as a trusted gate, so commands taken from a cloned repository's `package.json` become checks only after someone adds them in the gates window (G in `/workflow`), the desktop's Gates and checks screen, a chat ([below](#gates-from-chat)) or `.baked/config.json`. Gates in the repository's `baked/gates` still need the first-use confirmation.

```json
{
  "workflows": {
    "enabled": true,
    "importsEnabled": false,
    "defaultWorkflow": "sdd",
    "policy": {
      "maxConcurrentReadAgents": 3,
      "maxConcurrentWriters": 1,
      "maxAgentSteps": 24,
      "maxAgentCalls": 80,
      "maxTotalTokens": 800000,
      "maxActiveMs": 3600000,
      "maxCostUsd": null,
      "maxRepairRounds": 2,
      "maxConvergenceRounds": 2,
      "maxCorrectiveTasks": 20,
      "scopeApproval": "required",
      "reviewProfiles": { "correctness": "reviewer", "security": "security-reviewer", "language": null },
      "skepticProfileId": "skeptic",
      "forceSecurityReview": false,
      "allowedTools": ["read", "bake_find", "bake_impact", "navigate", "edit_file", "multi_edit", "delete_file", "request_check", "submit_result", "submit_artifact", "submit_review"],
      "network": "deny",
      "checks": [
        { "id": "typecheck", "executable": "bun", "args": ["run", "typecheck"], "cwd": ".", "timeoutMs": 120000, "required": true, "scope": "feature", "envNames": [], "writes": [] },
        { "id": "lint", "executable": "bun", "args": ["run", "lint"], "cwd": ".", "timeoutMs": 120000, "required": true, "scope": "feature", "envNames": [], "writes": [] },
        { "id": "tests", "executable": "bun", "args": ["test"], "cwd": ".", "timeoutMs": 600000, "required": true, "scope": "feature", "envNames": [], "writes": [] }
      ]
    }
  }
}
```

Check definitions contain an executable and argv. They run reviewed project code. With the policy's `sandbox` (or the `sandbox` setting, or `--sandbox`) they run in an OS sandbox: see [Sandbox](#sandbox) below and [OS sandbox](../sandbox.md); without it they run unsandboxed. `envNames` allows specific additional variables on top of `buildSafeEnv`; values are not stored in definitions. `writes` marks only generated outputs. An optional `fix` (`{"executable": "ruff", "args": ["check", "--fix"]}`) names the fixer for what the check reports; an implementer runs it with `apply_fix` on each file its task writes (the path is appended to the arguments; the whole-project formatters take it in their own form: `cargo fmt -- <file>`, `gofmt -w <file>` for a `go fmt` fixer, `dotnet format ... --include <file>` and `sbt "scalafmtOnly <file>"`; a fixer whose command takes no file, an sbt task other than an `...Only` one such as `scalafmtAll`, a Maven goal, or Gradle's `spotlessApply` and `ktlintFormat`, runs once as configured), each run recorded like an edit, and gets back each run's exit code (the output's last 2,000 characters too when it failed) in `fixed` and the check's result (#2436). The host's fixer passes below record each run in `fixers/<check>/<attempt>.json` (kind `fixer-run`: the targets, the command line, the exit, whether a target changed, the output tail), and a run that failed without changing its files is kept in `fixers/failed.json` until a later pass runs on those files; the next attempt of any task that writes them gets it as `failedFixers`. Bench round 77 pairwick configured `sbt --server -batch scalafmtAll`: the appended path made sbt refuse the command on every run, `apply_fix` returned only `exit 1`, and the models hand-formatted Scala against scalafmt for 17.13M of the initiative's 21.09M tokens. Before each fixer process, the runtime journals the pre-image of every existing file the task writes, then records each file's resulting hash. This also covers sibling task files rewritten by a directory argument such as `biome check --write .`, `ruff check --fix .`, `eslint --fix src`, `prettier --write .` or `black .`, and by a wrapper script. Those edits remain owned by the attempt for later edits, undo and a plain resume, including in the `fixUnsafe` tier. Directory arguments stay as configured, and `cargo fmt` keeps its once-per-call batching. TUI, headless CLI, the local API and desktop runs use this same core journal. A whole-project formatter (`cargo fmt` formats the whole crate) may rewrite files of other tasks too: the runtime keeps a copy of every file outside the fixer's scope before it runs and puts back those it changed (removing a file it created), and the result lists them as `reverted`, so a fixer never leaves another task's file edited in the worktree; the put-back also runs when a fixer run fails partway. A change it cannot put back fails the call. `cargo fmt`, which formats the whole package whatever file it is given, runs once per call for all of the task's code files (`cargo fmt -- <file> <file>`), each still recorded as an edit; documentation and data files the task writes are not passed to it. When a required check with a fixer fails at a check stage, the runtime first runs the fixer itself on every existing file the run's tasks write, before any repair task: each run is a journaled `file_patch` effect (`tool: apply_fix`, with the check ID, the task that writes the file last, and the pre-image), so `workflow undo` treats it like that task's own edits; `cargo fmt` runs once for all of those code files (`cargo fmt -- <file> <file>`) instead of once per file, each file still a journaled edit. If a file changed, judged per file and over the whole pass (a `cargo fmt` run given one file may rewrite another planned file, whose own run then finds nothing to do), the runtime takes a new snapshot and reruns the stage's checks once; only the failures that remain become repair tasks, so a run whose only failure is fully auto-fixable passes without a repair attempt. Files outside the run's tasks that a fixer changed are put back the same way; one it cannot put back stops the run. Static feature checks cover the code tasks too: when a plan is accepted, every task that writes a source file (any code extension, not documentation) gets the feature-scope checks that analyze source without running it, meaning a check with a `fix` or one that runs a linter, formatter or type checker, added to its `checkIds` beside the planner's own (a test suite is not added). Checks that analyze a whole compilation unit (`clippy`, `cargo check`, `cargo fmt`, `rustfmt`, `go vet`, `tsc`, a `typecheck` script, `mypy`, `pyright`) are not added either: in a task's own worktree they fail until the sibling tasks' files exist (`cargo fmt` follows every `mod x;` and fails on a module file a later task writes), so they run in the checks stage after integration (other formatters and file-local linters such as `gofmt`, `ruff` and `biome` stay per task; `apply_fix` still runs the `cargo fmt` fixer on a task's files). A task may still name such a check (planners list `cargo fmt` and clippy on every task of a Rust plan) and run it with `request_check`; when it fails, the result adds `taskErrors`, the errors located in the task's own files that neither the baseline nor a pending sibling task explains, and a note that the check runs in the checks stage, so the implementer does not chase errors about modules later tasks write. A task that writes a module index (`lib.rs`, `main.rs`, `mod.rs`, a Rust parent module file such as `src/parser.rs`, which declares the modules in `src/parser/` in the 2018 layout, `__init__.py`, `index.ts` and its variants) gets a dependency on the tasks that create module files beside it (or a child directory's index), unless that would close a cycle, so a crate's `pub mod parse;` is checked after `parse.rs` exists. A task with checks that creates a Rust module file (not under `tests/`, `src/bin/` or another directory without an index) and does not write the `lib.rs`, `main.rs`, `mod.rs` or parent module file (`src/parser.rs` for `src/parser/lexer.rs`, #1412) that declares it is a plan problem the planner fixes before submitting: Cargo compiles only declared modules, so the task's checks would never build the file, and a plan that left every `pub mod` line to its last task (idpeek, bench round 31) found 11 clippy errors at once there. Tasks that declare their modules in the same index are ordered by the module index rule above. The same holds one level up (#1684): a task with checks that creates code in a new .NET project (`.cs`, `.fs`, `.vb` under a new `.csproj`, `.fsproj` or `.vbproj`), Maven module (`.java`, `.kt` under a new `pom.xml`) or Cargo crate (`.rs` under a new `Cargo.toml`) is a plan problem when a task that neither is it nor runs before it creates that project file, or modifies the registry that adds the project to the build: a solution (`.sln`, `.slnx`) in a directory above the project, the parent `pom.xml` (`<modules>`), or the workspace `Cargo.toml` (`members`). A registry that already lists the project (a `<module>` entry, a solution line with its path, a `members` glob such as `crates/*`) is not reported. Code in a project that already exists, and toolchains that build every file by directory (Go, Python, TypeScript), are not affected. Likewise a task gets a dependency on every task that writes a file it names in `readHints` (not one it writes itself), unless that would close a cycle, so a component that imports a sibling task's module runs after it instead of in the same wave. A task that writes only documentation (a licence, notice or authors file beside it still counts) and whose title, description or read hints ask to run the program or show its output (run the CLI, capture output, a worked example, sample output), or one of whose criteria or requirements asks for an example block or for output copied, captured or shown from the program, gets a dependency on every task that writes an entry point (`main`, `cli`, `app`, `server`, `program`, a Python `__main__.py`, or a script under `bin/`), unless that would close a cycle, so a README with captured command output runs after the command exists. Such a documentation task is never a prerequisite of code or tests: a planner edge from a task that writes code or tests to it is dropped first, so a plan that put the package after its README (the code task depending on the documentation task) gets the edge the right way round instead of running the README first. A task that reads a file the documentation task writes (in its `readHints` or `reference`), such as a test of the README's examples that reads `README.md`, keeps its edge to it: it can only pass after the README exists. A task that creates an entry point (`main`, `cli`, `app`, `server` or `program` in any source language, such as `Cli.java` or `cli.ts`) while later tasks create code in the same language is a plan problem the planner fixes before submitting, unless a task that runs after that code writes the entry point or another file the entry task created (a registry or handler it imports): otherwise nothing wires the new code into the shipped command and the first review finds it. Tasks that share one description (20 characters or more, ignoring case and spacing), and a task whose description says to create a file another task writes and it does not, are plan problems too (#1377): the vtsnap update plan of bench round 37 gave three test tasks the same title and description. A task that writes code or tests may name a `reference`: an existing file of the project (or one an earlier task creates) whose structure, naming, error handling and test style the new code should follow. The planner picks it with `bake_find`; a reference that does not exist and that no earlier task creates is a plan problem the planner fixes before submitting. A task waits for the task that writes its reference, as for a read hint. The implementer reads the reference first and follows it unless the spec asks otherwise, and the correctness review receives `references` (each changed file mapped to its task's reference) and may report a file that departs from its reference as an `inferred` finding, which is a note and starts no correction. A task whose `profileId` is not an implementation profile (a planner that wrote its own role, or an unknown ID) gets the default implementer, `implementer` or else the first implementation profile, instead of the whole plan being rejected. The runtime runs them after the task like its task-scope checks, so a lint error blocks and is corrected in the task that wrote the file, and before they are evaluated it runs the fixers of those checks on the code files the task wrote, each run a journaled `file_patch` effect like the run-level pass, so what a tool fixes mechanically never fails the task. That pass covers code files only; when a required task check with a `fix` still fails after the task's checks ran (for example on a documentation or data file, or in a task that changed nothing the pass could see), the runtime runs that fixer once on every file the task writes, takes a new snapshot and reruns only that check before the failure becomes a finding and a repair task. The fixer runs are journaled `apply_fix` effects like the others, in the run worktree for several writers and in the run root otherwise. An optional `fixUnsafe` beside `fix` (for example `{"executable": "biome", "args": ["check", "--write", "--unsafe"]}`, limited to the rules the project allows in its own tool configuration) is a second tier: every fixer pass above and `apply_fix` run it after `fix` on the same files, each run journaled the same way, so only what neither tier fixes reaches the implementer or a repair task. Without `fix` it is ignored. A command gate carries the same optional `fix` and `fixUnsafe` in `command`; a policy check keeps it when a v2 definition binds it as a gate. `fixUnsafeScope: "tests"` limits the `fixUnsafe` tier to test files, where a fix the tool marks unsafe cannot change what the program does; absent, it runs on the same files as `fix`. The suggested `ruff` check comes with `ruff check --fix` and, on test files only, `ruff check --fix --unsafe-fixes` (#2095): ruff 0.17's default rules flag two adjacent string literals in a collection as ISC004, whose only fix (wrapping them in parentheses, the value unchanged) is unsafe, and in bench round 67 (skillcomb) 22 such diagnostics on two test files cost 3.83M tokens of correctives. On a source file an unsafe-only diagnostic still goes to the implementer. Task sources, the execution configuration and the internal database must not be excluded from a check. `network: deny` forbids network agent tools and, in a sandboxed run, the network of check, fixer, repro and probe processes (only `allow` grants it; `ask` cannot prompt from inside a process). Without the sandbox it does not isolate the executed project script, and it never forbids calls to the selected LLM provider.

A required check whose executable cannot be started stops the run at once. At start (with the native executor), before any model call, every declared check is started once: a bare command name must resolve on the PATH the check runs with, and then the command is spawned the way the run will spawn it (same executable, arguments, environment and Windows batch handling) in an empty scratch directory and stopped as soon as it started, so only whether it starts is judged and nothing it does reaches the workspace. A required check that is not on its PATH or cannot be spawned (`EINVAL`, `EACCES`, a refused `cmd.exe` line) refuses the start (`CAPABILITY_DENIED`, `reason: check_unavailable`, naming each check, executable and error); an advisory one becomes a routing warning. Checks named by a path that does not exist yet (`./gradlew`) may be generated by a task and are not probed, nor is `bun`, the running binary. For a run with checks the result is recorded as its `preflight/checks.json` artifact (`{checks: [{checkId, executable, required, status, detail?}]}`, `status` one of `started`, `not_found`, `cannot_start`, `not_probed`) and returned as `preflight` by `workflow status` and the run view; the desktop run screen lists the advisory checks that could not start. During the run, the first `spawn_error` result of a required check (the executable is missing or cannot be spawned, for example `spawn mvn.cmd EINVAL`) blocks the run with reason `check_unavailable`, the executable and the command in the message, instead of being treated as an inconclusive result the task works around; install the tool or fix the check, then resume.

A monetary cap is currently rejected: the current BAKED provider metadata has no verified shared source of exact prices. Use token/time/call limits; usage left unknown by a crash keeps its reservation. Arbitrary command hooks and compound effects are forbidden inside a workflow; MCP tools run only where a profile and the policy grant them (see [MCP tools](#mcp-tools)). Approved commands run as checks. The ECC import does not enable hooks.

### Recovery advice

Provider-account stops, repeated edit refusals, convergence-limit stops, initiative blockers and budget shortfalls carry recovery advice from the core. The terminal app names the corresponding key, such as R to retry, A to accept changes, M to switch model or B to extend the initiative budget. Its doctor output names the recovery decision, and delivery advice names the `/workflow deliver` command in the app. The desktop names its buttons and decisions, including Retry, Accept changes, Switch model, Extend budget and recovery decisions in Needs you. Open the named feature run to handle a run blocker. Policy or spec changes still require revision before retrying; extending tokens does not raise a convergence limit.

The headless CLI keeps recovery commands in its messages. API clients receive optional structured `recovery` advice beside the legacy text. A `no_progress` stop on a repair limit includes a `repair` remedy, rendered as `O` in the TUI and One more repair in desktop. Stored advice belongs to one diagnostic and does not carry over to a later stop.

### MCP tools

Workflow agents get no MCP tool by default: the built-in profiles and the default policy grant none. A tool is offered to an agent only when both its profile's `allowedTools` and the policy's `allowedTools` grant it, the same two lists that bound the built-in tools. A grant names one tool as the chat sees it, `mcp__SERVER__TOOL`, or every tool of a server, `mcp__SERVER__*`, with the server's name as in `mcpServers` ([MCP servers](../extensions.md#mcp-servers)), characters other than letters, digits, `_` and `-` replaced by `_`. For example, a project profile `baked/agents/docs-researcher.json` with `"allowedTools": ["read", "navigate", "mcp__docs__search", "submit_result"]` and a policy with `"mcp__docs__*"` in its `allowedTools` offer the researcher only `search` of the `docs` server.

- The policy is part of what a person approves, so its grants are approved with the plan. Auto approval treats an MCP grant like any other tool: a renewal refuses a policy that adds one, and `mcp__docs__*` counts as a new grant next to an approved `mcp__docs__search`.
- Any agent role may grant MCP tools in its profile; the acceptance roles (`acceptance-walker`, `acceptance-writer`) run on their host tools only, and their profiles refuse MCP grants. A grant that is not of the form above refuses the profile.
- An attempt connects only the servers that both lists name, when it starts, and reuses a server that the chat or another attempt already connected. A server that is disabled or fails to connect gives the agent none of its tools; `workflow doctor` warns about a profile grant that no enabled server provides.
- Every call passes the same guard as the built-in tools: the run must be executing and both lists must grant the exact tool. Its input and result are recorded as read evidence of the attempt, so a result can cite them. The runtime does not know what an MCP tool changes: it is not journaled as a file edit, `workflow undo` does not revert it, and its arguments are passed to the server as given. Grant only tools whose effects you accept for an unattended run. `network: deny` does not stop an MCP server from using the network.

## First run

From source, the command runs through `bun run src/boot.tsx workflow`; after a build, through `bun run dist/index.js workflow`. The installed launcher uses `baked workflow`.

```sh
baked workflow run --feature status-json --prompt "Add JSON status for a run" --events
baked workflow run sdd --feature status-json --prompt-file request.txt --events
baked workflow status RUN_ID --json
baked workflow list --status completed
baked workflow artifacts RUN_ID
baked workflow artifacts RUN_ID --file ARTIFACT_ID
```

### Proposing the workflow from the request

```sh
baked workflow propose "Fix the typo in the README install section"
baked workflow run --auto-preset --feature readme-typo --prompt "Fix the typo in the README install section"
baked workflow run sdd --auto-preset --feature readme-typo --prompt-file request.txt
```

`baked workflow propose TEXT` (or `--prompt`, `--prompt-file`; `--json` for the whole proposal) prints the workflow and budget a run would take for the request, with the reason. `run --auto-preset` runs that proposal when no workflow is named, and prints it on stderr. The rules read the request text only, with no model call (`src/core/workflows/preset-router.ts`), in this order:

| Request | Workflow | Budget |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A review or audit of existing changes (diff, pull request, branch, commits) and no change verb | `review-only` | standard |
| Research (investigate, explore, evaluate, compare, "how could we") and no change verb | `spike` | standard |
| A check, test, build or CI that fails, is red or broken | `fix-ci` | standard |
| Docs only (README, docs, changelog, typo, comments) that names no code (API, command, flag, screen) and is at most 80 words | `quick` | quick |
| A small change (rename, bump, tweak, label, message) of at most 30 words | `quick` | quick |
| A large change (redesign, rewrite, migration, new service or module, several or multiple parts) or 150 words and more | the project default | extended |
| Anything else | the project default | standard |

Only a workflow that can start in this project is proposed: a preset with validation problems (for example `quick` or `fix-ci` without a feature check) falls back to `workflows.defaultWorkflow` (default `sdd`) with medium confidence and says so. A workflow named on the command line wins over the proposal, and `--budget` wins over the proposed budget; the proposed budget applies only when the proposed workflow runs. The run records the proposal in its state (`presetChoice`: `proposed`, `chosen`, `overridden`, `budget`, `kind`, `confidence`, `reason`), and the [run report](#run-report) says what was proposed and whether the person chose another workflow. The TUI new-run form and the desktop New run screen preselect the proposal while you type the request and show the reason under the workflow; picking another workflow or budget keeps your choice, and the run records both. A request taken from a GitHub issue is not proposed in the forms; `run --issue --auto-preset` reads the issue's text.

### Starting from a GitHub issue

```sh
baked workflow run --issue https://github.com/OWNER/NAME/issues/12 --events
baked workflow run standard --issue OWNER/NAME#12 --prompt "Keep the text output as it is"
baked workflow run --issue '#12' --feature json-status
```

`--issue` takes the request from a GitHub issue: an issue URL, `OWNER/NAME#N`, or `#N` (or `N`) for the repository of the `origin` remote. The run's prompt is the issue title with its repository and number, the body without HTML comments (issue template hints; clipped after 20,000 characters with a link to the rest), the `--prompt` or `--prompt-file` text under "Additional instructions" when one is given, and the issue URL. Comments on the issue are not read. The feature ID is `issue-N-` followed by the slug of the title (at most 64 characters), so reruns of the same issue are listed under one feature; `--feature` names another. The run stores the issue (repository, number, URL, title) in its state, and the pull request its [delivery](#delivery) creates opens with `Closes #N`, or `Closes OWNER/NAME#N` when the pull request goes to another repository, so merging it closes the issue and moves it to Done on a project board that tracks closed issues. A delivery with `--no-pr` closes nothing.

The issue is read once, at start, through the GitHub REST API at `workflows.delivery.apiUrl` (an issue URL must be on that host). The token from `workflows.delivery.tokenEnv` (default `GITHUB_TOKEN`) is sent when it is set, so private repositories work; public issues are read without one. The start is refused with `details.reason` `invalid_issue` for a reference that is not an issue (another host, a pull request, `#N` without a GitHub `origin`) and `issue_unreachable` when GitHub cannot be reached or answers with an error; a 401, 403 or 404 names the token variable to set or check. The same start is available in the TUI new-run form (GitHub issue field), the desktop New run screen (GitHub issue field) and `issue` in `POST /v1/workflow/runs`.

`baked workflow list` accepts `--status STATUS` to show only runs in that status. Supported values are `created`, `running`, `waiting`, `paused`, `blocked`, `completed`, `failed`, and `cancelled`. Combine it with `--feature`, `--limit`, and `--cursor` to further filter or paginate the results.

The process runs research → specify → plan → analyze and stores an approval request. All artifacts (research, spec, plan, tasks, reviews, findings) are written in English even when the request is in another language. The spec author validates its spec when it submits it: a requirement without a criterion, an engineering requirement whose criteria are all post-release, a criterion naming an unknown requirement and duplicate IDs return to it in the same conversation (two corrections), because only the spec author can add or relink criteria. The spec also lists the request's deliverables (see [request coverage](#request-coverage)). Three criterion problems return the same way: a manual criterion about a file of the repository or a property of its code, such as "input is read line by line" when "a reviewer inspects it" (it becomes automated, a test or the reviewer reading the file or code), and an automated criterion that claims behavior on several operating systems, browsers or real devices ("confirmed on Windows, macOS and Linux", "cross-platform", "on a real device"). The third is an automated criterion that needs a build ("the production build succeeds", `cargo build`, `mvn package`) when no check of the run's `executionContract.checks` builds the project (a check builds when it is named for a build or its command compiles the project first: `go test`, `go vet`, `cargo test`, `cargo check`, `cargo clippy`, `dotnet test`, Maven and Gradle test goals, `tsc` without `--noEmit`; a criterion whose Given or When names a test that runs the build command itself is proven by a test check): convergence proves criteria only with those checks, so the spec author rewrites it as what the checks prove or a file the reviewer reads, or marks it post-release. A run checks on one host, so no check can prove such a claim and convergence could only answer `inconclusive`. The spec author rewrites it as what this host can check (platform-neutral APIs, the tests pass here) and, when the request asks for proof on other platforms, adds a criterion that a CI workflow file runs the checks on a matrix of them, which the reviewer verifies by reading the file. A criterion that names a CI matrix or a simulated platform (`path.win32`, a mock, an emulator) is not sent back. If the author keeps such a criterion after the two corrections, the spec is accepted, and convergence records the criterion as a known limitation (see below). Every later stage reads the whole spec, so its size is checked against the request ([spec-size](../../src/core/workflows/spec-size.ts)): the host passes the spec author `extra.specSize`, which asks for one criterion per behavior group (inputs that exercise the same behavior go into one criterion as a table in its `then`) and names the most criteria the request needs, the larger of 12 and twice the examples and items the request lists (list items, table rows below a header, fenced code blocks). A spec with more criteria goes back to the spec author once with that count, before the planner reads it; the author's next submission is accepted whatever its size. The planner validates the package when it submits a result: errors from `validatePackage`, unknown profiles and checks return to it in the same conversation, and it may send a corrected plan twice. Before that validation the host derives each task's `requirementIds` from its criteria (every requirement the task's criteria verify, plus the ids the planner listed that exist in the spec; an unknown id is dropped), so a planner is never sent back for a `criterion mapping mismatch`. If errors remain, the run blocks with these messages and R repeats the stage. Before source files are edited, review `specs/status-json/spec.json`, `spec.md`, `plan.md`, `tasks.json`, `tasks.md`, the policy and the pending request. Canonical JSON is also stored in immutable database artifacts; Markdown is a rendering.

```sh
baked workflow approve RUN_ID --request REQUEST_ID --digest SHA256 --expected-revision N
baked workflow resume RUN_ID --expected-revision NEW_N --events
```

In the terminal, `approve`, `answer`, `evidence` and `recover` (#2415) record the decision and then resume and drive the run in the same command, with the exit codes of `resume` (`--events` streams the events as for `resume`); when another request still waits for a person, the run is not resumed and the exit code is 3. `--no-resume` only stores the decision, and `resume` then owns the subsequent execution. The local API keeps recording only: its decision endpoints return after storing the decision and the client calls `resume`. Use the revision from the latest response: it changes after every command. `--command-id ID` allows repeating the same management mutation after a lost response; different content with the same ID is rejected. A start with a previous ID does not create a second run.

When stderr is a terminal, `run`, `quick` and `resume` also print the long waits of the run on it: opening the project, the repository map scan (`Indexing files 412/1,380`, at most one count every two seconds, then each later phase) and starting the run. With `--json` or `--events`, or when stderr is not a terminal, nothing extra is printed, so scripts that read the error JSON on stderr are unaffected.

Automatic execution walks the task graph, the required checks, independent correctness/security/language reviews and convergence for every criterion. High/critical findings go to the skeptic. Every severity starts a correction in the first round; once a repair or convergence round has run, only critical and high review and convergence findings start another, and medium and low ones stay in the review artifact, unless a convergence finding is why a criterion fails: acceptance needs every automated criterion to pass, so that finding is repaired whatever its severity. A policy with `lowFindings: "note"` (in `workflows.policy` of the configuration or a `--policy` file; absent means `repair`) keeps low findings as notes from the first round on: they are listed under "Review notes" in the run report with the reason that they are below the severities the run repairs, and start no correction. A policy with `quickFix: "on"` (absent means `off`) starts a correction whose findings are all low, or all point at one file, as a quick fix: its first attempt has a cap of 16 steps (or `maxAgentSteps` when lower) and 150,000 tokens (or `maxAttemptTokens` when lower) and writes only the files its findings point at. When that attempt does not finish the fix (it reports something unresolved, blocks, or ends at its cap), the correction continues once with the usual cap of the task it corrects and all its files, keeping the edits made so far; `quickFixes` in the run state records `quick` or `full` per correction. A correction of a quick fix whose check then failed is a full one. A low convergence finding that is why an automated criterion fails, and a low spec-based review finding whose repro the host reproduces, still repair. A low README wording finding cost one bench run 663k tokens and an attempt cap stop before this setting existed. A spec-based review finding with a `repro` also stays repairable after the first round: the runtime runs its repro, and when the repro prints `actual` the finding starts a correction whatever its severity; otherwise it stays a note. A finding whose reviewer also set `noRepro` (no command can show the claim) gets no such repair: its repro is no evidence, so neither a finding kept a note for its confidence nor one below the repaired severities repairs on it, in the review and in the converge stage (skillplumb round 69 repaired a 0.1-confidence README note for 250k tokens because its repro, which needed setup the README runner had marked not run, printed the error the note quoted, #2194). The exception is a `partial` convergence finding (test strength or documentation completeness, not a defect in behavior) of low severity, or of medium severity when it is on a test or documentation file, a `missing` finding of low severity, or of medium severity, on a test or documentation file (an absent test or README section), and a low `correctness` or `contradicts` finding (a detail such as a quoting style in an error message or a fixed timeout in a test helper), when every required check passes and each criterion it names has evidence in the review: it passes with notes. A medium finding never passes with notes when a criterion it names has the verdict `fail`: such a finding repairs, so a run does not complete with a failing criterion that the brief orders, such as a README example. It starts no repair, a criterion that fails only because of such findings counts as met, and the finding is listed under "Known limitations" in the [run report](#run-report) (and so in the delivery pull request) as accepted by the notes rule. A finding on a file the request gives verbatim (a fenced file or diff for that path, read by `suppliedBlocks`) never settles a criterion the review marks `fail`, whatever its severity: the host holds the exact text, so a deviation the reviewer rates low is not a detail of how well the behavior is pinned, and it repairs (bench round 74 ewvet-update completed with "changes requested" on such a finding, a doubled backslash in the request's README example, #2332). An automated criterion that convergence reports `inconclusive` with no finding naming it (and no `fail` verdict among the criteria without a finding) is not an invalid output: the evidence it needs comes from an environment the run does not have, such as a claim about other operating systems on a single-host runner. The runtime records it as a known limitation (`unverified-<criterion>`, accepted by the notes rule, with the reviewer's explanation as the claim), counts the criterion as met for acceptance, and lists it in the run report and the run's `limitations` count; the run reaches acceptance instead of blocking with `Coverage is incomplete without an actionable finding`. A criterion whose `then` claims formatting (a formatter's name such as `gofmt` or `prettier`, `-formatted`, or code or files that "are formatted") or lint (`lint`, `lint-clean`, a linter's name) passes only when a static check that shows it passed on the reviewed snapshot: a format claim needs a formatter check (`fmt`, `format`, `gofmt`, `prettier`, `biome`, `pint`, `php-cs-fixer`, `stylua`, `shfmt`, or a `lint` script, which JavaScript projects run their formatter through), a lint claim a linter check (`lint`, `ruff`, `clippy`, `vet`, `eslint`, `rubocop`, `phpstan`, `psalm`, `luacheck`, `selene`, `shellcheck` and the like), and a claim of both needs one of each. Otherwise the runtime changes the walker's `pass` to `inconclusive` with the passing checks in its explanation, and the criterion becomes an `unverified-<criterion>` limitation whose impact says that no check of the run verifies it (bench round 41 passed "All new Go files are gofmt-formatted" from `go vet` and `go test` while a file was not). A criterion that convergence marks `fail` with no finding naming it goes back to the walker inside its attempt, once: it adds a finding for the criterion, or changes the verdict to `pass` or `inconclusive`. A walker that keeps its answer gets a finding from its own explanation (kind `contradicts`, severity high, the explanation as the claim), so the criterion is repaired like any other failing one instead of the run ending `invalid_output` (mdtoc-update 20f81464 blocked there with every check green). The same holds when a stopped convergence is accepted (by a person, the assume rule or the critic): each automated criterion its last review left `inconclusive` that no accepted finding names becomes an `unverified-<criterion>` limitation as well, so acceptance does not fail with `Criterion <id> did not pass` on it. A finding with basis `inferred` (an edge case of a standard or convention the spec does not name, such as an ISO 8601 form when the spec only says ISO 8601) is a note from the first round on: it stays in the review artifact and starts no correction unless it is a security finding or critical, and the review gate passes without it. To make such cases count, the spec lists them: when a requirement names a broad standard or format, the spec author states the forms the feature must accept, with an example of each, and reviewers treat unlisted forms as out of scope. For a criterion that takes open-ended input (numbers, lengths, counts, dates) without naming a range, the spec author records a domain a test needs only in the spec's assumptions ("Amounts Assumed: tested up to 1e9 with two decimals, because the request names no larger amount."), never as a bound in the criterion or a clause that puts larger inputs outside it, because the spec checks below send such a criterion back. A review finding that claims how the code behaves on an input carries a reproducible example (`repro`): a program the run's checks already start or the runtime of their toolchain (see the README examples below), such as `bun`, `node`, `java` or `python`, its arguments, and the `expected` and `actual` values the program prints. Either may be empty, for a program that prints nothing: an empty `actual` reproduces only when the whole output is empty. A program that reads its input gets it from the repro's `stdin` (text written to standard input) or `files` (up to eight `name` and `content` pairs, written to a fresh temporary directory outside the workspace that `{files}` in an argument names, such as `{files}/trace.txt`). Reviewers try a repro with the `run_repro` tool before they attach it, and the runtime runs it again before the finding starts a correction. The correctness reviewer also runs every empty, zero or single-element input that a criterion names through `run_repro` and compares the raw value with strict identity (`Object.is` in JavaScript), so a result that only prints like the expected one, such as `-0` for `0`, is caught in review. A criterion about the raw bytes or encoding of an input channel (standard input, a file's bytes, the environment: invalid UTF-8, a byte order mark, a NUL byte) is tested through the program's real entry point when the design passes that input in memory (a reader or string parameter): the planner gives the test task one test that starts the built program as a process with those bytes, the implementer writes it with the exact bytes on that channel, and the correctness reviewer checks how the entry point decodes the channel (lockorder round 28 decoded invalid UTF-8 on standard input with replacement characters while a file was rejected, and its in-memory tests could not show it). When the request or the accepted spec says the tests are hermetic, run in memory or start no other program, that brief wins: no test starts the program, the code that decodes the real channel is a function of its own the entry point calls, and a test passes it the exact bytes; the reviewer does not report the missing process test, and the spec reviewer knows the rule, so it reports a planned process test as a blocking inconsistency with such a request and never asks for one. A blocking analysis finding a person answered that the next analysis raises again (the same text, or the same kind and refs) is not asked again at once: when every blocking finding of that pass repeats an answered one, the run goes back to the stage the findings refer to once, with the answers quoted as binding decisions that outrank the built-in profile instructions; only a finding that survives that return is put to the person again, and the newer answer gets its own binding return when the finding repeats once more (pgnvet round 31 asked one finding four times, about 1.2M tokens). A finding whose repro does not print `actual` is a note: a refuted claim always, and a repro that cannot run, fails without the value, times out or changes files unless the finding is a security finding or critical. A correctness finding without a repro is a note unless it is critical. A reviewer that submits correctness findings with neither a `repro` nor `noRepro` (a short reason no command can show the claim) is told once, inside `submit_result`, to attach one or give the reason; a second submission without them is accepted. The review's context names `run_repro` and the programs it starts (`extra.reproTool`), and the same one-time advice turns back a `noRepro` that says no tool was available when `run_repro` is offered. The advice is given once per finding across review rounds too: a later round's finding that repeats (`repeatOf`) an earlier finding with a `noRepro` keeps that reason when it has neither a repro nor its own reason, and is not asked again. The run report's findings table has a **Repro** column: the outcome of the finding's repro (`reproduced`, `refuted`, `inconclusive`), `none:` with the reviewer's reason, or `unverified` for a correctness finding with neither. When none of the run's checks starts a program, there is nothing a repro may run and findings block as before. The outcomes are stored in a `repro` artifact of the run. Each review's gate also records which findings started a correction and the labels it decided from (each finding's severity, kind, basis, confidence, sibling note, repro outcome and whether it set `noRepro`, never its claim), in `review-gate/` (kind `review-gate`); bench traces keep them, and the replay corpus decides them again on the current rule (#1715). The convergence walker gets `run_repro` and `extra.reproTool` as well, with the same one-time advice, and its findings of medium or low severity pass the same gate: a correctness claim the host does not reproduce (no repro, a `noRepro` reason, or a repro that cannot show the value) and any claim whose repro is refuted starts no correction. Such a finding that fails a criterion is a known limitation of that criterion, accepted by the repro gate in the run report; one that fails none is a review note. This holds also when the finding is the only one naming an `inconclusive` criterion after the first round, when its severity alone no longer starts a correction: the criterion is limited, so acceptance does not stop the run on it. High and critical convergence findings, `missing` and other non-correctness findings without a refuted repro, and runs whose checks start no program repair as before. A static medium claim, such as an integer above Python's default 4300-digit conversion limit, cost about 1.1M tokens of corrective tasks before this gate. The convergence outcomes are stored in a `repro` artifact under `convergence/`. Each converge stage's gate also records each finding's verdict (repair, a limitation by the notes rule or the repro gate, or a note) and what it decided from (the review gate's labels, whether the finding is on a test or documentation file, whether it is on a file the request gives verbatim (`supplied`), each criterion's verdict and evidence count, whether the required checks pass), never a claim or path, in `convergence-gate/` (kind `convergence-gate`); bench traces keep them, and the replay corpus decides them again (#1715). Fixes are added to the task graph through a bounded correction chain within the already approved scope. Findings of one round on the same file become one corrective task that carries all their finding IDs, so several failed checks of one task are fixed in one attempt rather than one attempt per check, each reading the same files again. This holds when the findings resolve to different planned tasks, such as a file one task creates and a later task extends with findings under each task's criteria: the task may write what both planned tasks write and carries both sets of criteria, so each finding still needs its own evidence (md-site 23c03a8e spent 239k, 347k and 910k tokens on three tasks for three findings in one file). A finding that names no file joins a task already correcting its planned task. Within a task the findings are listed most severe first. The findings of one failing check run (they cite the same evidence, claim `Required check X failed`) and one profile become one corrective task that may write every file they name, the union of the writes its tasks may use, with the checks of all of them: one run is one failure, and a task per file paid for the same reading again and left later tasks with nothing to change. A corrective task whose attempt changed no file is `implemented` with its checks as the judge: when the checks its findings name and its own task checks all pass on the current files, the task counts as satisfied and the run continues; otherwise it blocks as before. A corrective task whose findings are all failed required checks (`Required check X failed`, or a task's failed required check) runs those checks once before its first attempt, with one writer, when the files changed since the checks failed: when they already pass, because another correction fixed the cause, the task is `implemented` without a worker and the passing runs are kept as `tasks/<id>/already-passing.json`. A corrective task whose task check fails only on test files that other corrective tasks still to run (pending, running, invalidated or interrupted) are correcting, none of them a test file of the planned tasks it corrects itself, opens no corrective of itself: its check runs again once those correctives are done. Test tasks that share one test command over every test file each saw the others' failures, and their correctives ran the others' failing tests too (md-site round 30: three correctives of correctives, 1.45M tokens). A corrective that already finished its attempt is not waited for, so two never wait for each other. During the implement stage a finding that already has a corrective task waiting or running (pending, running or interrupted) gets no second one: several test tasks that report one source defect record the same finding again, and each report used to open its own correction. The same holds for a planned task that runs again after a repair invalidated it (generation above 0) and finds nothing to change; a planned task's first run that changes no file still blocks. A rerun task with no such check (a README and licence task, whose project checks are for code) is satisfied when every file it writes still has the content its last implementation left, by the hashes in its `tasks/Tn/diff.json` artifacts; a file changed since still blocks. A check stage that finds a required check passing closes the open findings `Required check X failed` of that check, whatever the other checks report. A finding resolves to the planned task that writes its file and covers one of its criteria; when the plan gave the file to one task and the criterion to others of the same profile, it resolves to the task that writes the file, widened to that criterion, so the correction may also write the files of the tasks that cover it. A correction also gets a planned file its fix needs when another task of the same profile writes it: a file the finding's claim or suggested fix names, and the planned test of a file the correction writes, matched by name (`cli_test.go` for `cli.go`, `test_cli.py`, `cli.test.ts`, `CliTest.java`, or `tests/cli.rs`). A file no planned task writes never joins, so the approved scope still bounds every correction. A failed run-level check whose output names files of the plan (linters and compilers print the paths they reject) becomes one finding per task that writes such a file, on that file, so each is corrected from the task that owns it; output that names no planned file gives one finding for the first task with the check. A failed task check whose output also names planned files the failing task may not write gives its repair task those files when a task of the same profile owns them (the correction joins that task's scope, so one repair can format or fix what the check rejected), and a failure that names only files no task of the profile owns stops the run at once with `no_progress` and `out_of_scope_failure` naming the files, instead of repair attempts that cannot change them. Likewise a corrective task that reports `implemented` with no change while its check still fails only on planned files it may not write stops the run with `no_progress` and `out_of_scope_failure` naming them, with no second attempt; a resume tries it again only after those files or the plan changed. A check that exits nonzero while its output reports every test passing gets a note in its output that something the tests ran left a nonzero exit code, such as `process.exitCode` set by code called in-process. New requirements, checks or file scope require a revision and a new approval. `already_satisfied` requires host evidence and passes the same final checks: evidence of the run snapshot, or reads of the task's worktree as it is when the result is submitted, so a retry may cite reads of the edits an earlier attempt of the task left in place; a read taken before a later edit of the same attempt is stale.

Every review or convergence finding that starts no correction (an `inferred` edge case, a confidence below `reviewMinConfidence`, a severity below the ones the run repairs, an advisory review, or a claim its repro did not show) is also kept in the run state as a review note, listed under "Review notes" in the [run report](#run-report) with its review, why it was not repaired and **Repeats**: a note raised again in a later round on the same kind, file and symbol names the earlier note it repeats, because reviewers renumber and reword a re-raised claim.

For an initiative feature, review and convergence receive the approved feature boundaries as `extra.initiativeBounds`. A finding on a path outside this feature's boundary that a sibling owns stays a review note with reason `owned_by_sibling`, naming the path and owning feature. It starts no correction and does not stop the run as `input_changed`; the owning feature's review checks that path. Shared paths inside this feature's boundary still count as its responsibility. An unowned path outside the approved task scope still requires a revised plan. The decomposer keeps acceptance tests with the feature that requires them, or names the later integration-test owner in its contract instead of requiring those tests for the earlier feature's acceptance.

`resume --accept-workspace` accepts outside file changes only. When there are none, its refusal explains how to revise the run or replan the initiative feature; it cannot expand an approved task scope.

Review and convergence results are submitted through a path in which the host fills what it knows. The host sets `snapshotHash` itself and ignores the model's value. Every current evidence entry of the review snapshot has a short handle (`E1`, `E2`, ... in the order the host recorded them) next to its id in the context; a result may cite a handle, an id, or an id whose first 8 characters name exactly one current entry, and the host replaces each with the evidence id before checking it. The same repair applies to the evidence of a task result; an `already_satisfied` task may cite reads of the run snapshot and of its worktree, and an entry that belongs to both (the attempt changed nothing) counts once, so a mistyped tail is still repaired. An id in such a result that names no evidence of the run at all (for example the provider's id of the worker's own probe call) is dropped when another cited id is valid evidence; a result that cites only such ids, or a stale entry, is still rejected. A review that omits criteria is not rejected: the host lists them as `inconclusive`, and the converge stage settles coverage. A convergence report that omits a criterion the host settles itself (a manual criterion, which a person answers next, and a post-release criterion the approval deferred) records it as `deferred` and requests no change; an omitted automated criterion gets a `missing` finding and a corrective, and any other omission blocks with the omitted criteria and their methods named in the message (#2190). The host checks (unknown or stale evidence, a pass that contradicts a failed criterion, a request for changes without a finding, unknown IDs) run inside `submit_result`: the agent gets the exact message and the list of valid handles and ids and may resubmit twice in the same conversation, which costs a few thousand cached tokens instead of a new attempt. The one-step format correction of a review is told the hash and the valid ids too. A review that finished is kept in the run state before a sibling reviewer's failure is raised, and a resume reuses it when the snapshot has not changed. Before the review stage the host adds one review bundle to the review snapshot as `diff` evidence: a unified diff of every file the run edited against its content before the run (built from the edit journal and the kept pre-images, so it needs no Git repository and covers every task, not only the last one), the changed files with their hashes, a map from each criterion to the tasks that cover it, and each task's changed files (`taskFiles`). Check results are not copied into it: they are `command` evidence of the same snapshot and reach the reviewers next to the bundle. It is built once per snapshot, so a correction round gets a new one, and every reviewer, the security reviewer included, reads the change from it instead of reading files; a patch over 48,000 characters is cut and says so. Finding IDs are handles too: the host keeps the ID a reviewer wrote while it is free in the run and numbers it otherwise (`F1` becomes `F1-2`), so a later round that reuses `F1` for a new finding is recorded; a SQLite failure while storing blocks the run with reason `storage_failure`, not `invalid_output`. A fault of the host's own state (a stale snapshot, a missing artifact the host should have filled) blocks with reason `host_state`, not `invalid_output`, so a diagnostic separates a host fault from a model's invalid output; the run list, `workflow status` and the desktop show it. An exception of BAKED's own code that is not a workflow fault (a `TypeError`, a bad `JSON.parse` in the host) blocks with reason `host_error`, "BAKED failed internally; this is not a model problem" (#1653). The diagnostic keeps the message, `host-errors/<diagnostic id>.txt` keeps the stack, and nothing is added to the agents' prompt; resuming it is always accepted (the repeated-diagnostic refusal of `invalid_output` does not apply), and the advice is to resume once and, if it stops the same way, export the run (`baked workflow export ID --destination DIR`, `/workflow export` in the terminal app, Export on the desktop run screen) and report it. A failed automatic resume after a provider wait is classified the same way. A required reviewer whose verdict is `inconclusive` is asked once more with the list of the criteria it left open (and their explanations) before anything else happens. That second question has half the steps of the first. It leaves out a criterion whose explanation already names evidence the reviewer cannot produce: a test or check that would settle it, a command it cannot run, or something it cannot list or count. When every open criterion is of that kind, there is no second question and the criteria go on as evidence gaps (#1506). The correctness and security reviewers also get `workspaceInventory`, the host's list of the files in the reviewed workspace: git's tracked and untracked files without ignored ones (a walk that skips dependency and build folders outside a repository), files deleted on disk left out, up to 500 paths, with the total and the number of test files. A criterion that counts, lists or limits files ("exactly 26 production files") is settled from it. In fecsmith release (bench round 42) such a criterion ended inconclusive twice, cost 761k tokens and was accepted unverified. Criteria it still cannot establish (manual ones are `deferred` as before) are routed to the converge stage as evidence gaps: the review is recorded as `pass` (or `changes_requested` when it has findings), the gaps are kept in the run state (`reviewGaps`) and the convergence agent receives them as `evidenceGaps`, so an automated criterion without evidence becomes a convergence task that adds a test or a declared check; acceptance still needs the converge stage to pass every criterion. In a workflow without a converge stage, such as `quick`, a review that still has no finding while every required check passes records each open criterion as a known limitation (`review-inconclusive-<dimension>-<criterion>`, accepted by the notes rule, with the reviewer's explanation as the claim) and counts as `pass`, so the run continues instead of stopping on a verdict with no defect to repair; the limitations appear in the run report and the run's `limitations` count. With a finding, or with a required check that does not pass, the run blocks with `invalid_output` (`<dimension> review inconclusive`) as before. Reviewer repros (`run_repro`) and the host's rerun of a finding's repro run with the project importable: a Python project with a `pyproject.toml`, `setup.py` or `setup.cfg` and a `src` directory gets `src` and the project root on `PYTHONPATH`, and the repro runs in the run's worktree when the run has one.

### Fresh-context restart

Repair rounds (`maxRepairRounds`) count checks-stage passes that start corrections. Review passes that start corrections count review rounds, which have their own budget of `maxRepairRounds`: a checks stage that needed both of its rounds still leaves the review two rounds for its reproduced findings, so a run whose required checks are green is not stopped as `no_progress` only because the checks stage spent the repair rounds. Each counter refuses its next round with `no_progress: N finding(s) remain after the policy's maxRepairRounds (N)`, a high finding the review keeps reproducing and a required check that keeps failing included. The exception is a review pass whose findings are all medium or low while every required check passed on the current snapshot: once the review rounds are used, those findings become known limitations (accepted by the review round limit in the [run report](#run-report)) and the run goes on to its next stage, the converge stage where the workflow has one, instead of stopping as `no_progress` (stmtsplit-update and cfgtrace ended blocked on one low or medium finding with every check green, each round having resolved the findings it was given). Under `clarification: "assume"` the same holds for high findings that only reverse an earlier fix: every required check passes, no finding is critical, and each finding sits where a correction of an earlier finding changed the code (the same file and an overlapping symbol) while that earlier finding's repro no longer reproduces, so its fix holds and another round would undo it (expenses-py: the repair of an unterminated-quote finding split valid multiline fields). Such findings become known limitations decided by the assume rule, and each pair is recorded in `review/reversals-<round>.json`; the definition's loop bounds, `maxCorrectiveTasks` and the token, call and time budgets still apply. A checks pass that fails after a review or convergence correction ran gets one repair round of its own when the earlier failures already used `maxRepairRounds`: the failure may be a regression of that correction, which no earlier round could have repaired (md-site used both rounds on two early property tests, and a regression of its last convergence corrective was refused with every planned task done). The grant is one round per correction, kept in the run state as `extraRepairRounds`; a second failure in a row with no correction between them, and the same findings repeating, still stop the run, and the refusal still names the policy's `maxRepairRounds`. The run's `repair_rounds` and `review_rounds` record both counters; the budget view of a run (`budget.reviewRounds`) and `workflow compare` show them. The review round correction artifact is `corrections/review-N.json`. A correction started by a task's own failed checks during the implement stage spends none: it is bounded by `maxTaskRepairs` (default 2) corrections in a row from one planned task, so early task-level corrections no longer use the rounds the checks stage needs. `maxCorrectiveTasks` counts corrective tasks, and the findings of one failed check run are one task. A defect a task finds in another task's file goes to that file's owner at once: when an implemented task's summary or unresolved items name a file an implemented task writes, the run's required feature checks run once, and a failing check's findings on the named files (or on the named file, when the output names no planned file) become a corrective task of the owner, as a task-level correction with the failing check as the repro. A failing check that declares a fixer (such as `cargo fmt` for `cargo fmt --check`) first runs it on the named files, journaled as `apply_fix` edits of their owners like the checks stage's fixer pass, and runs once more; only a check that still fails starts a corrective task, so mechanical formatting differences cost no model attempt. `tasks/defects/TASK_ID.json` records the files, the check results (`fixed: true` on a check whose fixer changed a file) and the findings; a passing check starts nothing. A failure that unresolved findings (open or in correction) already name, by its failing test lines or by an open `Required check X failed` finding on the same file, is not charged to the reporting task again; the artifact lists it under `covered` with those findings. A finding placed on a named file carries the requirements of that file's owner task, not the reporter's. Corrective tasks are titled by their first finding's claim and file, such as `Correct: Required check tests failed (render.go) and 1 more`. A path inside a package (`pkg/globs.py`) names that file, not the package's `pkg/__init__.py`. While none of the plan's test files exists yet, a test check that collects no test (pytest's exit 5 with "no tests ran", or only "No files were found in testpaths" when its `testpaths` directory does not exist yet; "No tests found"; Go's "no test files"; a test target that holds no test yet, such as Swift's "Executed 0 tests" with exit 0, #2419) is recorded as `no_tests` and starts nothing, so a plan that writes its tests last pays no corrective task for the gap. Likewise a required check that names a path a later task creates (`ruff check src tests` before the test task) fails with only "path does not exist" (ruff and flake8 E902, mypy, black, pytest, eslint, phpstan, `go test`/`go vet`, luacheck) until that task runs: the host gives the failure to the unfinished tasks that write under the path. It defers the check until they finish when they do not depend on the task, and otherwise hands it to them (`tasks/handoffs/TASK_ID.json`), whose own check runs see the folder. A task-check failure that reports only some of the diagnostics the check had before the run, in no file the task writes, counts as that baseline too (rpn 09a1c786 blocked its first task for 482k tokens, #1913). A plan that writes no test file at all, as in a feature whose tests the decomposer gave to another feature (`testedBy`), never reaches that state by itself, so a required test check that collects no test (also Bun's `0 test files matching`, Swift's "no tests found", Dart's "No tests ran" and Gradle 9's "did not discover any tests to execute", and a test target that exists and holds no test yet, which the runner reports with exit 0 and a summary of none: Swift's "Executed 0 tests" and "Test run with 0 tests", dotnet's "No test is available in", and the zero summaries of ExUnit, OUnit, Alcotest, busted, testthat and Pester, unless another part of the same run reports tests, #2419) does not fail its checks stage, in v1 and v2 definitions alike: the check counts as passed, `checks/no-tests/<check>-<id>.json` records why (naming the feature that owns the tests, when there is one), and acceptance treats that run as the check's pass. The run report, `workflow status`, `workflow compare`, delivery, the TUI and the desktop run view read the same decision (`decidedChecks` in `src/core/verification/host-passed.ts`): the check reads `passed (by the host)` in the report's Checks table, with a line giving the reason, and `hostPassed` carries it in the run view's `checkDetails` (#1894). A plan that writes a test still fails when the check collects none (wheeltags, bench round 58: the base feature's pytest exited 5 five times and the whole initiative waited, #1864). A repair round that leaves the same failures as the round before it (the same claims, criteria and failure signature, where the signature leaves out what one more passing test changes: pass and total counts, `go test -v` run and pass lines, TAP `ok` lines and test numbers; a failed task check is compared without the corrective task's ID) repeats. The first repeats get another round with a note to isolate the cause with `run_probe` before editing again. When the failure has repeated `freshRestartAfter` times (a policy setting from 1 to 5, default 2: two corrections in a row that changed nothing about it), the next correction starts from a clean context instead: its implementer gets the spec, the task, the current files and only the evidence its own findings cite, without the earlier attempts' diffs and check logs, plus a host-written account of what the earlier corrections changed and that the failure stayed (`freshStart` in its input, stored as `tasks/TASK/fresh-start.json`). This correction is not refused by `maxRepairRounds`, though `maxCorrectiveTasks` still applies. If it leaves the same failure again, the run stops as `no_progress` with a message that the clean-context correction did not change it, so a person fixes the cause or revises the plan.

## Decisions and revisions

```sh
baked workflow answer RUN_ID --request ID --answers-file answers.json --expected-revision N
baked workflow evidence RUN_ID --request ID --file observation.txt --accept --expected-revision N
baked workflow reject RUN_ID --request ID --reason "Needs clarification" --expected-revision N
baked workflow pause RUN_ID --expected-revision N
baked workflow cancel RUN_ID --expected-revision N
```

Rejecting an approval with a reason pauses the run. When it resumes, it does not request the same package again: it goes back to specify (or to plan when the workflow has no specify stage) with the reason as correction input, and specify, plan and analysis run again before a new approval request. Each rejection is used once, at most three times per run, and a v2 run stays within its `maxStageVisits`; past that, resuming asks for approval of the same package again. An answer does not restart the run. It is appended to the request, and `resume` continues from the stage the answer affects: research questions continue with specify and keep the research report, spec questions repeat specify, and analysis findings repeat specify when any finding of the request, blocking or not, refers to the spec (the person may answer any of them), and plan when they all refer only to `plan.md` or `tasks.json`. Before asking, a run sends blocking analysis findings the planner can fix alone back to the planner once: coverage gaps that refer to `plan.md` or `tasks.json` (they may name the criteria the tasks miss, but a gap that also names a requirement, `FR-` or `NFR-`, or the spec without a criterion id goes to the spec author, #1893) and inconsistencies that refer only to `plan.md` or `tasks.json`, with the findings appended to the prompt; only findings that remain after that replan, or that involve the spec or other kinds, become a clarification.

Resuming a run that blocked with `invalid_output` repeats the stage, and the stage's agent gets the stored diagnostic (with the schema's message when the result failed validation) as a correction note, once per diagnostic. An answer does not restart the run. It is appended to the request, and `resume` continues from the stage the answer affects: research questions continue with specify and keep the research report, spec questions repeat specify, and analysis findings repeat specify when any finding of the request, blocking or not, refers to the spec (the person may answer any of them), and plan when they all refer only to `plan.md` or `tasks.json`. Before asking, a run sends blocking analysis findings the planner can fix alone back to the planner once: coverage gaps that refer to `plan.md` or `tasks.json` (they may name the criteria the tasks miss, but a gap that also names a requirement, `FR-` or `NFR-`, or the spec without a criterion id goes to the spec author, #1893) and inconsistencies that refer only to `plan.md` or `tasks.json`, with the findings appended to the prompt; only findings that remain after that replan, or that involve the spec or other kinds, become a clarification.

The planner cannot change the spec, so a criterion it cannot cover would otherwise reject every plan (an `uncovered criterion` submission error) until the run blocks. When the planner submits a plan that leaves the same criterion uncovered a second time, and its plan names that criterion (its reason, such as "covering it needs a CI workflow the approval limits prohibit"), the criterion stops being a submission error. The run then goes back to specify once, with the criterion and the planner's reason as correction input: the spec author removes a criterion the request does not ask for, and the requirement only it verifies, or rewrites it so a task in scope can verify it. A criterion that comes back after that revision is asked as a blocking `scope` finding (`PLAN-SCOPE-N`, refs `spec.json` and the criterion); the answer resumes at specify. A criterion the plan leaves out while a task implements one of its requirements is not a submission error: the host lists it on that task (on a test task for an automated criterion when one implements the requirement, else on the task whose title, description and files share most words with it) and records it in `plan-repairs/uncovered.json`. A plan that does not name a criterion no task can take keeps getting the submission error.

The analysis passes when no finding is blocking, whatever the model's verdict (`pass` or `needs_revision`). Non-blocking findings are notes; an `inconclusive` verdict still stops the run.

After each plan, the core records its graph transformations in `host-edits.json`. Each entry has `{task, field, kind, before, after, text}`; `task` is null for graph-wide counts and `text` holds appended host instructions. Entries cover module/read/test/command dependencies, tests-first ordering, covering checks, derived requirements, profile selection, sizing splits and slices, batching and existing-test notes, content-written task execution, and task/file counts. The planner gets `extra.hostAdditions` describing these changes before it writes the plan. Analyze receives the ledger as `extra.hostEdits`, alongside the stored graph. Text listed there belongs to the host and is not a planner inconsistency.

The host demotes a blocking `inconsistency` when its task-field references and quoted evidence identify only host changes. A task ID alone does not establish ownership. Quoted planner text, unrelated fields, and spec references keep the finding blocking. Host-only count comparisons are notes too. `analysis/host-notes.json` retains the demoted findings with their matching ledger entries. Existing rules for unstructured split, dependency and check findings remain for compatibility. Mechanical description checks reconstruct the planner's graph from the ledger so copied descriptions on generated parts do not reject the plan.

Read the ledger with `baked workflow artifacts RUN_ID` and `--file ID`, or through `/v1/workflow/runs/:id/artifacts`. The terminal Tasks tab and desktop run Tasks tab display it beside the task graph. The run report lists the ledger and explains host-only notes. Resume keeps the ledger; a fork carrying the graph copies it, and a replacement graph or a rebase that discards the graph clears its provenance. An answer to an analysis clarification also adds a research fact naming the finding and answer, declaring conflicting earlier statements stale.

With the policy field `clarification: "assume"` (absent means `ask`, the behavior above) a blocking question reaches a person only when no default is safe. The researcher and the spec author are told so up front (`extra.assumeDefaults`) and settle such a question in their first submission; one that is still blocking is returned to its agent at submission: it chooses the most conventional default, records it in the spec's assumptions as "QUESTION Assumed: DEFAULT, because REASON." and makes the question non-blocking; it may keep the question blocking only when every reasonable default risks data loss, security, money or an irreversible public interface. Blocking analysis findings that remain after the plan-only replan go back once to the stage they refer to (specify for a spec reference, plan otherwise) with the same instruction. In a run of a v2 definition both loop-backs move the run's position to the definition's specify or plan stage outside its transitions; each counts as a stage visit, and when the visit would pass `limits.maxStageVisits` the findings go to a person instead. Whatever is still blocking after that is recorded as an assumption instead of being asked: the findings are written to the run artifact `assumptions/analysis.json`, shown as notes, and the analysis passes, so the approval is the point where a person sees them. A `scope` finding that names a clause the spec drops from the request is never settled this way and becomes one clarification request.
