A `scope` finding that names a restriction the request does not state (an input domain, a rejected form, an exclusion) or a requirement the request never asked for (such as CI on Windows, macOS and Linux; the host recognises both by their wording, and never for its own `COVERAGE-1` finding) is decided for the request: it gets one more return to the same stage with the instruction that the request is authoritative (delete the restriction or the addition with the criteria and tasks that only serve it; a domain may stay only as a non-narrowing assumption), and a restriction or addition that survives is recorded in `assumptions/analysis.json` like the other assumptions instead of being asked. A finding whose dropped cases are the effect of the spec's own exclusion or limit ("the assumptions exclude inputs outside the test domains, which drops required rejection cases") is such a restriction too: the fix is deleting the exclusion. The spec author's profile says the same: a domain is an entry of the spec's assumptions, never an `outOfScope` entry or a clause of a criterion, and never removes something the request names. An `inconsistency` finding whose one side is what the request states ("the request allows it", "the brief's explicit ban", or a requirement whose sources name the request with what it rules, "FR-005 forbids named test types", #1830) is decided for the request the same way. Any other `inconsistency` finding (two statements that cannot both hold, such as two generated files that map to the same path) is a defect, not a decision: it gets one more return to the same stage with its text and an instruction to change the artifacts rather than assume it away, and when it still survives it also becomes a clarification request. The reroute budget is the run's, so a blocking inconsistency on references no earlier pass raised, which arrives after it is spent (often one the repair of earlier findings introduced), gets a defect return of its own, at most two per run (`lateDefectRounds`), so analyze runs at most five times before a person is asked. R56 hdrlens base asked a person about a stdin test double the spec author had added while reconciling two earlier findings, and the answer was the finding's own first option (2 min of wait at the head of the initiative, 175k tokens, #1830). A blocking `inconsistency` finding that names the same references as one an earlier pass raised, describes a different facet of it and offers alternatives ("either ... or ...") is recorded with its first alternative as an assumption instead of being asked, because the earlier pass already returned that defect once. The assumptions that are recorded reach the reviewers and the convergence agent as `knownRisks`, to check first. The assumptions are part of the spec, so the approval shows them and a person can reject the package. A run chooses the mode at start: `baked workflow run --clarification assume|ask`, the Questions field of the TUI new-run form, the "Open questions" checkbox of the desktop New run screen, or `clarification` in `POST /v1/workflow/runs`; each overrides the policy for that run and defaults to the configured policy. Only `assume` is stored in the run's policy, so a run that asks keeps the policy hash it had before the field existed. The approval request body carries `assumptions` (the spec's list), and the TUI approval, the desktop run decision and the desktop Needs you detail list them above the files in scope; `spec.md` has an Assumptions section when the list is not empty. Rejecting the approval with a note sends the run back to revise them when it resumes (see above).

**Revision by patch.** With the policy field `revision: "patch"` (absent means `regenerate`: the author writes the whole artifact again, as before; set it in `workflows.policy` of `.baked/config.json` or a policy file) analysis findings that send the run back answer with edit operations instead of a new spec or plan, when at most five findings block ([revision-patch](../../src/core/workflows/revision-patch.ts)). The author gets the findings and the instruction in `extra.revision` and submits `{"schemaVersion": 1, "operations": [...]}`: `set` replaces a field or a list element, `add` appends to a list, `remove` deletes a field or an element, and `replaceText` replaces text that occurs exactly once in a text field such as the plan's `markdown`. Paths name fields from the root of the spec (`criteria/AC-016/given`) or of the plan result (`taskGraph/tasks/T012/dependsOn`); a segment after a list is an element's `id` or its index. The host applies the operations to the current spec, or to the planner's last accepted plan (`revisions/base/plan.json`, before the host adds edges and splits tasks), and checks the result as it checks a submission; an operation that does not apply or a result that does not validate goes back to the author in the same attempt. A finding about the spec revises the spec and then the plan by patch; a finding about the plan only revises the plan. A sizing split (see [Plan-time sizing](#plan-time-sizing)) revises the plan the same way. Every patch is recorded as `revisions/spec-N.json` or `revisions/plan-N.json` with the findings, the operations and whether it was accepted. A patch that is still not accepted after the author's corrections falls back to the full re-author.

In either mode the researcher, the spec author and the spec reviewer are told to report every blocking question or finding in one submission, so a person answers them in one request instead of one per round.

Research, spec and analysis run before any code changes, so they cannot know check outcomes. A blocking research unknown or analysis finding that only says checks were not run (lint, typecheck, tests) is returned to the agent at submission to be made non-blocking; if the agent keeps it, the runtime records it as a non-blocking note. The planner and spec author take verification commands from the policy checks, and the spec reviewer treats a policy check that runs a superset of a named command (the whole test suite for one test file) as satisfying it and ignores project-instruction checks for files no task writes. The policy checks are the verification the operator chose for the run: a blocking finding that they are narrower than the project's usual verification (one test file instead of the whole suite, no build) is returned the same way and kept as a note. A requirement's verification and a criterion's method take only `automated`, `manual` or `post-release`, and `automated` includes the reviewer reading a file or the code; the spec reviewer is told so, and a blocking finding that asks for another method (inspection, review, demonstration) is kept as a note, because the spec author cannot satisfy it. Each analysis pass after a return sees the blocking findings earlier passes raised (`earlierFindings`) and is told to report every remaining one in that pass, so the findings do not arrive one per return. A criterion about how the implementer works (a command run before editing tests, an intermediate checkpoint) goes back to the spec author before planning: the implementer reads that instruction in the request, and nothing after the run can check the order of its steps. A criterion that measures the specification or plan rather than the delivered program ("at most 40 acceptance criteria", the number of tasks, what a `specs/<id>/spec.json` file holds) goes back the same way: such a rule is followed while writing the spec, and accepted specifications are host-owned, so a plan task that writes under `specs/` is rejected with a message saying so. Two scope checks send the spec back the same way: an `outOfScope` line whose every word is in one `inScope` line ("CI workflows" against "Add a GitHub Actions CI workflow"), and, when the request calls limits non-narrowing, an `outOfScope` line with a numeric bound or a criterion that puts inputs beyond a numeric bound outside what the feature handles. Such a domain belongs in the assumptions. Whatever the request says about limits, a criterion whose Given, When or Then excludes inputs beyond its domain ("larger inputs are outside this criterion's checked domain", "more arguments are not covered") goes back too, unless every number in the criterion is in the request, so a bound the request sets may stay. A criterion that states a size or count bound with a unit the request never names ("up to 100,000 rows", "lines up to 1 MiB", "1 through 256 characters") goes back as well: reviewers would test that extreme and the implementation would be repaired for a limit nobody asked for. These checks read the request together with the clarification answers a person gave, so a bound a person decided ("files up to 100 MiB") may stay, and they read a `k` or `M` suffix as its number ("10k rows" states "up to 10,000 rows"). A test size it needs belongs only in the assumptions, as the smallest one the request's examples need.

Research is scoped to the request. When the request names files that exist in the workspace, the researcher gets `4 + 2 × files` steps, at least 10 (the attempt step floor) and at most its profile limit of 12; a file the request asks to create is not counted or listed as a path to read and 14,000 tokens per step (at most the policy `maxAttemptTokens`); it is told to read the named files together, follow their direct dependencies with `bake_find`/`bake_impact` and submit once they are covered. A request that names no existing file keeps the profile step limit and the policy token cap. Every agent attempt is told to submit two steps before its step cap, so a submission that the schema or a validator rejects can still be corrected; the rejected payload is stored as a `diagnostic` artifact (`attempts/ID/rejected-N.json`). An attempt that ends at its step cap with no submission and no writes is repeated once with 1.5 times the steps and tokens.

Request coverage on a new project ([coverage](../../src/core/workflows/coverage.ts)). The host builds Workspace Facts ([workspace-facts](../../src/core/workflows/workspace-facts.ts)) before research and passes them as `extra.workspaceFacts` to the research, specify, plan and analyze stages: the files of the workspace at any depth, shallowest first (without `.git`, `node_modules`, `.baked`, `baked`, `.venv`, `dist`, `target`, `build`, `obj`, `.gradle`, `.idea` and `__pycache__`; the scan stops after 2000 entries and lists the first 80 files), with their sizes, so a Maven, Gradle or .NET project with its sources under `src/main/java/...` counts as `source`, the manifests among them, the scripts of the root `package.json`, and a `kind`: `empty` (no file), `scaffold` (files, but no source code: manifests, configs, a README) or `source` (at least one source file). The facts are stored as the run artifact `facts/workspace.json` (a new revision only when they change), so the run page of the terminal app, the headless CLI and the desktop API list them with the other artifacts. They are authoritative: the researcher states `scaffold: <files>` and reads the manifests the request touches instead of reporting that no project files exist, the planner modifies listed files and creates only unlisted ones, and an analysis finding of kind `inconsistency` that says a listed file does not exist is demoted by the host to a non-blocking note. When the kind is not `source` the program the request describes is the deliverable and is never out of scope because it does not exist yet. The planner then lays down the skeleton first (manifest, entry point, test setup) and plans no task that inspects code that does not exist yet; the quick planner skips its search. The reviewers get `extra.baseline.greenfield: true`, so a deliverable the request describes that the change lacks is a `missing` finding and no earlier behavior has to be kept compatible. The analysis compares the spec with the request, and the host adds a blocking `scope` finding `COVERAGE-1` (refs `spec.json`, `tasks.json`) when the workspace kind is not `source`, the request names something to build (a CLI, app, API, library, service, tool, tests and similar words) and the plan's tasks write no source or test file (only docs, licenses, manifests and configs). Like any finding that refers to the spec, it sends the run back to specify (once on its own with `clarification: "assume"`, otherwise to the person); it is judged on the request as the user wrote it, raised once per run, and not applied to a feature of an initiative (the person approved its boundary with the split), so an answer such as "documentation only" is not asked again. A request that asks for documentation only and names no deliverable is not affected. Not part of the gate yet: a deliverable-by-deliverable coverage table on the approval view and auto-approval declining on unmapped deliverables.

The answers file is a JSON object of strings keyed by the request's question or finding ids, such as `{"SPEC-CONFLICT-T006": "Keep the documented limit"}`. The shape the run stores and `workflow status --json` shows (`{"answers": {...}}`) is accepted too. Any other shape fails with `INVALID_DEFINITION`; the message names the wrong value (`question id "Q-1" has an array value`), lists the ids the request asks about and gives an example. The local API's `answers` field reads the same way (#1463). Manual evidence describes the user's actual observation. It is bound to a snapshot; a new source change makes it stale. Rejecting the request of a spec criterion (`accepted: false`; `workflow evidence` without `--accept`) settles it: the criterion becomes a known limitation (`unverified-AC-N`, `decidedBy: "user"`, with the answer in its claim), the run continues without asking the same question again, and the run report lists it. A rejected human gate confirmation still pauses the run. Post-release criteria require explicit consent in the scope approval and keep the deferred status. A criterion with method `manual` has no evidence while the work is being reviewed, so the required reviewers do not judge it: the host lists it as `deferred` in their coverage and a review that is inconclusive only because of manual criteria counts as a pass over the rest. The converge stage then raises the `manual_evidence` request for each of them. The spec author may not mark a criterion manual when it is about a file of the repository (README text, documentation, configuration, source): the specify stage sends the spec back with that correction, because only a person can answer a manual request and a reviewer or a command check can read the file itself. Manual stays for behavior only a person can observe.

Editing the Markdown by hand does not change the accepted spec. Prepare an approved JSON revision, then:

```sh
baked workflow candidate RUN_ID --spec spec.json --plan plan.md --tasks tasks.json --policy policy.json
baked workflow revise RUN_ID --candidate CANDIDATE_HASH --expected-revision N
baked workflow resume RUN_ID --expected-revision NEW_N
```

`revise` leaves the run paused. `revise ... --resume [--events]` resumes and drives it in the same command, with the exit codes of `resume`.

Candidate fields are optional: missing ones are taken from the current package. The plan must contain Context, Decisions, Components, Data, Verification and Risks; DEC-001 and references to requirements. A candidate explicitly accepts the current instructions and configuration hashes. A changed skill requires a new profile/run. Profile versions inside an existing run stay frozen; a v2 definition changes only through a definition revision (see [Workflow definitions](#workflow-definitions-v2)). A repeated analyze and approval are mandatory; with `autoApproval.renewal` the host may give that approval (see below).

The terminal states completed/cancelled/failed are immutable; `fork RUN_ID --expected-revision N` creates a new run with the parent's artifacts, a new approval and no passing evidence. It does not start the model before resume. There is no automatic rollback of user changes.

`fork RUN_ID --expected-revision N --from-stage STAGE` starts the new run at a stage after the implementation (a `checks`, `review` or `converge` stage; the status JSON lists them as `forkStages`) instead of from the beginning. It is for trying a stage again, on another model for example, without repeating the plan and the implementation. The fork keeps the parent's spec, plan, task graph and approval, so it asks for no new approval, and it carries the parent's change. Its first drive puts each file the parent's journal changed back to its content before the parent's first edit, takes the fork's baseline there, and then writes the parent's content through the fork's own journal (step `fork-seed`), so the review bundle, undo and delivery of the fork see the change as the fork's own edits. Every task is marked implemented. When the result matches the parent's snapshot, the parent's passing command and manual evidence carry over, and a fork at a `converge` stage also carries the reviews of that snapshot; otherwise the checks run again. The parent must not be running and must have an approved package, its workspace (or worktree) must still exist, and the journal must have kept each file's earlier content; otherwise the fork is refused. The `fork/seed.json` and `fork/seeded.json` artifacts record what was carried.

### Derivation audit

A spec author writes some criteria with literal rows, an input and the output it must give (`Input | expected` tables, or `C# → Db`), and derives the expected values by hand. A run policy with `derivationAudit: "on"` (in `workflows.policy` or a `--policy` file; absent means `off`) has the analyze stage check them ([derivation-audit](../../src/core/workflows/derivation-audit.ts)). `auditShape` decides which criteria are audited, in two shapes: `rows`, an automated criterion whose given, when or then has a table cell boundary (`a | b`, not `||`) or a `→` arrow; and `computed` (#2022), an automated criterion whose then states a value counted from an input (a `line:column` pair such as `1:6`, a column, offset, position, index, width, length or count with its number, or a number of characters, bytes or columns) while its given or when names a literal input (a code span or a quoted string). Each such value is a row of its own, written as the input and the value. R65 uritpl cli AC-005 stated `1:6` for the second name of `{a}{a}` in a given/when/then criterion; the rows-only audit never looked at it, and the wrong column cost 1.42M tokens as an implementer conflict. Every audited criterion is handled alike: the analyzer gets those criteria and their requirements in full as `extra.derivationAudit`, also when the spec is digested, recomputes each row from the requirements alone (a position, column, offset, length or count by writing out the index of each character, not by estimate), and records one entry per row in the analysis's `derivations` (`criterionId`, `row`, `recomputed`, `matches`, `derivation`). `recomputed` may be the empty string, the value of a row such as an empty document's, and findings and questions show it as `""` (#1947). An analysis that leaves an audited criterion without an entry is turned back inside `submit_result` until it is complete, at most twice. A row the request itself states is authoritative: when the requirements give another value, the analyzer records the mismatch and names the requirement as what is wrong. A derivation that rests on the request (the request states, gives or lists the row or its rule) copies the request's exact words into the entry's `quote`, and the host checks it (#1823): the quote must appear in the request or a person's accepted answer, ignoring case, Markdown code spans and emphasis, quote marks and spacing, with the parts around an ellipsis in order. An entry that cites the request without a quote, in its derivation or through a requirement of its criterion whose text says the request states something (a spec author can copy an invented example into a requirement too), is turned back inside `submit_result`. An entry whose quote the request does not hold, or that still has no quote, is recorded as `matches: false` with the reason, so it becomes a `DERIVE-` finding like any other mismatch; the derivation re-audit of a spec conflict is checked the same way. R55 routeshadow passed `GET // n` → `RS005 empty segment at 1:6` on a `GET // n` example the brief never gave, and the wrong column shipped (49 of 22,611 differential invocations). The host raises the rows with `matches: false` as one blocking finding per criterion (`DERIVE-<criterion>`, kind `inconsistency`, refs `spec.json`, the criterion and its requirements), each row with the recomputed value and the derivation. Such findings go back to the specify stage once, whatever the `clarification` setting, with the note that the requirements outrank the criteria (under `revision: "patch"` as a revision by patch of the spec and plan); a row the next analysis still cannot reproduce goes the usual way, to a person or, under `assume`, through the analysis returns. Each audited criterion costs the analyzer a derivation per row, so the setting is off by default; bench rounds 40 and 42 each had a spec with a wrong derived row that analysis did not recompute, which surfaced only as a failing test and a spec conflict (0.65M to 0.8M tokens of rework, and once a question to a person).

With `derivationAuditCarry: "on"` as well (absent means `off`), a later analysis pass audits only the criteria that changed. A pass records each audited criterion whose rows all reproduced, keyed by a hash of the criterion and the text of its requirements (`auditCarried` in the run state). The next pass sends the analyzer only the audited criteria that are new, whose text or requirement text changed, or that had a row the earlier pass could not reproduce. The note names the carried criteria, and the host keeps their earlier derivations in `analysis.derivations`. Carried rows still go through the host's quote check against the request, and a requirement change invalidates every criterion that derives from it. In bench round 57, modeplan foundation's second pass audited all 121 rows again (318k tokens, against 254k for the first) to answer one coverage finding (#1851).

With `derivationAmend: "on"` as well (absent means `off`), a derivation return whose only blocking findings are `DERIVE-` rows, at most five of them, amends those criteria in place instead of re-running specify, plan and analyze (#2219). The spec author answers the findings with a revision by patch of the spec (as under `revision: "patch"` above), whatever the `revision` setting (a patch that does not apply falls back to writing the spec again). The host then keeps plan.md and tasks.json when the amended spec differs from the analyzed one only in the given, when and then of the named criteria (same criteria, ids, requirement ids, methods and requirement text, so no task's criterion list or writes change), no corrected row is copied into plan.md or a task, and `rowsAsData` is off: the tasks are rebound to the new spec hash and the run goes straight to analyze, with no planner session. Anything else (a requirement edit, a changed or added criterion, a blocking question) goes on to the plan stage as the full return would. A return that also carries an ambiguity, coverage or other blocking finding is the full return as before. Each decision is recorded in `spec-repairs/derivation-amend-N.json` (`criteria`, `rows`, `planKept`, and `reason` when the plan was not kept). Combined with `derivationAuditCarry`, the second analysis audits only the amended criteria. In bench round 70, three of evalpin's six features on the claude-code series went back through specify, plan and analyze over one wrong cell each, USD 5.68 or 18 percent of the initiative.

**Criteria against supplied test files.** When the request gives the whole content of a test file (one fenced block attributed to its path, as host apply and pricing read it, for a file the task creates or with prose before the block saying it is the whole file), the analyze stage runs the task-end case walk on that text on the host, with no model call, whatever the policy (#2006). A criterion literal that no supplied test file of the task holds becomes a blocking `inconsistency` finding `SUPPLIED-AC-N` on `spec.json` and the criterion, for the spec author: rewrite the criterion so the supplied content satisfies it, or move the literal to a criterion a task with a test file of its own carries. A task that also writes a test file the request does not supply, and a criterion another task carries with a test file of its own, are left to the walk, since that file may hold the case. R64 mdtoc-update found such a literal only at the end of an implementer session and a second refusal (561k tokens). When such a literal still reaches an implementer and its `spec_conflict` cites no criterion by id but names the supplied file or says its content is fixed ("verbatim", "exactly this content", "byte for byte"), the criteria whose literals the supplied files lack are the conflict's (`suppliedConflictCriteria`), so under `clarification: assume` the request re-audit of [spec conflicts](#test-integrity) runs on them at the first report instead of after a second session; the decision records `supplied: true` in `conflicts/request/TASK_ID-N.json` and `replayRequestConflicts` decides it again as any other.

### Acceptance rows as data

`rowsAsData: "on"` in `workflows.policy` or a policy file enables host-written acceptance data and parameterized tests. The default is off. A run can override the policy with `baked workflow run --rows-as-data on|off` or `baked workflow quick --rows-as-data on|off`. The TUI new-run form has a Rows field after Start run; Tab from Start run reaches it, and left/right toggles it. The desktop New run settings have a Host-written acceptance rows checkbox. `POST /v1/workflow/runs` accepts `rowsAsData: "on"` or `"off"`. Explicit off overrides an enabled policy; absent and off add nothing to the frozen policy.

This setting requires an analyze stage. Workflows without one, including the default `quick` preset, ignore an enabled setting and start with ordinary agent-written tests. The frozen run policy leaves `rowsAsData` out. A persisted run warning explains the ignored setting in the TUI run overview, desktop run screen and API run view; the CLI also prints it to stderr before driving the run. This applies to both policy defaults and run overrides. Use `sdd` or a custom workflow that analyzes its plan to enable host-written rows. With the setting off, the planner is not told about `rows` and `rowTest`, but a model may still fill them from the task schema: the host drops both fields from every planned task and records it in `host-edits.json` (kind `acceptance-rows-off`), so the plan is not rejected for them (#1816: a quick planner on the 2026-10-21 smoke set them, was rejected twice with a policy name it could not change, and the run blocked after 181k tokens).

The planner can mark a pure call-and-compare test task with `rows: ["AC-001"]` and this contract:

```json
{
  "rows": ["AC-001"],
  "rowTest": {
    "framework": "bun",
    "harness": "tests/double-harness.ts",
    "test": "tests/double.test.ts",
    "data": "tests/double.rows.json"
  }
}
```

All three paths must be declared in the task's writes. The test and data must be new files with no other writer. The host writes those two files; the agent writes the harness and any ordinary tests the task owns. Every framework has the same contract: the host's test passes each row's input to the harness and compares its result with the expected value as JSON data (numbers by value, so `2` and `2.0` are equal).

| `framework` | Harness | Test | `package` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `bun` | a `.ts` or `.js` file exporting `actual(input)`, which may return a promise | a `.test.ts` file; `test.each` over the data file, loading the harness with a dynamic `import()` without its extension | not used |
| `pytest` | a `.py` file defining `actual(input)` | a `test_*.py` file; `pytest.mark.parametrize` over the data file | not used |
| `go` | a `.go` file defining `func Actual(input any) (any, error)`, in the test's directory | a `_test.go` file; one subtest per row, reading the data file with `encoding/json` | the Go package name |
| `junit` | a `.java` class named after its file with `static Object actual(Object input)` | a `*Test.java` JUnit 5 class; one `DynamicTest` per row | the Java package, whose directory holds both files |
| `rust` | a `.rs` file (best under `tests/support/`) defining `pub fn actual(input: serde_json::Value) -> serde_json::Value` | a `tests/<name>.rs` integration test that includes the data file; the project needs `serde_json`, at least in `[dev-dependencies]` | not used |
| `xunit` | a `.cs` static class named after its file with `public static object? Actual(JsonNode? input)` | a `.cs` class with an xUnit `[Theory]` per row, in a project that references xUnit | the C# namespace of both |
| `rspec` | a `.rb` file, not a `_spec.rb`, defining `actual(input)` at its top level; the test evaluates it in a module of its own, so it is no method of every object and its `require_relative` still resolves from its file | a `*_spec.rb` file; one example per row, reading the data file with `json` | not used |
| `phpunit` | a `.php` class named after its file, not a `*Test.php`, with `public static function actual(mixed $input): mixed`; JSON objects arrive as associative arrays | a `*Test.php` class that loads the harness with `require_once`; one `test` method over every row that names each failing row with what it got | the PHP namespace of both, with dots for backslashes (`App.Tests` for `App\Tests`), or none for the global namespace |
| `testthat` | an `.R` file, not named `test-`, `helper-`, `setup-` or `teardown-`, defining `actual(input)`; the test sources it into an environment of its own, so `actual` is no name of the test file | a `tests/testthat/test-*.R` file; one `test_that` per row named by its id, reading the data file with jsonlite, which testthat itself imports | not used |
| `busted` | a `.lua` module, not a `_spec.lua` or `_test.lua`, that returns a table with `actual(input)`; the test loads it with `dofile` from its own directory, since busted runs from the project root | a `*_spec.lua` file; one `it` per row named by its id, reading the data file with dkjson, which busted itself requires | not used |
| `minitest` | a `.rb` file, not a `*_test.rb` or `test_*.rb`, defining `actual(input)` at its top level; the test evaluates it in a module of its own, as for `rspec` | a `*_test.rb` or `test_*.rb` file; a `Minitest::Test` class named after the file (`AcceptanceRowsCalcRowsTest` for `calc_rows_test.rb`) with one test per row | not used |
| `pest` | a `.php` file, not a `*Test.php`, that returns a closure `function (mixed $input): mixed`; JSON objects arrive as associative arrays | a `*Test.php` Pest file that loads the closure with `require`; one `test` per row named by its id | not used |
| `dart` | a `.dart` file, not a `*_test.dart`, defining a top-level `Object? actual(Object? input)`; the test imports it with the prefix `harness`; JSON objects arrive as `Map<String, dynamic>` | a `*_test.dart` file whose `main` declares one `test` per row named by its id; `dart test` runs from the package root, so the rows are a literal in the test, as for Java and C#, and the data file holds them for review | not used |
| `exunit` | an `.exs` file, not a `*_test.exs`, whose last expression is `fn input -> ... end`; the test evaluates it with `Code.eval_file` in `setup_all`, so it defines no module another harness could; JSON objects arrive as maps with string keys | a `*_test.exs` file; a module named after the file (`AcceptanceRowsCalcRowsTest` for `calc_rows_test.exs`) with one `test` per row named by its id, reading the data file when it compiles; needs Elixir 1.18 or later, whose standard library has `JSON` | not used |

Java's input is built from `Map<String, Object>`, `List<Object>`, `Long`, `Double`, `String`, `Boolean` and `null`; its result may use any numeric type, arrays or collections. Java and C# hold the rows as escaped literals in the test (Java has no JSON reader in its standard library, and a .NET test runs from its output directory), and the data file still holds them for review. Two Go harnesses in one package directory would both define `Actual`, so the planner gets one per directory.

Each host-written test passes its language's default formatter, linter and typechecker as written: Biome (including its import sorting) and `tsc --noEmit` without `allowImportingTsExtensions` for `bun` (its only relative import is the data file, so no import sorter reorders it), `ruff` for `pytest`, `gofmt`, `rustfmt`, RuboCop's defaults (single quotes, the `frozen_string_literal` comment, 120 columns) for `rspec` and `minitest`, Pint's default preset for `phpunit` and `pest`, `dart format` and `dart analyze` with the `lints` package's recommended set for `dart`, `mix format`'s defaults for `exunit`, lintr's default linters (80 columns, double quotes) and styler's tidyverse style for `testthat`, StyLua's defaults (tabs, 120 columns, `AutoPreferDouble` quotes) and luacheck with busted's globals for `busted`, and no code line over 100 columns for Java and C#; it spells nothing a formatter option picks differently, so Prettier's `arrowParens: "avoid"`, Biome's `arrowParentheses: "asNeeded"` and google-java-format's string reflow leave it as written (#1961). A fixer that still lays a host file out again (a JSON formatter on the data file, tab indentation) is not a change: before each attempt the host compares an existing file with what it would write the way the inline mode does, and keeps the formatter's layout (#1942: in bench round 61 md-site, Biome reordered the bun test's imports and the next attempt over the same rows stopped `input_changed`). Any other change of a host-written file stops the attempt `input_changed`; the message says to delete the file and resume with `--accept-workspace`, so the host writes it again, or to revise or replan the rows (#1944).

**Inline mode.** A brief that names the test files a feature may write leaves no room for the harness, test and data files above, so in bench round 52 no planner selected any of 248 audited rows (#1763). With `"mode": "inline"` the task names only a test file it already writes, new or existing, and no other task writes it:

```json
{
  "rows": ["AC-001"],
  "rowTest": { "mode": "inline", "framework": "pytest", "test": "tests/test_text.py" }
}
```

The host appends the rows and their parameterized test to the end of that file, between `BEGIN acceptance rows` and `END acceptance rows` comments, before the agent's attempt; for Go it also puts an import block, with aliased standard-library imports, right after the package clause. For JUnit and xUnit the block is a class nested in the file's own class (the class named after the file, else the first type it declares), before that class's closing brace and indented one level, so the file keeps one top-level type, as briefs for Java and C# often require (#1804: bench round 54 lanewalk had a second top-level class in 7 of 17 test files). For PHPUnit the block is the `test` method and a JSON normalizer of the file's own class, which PSR-1 keeps to one per file; a file the host starts opens with `<?php`, the namespace of `package` and the `TestCase` import. For Dart the block goes in the file's `main`, before its closing brace, and its imports in a block after the file's last import. For ExUnit the block goes in the file's own module, before its `end`, which mix format writes at the start of a line, so it calls the module's private `acceptance_actual`. A file with no class yet gets the class named after it around the block; a block found outside the class, as earlier versions wrote it, is moved in. Each row is a dynamic test or a theory case, so surefire and `dotnet test` count every row as a test of the file. The agent defines the harness the block calls in the same file, next to the task's ordinary tests. After the attempt the host puts the block back if an edit dropped or changed it, so the checks always run the audited rows. A formatter's layout is not a change: whitespace, a trailing comma before a closing bracket, the quote style of a string and, in JavaScript and TypeScript, a semicolon at a line's end or start or before a closing brace (Prettier's `semi: false`, Biome's `asNeeded`, #1936) or the parentheses around a single bare arrow parameter (Prettier's `arrowParens`, Biome's `arrowParentheses`), and string literals joined by `+` read as the one string they spell (google-java-format's reflow of a long literal, #1961), may differ, so a project's `ruff format`, `cargo fmt`, `dotnet format`, biome or Prettier gate and its fixer can run on the file, and the host keeps the block as the formatter laid it out (the file mode's host-written files are checked the same way). The Python block itself passes `ruff check` (with isort) and `ruff format --check` or black at any line length: it imports through `__import__`, so below the file's code there is no import for E402 or I001, and its rows are short string literals (#1927: in bench round 60 two wheeltags test tasks each needed a clarification because the block failed ruff and could not be changed); a corrective task that carries the same rows gets the same block. The templates leave a formatter's options nothing to choose (#1961): the bun test's arrow takes a destructured parameter, which keeps its parentheses under `arrowParens: "avoid"`, and the Java rows split a string longer than 60 characters into literals joined by `+`, with the block's opening comment on two lines, so google-java-format reflows neither. The blocks use only the language's standard library and its test framework, so no dependency is added: Rust gets its own `AcceptanceJson` value type instead of `serde_json`.

The block's string literals never hold an invisible character raw: controls, format characters such as U+FEFF, U+200B and the bidi marks, and separators other than the space (U+00A0, U+2028, U+3000) are written as the language's escape. The JSON-text blocks (`bun`, `go`, `xunit`, `rspec`, `phpunit`, `testthat`, `busted`, `minitest`, `pest`, `dart`, `exunit`) escape them in the JSON itself, and the JUnit, Rust and pytest literals in their own escape forms. In bench round 64 a Go block held a raw U+FEFF, which Go rejects anywhere but the start of a file, and its checks failed before any model action (#1995). The check that the block is intact compares what it means, not its bytes: a formatter's layout, either quote style, and an escape that spells a row's character another way all read as the host's block, while a changed row has the block put back. The submit path and the host finish of a stopped corrective (#1978) run the same check. The first comment line reads `BEGIN acceptance rows: cases from the spec's acceptance table.` and names the harness to edit; blocks written with the earlier words (`host-written from the audited spec table.`) are still recognized as they are (#2004).

| `framework` | Harness in the test file | Host block | `package` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `bun` | `function acceptanceActual(input)`, which may return a promise | `test.each` over the rows | not used |
| `pytest` | `def acceptance_actual(input)` in a `test_*.py` or `*_test.py` file | `pytest.mark.parametrize` over the rows | not used |
| `go` | `func acceptanceActual(input any) (any, error)` in a `_test.go` file, one per package directory | a `TestAcceptanceRows<File>` with one subtest per row | the Go package name |
| `junit` | `static Object acceptanceActual(Object input)` in the `*Test.java` class named after the file | a `@Nested` inner class `AcceptanceRows` of the file's class, with one `DynamicTest` per row named after its id | the Java package of the file's directory |
| `rust` | `fn acceptance_actual(input: &AcceptanceJson) -> AcceptanceJson` in `tests/<name>.rs` | `AcceptanceJson` (with the `AcceptanceJsonRead` accessors and `From` for numbers, strings, booleans, `Vec` and `Option`) and one `#[test]` over the rows | not used |
| `xunit` | a static `object? AcceptanceActual(JsonNode? input)` in the class named after the `.cs` file | a public nested class `AcceptanceRows` of the file's class, with an xUnit `[Theory]` per row, naming the harness by its full name | the C# namespace of the file's class |
| `rspec` | `def acceptance_actual(input)` at the top level of a `*_spec.rb` file, above the block | the rows as top-level locals of the file and an `RSpec.describe` with one example per row; the harness is captured as a `Method` when the file loads, so another spec file that defines `acceptance_actual` changes nothing here | not used |
| `phpunit` | `public static function acceptanceActual(mixed $input): mixed` in the `*Test.php` class named after the file | the `test` method over every row and `acceptanceNormal`, as methods of the file's class | the PHP namespace, with dots for backslashes, of a file the host starts |
| `testthat` | `acceptance_actual <- function(input)` in the `tests/testthat/test-*.R` file, above the block | the rows as JSON text parsed by jsonlite and one `test_that` per row named by its id; the result goes through JSON as the expected value did, so a length-one vector is a scalar, `NULL` is null and a named list an object, whose entries compare by name (return a `list` for an array) | not used |
| `busted` | `local function acceptance_actual(input)` in the `*_spec.lua` file, above the block | the rows as JSON text decoded by dkjson, with JSON null as `dkjson.null`, and one `it` per row named by its id, whose failure message starts with the id and what it got; the result goes through JSON as the expected value did (`nil` is null) and compares with `assert.same` | not used |
| `minitest` | `def acceptance_actual(input)` at the top level of the `*_test.rb` file, above the block; the file loads Minitest itself, as the project's other tests do | the rows as top-level locals and a `Minitest::Test` class assigned to a constant named after the file, with one test per row named by its id, whose failure message starts with the id and what it got; the harness is captured as a `Method` when the file loads, as for `rspec` | not used |
| `pest` | `$acceptanceActual = function (mixed $input): mixed { ... };` in the `*Test.php` file, above the block | the rows, a JSON normalizer in `$acceptanceNormal` and one `test` per row named by its id; Pest includes each test file in a scope of its own, so the closures in variables are that file's and no two files share a function name | not used |
| `dart` | `Object? acceptanceActual(Object? input)` at the top level of the `*_test.dart` file, outside `main` | the rows as JSON text and one `test` per row named by its id, in the file's `main` before its closing brace (a file with none gets `void main() {}`), since package:test declares tests when `main` runs; `dart:convert` and `package:test` are imported with the prefixes `acceptance_json` and `acceptance_test` in a block after the file's last import, so they never clash with the file's own; the result goes through JSON as the expected value did and compares with `equals`, and the `reason` starts with the id and what it got | not used |
| `exunit` | `defp acceptance_actual(input)` (or `def`) in the module of the `*_test.exs` file | the rows as a module attribute of the file's own module, before its `end` (the module named after the file, also under a namespace, else the first; a file with none gets `defmodule CalcTest do use ExUnit.Case`), and one `test` per row named by its id, whose failure message starts with the id and what it got; the result goes through JSON as the expected value did and compares with `==`, so 2 equals 2.0; needs Elixir 1.18 or later | not used |

A written-tests check of a JUnit or xUnit file that holds the block names the block's class too (`-Dtest=Class,Class$AcceptanceRows`, `--tests Class --tests Class$AcceptanceRows`, `--filter FullyQualifiedName~Class.|FullyQualifiedName~Class+AcceptanceRows.`), so the task's own check runs the rows and not only the file's class. `Class` is the declared type the block is nested in, with its namespace or package (#2363).

**Row replay at the source task.** A source task's defect that an audited row already names used to surface only when a later test task failed on it, and that task cannot edit the source: in bench round 53 four of six correctives (1.80M tokens) fixed such defects, among them jsonsplice `parseSteps("")` accepting the empty path, found three generations later (#1617). Now a task that writes code, has no rows of its own and implements a criterion whose rows a row task that does not come before it tests (either mode) replays those rows before it is accepted. It gets `extra.rowReplay` (the criteria, the row count, the file and a note with the first rows) and the `replay_rows` tool. Its `harness` argument is the whole source of a one-shot test file in the row task's framework and directory (`baked-replay-t001.test.ts`, `test_baked_replay_t001.py`, `baked_replay_t001_test.go`, `tests/baked_replay_t001.rs`, `BakedReplayT001Test.java`, `BakedReplayT001Tests.cs`, `baked_replay_t001_spec.rb`, `BakedReplayT001Test.php`, `tests/testthat/test-baked-replay-t001.R`, `baked_replay_t001_spec.lua`, `baked_replay_t001_test.rb`, `BakedReplayT001Test.php` for Pest too, `baked_replay_t001_test.dart`, `baked_replay_t001_test.exs`) that defines the inline mode's harness and calls the task's code. The host appends the inline rows block, runs that one file with the project's written-tests command for it, removes it (and Python's bytecode of it), and returns the rows that fail, read from the failure lines of the output; a run that names no row is an error with its output. The file is never one of the task's writes, never journaled and never delivered. A submission that reports the task implemented replays the rows again with the last harness when a file changed since, and is refused with each failing row, its input and expected value, or asked to call `replay_rows` when it never did; the same list is not refused twice, at most two times per attempt, so a row the task cannot meet (code a later task writes, no test project yet) costs one refusal. Each replay is recorded in `tasks/TASK_ID/row-replay-ID.json` with the row task, the criteria, the status, the failing row IDs and the harness hash. Without audited rows of the task's criteria, or when no test check of the project runs such a file, the task gets neither the note nor the tool.

Selected automated criteria must have complete `Input | Expected` tables. A third column headed Scenario, Case, Name, Description or Label names each case; the host keeps it as the row's `label` and does not compare it, and an audit entry may quote the row with or without it. Each Input and Expected cell must contain a JSON literal, optionally enclosed in backticks. Objects, arrays, strings, booleans and null remain typed data. Unsafe numeric magnitudes, non-finite numbers, expressions and other columns are unsupported. A `|` inside a cell stays in it when it is inside a backtick code span or a double-quoted string, or escaped as `\|`; a row that still does not split to its header's width leaves the criterion to agent-written tests (#2044). Keep those cases, property tests, stateful flows, process tests and file tests in ordinary agent-written tests. With the setting on, the spec author gets the same table rules as `extra.acceptanceRows`, so pure call-and-compare criteria are written in this shape (several arguments go in one JSON array or object) rather than as call expressions or command lines, which no host-written test can read (bench round 50: 0 rows in four runs, #1660).

The planner gets the criteria whose text has a complete `Input | Expected` table of JSON cells as `extra.acceptanceRows.eligible`, the only ones `rows` may name, and a plan that names another is rejected with that list. A cell may hold a raw control character inside a JSON string (a CR or TAB the brief writes itself, read as `\r` or `\t`) and a code span may hold U+2028 or U+2029; a table with a row that is still not JSON gives no rows at all, and the planner's note names it ("AC-002 is not read as rows: row 2 input is not JSON: ...", `unreadTables`) so the author can fix the spec instead of losing the rows silently (#2097). A spec with no such table gives the planner no rows note at all, and the host drops `rows` and `rowTest` from its plan as a recorded host edit, as when the setting is off. Bench round 56 rejected 4 hdrlens plans in 3 of 9 features for rows on prose criteria, one more planner step each (#1833). A criterion marked `property` is never eligible, so its table and the flag do not go together: when the request bans generated inputs (`bansPropertyTests`, the run's property-test mode is then `off`), the host clears every `property` flag after the spec stage as a recorded host edit (`spec-repairs/banned-property.json`, next to `spec-repairs/boundary-property.json`), and otherwise the spec validator refuses a `property` on a criterion whose text holds a literal `Input | Expected` table of two or more rows, asking the author to remove property or the table. dirdrift-update (bench round 66, "no random values, no generated inputs") had its 25-row table marked `"strings"`, so no criterion was eligible and the planner heard nothing of rows (#2045). Enabling this setting also enables the derivation audit during analyze. Each selected row must have its own matching audit entry; a criterion-level entry cannot stand for several rows. An audit entry names its row by the row's whole text, by its stable row ID, or by the row's leading cells when they pick exactly one row, so an analyst that echoes only a row's input is not refused (#1892). Mismatches go through the existing spec revision process. After a successful analysis, the host stores a versioned `rows.json` artifact with spec, graph and analysis hashes, the original row text, criterion IDs and stable row IDs such as `AC-001:1`. The terminal and desktop Tasks views display its row data, and the Usage section of `baked workflow report` says how many rows the host wrote and how many tokens the harness tasks' attempts used, or that no criterion had a host-readable table. The artifact is also available through `baked workflow artifacts` and the existing API artifact endpoints. Task definitions expose `rows` and `rowTest` in `tasks.json`.

Before an implementation attempt, the host writes the selected JSON rows and framework tests through the effect journal in the attempt's directory. Serial tasks, parallel task worktrees and resumed services use the same code. A changed spec requires a fresh analysis. The host refuses to replace an existing file with different content. Generated files are read-only to agent edits, and submission checks reject altered row data or tests. The task-end case walk reads the JSON data alongside the tests. The generated tests run through the normal task checks and mutation gate, and reviewers receive an instruction to review the harness and preserve the audited data. Sizing excludes generated files and emitted row cases from the agent estimate, while preserving other cases and the criterion overhead. It keeps a rows task whole so a split cannot create competing writers of its data or test file.

### Project memory

A run starts with what earlier runs of the same workspace learned, so it does not ask the same question or rediscover the same facts again. At creation the run reads the 30 newest runs in `.baked/workflows.sqlite` ([project-memory](../../src/core/workflows/project-memory.ts)) and collects:

- the answers people gave to research and spec questions, from any run; the newest answer to a question wins (at most 12);
- the assumptions of the specifications that completed runs had approved, such as those recorded under `clarification: "assume"` (at most 12);
- the check commands that passed in completed runs, except the new run's own checks, which its execution contract already lists (at most 8);
- active [learned code rules](#learned-code-rules) (at most 12).

Answers to analysis findings are left out, since they are about one run's plan. The result is stored as the run's `project/run-memory.md` artifact, its hash joins the run's inputs, and a fork keeps it; a candidate revision keeps the memory the run captured. The researcher, the spec author and the planner receive it as `projectMemory` in their input, framed as background that the request, the answers in it and the run's policy override; implementers, reviewers and the auditor do not. When the project's memory settings turn on **Share with runs** (`shareWithRuns`, [memory](../guide.md#memory)), project memory also lists up to 10 of the project's chat memories that are pinned or are a decision, preference or gotcha, and chat sessions recall the run memory in return. A workspace with nothing to remember adds nothing. The policy field `projectMemory: false` turns it off (absent means on, so stored policies keep their hash), and runs removed by `baked workflow gc` drop out of it.

#### Learned code rules

Findings that keep coming back become rules for the agents that write and judge code (#643). A recorded review, convergence or task finding counts toward a rule by its kind and its directory: findings of the same kind on files in the same directory (or with no file) in at least two runs propose a rule. Refuted findings and `Required check X failed` findings do not count. The rule's text is the newest such finding's suggested fix, its scope the directory and everything below it (`src/api/**`, or `**` for files at the root or no file), and it lists the findings it came from. A proposed rule becomes active once its finding recurred in three runs, or when a person accepts or rewords it; forgetting it retires it, and restore makes it proposed again (unless three runs back it).

At creation a run captures the active rules as the artifact `project/learned-rules.json`, whose hash joins the run's inputs; a fork keeps it. Implementers receive the rules whose scope covers a file their task writes, reviewers, the critic and the auditor receive every rule with its scope (`learnedRules` in their input), and research, spec and planning read them as part of project memory. The policy field `learnedRules: false` turns them off (absent means on, so stored policies keep their hash); it works independently of `projectMemory`.

#### Viewing and editing project memory

People can review and correct the memory before the next run. Each entry has a stable ID (`a-` answer, `s-` assumption, `c-` check, `r-` rule, `n-` note) derived from its question, assumption or command, so an edit keeps applying when later runs record the same entry again. Changes are stored in the `project_memory_edits` table of `.baked/workflows.sqlite` and apply to runs created afterwards; started runs keep the memory they captured.

- **Add** a note of your own (at most 12, 600 characters each). Notes come first in what research, spec and planning read.
- **Accept** activates a proposed rule as recorded.
- **Edit** rewords a note, an answer, an assumption or a rule (a reworded rule is active). A reworded entry keeps your text even when a later run records a newer answer to the same question; the original stays visible as "runs recorded". Check commands cannot be reworded: forget them or change the workspace checks.
- **Forget** deletes a note, or hides a recorded entry from new runs. The desktop calls it Delete on a note and asks first, because a deleted note has no Forgotten entry to restore it from. A hidden entry frees its place, so the next entry of its kind moves in.
- **Restore** brings a forgotten or reworded entry back as runs recorded it.

```sh
baked workflow memory [list] [--json]          # what a run started now reads, then forgotten entries
baked workflow memory add --text "Public APIs keep their names"
baked workflow memory edit a-91d0c3e2aa --text "Yes, and mark them as failed"
baked workflow memory forget s-0b7e55d1c4
baked workflow memory accept r-5c1e0a9b27
baked workflow memory restore s-0b7e55d1c4
```

The listing marks a check the workspace policy already lists, and a proposed rule, as not sent; a rule shows its status, scope and how many runs had its finding, and `--json` returns `{enabled, entries, hidden, rendered}`, where `rendered` is the exact text the next run would receive (null when empty). In the TUI the **Runs** tab of `/memory` lists the same entries: Enter edits, Ctrl+N adds a note, Ctrl+A accepts a proposed rule, Ctrl+D forgets, Ctrl+R restores and typing filters. The desktop app shows them in the **Runs** part of the project's **Memory** screen, and the local API serves `GET|POST /v1/workflow/memory`.

### Diagnostics after edits

Implementers get the same [diagnostics after each edit](../tools.md#diagnostics-after-edits) as the chat: new errors in the edited file and in up to 8 files that import it, in the run's worktree when it has one. The policy field `editDiagnostics` sets what an edit that adds errors does during the run: `feedback` returns the errors with the tool result, `reject` also restores the file and fails the edit with the errors and the original lines. A run that starts without the field takes the project's `editDiagnostics` chat setting; only `reject` is stored in the run's policy, so the approval digest covers it and a run under `feedback` keeps the policy hash it had before the field existed. Set it for one run with `workflows.policy.editDiagnostics` in `.baked/config.json` or `--policy FILE`.

**Task-end type check.** Whole-unit checks (`tsc`, `typecheck`, `cargo check`, `clippy`, `mypy`, `pyright`, `go vet`) do not run as task checks, since a task's worktree lacks the files its sibling tasks write. When the policy has such a feature-scope check and the task writes code, the host runs it once when the implementer first submits the task as implemented, in the task's directory, and keeps only the errors that:

- are located in a code file the task writes;
- do not name a code file that a sibling task not yet done will write;
- are not an unresolved module or name while such a sibling is pending (`mod parse;` before `src/parse.rs` exists);
- were not reported by the same check on the code the run started from. The baseline stores these errors as `diagnostics` in `facts/check-baseline.json`.

Up to 20 remaining errors go back to the implementer with the rejected submission, and its next submission is accepted without running the check again. The check is advisory: the checks stage still runs it on the integrated change. Each run is journaled as `tasks/TASK/task-end-check-ID.json`.

**Task-end syntax check.** Every file an implementer's attempt wrote with `edit_file` or `multi_edit` is parsed with the tree-sitter grammars of the repository graph when the attempt submits the task as implemented, so a file that does not parse is caught in the attempt that broke it rather than by a whole-crate check after another task. It covers Rust (`.rs`), Go (`.go`), TypeScript (`.ts`, `.mts`, `.cts`, `.tsx`), JavaScript (`.js`, `.mjs`, `.cjs`, `.jsx`), Python (`.py`), C# (`.cs`), Java (`.java`) and R (`.R`, `.r`); a file of any other language, or one whose grammar does not load, passes unchecked. Only the parser's error and missing-token nodes count, and an edited file is blamed only when it has more of them than before the attempt's first edit of it, so syntax newer than a grammar in code the task did not write does not refuse it. While a written file does not parse, `submit_result` answers `accepted: false` with up to five errors per file as `Syntax error: path:line:column: message`, and the agent fixes them in the same conversation. A grammar can predate valid syntax (C# 12 primary constructors, Rust generic associated types), so the same errors submitted again are accepted, as is any submission after two refusals; each decision is journaled as `attempts/ATTEMPT/syntax-ID.json` with `accepted` and the errors. In a workflow attempt the `edit_file` and `multi_edit` results also end with the syntax errors the edit left in its file.

**Task-end case check.** A task that writes test files (test directories and the test file names of the supported languages) is checked against the cases its automated criteria quote when the implementer submits it as `implemented`. Concrete spans in double quotes or backticks in a covered criterion's `given`, `when` and `then` are looked for in the test files the task writes. A span needs at least two letters or digits and a concrete value, such as a digit, a flag, literal syntax, a code identifier, or text explicitly introduced as an input or output. Descriptive labels such as "repeated flag", "success", "Show(empty)" and "rank/unrank" are left to review, even when quoted. A case is present when one test file contains all its significant tokens, in any order or grouping, with equivalent string escapes decoded on both sides. The host decodes Unicode escapes, including braced code points, hexadecimal escapes, newline, tab, carriage return, escaped quotes and doubled backslashes. Java, C#, Python, Go and TypeScript spellings of the same value compare equally. The check also accepts `["--path", "src/cli"]`, a path defined before its flag, and `Join([]int{3, 0, -2})` for `Join([3,0,-2])`. Placeholders such as `<file>` or `{name}`, grouping punctuation and the conjunctions "and" and "or" are ignored; case and token boundaries still matter. This text check does not prove that a test executes the case or asserts its outcome; checks and review do that. Two more kinds of case come from the same fields (#1482). A list of identifiers (members with a digit, an underscore or an inner capital, such as `bf16, f32 or e5m2`) is a case per member when the spec spells out a set of three or more that holds them all in another place: its problem, outcome, scope, assumptions, requirements or another criterion field ("the six formats f16, bf16, f32, f64, e4m3 and e5m2"). "All six formats" or "each of the 4 modes" takes every member of the one spelled-out set of that size whose text names the noun. Members are looked for as whole words, so `bf16` does not stand for `f16`. Plain words, the spec's own ids (`AC-001`) and file names are not members. In a criterion's table (`input | expected` rows), each code or double-quoted input span in the first cell is a separate case. For example, `` `input-1`, `input-2` as `url` `` gives two cases. Context words outside those spans and a role annotation after "as" are left out. A row is split into cells only at a `|` outside a backtick code span and a double-quoted string and not escaped as `\|`, so `` `"$[?@[0,0]==2 || @.b]"` `` is one case; a row that does not split to its header's width is skipped, never demanded (nodepluck, bench round 66: the walk demanded half a JSONPath query three times, #2044). An unquoted first cell is a case only when it names a concrete literal. Headers, separators, placeholders and descriptive scenario labels are left out. Equivalent escaped spellings count once, and sizing uses these same separate cases for pricing and task splitting. A dimension such as "with both settings" is left to the review. In fpbits (bench round 41), three of six features paid 1.63M tokens for corrections of test files that left out a format or a table row their criteria listed in plain words. A case no test file contains sends the submission back with one line per case, so the task adds the test in its own attempt instead of a review finding raising it after integration. A case tested in another spelling can be submitted again unchanged: the same list is not refused twice, and no attempt is refused more than twice. Each refusal is journaled as `attempts/ATTEMPT/cases-ID.json`. A quoted command of the project's checks is not a case: a test, type check, lint, build or format command of the usual runners (`bun run typecheck`, `npm test`, `cargo test`, `python -m pytest`, `go vet`, `dotnet format`, `deno test`, `node --test`, `make test`), also when a runner starts the tool (`npx tsc`, `bunx biome check`, `pnpm exec vitest`, `uv run pytest`, `poetry run pytest`, `python -m unittest`, #1427), or one of the policy's checks, since the host runs those itself. A policy check counts in each spelling a spec gives it (#1762): its full command, the program a launcher or interpreter runs (`python -m ruff check .` for `.venv/Scripts/python.exe -m ruff check .`), either with extra flags (`vendor/bin/phpunit --testdox`), or its arguments alone when they are more than one bare subcommand (`-m ruff check .`, `ruff check .`, `run typecheck`, `test ./...`); `-m` followed by one of the tools above counts without a policy check. A lone fragment such as `test` or `check .` is still a case. Pricing, split weights, history samples and the spec-conflict rows read the same checks, so none of them counts a host command as a case. In srcwhere (bench round 52), AC-012 named "python.exe with `-m ruff check .`, `-m ruff format --check .` and `-m pytest -q`", the walk refused T007 four times and a person had to answer its spec conflict (1.02M tokens). The spec author is told to name a check by its id rather than quote its command line. A criterion the plan gives to more than half of its tasks (with three or more tasks) has no cases either: it is about the whole repository, not what one test file holds (md-site 7b362eb4, bench round 36: a front matter property test was refused twice for not containing `bun run typecheck` and `bun run lint`, and the run stopped `invalid_output`, #1366). A case that a test file written by another planned task already tests is held: the walk reads those files when they exist in the attempt's workspace, with the same evidence rules (a comment or an assertion message holds nothing), so the task is not refused for it and does not add a duplicate test. In bench round 57, expenses-py T009 was refused for six CLI argument strings that T007's `tests/test_cli.py` tested, and about 435k tokens followed (#1886). Where the request prescribes the rows of a test file (a fenced block whose first line names the file and says it holds rows, read by `suppliedRows`), the walk also reads those rows for a case no file on disk holds (#2501): a case a row of a file another task writes holds is held by that task, whether it has written the file yet or not, and a task whose test files are all prescribed row files is not refused for a case no prescribed row holds, since it cannot add a row to a file the request fixes row for row. A case a row of the task's own file holds is still demanded. The second kind is recorded as a `case_not_prescribed` would-stop (shadow) and noted at analysis (`NOT-PRESCRIBED-<criterion>`), so the spec author removes the example from the criterion. A literal that describes rows instead of quoting one is neither recorded nor noted, and not refused either (#2524, `citedKind`): a number of rows (a bare number followed by "rows", "cases" or "tests", or equal to a prescribed file's row count or their total), a test file's name (`filter_args` for `test/filter_args_test.dart`), an envelope with upper-case placeholder words (`slotprompt: LINE:COL: MESSAGE\n`), and a described character (`U+0000`, a lone control escape such as `\u0000` or `\^@`, or a literal followed by "(backslash followed by letter n)"). The walk records it as `prescribed: "cited"`. In bench round 81, ratdec exact-cli T007 was refused for six rows of T006's `test/literals_test.exs`, written in parallel (377k tokens), and T011 for `["eval","--as","frac","7/3"]`, which no row of the brief holds: it added a 14th row and a corrective removed it (368k). Nor does the walk demand a case another planned task owns (#1771). The text of a criterion whose Given or When describes a project document (a README, changelog or usage guide, or a page under `docs/`) that this task does not write and another task does belongs to that document's task. A code identifier, such as a test name, that another test-writing task's description names and this task's does not belongs to that task. A task whose description limits the edit ("change exactly three lines and nothing else") owes only the cases its description names. In grundykit (bench round 52), such a task was refused for README fragments and sibling test names, added them, and a corrective reverted them for 258k tokens. Pricing uses the same rules. Every walk also records, per case and without its literal, whether the task's own test files, the host's intact rows or a sibling task's files hold it (and, with prescribed rows, `prescribed`: `own`, `sibling` or `none`), and which cases it refused, in `case-walk/tasks/TASK_ID.json` (kind `case-walk`); bench traces keep them, and the replay corpus decides them again on the current rule (#1715).

The case walk excludes scope paths, including relative source paths and files the task writes, unless prose explicitly introduces them as input or output values. A bare count following "at least", "minimum" or "count" in its sentence, and a test-coverage threshold cell, belong to review. A bare backticked number in a sentence about a length or a boundary ("name boundaries `1`/`64`", `at most`, `characters`, `limit`) is a boundary: a test holds it by the number or by a one-line string literal of that many characters, the way it spells a boundary value out (#2096). R67 skillcomb T010 was refused four times for `64` against a test that spelled the 64-character name out. A file the task writes is not a case by its bare name either (`UnitTest1.cs` for `tests/App.Tests/UnitTest1.cs`). A `key=value` cell contributes its value rather than its field name. Boolean or numeric expectations for a field named in the expected column contribute no literal case. Paired quoted inputs remain separate cases. Labels the criterion's Given or When binds ("E1 through E9", "DOC1 is the document", "a document called DOC1") are not cases, and a quoted case that names one asks only for the rest (`tree DOC1` asks for `tree`): a test reaches such values by index or a variable of its own. A case that is a call expression (`Position("nth-child", E4)`) asks for its literal arguments, the quoted strings, numbers and value lists, not its spelling. Lists and expressions accept formatter whitespace and quote-style changes, and a literal with an escape no decoding layer knows (`\1`, `\!` and `\0` in `s/(a)/&\1\n\t\!\0/`) is raw escape text, held by a test that spells it with each backslash doubled, as a C#, Java, Go or JavaScript string does (#1709); a literal with only `\n`, `\t` and the like keeps the one-layer decoding of #1614. A requirement or criterion id (`FR-005`) and a name expression of the code under test (`[src, itemIndex]`, `root.items[itemIndex].value`: a bracket, identifiers only, no digit or quote) are references, not cases. Code spans are read before double-quoted spans, so a double quote inside a code span (`` `{"a":1}` ``) opens no quoted span. A line whose backticks do not pair (an odd count, or a span that starts or ends in a space) has no code spans the walk trusts, and a double-quoted span across a backtick is not a case, so the prose between two code spans is never demanded (#1790). Spaced hex bytes (`7B FF 0A`) are held by the spelling of each toolchain: `0x7b, 0xff, 0x0a` in a list (also with Java `(byte)` casts and Kotlin `.toByte()`), `\x7b\xff\n` in a byte string, decimal values, or one hex string (`"7bff0a"`). Some spans name something a test spells in parts or extends (#1807). A qualified test or member name (`PropdriftTest.validatesArgumentsInTheSpecifiedOrder`, a C# `Class.Method`, a Python `TestClass.test_x`, a Rust `module::test`) is held by its last two segments as words, while `README.md` and `config.yaml` stay paths. An upper-case metavariable after a flag or a preposition (`--write-baseline FILE`, `wrote 1 finding to FILE`) is a placeholder, unless it is the whole span. An anchored regex (`^[A-Za-z0-9_-]{7}$`) is held by its body inside a regex literal or a raw string, and a base URL (`https://sho.rt/`) by any URL that begins with it. A property test (one with the ecosystem library, a seeded generator or an exhaustive loop), or any test of a criterion marked `property`, holds a case when it names every flag and word of it in any letter case: the dates and numbers are what its strategies draw. A span written in the spec's own notation is held the way a test builds it (#1820). A JSON value that holds a string or an object (`{"op":"params","segs":[{"Lit":"a"}]}`, `[2,"","invalid path"]`) is held by its scalar leaves, the strings and numbers, since a test builds it with constructors; its keys and punctuation are notation, and a list of numbers keeps its own spelling. A command line led by the project's own program (`youngkit rank 4,3x2,1`, the name from `Cargo.toml`, `go.mod`, `package.json`, `pyproject.toml` scripts, `pom.xml`, the Gradle settings or a `.csproj`) is held by its arguments, as a test of the entry function passes them. A request line (`GET /links/test_1/stats`) is held by its path and, for a method other than GET, the method; a header line (`Location: https://example.com/a`) by its value and its name in any letter case. A pointer type (`*base.Error`), a token shape made of metavariables (`PARTxCOUNT`) and a single capitalised name in a code span (`Msg`, "8 `Test` functions") describe the test and are not cases, unless the criterion quotes the word as a value ("prints `Usage`"). A byte string may mix printable characters with escapes (`b"{\xff\n"` for `7B FF 0A`), and two bytes may be one quoted hex string. A corrective owes the cases of the criteria its findings name, by criterion, by requirement or, for a finding that names neither, by the planned tasks that write the file it names; one whose findings name none of its criteria keeps them all. These extraction rules also apply to case counts used for pricing and splitting. At task submission, literals found only in recognized assertion-message arguments are reported as decoys and do not satisfy the walk. The diagnostic arguments come from one table of test frameworks ([test-frameworks](../../src/core/intelligence/test-frameworks.ts)), which the [test-integrity guard](#test-integrity) also reads for tests and assertions: Rust `assert!`/`assert_eq!`, Go `t.Errorf`/`t.Fatalf` and testify `assert.*`/`require.*`, Python `assert` statements (across lines), unittest `assert*` and `msg=`, Jest and Vitest `expect(value, message)`, Node and Chai `assert.*`, JUnit 5 and kotlin.test (message last), JUnit 4 (message first, read from its imports), Hamcrest's reason, AssertJ `as`/`describedAs`/`withFailMessage`, Kotest `withClue`, NUnit `Assert.That`, xUnit, MSTest and FluentAssertions `because`. Inputs and expected values used outside those diagnostic arguments still count. Host-written JSON row data remains evidence. Comments are never evidence (#1846): the walk reads a test file with its comments removed, by the comment syntax of the file's language (the `MASK_SYNTAX` record in `src/core/workflows/quality/code-mask.ts`: `//` and `/* */`, `#`, `--`, `(* *)`, `<!-- -->`), and a literal found only in a comment is refused with a note that a comment tests nothing. R57 modeplan answered a refusal with `// Expected rows include (6,d,1777,0777,"/tmp")`. A row in tuple notation (`(6,d,1777,0777,"/tmp")`, three cells or more) is held by its cells in order within one statement or table line (`InlineData(6, "d", 1777, ...)`, `"6    d     1777  0777  \"/tmp\"\n"`), not by tokens anywhere in the file. A JSON object with no non-empty string (`{"flags":[],"values":{"--umask":""},"args":[]}`) is held by its keys and an empty string. A literal whose decoding leaves `\n`, `\t` or `\r` (the host message's own escaping, `STEP  ACTION\\n0`) is also held by the twice-decoded text. A multi-line literal is held whole: its lines in order and next to each other, in the file or in its adjacent string literals and string lists joined; R57 foundation T013 left out the middle line of `f 0644 a\n# c\nf 0600 a` and passed. A blank line between two lines is a line of the literal and is held as a line break of its own; blank lines before the first line and after the last do not count (#2021). R65 lint T004 `a\n\n{b\n` and md-site T004 `Intro\n\n# First\n\n# Second` were refused against tests that spelled them exactly, since the blank lines were dropped and the remaining lines demanded next to each other. A literal's lines are split and trimmed on ASCII spaces and tabs only: a bare CR, a BOM, an NBSP, U+2028, U+2029 or U+3000 is part of the literal, held by a test that spells the character raw or with its escape (#1979), and an empty quoted literal (`gives "";`) is a span of its own, not a case, so it never pairs with the next span's quote (#1980). The left side of a before/after pair in a criterion's Then is the state before the change, not a value a test must spell (#2000): `"A" -> "B"`, `"A" => "B"`, `"A" → "B"` and `from "A" to "B"` in a sentence about the project's own state (a version, a key, a setting, a manifest, a rename, a move, a change, an update), and `"A" becomes "B"`. A sentence that names an input, an output or a transformation (`the input "Hello World 2" becomes "hello-world-2"`, `returns`, `prints`, `maps`) keeps both sides, as does a range (`lists the entries from "2024-01-01" to "2024-01-31"`) and the same pair in a Given or When. R64 mdtoc-update T004 was refused three times for `0.1.0` in `Only version "0.1.0" -> "0.2.0" ... differ`, against a test file the request supplied verbatim (784k of the run's 1.28M tokens). Rows the host wrote are held by construction (#1997): while a task's inline rows block is intact, the walk asks no test text for the table cells of the criteria in its `rows` (the block's parameterized test replays every row, and a changed block is refused on its own); a cell the criterion also quotes outside its table is still a case. For the other cases the test text is decoded until no escape layer is left, octal escapes (`\015\012`) included, so JSON text a test holds in a quoted string (`JSON.parse("[\"a\\r\\nb\"]")`) has its line breaks. R64 cfgquill lint had 12 refusals of AC-004 cells the host's own Go block held, and a JUnit block spells the number `1777` as `1777L`.

With `caseSourceProbe: "on"` in `workflows.policy` or a policy file (absent means `off`), the case walk first asks the source about an expected output it would refuse a task for (#1836). The case must be a span of the criterion's `then`, and the criterion's `when` or `given` must quote a plain command line in a code span whose program is the project's own (an entry point, as for the documented examples: a Cargo bin, `go run`, a package bin, a Python script, `dotnet run`, `java -cp`). An implemented task of the same criterion must also have written source code outside the task's own writes. The host then runs the command once in a copy of the workspace, as for a documented example, up to four commands per attempt. When no line of what the program prints is the expected output, the refusal line for that case names the command, what the program printed, the source files and their owner task. It tells the task to write the test with the criterion's value although it fails, name the files in its summary and report implemented.
