The failing test check then becomes a correction whose scope is the failing tests and those source files. `tasks/TASK_ID/case-probe-*.json` records each probe. A command that cannot say (not the project's program, not built, an input nobody supplies, a timeout) leaves the ordinary refusal. In bench round 56, hdrlens negotiate T004 was refused for `invalid offer "text/*"`, which T002's code printed after an `"a.txt": ` prefix. The test task could not change the source, looped, and the review's corrective cost 1.0M tokens, about 2.8M tied to the one defect.

**Task-end repro check.** A corrective task whose findings carry a repro the run can start (a program its checks start) runs those repros on the attempt's files when the implementer submits the task as `implemented` or `already_satisfied`. A finding that still prints its `actual` output sends the submission back with the repro command, its input files and stdin (each cut at 8,000 characters) and its output, at most twice per attempt. The same check is the corrective worker's `run_repro` tool (no arguments, up to 8 runs per attempt): it runs the findings' repros the way the host will at submit, including a reviewer's program written to `{files}`, so the worker sees the verdict while it edits instead of after a refused submission (ecfit 25614519 spent 4.08M tokens on one such finding with only `run_probe`, #1283). The correction's description tells it to call `run_repro` before editing and again after the fix. When an accepted result changed nothing or reports `already_satisfied` and a repro still reproduces, the host refuses it: the task restarts once in a fresh session that is told which finding still reproduces, and otherwise blocks the run with reason `invalid_output`. A correction no longer closes a reproduced finding as already satisfied and leaves it to the next review round. Each run is journaled as `tasks/TASK/repro-check-ID.json`.

**Repros that copy the code under test.** A repro must run the workspace's own code, so a fix can change its result. When one of its input files has the base name of the finding's file, of a file the run changed or (in the task-end check) of a file the task writes, and shares at least three of its code lines (trimmed lines of 16 characters or more; all of them when the input has fewer), the repro runs a copy of that file: the host returns `inconclusive` without running it and names the file. A reviewer's `run_repro` gets that verdict while it can still rewrite the repro against the live code (a single test, the package path, a project script, or a probe file that calls the code without copying it); a finding whose repro is a copy is gated like any inconclusive one, and a corrective is not held by it. narrowfit 3c2e3629 ran `go run {files}/sum.go`, a pasted copy of `internal/constraint/sum.go`: the fix could never change the copy's output, and the run spent 1.25M tokens and four operator commands before a replan (#1348).

**Repros bound to another worktree.** A repro's files and arguments name the workspace's code as `{root}`, which the host replaces with the root the repro runs in (`/`-separated in a file's content). When they name another root of the run instead, by its absolute path (the review's worktree `.baked/worktrees/<run>/main`, another task's worktree, or the workspace itself while the repro runs in a worktree), `runRepro` replaces that path with the root it runs in, keeping an argument's own separators, and lists the replaced paths in the outcome's `rewritten`. Every executor (the reviewer's and implementer's `run_repro`, the task-end repro check, the gates that rerun a finding's repro) goes through it, so a correction's run of a repro sees its own edits. A path outside the run's workspace is left alone. evalpin `checks` b38e180d: the reviewer's probe Cargo.toml named the review worktree as a path dependency, so the corrective T003's 18 runs of the repro built the unchanged code and reproduced after the fix, and the task spent 2.45M tokens on a one-line change (#2279).

**Repros blind to the correction (shadow).** A copy the host cannot rewrite (an installed package, a cached build output, a path through a symlink) still keeps a repro from seeing a fix. Each task-end repro check records the hash of the finding's file next to each outcome (`fileHash`), and compares each outcome with the task's previous repro check (#2287): when the repro still reproduces, the finding's file changed since that check, the output is byte for byte the same, and the repro's arguments or input files name an absolute path outside the root it runs in after the run's own roots are rewritten (`reproOutsidePaths`; `/dev/` and `/proc/` paths do not count), the repro is blind to the correction (`reproBlind` in [repro](../../src/core/verification/repro.ts)). The check lists such findings under `blind` and records a `repro_blind` would-stop naming the finding, the task and the outside paths. Shadow: the outcome stays `reproduced` and still sends the submission back; once the reason is promoted, such a repro is `inconclusive` for that attempt. R73 evalpin `checks` b38e180d spent 2.45M tokens on a probe that printed the same bytes after every fix. A task's first repro check has no previous output to compare with.

**Repros of documentation findings.** A finding about a document (a README, `.md`, `.mdx` or `.rst` file) whose repro never names that document runs code, so no edit of the document can change its output. Its repro still counts as evidence when the review gates the finding, but the task-end repro check and the corrective's `run_repro` leave it out: the corrective that rewrites the document is not sent back, and does not raise a spec conflict, because the code still prints what the finding itself says it does. The correction's description shows such a repro as evidence only, instead of telling the worker to run it before and after the fix (#1422). ranktally d8bc82f7 asked a person about such a repro, which expected `[]` where the claim said both tied candidates win (#1375).

When several test tasks carry one criterion, the task-end case check, live pricing, history samples and host split weights use the same case ownership. A row belongs to the first carrier whose description names its literal, using the same token matching as the case check. Code and documentation tasks do not claim rows. Unnamed rows go to the last test carrier in dependency order, with plan order breaking ties. Slices of the same original task divide unnamed rows by their `sizingSlice.share`, with the final slice receiving the rounding remainder. Row slices the host cut from a file's prescribed rows (`sizing.rowVolume`) carry their rows (`sizingSlice.rows`) and own a literal by those rows instead (#2466): the first slice whose rows hold it, since every later slice appends to the same file and finds it there; a literal no row holds (a criterion example, or a tuple spelled other than the rows' notation) goes to the last slice, whose file holds every row. In bench round 79 verhop T004 (rows 1-7 of `tests/check.bats`) was dealt 7/15 of AC-003's literals by its share, all of them in rows 10 and 13, and raised `spec_conflict` three times (946k tokens) before a person answered. Explicit row assignments also apply when a criterion exceeds the broad-criterion threshold above; unassigned repository criteria keep that exemption in the case check. Outside the broad-criterion gate exemption, a single test carrier keeps every row. Final checks and review still judge the whole criterion.

**Repros of findings about comments and docstrings.** The same holds for a finding about prose inside a source file: a docstring, a comment, or a bound or justification it states. Before a corrective task's first attempt, the host keeps the files its findings name (`tasks/TASK_ID/finding-files.json`). When the corrective's version of such a file differs from the kept one only in comments and docstrings, the task-end repro check does not run that finding's repro. It lists the finding under `evidenceOnly` in `tasks/TASK_ID/repro-check-*.json`, since no edit of the prose can change what the code prints. Comments are told apart from code per language with string literals kept whole: `#` for Python, Ruby, shell and Elixir, `--` and `--[[ ]]` for Lua, `//` and `/* */` for the other code languages (PHP takes `#` too), and a Python triple-quoted string that starts a statement counts as a docstring. Reviewers are told that such prose is wording and takes `noRepro`, and that a repro's `expected` is the value correct code gives, never the value the claim calls wrong. intsmith ca6f3023 asked a person after a repro expected the false period bound that the finding itself called wrong (#1517).

**Resume after a repeated invalid output.** A resume of a run blocked with `invalid_output` gives the next attempt the stop's diagnostic. When the run stopped again with that same diagnostic after the resume, another resume is refused (`STALE_DECISION`, reason `repeated_invalid_output`): it would repeat the attempt. "The same" means the same diagnostic with the same run inputs: the prompt (which `revise` changes) and the routing revision (which `reroute` changes; the same attempt on another model is not a repeat). When the attempts that ended in the stop carried writes the tool policy refused ("Create target already exists", "Modify target is missing", "File changed outside this attempt"), "the same" means the same sorted (refusal, path) pairs with the same routing revision, whatever the diagnostic says: a worker that restates one host refusal in new words repeats the attempt (bench round 82 slotprompt blocks: four attempts, each refused the same `create`, each blocked reason worded anew, [#2520](https://github.com/Gulliby-development/baked/issues/2520)). The key is built from host-observed facts, never from a model's wording; the diagnostic's hash keys it only when nothing was refused. Each resume that passes records the key in the `invalid-output/repeat-key.json` artifact; the note itself goes into the prompt once. Each `invalid_output` stop records its decision with its inputs under `repeats/invalid-output/DIAGNOSTIC.json` (kind `repeated-failure`), and `replayRepeatedFailures` decides it again (`kind: "invalid-output"`). So `baked workflow reroute RUN_ID --route STAGE=MODEL --resume` (or a reroute followed by a resume) drives the stage again, and a plain resume after the rerouted attempt stopped the same way is refused again. The message names the ways out: switch the failing stage to another model with `reroute`, revise the run, run an initiative feature again with `baked workflow initiative replan ID --feature F`, or cancel it; its recovery advice carries the `reroute`, `revise`, `replan` and `cancel` remedies. The run views know this before anyone presses retry: while a plain resume would be refused, the stop's recovery advice (`recovery` of `baked workflow status`, `/v1/workflow/runs/ID` and the run view) is that same advice, the terminal run view offers no retry and leads with switch model, and the desktop run screen says a retry would repeat the stop and points at the model switch ([#2201](https://github.com/Gulliby-development/baked/issues/2201)). The check reads the same repeat key and writes nothing.

**Recurring findings.** When a correction's finding names the same symbol (by its last name: `Parser.parseInline()` is `parseInline`, in the same file when both name one) as an earlier finding of the run that a finished correction already fixed, or names that finding in `repeatOf`, the correction gets `extra.recurringFindings`: the earlier findings' claims and repros, and the instruction to rewrite the symbol for its whole input class instead of adding another special case. The rewrite states the grammar or contract first and adds table-driven tests with one row per finding. The earlier findings' repros join the task-end repro check and `run_repro` as a regression set, so a rewrite that brings an earlier finding back is sent back like an unfixed one. md-site (bench round 33) patched its inline and front matter parsers once per review pass for five findings (5.44M tokens of corrections) before this. When a finished correction already fixed a finding on another symbol of the same file, the correction gets `extra.siblingFindings`, beside `extra.recurringFindings` when both apply (#1423): those findings' claims and repros, and the instruction to name the construct the reported case is about, make it work in every context it can appear in (a blockquote, a list item, a heading, nested in another construct, next to a line that looks like a block marker) and cover them in one table-driven test. Those repros join the regression set too. In bench round 39 md-site got three findings about multiline inline code in `src/markdown.ts` one review round after another, each on another function (1.85M tokens of corrections).

**Large check output.** The output of a check that `request_check` or `apply_fix` returns to the model is bounded at 40,000 characters. Longer output comes back as its first 4,000 characters, the lines naming failed tests in the part left out (`FAILED`, `ERROR`, `(fail)`, `--- FAIL`, `not ok`, `... FAILED`; at most 40), and its last 32,000 characters, with `truncated: true`, the full length in `outputChars` and the evidence ID that holds the whole output. A failing property test that prints a diff of megabytes of generated data therefore no longer overflows the model's input. The check artifact stores the output once, in `output`, which already interleaves standard output and standard error.

**Whole-unit formatters.** `cargo fmt` and `rustfmt` also follow every module of the crate, so before integration they report the files of every other task. They are not part of the task-end check, and a planned task's `request_check` of one is not run: it answers `status: "deferred"` with a note that the formatter runs in the checks stage. There, when it fails, the host applies its fixer (`cargo fmt`) to the files the run's tasks write, journaled as edits of the task that last writes each file, and runs the check again. A corrective task, which runs after the checks stage, runs it as usual.

An implementer that submits `blocked` or `failed` after its attempt applied file edits gets the submission back once, naming the files it wrote and saying that only BAKED's edit tools change files and no sandbox setting refuses them. Its next submission is accepted. This catches an agent that hit a refusal from a tool outside BAKED, such as Codex's own read-only sandbox, and reported "no changes were made" over edits BAKED had applied.

### Library docs

When `libraryDocs` is configured (global or project config; a run freezes it into its policy, and a policy that names its own source keeps it), each implementer task gets docs for the versions of the libraries it uses. The versions come from the workspace's lockfiles:

- `package-lock.json` and `bun.lock` (npm);
- `Cargo.lock`;
- `uv.lock` and `poetry.lock` (PyPI);
- the `require` lines of `go.mod`.

A task uses a library when a file it writes or reads already imports it (`import`/`require` in JavaScript, `use` or `crate::` in Rust, `import`/`from` in Python, a quoted module path in Go), or when its title or description names it. Each task gets docs for at most 3 libraries. A `dir` source reads `<name>@<version>.md` or `<name>/<version>.md` below the directory (absolute, relative to the workspace, or under `~`). An `mcp` source calls the tool of a server in `mcpServers` with `args`, replacing `{name}`, `{version}` and `{ecosystem}` (`npm`, `cargo`, `pypi`, `go`); without `args` it sends `{library, version}`. The docs reach the implementer as `libraryDocs` in its input, each cut at `maxChars`. Each lookup, found or not, is journaled as `tasks/TASK/library-docs-ID.json`. A library the source has no docs for is left out. Without a source nothing changes. `workflow doctor` warns when the directory does not exist or the MCP server is not configured or is disabled. The source is also a setting: `/code-quality` in the terminal (dialogs for the directory, or the server, tool and `;`-separated `key=value` arguments, and the cap), the desktop **Code quality** page, and `libraryDocs` in `PATCH /v1/settings`, each in the project or global config; the terminal row, the desktop page and `GET /v1/settings` show the same warning.

### The run's own edits and undo

A run keeps the edits it executed. A revision, a failed or interrupted task attempt and a fork leave the files as they are, and the runtime recognises content the run itself wrote by the effect journal:

- Plan consistency and the implementer accept a `create` target that exists only because this run, or the run it was forked from, created it and nothing changed it since. Any other existing file is still a conflict ("already exists; use modify").
- Before approval, a replacement plan removes dropped pending tasks and replaces their dependency edges, including sequential test slices. Findings remain on record, with their repair assignment cleared when the assigned task is removed. A package revision after approval retains dropped tasks as cancelled history and invalidates the remaining tasks or reuses their implementations as described below.
- A `modify` target that does not exist and that no earlier task (an ancestor in the dependency graph) creates is a plan problem the planner fixes before submitting ("does not exist and no earlier task creates it").
- A failed run-level check's finding goes to each planned task whose file the output names. Besides paths, the host reads the class names JVM and .NET test runners print instead of paths (#1270): a fully qualified class (`in com.acme.engine.QueryTest`) names the file at that package path, and a stack frame or failure line (`Query.java:57`, `[ERROR]   QueryTest.depthCuts:42`) names the one planned file with that name, when no other planned file shares it. The planned sources a named test file tests (`Query.java` for `QueryTest.java`) count as named too, each with its own finding, so the correction, its first attempt included, may write the code under test and not only the files of the tasks that cover the failing criteria.
- A corrective task writes each file of the tasks it corrects once. The action follows the file, not the order of the plan: a path a planned task creates is `modify` when the integrated change already has it and `create` while it does not, even when a later task of the plan modifies it, so a correction that runs before the creating task can create the file.
- The workspace check before each stage accepts a file that differs from the last snapshot when it holds exactly what the run's last journaled write produced, and records it in the expected baseline. Pausing or interrupting a task no longer blocks the resume with "Sources changed outside the active task"; a change by anyone else still does.
- A package revision implements again only the tasks it changed. When the plan and project instructions are unchanged, the spec is unchanged apart from added acceptance criteria (and its revision number), no task's write scope changed and the revision is not a definition revision, an implemented task whose definition is unchanged apart from criteria added to its `criterionIds` keeps its implementation, unless it depends on a task that is implemented again or shares a file with one. A changed or removed criterion, or a criterion dropped from a task, keeps nothing of the affected tasks. The criteria a kept task gained are covered by the run's checks, review and convergence. Its evidence is invalidated like all other evidence: after the new approval the implementation stage runs its task checks again without calling the implementer, and the check stage reruns every check on the current snapshot. A policy-only revision, such as a changed check command, therefore reruns the checks and implements nothing.
- A planned test task whose test check fails waits for an open correction of a sibling test task when its failing test files import a source that the sibling's failing tests import too: one defect in that source fails both. Its check runs again once that correction is done, and a failure that remains then gets a correction of its own (url-shortener b69757cd: one defect, two parallel test tasks, two correctives).
- A repeated task receives the paths that still contain its earlier attempt's edits (`earlierEdits` in its input) and must read them before editing. Those edits count as the task's changes, so a repeated attempt that finds the work already done does not fail with "did not complete its implementation". Its edit tools accept those files as its own: a file whose content is the last journaled write of an earlier attempt of the same task, with no write after it, is not refused as "File changed outside this attempt". Nor is a file that is back to its content from before the task first wrote it (for example missing again, when a restarted task gets a rebuilt worktree without the file an earlier attempt created): the earlier write no longer counts, and the attempt creates or edits the file as if the task had not touched it. The same holds for a file that holds any other state the task's uncommitted writes passed through, such as the integrated file after the task was invalidated more than once, or the first attempt's content after a second lost worker. It also holds for a check fixer's output from another root: when the checks stage's fixer rewrote the task's file in the integration root and the task restarts in a worktree rebuilt from it, the formatted file counts as the task's own, and so does a file that holds the run's recorded baseline for it (another task or a fixer changed it in the integration root after the task's last write), so a plain resume continues. `workflow undo` restores such a file too: content a check fixer journaled for the path in another root counts as the run's version, not as a change made outside the run. A fixer that formats more than the path it is given (`cargo fmt -- <path>` formats the whole crate) has each file it rewrites journaled as that file's own edit with its real content before, so undo and the hash check see every change it made. A plain resume after an attempt-cap stop takes this path in a run with several writers: the stopped task is invalidated and restarts in a fresh worktree from the run's content. An edit the hash check does refuse is not retried for ever: at the third refusal of the same path in one attempt the attempt stops with `input_changed` and names the path, instead of reading and retrying until its token cap.
- A `delete` write is done with `delete_file`. Its `expectedHash` is optional: the implementer passes the `hash:` footer that `read` printed for the file (the file's SHA-256, or a prefix of it of at least 16 characters, is accepted too), and a hash that no longer matches the file refuses the delete as `Delete target changed`. Without `expectedHash` the delete has the same guard as `edit_file`: it is refused when the file changed outside this attempt. The deleted content is kept as a `deleted-source` artifact for undo.

Failed task checks, reported defects and `request_check` use the same failure ownership decision. When every failing file is a test with an unfinished planned writer, the host hands the failure to that writer, even if it depends on the reporting task. Task checks record `tasks/handoffs/TASK_ID.json`; reported-defect diagnostics record `handoffs` with the check, evidence and owner IDs. This applies to path diagnostics and JVM or .NET class diagnostics. The checks stage verifies the integrated result after all planned tasks finish.

When output names a production file outside the test task's writes that a finished task of the same profile owns, the host opens a correction without continuing the test task, including after a cutoff. Its first scope contains the named production files and the failing tests. Only output without named production owners falls back to the tests' import closure. Sibling corrections use the same preference, so Go package imports and JVM package references do not merge failures with distinct named owners. A failed `request_check` includes `failureOwner` and advice to submit the required tests and let the owner settle the failure. The advice grants no additional write access. A summary report can also route to an owner with a different profile; that owner's correction uses its own approved paths.

A wrong-value assertion names only the test file and its line, never the source it calls, so that rule kept the failure with the test task: namesound soundex T003 (bench round 70) ran two sessions (545k tokens) on a spec whose three examples expected the criterion's `"B1905"` while the finished T002's `RefinedSoundex.key` returned another string (#2220). When the ordinary owner of a planned test task's failure is the task itself, the host reads the expected side of every assertion the output prints (rspec, bun and Jest, Minitest, testify, xUnit, JUnit, Dart, alcotest, ExUnit and Rust `right:`, Go `want`, pytest `assert a == b`, XCTest, PHPUnit, Zig) and compares each with the case walk's literals of the task's criteria (`criterionExpected` in `src/core/workflows/source-defect.ts`). When every expected value is one of them and the finished planned tasks of the same profile that carry those criteria wrote sources the failing tests import, `sourceDefectDecision` routes the failure to those tasks as the `finished` owner above: no continuation of the test task, also after a cutoff, and its corrective writes the tests and those sources at the first failing check. A test whose expected value is not a criterion literal, or whose framework prints no expected side (GoogleTest, Catch2), keeps the current path. Each decision records `source-defect/tasks/TASK_ID-SIGNATURE.json` (kind `source-defect`, kept in bench traces: counts, task ids and paths, no literals). With the policy field `sourceDefectRoute: "on"` (setting `quality.sourceDefectRoute`, default `off`; absent means off, so stored policies keep their hash) the route applies; with the field off the record is shadow and the test task keeps the failure. `replaySourceDefects` decides each record again (`newSourceDefectRoutes`).

A task-level repair runs before ready planned tasks that depend on the files it repairs. Unrelated tasks can still run in parallel. Its test write scope includes only files already written by finished tasks, so it does not create a later task's tests. If another task uses files the repair writes and its assertion values or message match the recorded command evidence of an open finding with an unfinished repair, its first failed check defers to that repair. The host checks it again after the repair finishes without spending another implementation attempt. Test names and diagnostic locations do not identify the shared failure. These rules apply through the shared core to the terminal app, headless CLI, local API and desktop app.

Undo is an explicit command:

```sh
baked workflow undo RUN_ID --expected-revision N [--path PATH]
```

It restores the files changed by the run's uncommitted task edits: edits of a task that has no snapshot because its attempt failed or was interrupted, a revision invalidated it or dropped it, or the run was cancelled. A task's edits are committed when the runtime snapshots them after the task; to undo implemented work, apply a revision that changes the task first (a task the revision keeps stays committed). Before each task edit the runtime stores the file's previous content as a `pre-image` artifact. Undo restores a file only while it still holds exactly what the run wrote last, and never overwrites a later change by a person or another tool. Before replacing a file, it keeps the run's version as a `rolled-back` artifact. Each restore is a journaled `file_patch` effect with the tool `rollback`, and the outcome is an immutable `workspace-rollback` artifact that lists restored files and the files left in place with the reason. The command prints that report. In the TUI the run screen does the same with U (see [TUI](#tui)). Files restored or left in place are settled and are not reported again; a file whose restore failed, or whose edit has an unknown outcome, is retried by the next undo. The expected baseline follows each restored path, so the next resume does not treat the restore as an outside change. The run must not be running: undo takes the run's execution and mutation leases.

When a repair task for a review or check finding passes its task checks, it restores the task it corrects, and the tasks that depend on that task are looked at again. A dependent is invalidated (its agent runs again) only when the repair changed a file it names: the file is among its writes or read hints. A dependent that writes only documentation files (`.md`, `.mdx`, `.rst`, `.txt`, `.adoc`) is reached only that way, so a documentation writer whose read hints do not list the changed file is not rerun. A dependent that merely imports a changed file (a relative import that resolves to it, or a dotted module name that ends in its path) keeps its implementation when a check guards it: a check it declares (any scope) or a required feature-scope check of the run. The host runs its task checks again, the checks stage runs the feature checks on the result, and a failure becomes a repair task; only an unguarded dependent runs its agent again (cfgtrace round 28: a one-file correction of a shared module reran five tasks for 2.0M tokens). A dependent that names a changed file but whose every written file the corrective tasks of the run rewrote (a deletion aside) is superseded: when it declares any check, of any scope (a test task whose only check is a feature-scope build such as Maven `verify` counts), it keeps its implementation and only runs its checks, because its agent would start from its original description and overwrite the correction (md-site round 6: one planned task ran three times, another four). A dependent that does not touch the changed files keeps its implementation and only runs its task checks again, with no agent call. A planned documentation task (every file it writes is documentation, a licence or a notice) keeps its implementation the same way unless the corrective tasks changed a file it writes, even when its read hints name the corrected code: the review stages still read the README and run its examples in the host (idpeek round 31: a README and licence task reran twice after correctives that changed only error handling, 645k tokens). Whenever a planned task that was implemented before is invalidated and ready to run again, the host runs its declared checks (any scope) first, in one-writer and parallel runs alike; when its earlier implementation passed its task checks under the same definition (a revision that changed the task always implements it again), they all pass and its written files exist, the task is implemented by supersession with an empty diff and no agent call (`tasks/Tn/host-first-G.json` records the outcome for generation G), and its gate check runs as for any implemented task. When a check fails, the agent runs as before. The changed files come from the `tasks/Tn/diff.json` artifacts of the repair tasks; when one is missing, or a written file is not a source file the scan understands, every dependent is invalidated as before. Dependents of an invalidated task are invalidated too. An invalidated planned task that writes only documentation (Markdown, a README, a licence, a changelog) waits while a corrective task is running or ready to run, and runs again once after them: a README cannot be right before the last source correction of the pass (cfgtrace round 26: one README and licence task ran four times for 902k tokens). An invalidated planned task that declares checks and whose earlier implementation passed its gate under the same definition waits the same way, so its host-run checks see the result of every pending corrective: a whole-project check such as `pytest -q` or `go test ./...` stays red until the last of them lands, and the agent reran tasks that were already right (zonevet and ecfit in round 30, 1.07M tokens). A corrective that failed or is blocked is not waited for.

A task whose implementation did not complete (the implementer reported `blocked`, `failed` or an `unresolved` item, or its result was rejected) stops the run `blocked` with reason `invalid_output`; the diagnostic names the unresolved items, or, when the task reported `implemented` and changed none of its files, why that was not accepted (a planned task's first attempt that changed nothing, or the check that still fails with its evidence id and the files it names). A task that reported `implemented`, changed its files and lists as unresolved only failing tests or lint findings (up to five items, each naming a failing test, check or linter, or a rule code such as `ISC004`) is accepted instead: its task checks run as usual, and when no task check ran, each item that names no other planned task's file becomes a `partial` finding on the task's file, with `tasks/TASK_ID/unresolved.json` as evidence, and a corrective task fixes it. An item that names another task's file (a README assertion before the README task ran) is left to the checks stage and review. The task is not restarted for such a report, which a second attempt repeated before the run blocked. The implementer is told that a defect in a file outside its write scope is not unresolved: it finishes its own files and reports `implemented`, the failing task check becomes a corrective task, and that correction may write the files of the planned tasks that share its criteria. A required task check of a planned task that fails only on planned files that tasks still to run own (a file importing a module of a later task; none of the files named is the task's own, and no owner depends on the task), or that fails while a task still to run writes a file in its `readHints` (a type error names the importing file, which is the task's own), starts no corrective task: the task stays implemented without its check gate, the failing evidence stays in the run, and the check runs again once those tasks are done (`deferredChecks` in the run state). When no task can run any more, the wait ends and the check runs and is repaired as usual. A required task check of a planned task that fails only on existing test files that a planned task still to run rewrites (the plan changes a signature in T001 and updates the expectations in T003, which depends on T001) starts no corrective task either, and nothing waits: the task gets its check gate, the failure is recorded as `tasks/handoffs/T001.json` (`taskId`, `checkId`, `evidenceId`, `owners`), and the tests are the owner's to settle: its own task checks, and the checks stage, run them after it. This holds for any task check (type check, tests, affected tests): where the output locates failures in lines of their own (`tests/cli.test.ts(258,45): error TS2769`, a test file header, a `FAIL` line), only those files count, not the source files that indented related-information lines or stack frames name. Two test runners locate assertions differently and count too: `go test` prints an indented base name under `--- FAIL` (`    cli_test.go:42:`), which names the planned test file of that name, or, when several share it, the one in the directory of the `FAIL` package line; and a Rust test's `panicked at tests/cli.rs:12:5` names that file. A crate's `tests/*.rs` files are test files for this rule. When the failures are also located in another file, such as the task's own source, or the owner is already done, the failure is the task's own as before. The corrective task of a task that broke existing tests leaves alone the tests that planned tasks still to run update. `resume` sets such a task, which has no snapshot, back to `pending`, and the next attempt continues on the edits already in place. A typical recovery from a rejected implementation that needs a different plan: `candidate`, `revise`, optionally `undo` to start the tasks from the original files, then `resume`.

### Test integrity

Agents game tests: they delete or skip them, weaken assertions, special-case test inputs and add suppressions, and prompts against it barely help. The runtime guards the tests itself ([test-integrity](../../src/core/workflows/test-integrity.ts)). The guard is always on; the policy field `testIntegrity` only chooses what its findings do: `block` (the default; absent means `block`, so stored policies keep their hash) or `warn`. A run chooses it at start with `baked workflow run --test-integrity block|warn`, the Tests field of the TUI new-run form, the "Only warn about test gaming" checkbox of the desktop New run screen, or `testIntegrity` in `POST /v1/workflow/runs`; each overrides the policy for that run. Only `warn` is stored in the run's policy.

- **Existing tests are read-only.** A test file that existed before the run (a test path no task of the approved graph creates: `*.test.*`, `*.spec.*`, `*_test.go`, `test_*.py`, `*Test.java`, `*Tests.cs`, files under `tests/`, `test/` or `__tests__/` and similar) can be modified or deleted by an implementer only when the plan gives that write a `reason` (`{"path", "action", "reason"}` in `tasks.json`), which the approval covers. Otherwise the edit is refused with `CAPABILITY_DENIED` and the implementer is told to make the code pass the test. A plan that modifies or deletes an existing test without a reason is returned to the planner at submission. Corrections and integrators keep the reason of the write they repeat. Under `warn` neither applies. A name its language shares with domain code (`PermTest.cs`, `FeatureSpec.kt`, `perm_test.rs`) outside a test directory counts as a test only when the existing file holds a test marker of its language (a test attribute, a test function or a test framework's import; `TEST_MARKERS` in `src/core/intelligence/language-traits.ts`), so a source stub named like a test is the implementer's to edit, and the written-tests check does not run it (#1848). A file that does not exist yet keeps the name rule.
- **Diff check.** After each task (and after each integration in a run with several writers) the host compares every changed file with its content before the task and records what it finds in `tasks/TASK_ID/integrity.json`: a deleted test file, fewer tests or fewer assertions in a test file (assertions include FluentAssertions `.Should()` and Kotest `shouldBe`-style matchers; tests include Rust `#[case]` rows; a line that starts with `#` is a comment except a Rust attribute, a C or C++ `#if`, `#ifdef` or `#elif`, and Swift's `#if os(...)`, `#elseif`, `#expect` and `#require`, #1492), a test that is gone by name while the totals stayed level (`test-removed`: a `def test_*`, `func Test*`, a Go subtest `t.Run("name", ...)` (named `TestX/name`), an `it`/`test` title, a Kotest spec block (`"name" { }`, `should("name")`), or the function under `#[test]`, `#[tokio::test(...)]`, `#[rstest]`, `#[test_case(...)]`, `@Test` or `[Fact]`, so a strong test swapped for a weaker one under another name is caught; a removed name is also listed in a `test-count` finding; bats `@test "name"`, GoogleTest `TEST(Suite, Name)`, Catch2 `TEST_CASE("name")` and Unity `void test_name(void)` titles are read too, while the titles of PHPUnit `function testName()`, ExUnit `test "name" do`, zig `test "name" {`, Alcotest `test_case "name"`, OUnit `"name" >::`, ppx `let%test "name"`, Objective-C `- (void)testName` and Foundry `function test_Name()` are read only in shadow (#2147): the guard measures each scan again with them and records in `integrity/decisions/` (`shadowTitles`) the findings they would add or clear on files of those languages, counted nowhere until a bench round shows what they raise; a test that is gone by name while a new test keeps at least 60 percent of its lines, or every string literal it compared against, is a retitle or a merge and not a removal, and tests and assertions fewer only because cases were merged into one table test are not reported) (commented-out ones do not count; not reported for a write with a reason; fewer assertions are not reported either in a test file that a correction's own finding names when that finding is `contradicts` or names a lint rule, such as ruff `BLE001`, as long as every test case stays and the file still asserts; the same holds when that finding says the test checks too little, a finding of kind `partial` or `missing` or a claim that the test omits the expected output, only counts or only checks a prefix, provided the file also holds more expected literal text than before, as when three `toContain` checks become one comparison with the full output), new `skip`, `only`, `todo`, `xit`, `@pytest.mark.skip`, `#[ignore]`, `t.Skip`, pgTAP's `SELECT skip(` and `SELECT todo_start(`, Pester's `-Skip` and `Set-ItResult -Skipped`, testthat's `skip()` family (`skip_on_os`, `skip_if_not_installed`, `skip_if_offline` and `skip_on_cran` count as capability guards) and similar markers, and in Ruby files RSpec's `xit "..."`, `xdescribe`, `fit`, `pending`, `skip: "..."` and `:focus` metadata and Minitest's `skip`, PHPUnit's `markTestSkipped` and `markTestIncomplete` and Pest's `->skip()` and `->todo()` (#1491), in Dart files package:test's `skip:` argument (not `skip: false`), `@Skip`, `solo: true` and `markTestSkipped`, in Lua files busted's `pending(...)` and LuaUnit's `lu.skip` (#1495), and in shell files bats' `skip` and shunit2's `startSkipping` (#1497), and in C and C++ files GoogleTest's `GTEST_SKIP()` and `DISABLED_` names, Catch2's `SKIP()` and hidden `[.]` tag, Unity's `TEST_IGNORE()` and CMocka's `skip()` (#1490), and in Scala files ScalaTest's `ignore("...")`, `it should "..." ignore`, `pending` and `cancel()` and munit's `test("...".ignore)` and `.only` (#1496), and in Swift files `XCTSkip`, `.disabled(...)`, `XCTExpectFailure` and `withKnownIssue` (#1492) (a framework's own spellings are a column of the test-framework table, read only in files of its language, #1493, #1494) (except a capability guard: a skip whose line or the three lines above it check the platform, such as `runtime.GOOS`, `sys.platform`, `RUBY_PLATFORM`, `Gem.win_platform?`, `PHP_OS_FAMILY`, `extension_loaded(...)`, Dart's `Platform.isWindows` or Flutter's `kIsWeb`, Lua's `package.config` or `jit.os`, `uname`, `$OSTYPE`, `command -v`, a preprocessor guard on `_WIN32`, `__APPLE__` or `__linux__`, Scala's `Properties.isWin`, Swift's `#if os(...)` and `#if canImport(...)`, or a call the machine may not allow, such as a failed `os.Symlink`, `exec.LookPath` or an `ImportError`, so the test still runs where it can), new suppressions in any file, by the spellings of every language in `SUPPRESSION_SPELLINGS` ([suppressions](../../src/core/workflows/quality/suppressions.ts), #2274), each matched in every file since one file can hold several languages (`@ts-ignore`, `@ts-expect-error`, `eslint-disable`, `biome-ignore`, `oxlint-disable`, `prettier-ignore`, istanbul, c8 and v8 `ignore` hints, `any` in TypeScript, Svelte's `svelte-ignore`, `# type: ignore`, `# noqa`, `# pylint: disable`, `# pyright: ignore`, `# mypy: ignore-errors`, `# pragma: no cover`, Go's `//nolint` and staticcheck's `//lint:ignore`, `#[allow(...)]`, `#[expect(...)]` and `#[cfg_attr(..., allow(...))]`, `@SuppressWarnings`, `@SuppressFBWarnings`, `// CHECKSTYLE:OFF`, `// NOPMD`, `NOSONAR`, Kotlin's `@Suppress` and `@file:Suppress` (read by detekt too) and `ktlint-disable`, `#pragma warning disable`, `[SuppressMessageAttribute(...)]` in C# and PowerShell, `#nullable disable`, `// ReSharper disable`, `# rubocop:disable`, `# standard:disable`, `@phpstan-ignore`, `@psalm-suppress`, `phpcs:ignore`, `@codeCoverageIgnore`, Dart's `// ignore:` and `// ignore_for_file:`, `-- luacheck: ignore`, `-- selene: allow`, `# shellcheck disable=`, `// NOLINT`, `#pragma GCC diagnostic ignored`, MSVC's `#pragma warning(disable: ...)`, `// cppcheck-suppress`, Scala's `@nowarn`, `// scalafix:off` and `// scalastyle:off`, `// swiftlint:disable` and `// swift-format-ignore`, credo's `# credo:disable-for-next-line` and `@dialyzer`, OCaml's `[@@@warning "-32"]`, ReScript's `@@warning("-27")`, solhint's and slither's disable comments, SQLFluff's `-- noqa` and lintr's `# nolint`; Zig has none), branches on running under a test runner (`NODE_ENV === "test"`, `process.env.VITEST`, `"pytest" in sys.modules`, `Rails.env.test?`, Flutter's `FLUTTER_TEST`, XCTest's `XCTestConfigurationFilePath` and similar), comparisons in source files with a literal that a test file of the task uses as input and that the request (with its fenced examples), the spec and the plan do not name, so a message, directive or flag the brief prescribes is not a test special case, and new `--no-verify` or `--passWithNoTests`. A correction also records the tests it added, by file and name, with the findings it corrects (`tasks/TASK_ID/tests.json`); a later correction of other findings that changes such a file and leaves one of those tests out gets a `test-removed` finding naming the task and finding that added it, unless its own finding has `basis: spec` and explicitly requests removal or relocation of those tests. Names in the claim or suggested fix and identifier patterns such as `lint_adapter_*` select the tests. Only their removal is excluded from the test and assertion counts; unrelated tests and assertions stay protected. When no names match, an explicit instruction to remove or move the tests of the named file permits the file-wide count change or deletion. A scope claim without a removal instruction grants no exception. A replacement is decided on the diff, not on the fix's wording (#2139): a removed test that a spec-basis finding on the file names in its claim (quoted, or as a whole word when the title is no plain word: it has a space, an underscore, a digit or an inner capital) is replaced, not removed, when the same change adds a test under a new title to the file, one new title for each replaced test, whatever verb the fix uses ("add", "replace", "rewrite", "test the facade instead"); a fix that says not to remove tests grants nothing. Its cases and assertions leave the counts like a requested removal, it is not lost when a correction of another finding added it, and the decision record keeps the count and the finding ids (`replacedTests`, `replacedBy`). A correction whose spec-basis finding says the tests generate what the request wants as literal rows ("construct inputs and expected results rather than using the required literal object rows", interpolation, loops, a table-driven test; `consolidateTargets`, #2467) may merge tests into literal tables: fewer test functions are not a `test-count` or `test-removed` finding while the file still declares a test and keeps at least nine tenths of its assertions, which also exempts that drop from the `assertions` rule (R79 deltaglue chunks: T003 merged 10 generated tests into 5 tables, 66 assertions to 64, and the guard's finding opened T004, which restored them: 8.28M tokens). ewvet T003 replaced a test that finding F1 said checked the wrong path, F1's fix said "add", and three correctives restored one test or the other (587k tokens). Other conflicts still require restoration or `spec_conflict` naming both findings. The finding's fix lists every such test of the file, so a repair does not trade one for another. A retitled or merged test is recorded with its new name (`renamed` in `tests.json`) and keeps its protection under it, and the tests added for findings that a settled conflict decided (an assumption under `clarification: assume` or a person's answer) are no longer protected, since the settlement may change them. Each rule counts occurrences before and after, so code that was already there is never reported, and documentation files are not checked for suppressions. A suppression the request, a person's accepted answer, the spec or the plan names verbatim is prescribed, not added (#2270): the text from the suppression to the end of its line, or the whole line, with whitespace collapsed, occurs in that text and no word before it forbids it (`not`, `never`, `no`, `without`, `avoid`, `instead of`). A bare marker such as `# noqa` is never prescribed; one with its arguments, such as `#[allow(unused_variables)]` or `# noqa: ARG001`, is. When a person answers a task's spec conflict whose findings include a suppression finding on a file, later scans of either scope no longer count suppressions in that file. In R72 evalpin foundation the brief prescribed a cli stub that `cargo clippy -D warnings` rejects for its unused parameters; the implementer added `#[allow(unused_variables)]`, every scan counted it again although a person answered twice that the request wins, and six correctives ended in `no_progress`. The counts read code and comments only: in a code file the content of string literals and docstrings is left out (by the comment and string syntax of the file's language, including Python triple quotes, JavaScript templates, Go backticks, Rust raw strings and C# verbatim strings), so test data such as `"custom_value: any text"`, a fixture holding `# noqa` or `#[allow(dead_code)]`, or a test runner's test quoted as text is no suppression, test or skip; test titles and a short word such as `"test"` in `NODE_ENV === "test"` are still read, and `--no-verify` and `--passWithNoTests` count inside strings too, since a command in a string runs. md-site 43b0daed paid two correctives (448k tokens) for a front-matter test string read as a `: any` annotation (#1549). Under `block` every finding becomes a task failure (`Test integrity (RULE): task T001 ... in PATH`) that a correction task must undo, like a failed task check; under `warn` the record is all. The checks stage repeats the scan at feature scope, over every file the run changed from its content before the run (the effect journal and the task diffs) to now, and records it in `integrity/run.json`; under `block` what it finds (`Test integrity (RULE): the run ... in PATH`) is repaired like a failed check, from the task that writes the file, so a problem a correction left in place or one spread over several tasks does not pass. Every scan, at either scope and whether or not it finds anything, also records the counts and flags it decided from, without names or code, in `integrity/decisions/` (kind `test-integrity`); bench traces keep them, and the replay corpus decides them again on the current rules (#1715). A task whose files no session edits (a host-written task, one that may write nothing, or a correction whose failing checks already pass) records a decision with `skipped` (`host-written`, `no-writes`, `checks-passing`) instead of no record at all (#2277). Each record also lists under `shadowPrescribed` the `test-hook` and `verification-bypass` findings that a prescribed exemption would clear: the request, an accepted answer, the spec or the plan names the hook's or the bypass's whole line, or the quoted command holding it (`"vitest run --passWithNoTests"`), not to forbid it; those findings stay live until bench rounds show the exemption is right (#2277).
- **Per-file test caps.** A brief that caps the tests of a file or a class ("No file has more than 12 `test(...)` blocks", "at most 12 test methods per class", "each test file has at most 12 tests", or `maxTestsPerFile: 12` as data) is read from the request, spec and plan (`testCapsOf` in [test-cap](../../src/core/verification/test-cap.ts), #2368); the lowest cap of each scope wins. Tests are counted by declaration with the `TEST_DECLARATIONS` record ([test-declarations](../../src/core/intelligence/test-declarations.ts)), one row per supported language over `maskNonCode` text: Dart `test(` and `testWidgets(` blocks, TypeScript and JavaScript `test(` and `it(`, Python `def test_`, Rust `#[test]`, Go `func Test` (not `TestMain` or `t.Run` subtests), Java and Kotlin `@Test`, C# `[Fact]`, `[Theory]`, `[Test]` and `[TestMethod]` (not their `[InlineData]` or `[TestCase]` rows), and so on; a per-class cap counts the tests below each class declaration, up to the next. At task end, after the case walk, each test file the task writes is counted against the caps and the check is recorded in `test-cap/tasks/TASK_ID.json` (counts and caps, no code, kept in bench traces and replayed by `replayTestCaps`). Under the `test_cap` reason's shadow default a file or class over its cap records a would-stop and the submission is accepted; `stopModes: { test_cap: "enforce" }` sends the submission back naming the file, its count and the cap, twice at most, and the same list is not refused twice. The guard's `test-count` rule reads the same cap: a correction may drop the tests above it (`capExcess` in the file's integrity measure) and their assertions without a finding, so a correction that brings 13 tests down to 12 under a cap of 12 passes and one that drops to 11 is still a `test-count` finding. R75 latcell's commands feature spent 1.86M tokens on three correctives over a cap of 12 `test(...)` blocks that only the reviewer counted.
- **Spec conflicts.** A conflict naming both a test-integrity host finding and a spec-basis finding with confidence 1 that explicitly requests the same test removals is settled for the spec, including under `clarification: ask`. The host records `assumptions/conflicts/TASK_ID.json` with the authorizing spec finding IDs, decides only the host findings, and retries the task with those narrow removal permissions. A repeated conflict still follows the normal clarification rules. An implementer that finds that the spec and a test (or a criterion) cannot both hold submits the outcome `spec_conflict` with the test, the requirement and why, and each side in `conflictSides` with its exact words: a criterion or a requirement by id (`ref`) quoting the spec, the request quoting the request (an example it gives counts), a test by its path. Under `clarification: assume` the host first decides whether the request settles the conflict, on quotes it checks itself and on every report of the task, not on the report's wording (#2016). A criterion side counts when its quote is in that criterion's given, when or then, a requirement side when its quote is in the requirement, and a request side when its quote, at least 12 characters, is in the request or a person's accepted answer (matched as the derivation audit matches quotes) and in none of the cited criteria. A report without `conflictSides` has as sides the automated criteria it names by id and each span it quotes (double quotes, backticks or typographic quotes) that the request holds and no cited criterion does. When every request side checks out and a criterion or requirement side does too, the request wins at once. When the report cites criteria but no request side checks out, the analyze profile re-audits the cited criteria against the request once per task (step `reaudit-request-TASK_ID`, its derivations in `tasks/conflicts/TASK_ID-request-reaudit.json`, the same input as the derivation re-audit below with its own note), and a criterion whose re-audit entry does not match and quotes the request, with the quote found in the request, loses to the request. A test file of the conflict that the run did not write, or that was written first and approved by a person, leaves the decision to the paths below. When the request wins, `assumptions/conflicts/TASK_ID-request.json` records the checked sides and the re-audit's verdicts, `amendments/conflict-TASK_ID.json` an amendment with authority `request` for the overruled criteria, and `decisions/clarification-request-TASK_ID.json` the overruled criteria (`criterionIds`), so the case walk stops demanding their literals; the task runs again told to follow the request, to change only an assertion this run wrote that follows the overruled criterion, and to report `implemented` or `already_satisfied` when its tests already assert what the request states. Each report's decision records its inputs in `conflicts/request/TASK_ID-N.json` (kind `request-conflict`: each side's kind, ref and whether its quote was found, never the quote; the foreign tests, the cited criteria, the re-audit's verdicts and the decision), which bench traces keep and `replayRequestConflicts` decides again. R65 uritpl cli T003: AC-005 gave column 6 where the request's example shows name columns (5); the first report said "the detailed contract defines", the host settled for the run's own test instead, the second report said "the request defines" after the one-shot rule was spent, and a person was asked (1.42M tokens, 19.7 minutes). Then the host compares the report with the criteria it names by id (#1709). When a test file the task writes spells a quoted literal of such a criterion with a small change (a string within a few characters of it, escapes aside, while no file holds it as written: a dropped `\!`, flag or space), the conflict is not one: `assumptions/conflicts/TASK_ID-misquoted.json` records the literal and the test's spelling, and the task runs again told to copy the row as the criterion states it, with the criterion quoted verbatim. With the policy field `misquoteFix: "on"` (setting `quality.misquoteFix`, default `off`; absent means off, so stored policies keep their hash) the host corrects the test itself when the replacement is exact and unambiguous (#2047): every misquoted literal's spelling occurs exactly once in the task's test and row data files, holds no backslash, the criterion's literal uses only characters the spelling already holds or ones no string literal of C#, Go, Java, JavaScript, Python or Rust escapes or interpolates (no quote, backslash, `$`, braces or `%` of its own), and the files then hold the literal as written. The host replaces the spelling as a journaled edit of the attempt and runs the task's required checks on it (the checks the task ends with; with none, nothing counts as evidence). When they all pass, the misquote record lists the edited files under `hostFix` and the task goes on as `implemented` through its usual task checks, reusing those runs, with no second session; when one fails, the edit is put back and the task runs again as above. The decision is `misquoteFixDecision` in `src/core/workflows/misquote-fix.ts`, and every misquote settle records its inputs in `conflicts/misquote/TASK_ID.json` (kind `misquote-fix`: per literal the occurrence count and whether the spelling is unescaped, the literal plain and held after the replacement, never the text; the mode, `shadow` when the field is off, the verdict and reason, the check outcome and the edited files), which bench traces keep and `replayMisquoteFixes` decides again (`newMisquoteFixes`). With the field on, a misquote the host does not correct (an escaped, ambiguous or missing spelling, a literal that needs escaping, or a host edit whose required check failed and was put back) is corrected by a bounded continuation of the task instead of a full new session (#2058): the record's `fallback` is `continue`, the next attempt runs at most 8 steps and 150,000 tokens (`MISQUOTE_CONTINUE_STEPS`, `MISQUOTE_CONTINUE_TOKENS`, lowered to the policy's caps) and gets `misquoteContinue` (the one-line finding naming each criterion, its literal and the test's spelling, and the failing check's output when the host's edit failed it), recorded as `tasks/TASK_ID/misquote-continue.json`. A continuation that does not finish, or reports a conflict again, falls back once to the full cap; a task that already used its continuation, and every shadow decision, open the full session as before (`fallback: "session"`). A best-of-N candidate's misquote is decided per candidate in its own worktree, before selection (#2181), and recorded as `conflicts/misquote/TASK_ID-cN.json`: with the field on, a host edit whose required checks pass makes it a finished candidate; a candidate is never continued, so one the host does not correct stays a conflict (`fallback: "session"`) and the chosen one is settled at integration as before. The other settles that change one assertion take the same fallback rule (#2181, `settleFallback`): when the audit-verified rows win (`assumptions/conflicts/TASK_ID-audited.json`) or the request wins (`assumptions/conflicts/TASK_ID-request.json`) and the settle names exactly one criterion, the host finishing nothing, the task's next attempt continues at the same bounded cap with the settlement as its `misquoteContinue` finding (and a note for that settle kind); several criteria, or a task that already continued, open the full session. Each such settle records `conflicts/misquote/TASK_ID-SETTLE.json` (kind `settle-continue`: the settle kind `audited` or `request`, the mode, `shadow` when the field is off, the criteria and the `fallback`). A conflict the host settles for the request (on checked quotes or the first report's wording), for the requirements (a report that names both, or the derivation re-audit) or for the platform does not open another session when the reporting attempt's tree already does what the settlement asks (#2033, R65 uritpl cli T003: the test already asserted the request's column and the session after the settlement, 410,310 tokens, only recorded that). With the policy field `conflictFinish: "on"` (setting `quality.conflictFinish`, default `off`; absent means off, so stored policies keep their hash), when the attempt changed files, the host walks the task's criteria cases with the overruled criteria released and finds every case in its test files, finds no expected value of an overruled criterion (its `then`, minus literals a criterion that holds also names) still in them, and finds the host's row blocks intact, it runs the task's required checks on the attempt's tree (the written-tests check among them, results reused by the task checks). When at least one ran and all pass, the task goes on as implemented without another session and the host records `tasks/TASK_ID/host-finish.json` with the settlement's path, the checks and their evidence; otherwise the settlement's session opens as before. A settlement that asks for an edit (a test the run wrote, or another task's test, that contradicts the spec), a documentation example and a reviewer's repro never finish this way. The decision is `conflictFinishDecision` in `src/core/workflows/conflict-finish.ts`, and every such settlement records its inputs in `conflicts/finish/TASK_ID-BRANCH.json` (kind `conflict-finish`: the branch, the edit, case, overruled-literal and check counts and statuses, never the text; the mode, `shadow` when the field is off, which records the walk and runs no check), which bench traces keep and `replayConflictFinishes` decides again (`newConflictFinishes`). A task worktree with several writers settles the conflict in its worker only with `misquoteFix` or `conflictFinish` on, so the shadow record there comes from tasks that run alone. On the parallel task path the worker settles the conflict when the field is on, so it goes on in its worktree; candidate runs correct a misquote on the host per candidate with the field on and leave every other settle to the integration step. R66 nodepluck eval T004 spelled AC-007's query with ten `[0]` segments where the criterion has eleven, and the second session that changed the literal cost 1.23M tokens. When the derivation audit recomputed every row of the named criteria from the requirements and they all match, the host does not take the analyzer's arithmetic over the implementer's run on trust (#1788): the analyze profile audits those criteria once more (step `reaudit-TASK_ID`, `extra.derivationAudit` with the task's report and an index table of each quoted input, UTF-16 indexes and, for a non-ASCII input, UTF-8 byte offsets), told to read every position, column, offset, length or count off the table and cite the indexes, and `tasks/conflicts/TASK_ID-reaudit.json` keeps its derivations. When the second audit gives another value for a row, the requirements win over those criteria: `assumptions/conflicts/TASK_ID-reaudited.json` records both derivations, `amendments/conflict-TASK_ID.json` records an amendment with authority `requirement`, `decisions/clarification-reaudited-TASK_ID.json` records the overruled criteria (`criterionIds`) so the case walk stops demanding their literals, and the task runs again told to implement the requirements and change only the expected value that follows the row. When it agrees, the rows win: `assumptions/conflicts/TASK_ID-audited.json` and `decisions/clarification-audited-TASK_ID.json` record it, and the task runs again told to keep the rows exactly as written and make the code give them. A second audit that cannot run leaves the conflict to the usual way. R53 jsonsplice T006 had an audit-verified row that counted a comment's column one short; the host settled for the row, the task reported the same conflict again and a person was asked (1.73M tokens). A report whose quoted spans are all commands of the run's checks, in the spellings above (`AC-012 names "-m ruff check ."`), is settled by the host too (#1762): `assumptions/conflicts/TASK_ID-checks.json` and `decisions/clarification-checks-TASK_ID.json` record that host-run checks are not test requirements, and the task runs again told to write the tests for the criteria's other cases. Each settles once per task, whatever the `clarification` setting; a further conflict goes the usual way. A question that is asked quotes each named criterion as written (Given, When, Then), the audit's verdict on each of its rows and any literal the test changed, next to the task's own summary, and the person's answer records the criteria in `decisions/clarification-REQUEST_ID.json` (`criterionIds`): the task's case walk no longer demands the literals of the criteria the answer names (all of them when it names none), since the answer, not the overruled row, is what its tests now follow. R51 sedstep T006 dropped `\!` from an audit-verified row, reported column 14 against the row's column 16, and the person, shown only the paraphrase, overruled the row; the walk still demanded `column 16` and the task ran three generations (910k tokens). The task stops without changing anything further and becomes ready again (`interrupted`), `tasks/conflicts/TASK_ID.json` records its account, and the run waits on a clarification request with the question `SPEC-CONFLICT-TASK_ID`, which the TUI decision view, `baked workflow answer`, the desktop run decision and Needs you show like any question. The answer is appended to the request, and the task runs again with it; it still may not weaken the tests. The answer is also a decision of the run: `decisions/clarification-REQUEST_ID.json` records the task, the findings it corrects and the answer, and those findings are decided, so their host repros no longer gate the task (a correction that keeps the code because the answer says the repro is wrong is accepted). When the same task reports a conflict again after an answer, the question is not asked again: the task runs once more told that the earlier answer is final (`tasks/conflicts/TASK_ID-under-REQUEST_ID.json`), and only a further conflict asks a person, with the earlier answer shown in the question. This also applies under `clarification: assume`, because every default would either fit the tests or drop a requirement, with one exception besides the verified quotes above: when a task's first report names what the request itself states as one side ("the request's explicit ban on property-based tests", "the request says this line is valid"), the request wins. That reading of the report's words is a fallback for reports without a quote the host can check, and it is not extended to later reports or other phrasings (#2016). The decision is recorded in `assumptions/conflicts/TASK_ID.json`, `decisions/clarification-assumed-TASK_ID.json` decides the findings the task corrects so their repros no longer gate it (a repro that expects what the request rules out would otherwise refuse every retry, #1407), a note that the request is authoritative is appended to the request with the task's account, and the task runs again without a person; a second conflict of the same task asks. The other exception is a test the plan's own test task wrote: when the report names a test file that an implemented test task of the plan wrote and the reporting task cannot write (and the file is not one of the tests written first and approved by a person), the approved spec wins over that test. `assumptions/conflicts/TASK_ID.json` records it with the test files, a high finding against each file starts a task-level correction of the test task that may change only the assertions contradicting the spec, and the reporting task waits for that correction, then runs again. A second conflict of the same task asks. A test this run created (absent from the baseline snapshot) that the reporting task may write itself, such as a generated test a correction of a failed test check meets, is decided the same way without a correction task: two things the run wrote are never a question for a person. `assumptions/conflicts/TASK_ID.json` records that the approved spec and the request win over the test files, and the task runs again told to change only the assertions that contradict them and keep every other assertion. The report may name a test file the way models write paths, in a Markdown code span or emphasis and with a test name after it (`` `tests/test_cli.py::test_x` ``); the same matching maps review findings to the tasks that write the files they name. The test files of a conflict are the ones the report names and the ones the output of the task's last failing check locates (a `cargo test` panic at `tests/x.rs:90:5`, a pytest `FAILED tests/test_x.py::t` line, a `go test` `x_test.go:42:` line, a bun file header, a JVM class), and, for a test task whose report and checks name none, the test files it writes: a report often cites a failing test by function name only (tonemath 241fc475 asked a person and waited 35 minutes, #1459). Tests that existed before the run and tests written first and approved by a person stay a question. A report that names a criterion and a requirement of the approved spec ("AC-008 says G##/Db cannot be spelled, but FR-003 allows the double sharp") is settled for the requirement when no test that existed before the run, or that a person approved first, is involved: `assumptions/conflicts/TASK_ID.json` records that the requirements win over those criteria, `amendments/conflict-TASK_ID.json` records a spec amendment with authority `requirement` that every later agent reads and the run report lists, `decisions/clarification-assumed-TASK_ID.json` decides the task's findings and records the overruled criteria (`criterionIds`), so the case walk stops demanding their literals, and the task runs again told to implement the requirements and to change only an assertion this run wrote that follows the contradicting criterion. When the report also names a test this run wrote, the amendment is recorded next to the test branch above. A conflict whose one side is what the platform cannot do at all is not a choice for a person either: when the report names a platform limit (the implementer is told to write "platform limit" and name the nearest feasible behavior; reports that a protocol, runtime, header or library cannot carry, represent or encode a value, or that a value is not a ByteString, count too), `assumptions/conflicts/TASK_ID.json` records that the nearest feasible behavior holds, `decisions/clarification-assumed-TASK_ID.json` decides the findings the task corrects so their repros no longer gate it, and the task runs again told to implement that behavior (for a value a protocol cannot carry, its standard serialized form) and to change only an assertion this run wrote that demands the impossible value. url-shortener 0cf3a1af waited on a person about the literal IDN in a Location header, which no HTTP header can carry, and spent about 4.0M of 8.9M tokens on it (#1384). A conflict with a reviewer's repro is not a choice for a person either. When the task's latest repro check (`tasks/TASK_ID/repro-check-*.json`) shows a finding it corrects still reproducing while every required check of the task passes, the repro's `expected` is the finding's claim, not the spec. `assumptions/conflicts/TASK_ID.json` records that the approved spec and the request win over those repros. `decisions/clarification-assumed-TASK_ID.json` decides the task's findings so their repros no longer gate it, and the task runs again told to keep the behavior the spec and the checks require and to correct what the claim says is wrong. intsmith ca6f3023 spent 2.2M tokens on such a correction and asked a person (#1517). A documentation task (every file it writes is documentation, a licence or a notice) whose report sets the rule for examples against the request's worked example ("FR-001 needs the worked example in the README, but the instruction forbids writing example output from the spec") is decided for the request too: `assumptions/conflicts/TASK_ID.json` records that the worked example is authoritative, `decisions/clarification-assumed-TASK_ID.json` decides the task's findings the same way, and the task runs again told to copy every example output the request or the spec gives. A second conflict of the same task asks. An analysis finding of kind `inconsistency` whose one side is the request (a spec assumption excludes `tests/**`, the request allows it) is decided for the request the same way as a scope finding that adds a restriction, as described for `clarification: "assume"` above. These three decisions, and the review amendment for the request below, rest on evidence the host checks, not on the wording of the report or finding (#2032). A platform limit settles on every report of the task, once per task, when `conflictSides` has a `platform` side whose `ref` is a check of the task whose latest run failed (or `probe` for a `run_probe` the task ran) and whose quote, at least 12 characters, is a line of that recorded output; a documentation task's worked example settles the same way when a request side's quote (or, without request sides, a span the report quotes) is in the request. An analysis finding of kind `inconsistency` is settled for the request when its optional `quote` is in the request and in none of the criteria it names (without `quote`, a span its description quotes that the request holds counts), on every pass; a review finding of kind `contradicts` whose fix changes the spec records a spec amendment for the request (#1034) on the same check of its optional `quote` or a span its claim or fix quotes. Offered evidence the host cannot find settles nothing, whatever the words; a report or finding that offers none falls back to the wording rules above, a conflict only on the task's first report. Each decision records its inputs in `conflicts/evidence/SITE-REF-N.json` (kind `evidence-decision`, sites `analysis-request`, `review-request`, `platform-limit` and `docs-example`: whether the site decides it, the evidence as `verified`, `unverified` or `absent`, whether the wording matched, whether its fallback applied and the decision, never the quote), which bench traces keep and `replayEvidenceDecisions` decides again (`newEvidenceDecisions`). The reviewers and the convergence agent get the conflicts the run settled as `extra.settledConflicts` (each `assumptions/conflicts/TASK_ID.json` with its account, and each person's answer in `decisions/clarification-REQUEST_ID.json`, the latest twelve), and their profiles rank a rule the request states and a settled conflict above a suggested fix: a finding whose fix would break one is not raised, or is raised with basis `inferred`, which starts no correction. A task that meets a platform limit and implements the nearest feasible behavior itself, instead of reporting `spec_conflict`, declares it in its `implemented` or `already_satisfied` result: in the optional `platformLimits` list (each with the claim and the probe or check that showed it), or in its summary in the words the implementer rule asks for ("platform limit: ..."), which the host reads the same way as a conflict report. `assumptions/limits/TASK_ID.json` records the declared limits (`declared: true`), they join `extra.settledConflicts` for the reviewers (correctness and language), the convergence agent and the acceptance walker, and a finding that asks again for what a declared limit rules out is raised with basis `inferred` unless its repro shows the platform can do it. url-shortener ae4b9d3d declared the URL-serialized Location in an implemented corrective, the next review asked for the raw URL again, and a second corrective spent 1.2M tokens to report the same limit as a spec conflict (#1536). The run report lists the settled conflicts and the declared limits under Decisions.
- **Spec amendments.** A review finding of kind `contradicts` that sets the approved spec against the request (its claim or fix names the request or brief) and whose fix says to change a criterion, a requirement, the spec, the plan or the task graph (for example "remove the generated-case count and seed from AC-003, the plan and T007") records a spec amendment in `amendments/FINDING_ID.json` ([spec-amendment](../../src/core/workflows/spec-amendment.ts)): the finding, the criteria and requirements it names, and the test file it names. The request is authoritative over them: the run's request gets one line saying so, which every later agent reads, so the correction makes code and tests follow the request and later reviews and convergence judge those criteria by what the request states. A correction cannot write `spec.json`, so it changes the tests that served the contradicting wording; the diff check accepts fewer tests or assertions in the test files of a correction of an amendment and in the test file the amendment names, as for a write with a reason. Skips, suppressions and the other rules still apply. The [run report](#run-report) lists the amendments under Decisions, so the delivered pull request names what the request overrode. Before this, a correction that replaced generated tests the request ruled out was flagged as a dropped test, and the guard's own correction restored them, until the run ended `no_progress` after 1.19M tokens of corrections (pngvet a2da6c24). Under `clarification: assume`, a blocking analysis finding that refers to the spec (`spec.json`, `inScope`, `outOfScope`, a criterion or requirement ID) and that the request settles (a restriction or addition the request lacks), and that survives the last return to the spec author, records an amendment too, in `amendments/analysis-FINDING_ID.json`. Implement then starts with the request authoritative over that point of the spec, and review does not raise it against `spec.json` again.
- **Findings on run documents.** A review or convergence finding whose file is one of the run's own documents (`spec.json`, `plan.md`, `tasks.json` or `research.json`, in any directory, unless a planned task writes a project file of that path) gets no corrective task, since no task can write it. It records a spec amendment in which the implementation wins: typically a correction deliberately departed from the approved wording (a collision policy the spec did not name) and the review reports the spec as the stale side. The run's request gets a line saying the implementation is authoritative over the criteria and requirements the finding names on that point, the run documents are not rewritten, and the finding is resolved.
