The other findings of the round are corrected as before; a round with only such findings adds no task, keeps the reviews and judges the convergence again, and it still counts as a round toward its limit. The run report lists these amendments separately ("the implementation wins over the spec"). Before this, such a finding stopped the run with `input_changed` ("Correction requires writes outside the approved task scope"), with every check green (md-site eed93dab at 5.30M tokens, colfit 0149cea7).
- Writers see the tests themselves, never the internals of acceptance gates or graders beyond them.

### Tests first

Tests written by the implementers share their wrong assumptions; failing tests written before the code are the stronger correctness filter. With tests first on, the planner plans a task that writes only test files for each criterion (a test task may cover several), and each test task names a task-scope check that runs its tests. A plan without a test task for a criterion, or with a test task that names no task check, is returned to the planner at submission. The policy field `testsFirst` chooses the criteria: `off` (the default; absent means `off`, so stored policies keep their hash), `criteria` (every criterion with the `automated` method) or `all` (every criterion except `post-release` ones). The `tdd` preset is `standard` with `testsFirst: "criteria"`, and `full` has it on too; start it with `baked workflow run tdd`, the workflow field of the TUI new-run form, the workflow picker of the desktop New run screen or `workflow: "tdd"` in `POST /v1/workflow/runs`. A workflow definition's `testsFirst` wins over the policy's.

- **Order.** Test tasks run first and depend on no code task; every code task waits for the test tasks that share one of its criteria. Until a person approves the tests, only test tasks run.
- **Red for the right reason.** After a test task the host runs its task checks on the code as it is and records `tests-first/TASK_ID.json`. The tests must fail, a failing output must name one of the task's test files, and no failing output may report a syntax error in them. Otherwise the reason (`Tests first: ...`) becomes a finding that a correction of the tests must fix, like a failed task check.
- **Approval.** When every test task is red, the run waits on an approval request whose body holds the criteria and the test files with their content; its digest is the hash of the files. The TUI decision view, the desktop run decision and Needs you show the tests next to the criteria; `baked workflow approve` and `reject` answer it like any approval. Rejecting appends `User rejected the tests written first: NOTE` to the request and writes the tests again. A run whose plan was approved automatically ([auto approval](#auto-approval)) approves the tests the same way.
- **Read-only after approval.** The approved test files are read-only for code tasks: an edit without a planned `reason` is refused with `CAPABILITY_DENIED` and the implementer is told to make the code pass. Implementation then runs until the tests pass, followed by the checks and review of the workflow.

### Auto approval

Auto approval is off by default. Enabling it is a human act: set `workflows.policy.autoApproval` in `.baked/config.json`, or pass it in a policy file or a revision candidate. The policy is part of the approval digest, so the setting is frozen with the run. A `workflows.policy` in a config file may name only the fields it changes: the rest come from the default policy, nested objects such as `reviewProfiles` merge field by field, and lists such as `checks` replace the default. An invalid field stops the command with one line naming the file and the field.

```json
"autoApproval": {
  "renewal": true,
  "limits": { "maxTasks": 3, "maxFiles": 5, "allowedPaths": ["src/**", "tests/**"],
              "allowDelete": false, "allowNewDependencies": false, "maxTotalTokens": 800000 }
}
```

At the approval stage the host compares the package with the setting. Either mode is enough to approve:

- `renewal` re-approves a revised package that stays within the latest human approval of the same run. Every write must be a path that approval covered: a delete only where a delete was approved. Every task must use a profile that approval covered. The workflow, profile and skill hashes must not change. Capabilities may stay or shrink but never grow, whoever changed them: no tool outside the approved `allowedTools`, no wider `network`, the same review profiles and skeptic, and `forceSecurityReview` stays on if it was on. The other policy settings (checks, budget limits, `maxAttemptTokens`, `maxCostUsd`, `checkpointTokens`, `autoApproval`) must either match the approval, with each budget limit (`maxTotalTokens`, `maxAgentCalls`, `maxAgentSteps`, `maxActiveMs`, `maxConcurrentReadAgents`, `maxRepairRounds`, `maxConvergenceRounds`, `maxCorrectiveTasks`) the same or lower, or be exactly the policy an operator command set later. Budget growth the host made on its own after that approval or command (the `approvedBy: "sizing"` revisions of plan sizing, the planning floor, auto-growth and auto-extend, which grow `maxTotalTokens`, `maxActiveMs` and a growable `maxCostUsd`) is not a change: the renewal compares the limits as they were before it, so a revise the host then grew still renews without a person. `workflow extend` and a `workflow revise` that changes the policy each write a `policy-revisions/COMMAND_ID.json` artifact with `approvedBy: "user"` and the new policy hash; that command is the human decision on the policy, so a re-plan with the same writes after `extend --tokens N` or after a revised check command renews without a person. Only these host-written records count; nothing in a candidate's spec, plan or graph, or in any model output, is treated as consent. A fork starts without a human approval, so its first approval is always human. Approvals recorded before the policy, or before the task profiles, were stored in the request cannot be renewed; the next approval goes to a person once.
- `limits` approves a package with at most `maxTasks` tasks and `maxFiles` distinct files. Only planned tasks an agent runs count against `maxTasks`. The slices and by-file parts sizing cut from one task count once, whoever cut them: tasks that share a `sizingSlice.taskId`, or whose sizing note names the task they are parts of. A task the host writes from the plan's content (an empty `__init__.py`, a fixed stub) is left out. The tasks the planner added when it answered a sizing split are fixed at that replan and left out on every later plan, so the same graph gets the same decision on every planning pass (#1711: bench round 51 declined url-shortener's 13 tasks in 8 planned ones and md-site's 14 in 7). The reason of a refusal says what it did not count ("10 tasks exceed 8 (2 sizing slices, 1 host-written not counted)"). Their files still count against `maxFiles`. Every write must match one of the `allowedPaths` globs, compared without case. Deletes are allowed only with `allowDelete`. The run's `maxTotalTokens` must not exceed `limits.maxTotalTokens`, not counting what the host added to the budget on its own (the policy revisions with `approvedBy: "sizing"`: planning floor, sizing and growth, see [Plan-time sizing](#plan-time-sizing)); a budget the host raised that way is approved only while it stays within `sizing.maxRunTokens`, otherwise the reason names the sizing ceiling. An initiative feature whose share the planning floor raised to the start estimate is therefore approved without a person when its plan stays inside the other limits. `allowNewDependencies` can only be `false`. A setting with `renewal: false` and no `limits` is rejected.

Some packages always go to a person, whatever the setting. These are writes under `.git`, `.baked`, `baked/`, `specs/`, `.github`, `.gitlab`, `.circleci` or `.husky`, writes to package manifests (including `*.csproj`, `*.fsproj`, `pom.xml`, `build.gradle*`, `settings.gradle*`, `Directory.Packages.props`/`Directory.Build.props` and `Directory.Build.targets`) and lock files, `tsconfig*`, `biome*` (a task that creates a manifest or check config that does not exist in the workspace yet, such as `package.json`, `pyproject.toml`, `tsconfig.json` or `biome.json`, is not held back: that is how a new app starts; lock files are never covered), `.env*`, `AGENTS.md`, `CLAUDE.md`, `constitution.md`, key and certificate files, and any spec that leaves a deliverable of the request unmapped (see [request coverage](#request-coverage)) or has post-release criteria (deferring them needs explicit consent), except under `clarification: "assume"`: there the automatic approval defers them, lists each deferred criterion in the approval request (`deferredCriteria`) and in its decision record, and acceptance reports them as deferred. The task limit counts tasks before host sizing splits. Tasks the host adds by splitting oversized tests do not count. When sizing sends a plan back for smaller tasks, the runtime saves that pre-split count for the replacement plan, whether the planner submits a full plan or a patch. Extra slices in that replacement do not consume the task allowance, including under initiative tiered approval. A plan already above the task limit still needs a person. File, path, deletion and other approval checks use the full replacement graph. The sizing note includes the task limit and asks for fewer, larger slices when they fit the sizing capacity. Later unrelated replans get a fresh task count.

A spec lists the deliverables of the request in `deliverables`: each has a `quote` copied from the request (a CLI, an API, a library, tests, documentation) and the `requirementIds` that cover it, or, for something the request itself leaves out or defers, no requirements and an `outOfScope` quote of the request's words that say so. The spec author's submission is checked against the request text, ignoring case, quotes and whitespace: a request that describes a program needs the list, a quote that is not a passage of the request, an unknown requirement, a deliverable with neither requirements nor an exclusion, and an exclusion the request does not state go back to the spec author like the other spec corrections. A deliverable with neither requirements nor an exclusion is mapped by the host first, and recorded in `spec-repairs/deliverables.json`: an entry of the spec's `outOfScope` that quotes the request and names it becomes its exclusion, else the requirements whose text holds its words cover it, else a request sentence that turns it down ("No CI workflow is wanted") becomes its exclusion; only a deliverable none of these fits goes back. A spec that still leaves a deliverable unmapped is accepted, but auto approval declines it (`Request deliverable not covered by the spec: ...`) and a person decides. The approval request carries the table as `requestCoverage`; the terminal approval prompt shows it under "Request coverage" and the desktop app under "What the request asks for and what covers it", with uncovered rows marked. A feature of an initiative is not checked: its boundary is approved with the split. Specs without `deliverables` (from earlier runs, or written for a request that describes no program) keep working unchanged.

The planner plans with these limits in view. When `limits` is set, its input carries `approvalLimits` (task and file counts, `allowedPaths`, `allowDelete` and the always-human paths), and it is asked to keep its writes within them unless the requirements need a file outside them. When a submitted plan would still go to a person, the planner is told once per run which writes cause it and may drop optional ones; a plan submitted again with the same writes is accepted and waits for a person at approval as before. Later plan generations of the run (a revision, a resumed plan stage) are not told again (`plan-repairs/scope-advice.json` records that the advice was given). The advice leaves out writes the planner cannot drop: a file the request names by path or file name, and the delete of a .NET template placeholder (`UnitTest1.cs`, `Class1.cs`). When only such writes need a person, the plan is accepted at its first submission. Only the host decides the approval; the advice changes what the planner proposes, not what the host approves. The advice also tells the planner that the person's approval covers every write the plan lists, and to keep approval out of task text. The host removes the sentences that make a task wait for approval (such as "this workflow path requires human approval") from each task's description and rationale before storing the graph. The implementer is told that the approved plan authorizes every write in its task's scope, so it does not refuse an approved write to a CI workflow or a manifest.

**Manifest edits the request names.** `"requestedManifestEdits": true` in `autoApproval` (off by default) stops holding back an edit of an existing package manifest that the request names, such as the scaffold's `src/App/App.csproj` that a multi-project .NET plan must reference its new library from. The request names a manifest by its whole path (`packages/api/package.json`, also with `./` or backslashes), not by a part of a longer path, so `package.json` names only the root manifest. A bare file name counts only for a name packages do not share (`App.csproj`); `package.json`, `pyproject.toml`, `requirements*.txt`, `Cargo.toml`, `go.mod`, `pom.xml`, `build.gradle`, `build.gradle.kts`, `settings.gradle`, `settings.gradle.kts` and `Gemfile` need their path. A sentence that tells the run to leave the file alone ("keep package.json as is", "do not edit package.json") does not name it. Such an edit is also in scope for the limits when `allowedPaths` leave the manifest out, as an update scaffold's `src/**` and `tests/**` do (bench round 45: the brief named the `[project.scripts]` table of `pyproject.toml`, and the approval declined it as outside the allowed paths), and a plan that model review would judge does not go to the reviewer for it. Only that write is let in; the rest of the manifest family keeps its rules. The planner's scope advice names manifests by the same rule, so it never calls fine an edit the approval declines and never skips one the request only says to keep as is; for other files it still matches names loosely, since it only decides which writes the planner is told it may drop. One table of manifest and tool-config kinds decides each request-named write, for the approval, the limits' scope check and the planner's advice alike (#1710):

- Exempt whenever the request names them: the manifests the [dependency check](code-quality.md) reads (`package.json`, `pyproject.toml`, `requirements*.txt`, `Pipfile`, `setup.cfg`, `Cargo.toml`, `go.mod`, `*.csproj`, `*.fsproj`, `*.vbproj`, `pom.xml`, `build.gradle`, `build.gradle.kts`, `settings.gradle`, `settings.gradle.kts`, `libs.versions.toml` and `Gemfile`), solution files (`*.sln`, `*.slnx`) and `go.work`. A package the edit adds still blocks the run unless the request or the approved spec names it, since `allowNewDependencies` is always `false`.
- Exempt only when the plan writes the whole file in a host-written task (the write carries its `content`) and that content runs no code and fetches nothing: `Directory.Build.props` and `Directory.Build.targets` with only `PropertyGroup` properties (no `Import`, `Target`, `Exec`, `UsingTask`, item, `Sdk` attribute, property function, or property that hooks a command or target such as `PostBuildEvent` or `*DependsOn`); `Directory.Packages.props` with those properties and `PackageVersion` pins; `global.json` without `msbuild-sdks`; `NuGet.config` without package sources or credentials; `gradle.properties` with plain properties and no JVM agent; `.mvn/maven.config` with only `-D`, `-P`, `-T`, `-B`, `-q`, `-e` and `-ntp` options; `rust-toolchain.toml` without a toolchain `path`.
- Never exempt: `.cargo/config.toml`, `setup.py`, `tox.ini`, `*.gemspec`, `bunfig.toml` and lock files other than the four below.
- Check configs (below) are decided by `requestedCheckTightening`, not by this setting.
- Lock files that record the project's own version (#1934): `Cargo.lock`, `package-lock.json`, `uv.lock` and `Gemfile.lock` are exempt when the request names them, as the one-line follow-up of a version bump. The [manifest field check](#auto-approval) then holds every entry of the lock other than the project's own (the root of `package-lock.json`, the path packages of `Cargo.lock` and of `Gemfile.lock` with `remote: .`, the editable or virtual package of `uv.lock`) to what it was, whatever the request names: a lock that a dependency change rewrites still needs a person. Other locks (`bun.lock`, `yarn.lock`, `pnpm-lock.yaml`, `poetry.lock`, `go.sum`, ...) carry no project version or are not read, and keep their rule.

A request-named write that its kind keeps with a person is declined as `PATH needs a person: RULE`, with the rule of its kind, instead of "always needs a person" or "outside the allowed paths", the model reviewer does not decide it, and the planner's advice lists the same sentence. Deletes and manifests the request does not name keep needing a person, and the other limits and renewal rules still apply. A manifest can run code at build or install time (package.json scripts, MSBuild targets, Maven plugins), so turn this on only for requests you trust to name the manifests they want changed.

**Check configs.** One table of check-defining configs for every supported language (`CHECK_CONFIGS` in `src/core/workflows/quality/check-configs.ts`, #2221) decides what a linter, formatter or type checker enforces: `tsconfig*`, `jsconfig*.json`, `biome*`, ESLint (`.eslintrc*`, `eslint.config.*`, `.eslintignore`), `.oxlintrc.json` and Prettier configs for TypeScript, JavaScript, Vue, Svelte and ReScript (and `rescript.json` warnings); `ruff.toml`, `mypy.ini`, `.flake8`, `.pylintrc` and `pyrightconfig.json`; `.golangci.*` and `staticcheck.conf`; `clippy.toml` and `rustfmt.toml`; `checkstyle*.xml`, `pmd*.xml` and SpotBugs exclude files for Java and Kotlin, `detekt.yml` and `.editorconfig` (ktlint) for Kotlin; `.scalafmt.conf`, `.scalafix.conf` and `scalastyle*.xml`; `.editorconfig`/`.globalconfig` analyzer severities, `*.ruleset` and `stylecop.json` for C#; `.clang-tidy` and `.clang-format` for C, C++ and Objective-C; `.rubocop*.yml` and `.standard.yml`; `phpcs.xml`, `phpstan.neon`, `psalm.xml` and `.php-cs-fixer.php`; `.swiftlint.yml` and `.swiftformat`; `.luacheckrc`, `stylua.toml` and `.luarc.json`; `.credo.exs`, `.formatter.exs` and `.dialyzer_ignore.exs`; `analysis_options.yaml`; `.shellcheckrc`; `.ocamlformat`; `.solhint.json`; `.sqlfluff`; `PSScriptAnalyzerSettings.psd1`; and `.lintr`. Zig has none (its compiler and `zig fmt` read no project config). Modifying or deleting one of them goes to a person like a manifest; creating one that is not there yet, or editing one on a scaffold, does not. `tsconfig*` and `biome*` were always kept with a person; the others are new and decline only under `"stopModes": { "check-config-approval": "enforce" }`, and by default (`shadow`) the approval goes on and records a `decision.would_stop` naming each such write (see [Stop reasons](#stop-reasons-and-shadow-mode)).

`"requestedCheckTightening": true` in `autoApproval` (off by default) approves an edit of a check config that the request names (by the rule above) when it only makes the check stricter in ways the request names: a flag turned on (`noUncheckedIndexedAccess`, `strict`, mypy `disallow_untyped_defs`, a RuboCop cop's `Enabled`), an allowance turned off (`skipLibCheck`, `allowUnreachableCode`), a rule added to an enabled list (ruff `select`, golangci `linters.enable`, a checkstyle module, a phpcs `rule`), an ignore or exclusion removed, a severity raised (Biome and ESLint rule levels, `.editorconfig` `dotnet_diagnostic.*.severity`, Dart `analyzer.errors`), or a threshold lowered (clippy `*-threshold`, RuboCop `Max`). The request must name the setting, rule or list item. Anything else loosens: a flag turned off, a rule disabled or ignored, `extends`, `include`/`exclude`, `paths`, a threshold raised, a tightening the request does not name, and any change of a config the host reads as text (code configs such as `.credo.exs` or `eslint.config.js`, and formatter layouts). Without the setting, a named `tsconfig.json` edit is declined with `tsconfig.json needs a person: a tsconfig config sets what the checks enforce; autoApproval.requestedCheckTightening approves a tightening the request names`. A plan that writes the whole file in a host-written task is compared with the file it replaces at approval and keeps the person when it loosens; an implementer's edit is compared after the run by the manifest field check below, which reports each loosened field as `Check config PATH changes FIELD, which is not a tightening the request names` and the correction restores it (bench round 70: nodepluck-update waited 7.7 minutes for a person on a brief that named tsconfig.json and `noUncheckedIndexedAccess: true`).

**Manifest field check.** The approval sees a manifest write's path, not its content. So when a plan's automatic approval let a request-named manifest in, the checks stage compares that manifest's executable fields before and after the run (#1793), and every one the run changed must be named by the request or the approved spec: the field's label or its last part as a whole word (`engines`, `postinstall` for `scripts.postinstall`). The executable fields are, per manifest: `package.json` `scripts.*`, `bin`, `main`, `module`, `browser`, `exports`, `imports`, `workspaces`, `trustedDependencies`, `overrides`, `resolutions`, `patchedDependencies` and `packageManager`; `pyproject.toml` `[build-system]`, `requires-python`, `[project.scripts]`, `[project.gui-scripts]`, `[project.entry-points.*]` and the build, script and package-source tables of hatch, setuptools, poetry, pdm, uv and maturin; `requirements*.txt` option lines (`-e`, `--index-url`) and direct references; `Pipfile` `[source]` and `[scripts]`; `setup.cfg` `[options.entry_points]`, `[build_ext]`, `[build_py]`, `[aliases]` and the `cmdclass`, `scripts` and `setup_requires` options; `Cargo.toml` `build`, `links`, `default-run`, `[[bin]]`, `[lib]`, `[patch.*]`, `[replace]` and `[target.*]`; `go.mod` `replace`, `toolchain`, `godebug` and the `go` line (it selects, and with `GOTOOLCHAIN=auto` downloads, the toolchain that runs the checks); MSBuild projects' `Target`, `UsingTask`, `Import`, `Sdk` and properties that hook the build; `pom.xml` `plugins`, `extensions`, `repositories`, `pluginRepositories` and `profiles`; every top-level Gradle statement other than `dependencies`; the `[plugins]` of `libs.versions.toml`; and every Gemfile line other than `gem`, `group`, `end` and `ruby`. The files beside the manifests that choose the toolchain or tool distribution are read the same way (#1963): `rust-toolchain(.toml)` channel and path, `global.json` `sdk.*` and `msbuild-sdks`, the `distributionUrl`, `wrapperUrl` and `wrapperVersion` of `maven-wrapper.properties` and `gradle-wrapper.properties`, and `.python-version`. Of these only `rust-toolchain` and `global.json` can be request-named exemptions; a wrapper or `.python-version` write outside the allowed paths always goes to a person. Each change the request does not name is a security finding, `Manifest PATH changes FIELD, which the request does not name`, with the evidence `quality/manifest-fields.json`, and the correction restores the field. Other fields (`engines`, a version, a description) are free, and dependency fields stay with the dependency check. A request-named lock (above) is read the same way, with each entry other than the project's own as a field that no request names (#1934). The check settings a manifest holds are held to named tightenings like a check config (#2221), whatever the request names: `pyproject.toml` `[tool.ruff]`, `[tool.mypy]`, `[tool.pyright]`, `[tool.basedpyright]` and `[tool.pylint]`, `setup.cfg` `[flake8]` and `[mypy*]`, `package.json` `eslintConfig`, `Cargo.toml` `[lints]` and `[workspace.lints]` (a lint raised from unset to `deny` or `forbid` tightens) and the analyzer properties of an MSBuild project (`TreatWarningsAsErrors`, `WarningsAsErrors`, `NoWarn`, `Nullable`, `AnalysisMode`, ...). A plan a person approved is not checked.

**Approval reviewer.** `"modelReview": true` in `autoApproval` (it needs `limits`), or `--approval-review` on `baked workflow run` and `baked quick` (`approvalReview: true` on `POST /v1/workflow/runs`), sends a package that leaves the limits only by its task count, file count or paths to the built-in `approval-reviewer` profile (role `reviewer`, model class `$review`, read-only). It reads the request, the spec's scope and requirements, every task's writes and the reasons auto approval declined, and returns `ApprovalReviewSchema`: `verdict` (`approve` or `escalate`), `risk` (`low`, `medium` or `high`) and its reasons. Only `approve` at `low` risk approves; the approval is stored like any automatic one, with mode `model-review` and the verdict in its answer. Anything else, a missing profile, an invalid answer or a failed call leaves the request with a person, and the request body carries `autoApproval.review` or `autoApproval.reviewError`, which the TUI approval prompt and the desktop approval card show under the declined reasons. The reviewer never sees packages with always-human writes, deletions, a token budget above the limit, uncommitted user changes in scope or post-release criteria, and it cannot change the plan, paths, tools, network or budget. A model approval is not a human approval: renewal still compares with the last approval a person gave. Chat tool approvals are not covered ([#599](https://github.com/Gulliby-development/baked/issues/599)).

An automatic approval is stored on the same approval request with the same digest a person would sign. Its actor is `auto-approval`, and its answer contains the mode and a hash of the limits. The event log shows `Decision accepted (auto approval)`. When auto approval declines, the request stays pending for a person, and `autoApproval.declined` in its body lists why. The bench-trace replay decides every plan approval of a recorded round again on the run state the scheduler held when it asked (`replayPlanApprovals`, #1707), so a change that would send an auto-approved plan to a person, or approve one a person was asked about, shows on real rounds before it merges. The trace keeps a request's named file paths in place of its prose for the manifest rule; deliverable coverage is not replayed. On R51 it shows #1711: url-shortener and md-site were declined with 9 and 14 tasks of 8, and main approves both on the same state.

### Request coverage

### Notifications when a run waits

A run that starts waiting for a person is announced once: a pending request (approval, clarification, manual evidence, recovery, checkpoint, delivery) or a run that becomes blocked, such as one whose token budget ran out and needs `workflow extend`. The process that drives the run announces it: the terminal app, `baked workflow run|resume` or `baked serve`. The database records each announced request in `attention_notices`, so a request is announced once even when several processes share the workspace; a run blocked again later is announced again. Requests already waiting when BAKED is upgraded are not announced.

Each announcement goes to three places:

- **A `RunAttention` hook** ([extensions](../extensions.md#hooks)), matched against the request kind or the blocked reason. Its stdin adds `run_attention`: `run_id`, `feature_id`, `request_id` (null for a blocked run), `kind`, `blocking` and `status`.
- **A webhook**, only when `notifications.webhook.url` is set. It receives one `POST` with a JSON body; it carries no request body, prompt or file content:

  ```json
  {"event": "run.attention", "project": "shop", "workspace": "/home/me/shop", "runId": "...",
   "featureId": "checkout", "requestId": "...", "kind": "approval", "blocking": true,
   "status": "waiting", "message": "Waiting for approval", "createdAt": "2026-10-11T08:00:00.000Z"}
  ```

  `headers` adds request headers, such as an authorization token; `blockingOnly: true` skips advisory acceptance decisions (`blocking: false`), which the run does not wait for. Only `http://` and `https://` URLs are used. A failed delivery is logged and not retried.
- **A native desktop notification** from the terminal app and from `baked workflow` run in an interactive terminal, for blocking items only: `osascript` on macOS, `notify-send` on Linux (when installed) and a PowerShell toast on Windows. `notifications.system: false` turns it off, and so does the environment variable `BAKED_NO_SYSTEM_NOTIFICATIONS=1`, which the test suite sets so a test run never reaches the developer's notification centre. `baked serve` shows none: the desktop app raises its own from `GET /v1/attention`.

```json
"notifications": {
  "system": true,
  "webhook": { "url": "https://example.com/baked", "headers": { "authorization": "Bearer ..." }, "blockingOnly": false }
}
```

The desktop app edits the same settings under Settings, All projects, Run notifications. In the terminal app, `/notifications` shows them and opens a menu, and `/notifications system on|off`, `/notifications webhook URL|off` and `/notifications blocking-only on|off` change them in the global config; an idle workflow worker restarts to apply the change, and a worker driving a run applies it after BAKED restarts. Headers stay in the config file on both.

## Gates, budgets and estimates

### Gates window

A gate is one entry of `workflows.policy.checks` in `.baked/config.json`. `/workflow gates` (or G on the runs screen) opens the window that lists, adds, edits and removes them. N adds a gate, E edits the selected one, D removes it after a confirmation, R runs it now and shows the outcome, the exit code, the duration and the last 40 lines of output, and S shows what the project already offers.

- The command is one line split into an executable and arguments without a shell; quotes group words. Timeout is in seconds. Required, scope (`feature` or `task`), the env names that may be passed through and the generated outputs (`writes`) are the existing check fields. The same validation as for a run applies, so a duplicate ID or a directory outside the workspace is refused at save time.
- Saving writes only `workflows.policy.checks` and keeps every other setting of the project config. A project without a `workflows` block gets one with the default policy and `enabled: true`.
- A run takes a copy of the policy when it starts. Changing gates affects the runs created afterwards. Changing the gates of a started run still goes through `candidate` and `revise`.
- Run now goes through the workflow worker, so the interface stays responsive; Esc stops the process tree. It uses the same safe environment and timeout as a run. It records no evidence. It is refused while a run holds the workspace, because the command could change the files that run is checking. It needs workflows to be enabled, so a disabled project starts no worker.

### Gates from chat

Every chat session (the desktop chat, `baked session`, and the coordinator in the desktop app, the terminal UI and `baked session coordinator send`) has two gate tools. `list_gates` shows the configured gates, the ones BAKED detects in the project and the workflows that cannot start until a feature gate exists; it runs nothing. `save_gates` adds, replaces (same ID) or removes gates: a detected gate by its ID alone takes its command, timeout and fixer, any other gate needs a command. Each save shows an approval prompt listing the commands and writes nothing unless you approve it. Saving writes `workflows.policy.checks` like the gates window and gives new runs of the same process (the server, the terminal UI's workflow worker) the new list; started runs keep theirs. The coordinator also sets missing gates up together with a run, on its Run workflow card ([coordinator](../coordinator.md#workflows)).

### Baseline comparison in update runs

A check passes on its exit code, so a linter that exits 0 with new warnings, or a test suite that now runs fewer tests, used to pass an update run unnoticed. In a run whose workspace already held source code (facts kind `source`), the runtime runs every feature check once before the approval request, on the code the run starts from, and stores the counts its output reports in `facts/check-baseline.json`: the diagnostics of Biome, ESLint, Ruff, tsc, Clippy (`cargo clippy`/`cargo check`), `dotnet build` and `dart analyze` (#1494), and the tests of `bun test`, Jest, Vitest, pytest, `cargo test`, `dotnet test` (whose counts are padded with spaces), Maven Surefire, `go test -v`, Gradle (by the JUnit reports the host appends, see the gate table, #1489), Maven (the surefire summary of each module, added up, or under `-q` the reports under `target/surefire-reports` the host appends the same way; a run narrowed with `-Dtest` that wrote no report selected no test class and counts as zero tests run, so the written-tests check fails instead of passing on nothing, #2378), RSpec (`5 examples, 1 failure, 2 pending`, pending and `xit` examples not run) and Minitest, also through `rake test` (`4 runs, 2 assertions, 1 failures, 1 errors, 1 skips`) (#1493), `dart test` and `flutter test` (the last `+N ~N -N: Some tests failed.` line, passed, skipped and failed; a test file that does not load is one of the failures and is no test, #1494), sbt (one `Passed: Total N, ..., Ignored N, Pending N` line per project that ran tests, over every framework; ScalaTest's own `Tests: succeeded N, failed N, canceled N, ignored N, pending N` where it is the only framework and sbt prints no line, #1496; an sbt run that ends `[success]` with neither a summary nor a suite line ran no test, as sbt 2's `testFull` prints on a build without one, and sbt 2's `No tests to run for Test / testQuick` is a run of no test, never a build without tests, #2440), `swift test` (XCTest's `Executed N tests, with N tests skipped and N failures` after `Test Suite 'All tests'`, plus swift-testing's `Test run with N tests`, less the tests it lists as skipped, #1492), and for any other runner a JUnit XML report printed to the output (the `tests` attribute of `<testsuites>`, else the sum over `<testsuite>`, else the `<testcase>` elements) or TAP (prove's `Files=N, Tests=N` line, else the sum of the top-level `1..N` plans; indented subtest plans are not counted). A test count is kept only from a passing run. Each diagnostics parser counts errors and warnings, never infos or hints (Biome's `Found 6 infos` for `useTemplate` counts nothing, #1791), and records how many of them are errors where its summary says (ESLint, Biome, `dotnet build`, `dart analyze`; every Ruff and tsc diagnostic is an error, a finished Cargo build has none). A new tool's parser states which severities it counts, and `tests/gate-matrix.test.ts` has a cell for it under "diagnostics ratchet severity". When a required check later passes with more diagnostics, or fewer tests, than its baseline under the same command and tool, it counts as failed. More diagnostics give the finding `Required check X passed with more diagnostics`, which says by how much (`warnings rose from 0 to 2 against the code the run started from (biome)`) and is `low` when only warnings rose (a quick fix when `quickFix` is on) and `high` when errors rose or the tool does not split them; fewer tests give `Required check X failed` at `high`. The check's fixer runs first when it has one, the rest is repaired like any failed check, and a later passing run of the check closes either finding. Fewer tests are accepted when a task of the plan deletes a test file. Output no parser recognizes, a truncated or inconclusive baseline run, a changed command, a scaffold or empty workspace, and a run resumed after a task started measure nothing and compare nothing. The baseline costs one extra run of each feature check per update run; it records no evidence and does not appear in the run report's checks.

The same baseline run records, by command, the output of each required check that already fails before any change (`failures` in `facts/check-baseline.json`). A scaffold or empty workspace runs only its required checks for this and stores no counts. The baseline runs before the approval request, so the request body carries `baselineFailures`, the IDs of those checks, and the TUI approval and the desktop run decision and Needs you detail show "Required checks that fail before any change" under the checks. A greenfield test check usually fails there because its tests do not exist yet; one whose failure the plan cannot change, such as a stale `build` script, is the one to fix before approving. When a required check in the checks stage fails with the same output as before any change (colours, durations, timestamps, linter and type-checker summary counts such as Biome's `Checked 2 files` or tsc's `Found 3 errors in 2 files`, mypy's `checked 4 source files`, `ruff format` and black's unchanged-file counts, and the passed, skipped and total counts of test summaries (pytest, jest, vitest, bun, cargo, dotnet, Maven, Gradle; failed counts stay, #1425), and the workspace path left out; a path into any of the project's worktrees, `.baked/worktrees/<run>/main` or a task's `.../T005`, and the working copy itself all count as the workspace, so a stack trace that names another worktree is still the same failure), nothing the run changed affects it: a corrective task could only spend attempts on it, so the run stops at once as `no_progress` with `baseline_failure: required check X failed before any change and fails the same way now`, naming the evidence. Fix the check or the project setup (for example a `vite build` script left in the scaffold of a Bun CLI), or revise the plan, then resume. A task's check that fails the same way and names no file the task writes opens no corrective task: the task did not cause it and a correction could not reach it (a scaffold's unformatted `biome.json`), and the checks stage stops on it as above. A check that fails without output, and one whose output changed (a scaffold's tests fail one way before the tests exist and another way after), are repaired as before.

That comparison matches durations and times by a few spellings, so a value that changes between two runs of the same failure in another shape tells them apart: Gradle's `BUILD FAILED in 1m 3s` after `8s`, dotnet's `< 1 ms`, CTest's columns padded to the width of the duration, Maven's `01:05 min`, an address with or without `0x` (a Lua `table: 00000184D28BE670` on Windows, a Go panic), a random seed (pytest-randomly, GoogleTest, RSpec, ExUnit), a run ID (Alcotest), vitest's `Start at`, node:test's `duration_ms`, zig's `MaxRSS` or a temporary path (#2295). The baseline therefore also records each failing check's shaped signature (`shapes` in `facts/check-baseline.json`): the same output with those values taken out by shape through `normalizeVolatile` (`src/core/verification/volatile.ts`), the one table the repeated-failure hash and the check finding's failing lines read too, while a whole number with a unit that no word introduces as a time (`expected 5s`) stays as an asserted value. Each comparison at the checks stage and at task end records both signatures, line by line, as `baseline-signature/<check>-<hash>.json` (kept in bench traces, decided again by `replayBaselineSignatures`, `newSignatureDecisions`), and one that only the shaped signatures match records the `baseline_signature` would-stop. The reason is in shadow: the run still decides on the live signature, so a change of values alone still opens correctives until it is promoted.

The policy field `staticBaseline: "relative"` (absent means `stop`, the behavior above, so stored policies keep their hash) lets a run complete in a project with lint debt elsewhere. The baseline run then also stores the located diagnostics of each failing required static check (a linter, formatter or type checker, not a test suite) as `debt` in `facts/check-baseline.json`, each by its file and its text without line numbers. Diagnostics are read from one-line formats (`path:3:5: msg`, tsc, Biome) and from a header followed by a `-->` location line (rustc and clippy, and ruff's default `full` format with its rule code on the header). A later failure of that check, at task end or in the checks stage, counts as passed when every diagnostic it reports is in that list, none is in a file a task of the plan writes, and the diagnostics account for the tool's whole summary count (Biome's `Found N errors`, ruff, tsc); a tool without a summary line, or one that reports more than it lists, fails as usual. Each such pass is recorded as `checks/baseline-debt/<check>-<id>.json` with its evidence, and acceptance treats that failed run as the check's pass. Run definitions with their own check stages (v2, such as `quick`) apply the same rule in their command-gate stage (#1393): before, the task-end check accepted the debt and the final gate then stopped the finished run with `baseline_failure`.

A required check that fails before any change on files no task of the plan writes is known at the baseline, before the approval, yet the stop above comes only at the checks stage, after every task (#2215: R70 namesound docs spent 1.03M tokens on its tasks while rubocop failed on three `[Correctable]` offenses in a file of an earlier feature). The baseline run now decides, for each such check that lints or formats one file at a time (static, not a test suite, not a whole-unit analyzer such as tsc or clippy, whose errors the plan's files may cause), from the located findings of its output (`baseline-fix.ts`):
- `none`: the output names no located finding, the findings do not account for the tool's summary count (RuboCop's `N offenses detected`, Ruff, Biome), a task of the plan writes a named file, or `staticBaseline: "relative"` passes the failure as debt anyway;
- `repair`: with the policy field `baselineFix: "on"` (absent is off; the `quality.baselineFix` setting), the check has a `fix`, git tracks every named file and the tool marks every finding as one its fixer corrects (RuboCop's `[Correctable]`, Ruff's `[*]`, Biome's `FIXABLE`, the `CORRECTABLE_MARKERS` table);
- `would_stop`: otherwise; the `baseline_unrepairable` stop reason records a would-stop (shadow: the run goes on and stops at the checks stage as before).

The decision's inputs are recorded as `baseline-fix/<check>.json` (finding and correctable counts, the named files, how many a task writes or git does not track, whether the check has a fix, the flag, the decision), kept in bench traces and decided again by `replayBaselineFailures` (`newBaselineDecisions`). A `repair` runs before the first task, at the task stage of either definition (v1 and v2 `quick`): the host runs the check's fixer on the named files, journaled as `apply_fix` edits with their pre-images like the checks stage's fixer runs, runs the check again and keeps the edit only when it passes; otherwise it puts the files back, as journaled edits too, and records the would-stop. Either way it writes `baseline-fix/<check>-repair.json` (`changed`, `passed`, `kept`). A kept repair measures the check baseline again, so the checks stage compares with the repaired code, and the repaired files ship with the run's result like any other journaled edit. A resumed run whose tasks started repairs nothing.

A [planned-ahead](#initiatives) run approves on a provisional base and starts its tasks on its upstream features' results, so its release measures the check baseline again and decides its failures there (`baseline-fix/<check>.json` with `release: true`; its would-stop is keyed `release-check-<check>`), and only a check that the latest baseline records as failing is repaired (#2228). In an initiative feature run, a failing check's located findings in files the boundary of an accepted upstream feature owns (and not the run's own boundary or plan) record the `upstream_baseline_debt` would-stop, naming each owner and its files: the debt is that feature's, and the promotion asks it for an amendment instead of letting this run pay for it.

### Custom gates

A gate can be any script or container that reports its result as JSON: set its result to JSON report (`"report": "json"` on a policy check, or on `command` in a gate file under `baked/gates/`; the Result toggle in the TUI form and on the desktop Setup > Gates form). The command prints one JSON object as its last JSON line on stdout or stderr; earlier log lines and other text are ignored:

```json
{"status": "failed", "summary": "2 leaked keys", "findings": [{"severity": "high", "message": "AWS key in fixture", "path": "tests/fixtures/env.json", "line": 4}]}
```

- `status` is `passed`, `failed` or `inconclusive`; `summary` (up to 2000 characters) and `findings` (up to 100, each with `severity` `critical|high|medium|low`, `message`, optional `path` and `line`) are optional.
- The report decides the check. A `passed` report counts only with exit code 0; a pass from a command that exited nonzero, a missing report or a malformed one makes the check inconclusive (reason `report_exit_mismatch` or `no_report`), which blocks a required gate without starting a repair. A `failed` report fails the check like a nonzero exit and starts the repair.
- The report is appended to the check's output as `Gate report: STATUS. SUMMARY` with one `- [SEVERITY] PATH:LINE MESSAGE` line per finding, so the repair task and the run report see where to look.
- A container is a command like any other, for example `docker run --rm -v .:/work -w /work IMAGE audit --json`; it runs without a shell, with the gate's timeout and the env names it declares. Run now treats an `inconclusive` report, or a pass that exited nonzero, as an error.

A check is static (a linter, formatter or type checker) when it names a fixer, or when its ID or command line holds `lint`, `format`, `fmt`, `check`, `typecheck` or `vet`, or one of the tools of `STATIC_TOOLS` ([static-tools.ts](../../src/core/workflows/static-tools.ts)), the linters, formatters and type checkers of every supported language by name: `rubocop`, `phpcs`, `swiftlint`, `luacheck`, `shellcheck`, `credo`, `solhint`, `ocamlformat` and the build-tool tasks named after a tool, such as Gradle's `spotlessCheck`, `ktlintCheck` and `checkstyleMain` (#2110). A static feature check runs after every code task that writes a file it covers, a failure is corrected as diagnostics only, and it is never a test check, also when its arguments name the test files (`solhint 'test/**'`, `scalafmt --test`). Tools marked whole-unit there (tsc, mypy, Clippy, PHPStan, clang-tidy, SpotBugs, Scalafix, Dialyzer, Slither) wait for the checks stage instead. A CI step's command is labelled `lint`, `format` or `typecheck` from the same record.

Detection offers gates; nothing is added until the user confirms. It reads the project root only:

| Ecosystem | Found by | Suggested gates |
| --- | --- | --- |
| bun, npm, pnpm, yarn | `package.json` scripts; the runner follows the lock file | `typecheck` (also `type-check`, `check-types`), `lint`, `format` (`format:check`, `fmt:check`, `check-format`, `format-check` or `prettier:check`, with a `format`, `fmt` or `prettier` script as its fixer), `tests` (the npm placeholder test script is ignored), `build` (advisory, not preselected). Without a format script, a configured formatter is offered as `format` but not preselected, since the project may run it only in an editor: `biome format .` for `biome.json(c)`, `dprint check` for `dprint.json(c)`, `prettier --check .` for a `.prettierrc*` or `prettier.config.*` file or a `prettier` key in `package.json`, through `bunx`, `npx`, `pnpm exec` or `yarn` (#1685); `bun fmt --no-script --check .` (fixer `bun fmt --no-script .`) for a `[fmt]` section in `bunfig.toml`, run by Bun itself, ahead of a Prettier config (#1920) |
| Python | `pytest.ini`, `[tool.pytest]` in `pyproject.toml`, `[tool:pytest]` in `setup.cfg`, `[pytest]` in `tox.ini`, `conftest.py`, or a declared pytest dependency | `pytest`: `python -m pytest -q` (`python3` outside Windows); in a project whose lockfile names its environment (`uv.lock`, `poetry.lock`, `Pipfile.lock`, `pdm.lock`, in that order) the gates run in it, `uv run pytest -q` and `uv run ruff check .` with `uv run ruff check --fix` as the fixer, since the bare interpreter outside an activated environment has no pytest (#1655). An unmanaged `.venv` is not used: activate it before starting BAKED, or give the check its interpreter |
| Python lint | `ruff.toml`, `.ruff.toml`, `[tool.ruff]`, or ruff declared in `pyproject.toml` or a `requirements*.txt` file (the same rule picks ruff for the edit tools' formatter) | `ruff`: `ruff check .`; `ruff-format`: `ruff format --check .` with the fixer `ruff format`, preselected only when the project configures the formatter (`[tool.ruff.format]`, or a `[format]` table in `ruff.toml`), since a project that uses ruff only as a linter may format with another tool |
| Python format and types | `[tool.black]` or black declared in `pyproject.toml` or a `requirements*.txt` file; `pyrightconfig.json` or `[tool.pyright]`; `mypy.ini`, `.mypy.ini`, `[tool.mypy]` or `[mypy]` in `setup.cfg` | `black`: `black --check .` with the fixer `black .`, when ruff does not configure the formatter; `pyright`, or `mypy .` when no pyright config is found. All are preselected and run in the lockfile's environment as above. The type checkers are whole-unit checks and run in the checks stage (#1685) |
| SQL (SQLFluff, dbt) | `.sqlfluff`, `[tool.sqlfluff]` in `pyproject.toml`, `[sqlfluff]` in `setup.cfg` or `tox.ini`; `dbt_project.yml` | `sqlfluff`: `sqlfluff lint .` with the fixer `sqlfluff fix`, applied to the task's file; it reads one file at a time, so it covers each task that writes SQL. With `templater = dbt` the gate is `sqlfluff-dbt`, a whole-unit check that runs in the checks stage, since the dbt templater compiles the whole project. Like the Python tools it runs in the lockfile's environment (`uv run sqlfluff lint .`). A dbt project also gets `dbt-build`: `dbt build`, offered but not preselected, since it needs the project's database profile (#1499) |
| PowerShell (Pester, PSScriptAnalyzer) | a `*.Tests.ps1` file at the root or in `tests/`, `Tests/`, `test/` or `Test/`; `PSScriptAnalyzerSettings.psd1`, a `.psd1` module manifest or a script at the root | `pester`: `pwsh -NoProfile -NonInteractive -Command "Invoke-Pester -Configuration @{ Run = @{ Exit = $true }; Output = @{ RenderMode = 'Plaintext'; CIFormat = 'None' } }"` (Pester 5.5 or later; plain text, since Pester colours redirected output; no CI annotations, which on GitHub Actions or Azure Pipelines prefix the failed-test lines; and no `-CI`, which writes `testResults.xml` into the checkout); the written-tests check runs only the task's `*.Tests.ps1` files through `Run.Path`. `psscriptanalyzer`: `Invoke-ScriptAnalyzer -Path . -Recurse -Severity ParseError,Error,Warning -EnableExit`, one sorted `path:line:column: Severity Rule: message` line per finding, with the fixer `Invoke-ScriptAnalyzer -Fix -Path <file>`; preselected only with `PSScriptAnalyzerSettings.psd1`, since the default rules may already fail on the project's code. `-EnableExit` alone counts Information findings, and without `ParseError` a script that does not parse passes. On Windows without PowerShell 7 the gates run `powershell` (Windows PowerShell 5.1) (#1498) |
| R (testthat, lintr, styler) | `DESCRIPTION` (a package), `tests/testthat`, `.lintr` | `testthat`: `Rscript -e "testthat::test_local(reporter = testthat::CheckReporter$new())"` for a package, `testthat::test_dir('tests/testthat', ...)` for a project of scripts, when `tests/testthat` exists; the check reporter prints no spinner or running counts, and the written-tests check runs only the task's test files through testthat's `filter`. `lintr`: `lintr::lint_package()` (or `lint_dir('.')`), failing on any lint and writing one `path:line:column: type: [linter] message` line per lint itself, since lintr prints GitHub annotations instead under `GITHUB_ACTIONS`; preselected only with a `.lintr`, since the default linters may already fail on the code. `styler`: `styler::style_dir('.', dry = 'on')`, failing with one `path: would be reformatted by styler` line per file, offered but not preselected, with the fixer `styler::style_file` on the task's file; the format-written check holds the files a task writes to it. On Windows without `Rscript` on PATH the gates run the newest `%ProgramFiles%\R\R-<version>\bin\Rscript.exe` (#1500) |
| Go | `go.mod` | `gofmt`: `gofmt -l .` with the fixer `go fmt ./...` (applied per file as `gofmt -w <file>`); `gofmt -l` exits 0 whatever it lists, so a check that runs `gofmt` with `-l` fails when it lists any file. `go-vet`: `go vet ./...`, `go-test`: `go test ./...` |
| .NET | a `.slnx`, `.sln`, `.csproj`, `.fsproj` or `.vbproj` file in the root (solutions first; with several such files the first is named on the command line, because `dotnet` refuses an ambiguous folder) | `dotnet-build`: `dotnet build`, `dotnet-format`: `dotnet format --verify-no-changes` with the fixer `dotnet format`, `dotnet-test`: `dotnet test` |
| Rust | `Cargo.toml` | `cargo-fmt`: `cargo fmt --check` with the fixer `cargo fmt`, `cargo-clippy`: `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings`, `cargo-test`: `cargo test` |
| Java (Maven) | `pom.xml` | `maven-verify`: `mvn -B -q verify`, through the project's wrapper when it has one (`./mvnw`, or `cmd /d /c mvnw.cmd` on Windows), since the wrapper pins the Maven version and builds on a machine without `mvn` |
| Kotlin, Java, Scala (Gradle) | `build.gradle.kts`, `build.gradle`, `settings.gradle.kts` or `settings.gradle`, without a `pom.xml` | `gradle-check`: `gradle --console=plain -q check`, through `./gradlew` (or `cmd /d /c gradlew.bat` on Windows) when the project has the wrapper. Gradle's `check` runs the tests and every verification task the build applies (ktlint, detekt, Checkstyle, Spotless) (#1749). A build that applies Spotless (`com.diffplug.spotless`) or ktlint-gradle (`org.jlleitschuh.gradle.ktlint`), in a build script or `gradle/libs.versions.toml`, also gets `spotless`: `gradle --console=plain --warn spotlessCheck` with the fixer `spotlessApply`, and `ktlint`: `ktlintCheck` with the fixer `ktlintFormat`, before `gradle-check`, so a format violation is fixed instead of failing the test gate; `--warn` keeps the violations ktlint prints. `gradle-check` then leaves Spotless out with `-x spotlessCheck`; ktlint-gradle hangs each source set's task on `check` directly, so `check` still runs them, up to date once the ktlint gate passes. Both fixer tasks format the whole source set and take no file, so they run once for a task's files and the host restores every other file they touch (#2121). Gradle prints no test total, so after any Gradle check (`gradle`, `./gradlew`, `gradlew.bat`) the host reads the JUnit XML reports that run wrote under each project's `build/test-results/` (four folders deep at most) and appends their totals and failed tests to the output as one `<testsuites>` block, which the check baseline, the failed-test list and the no-tests rule read; each failed test keeps its report's `message` (500 characters at most), `type` and the first 10 lines of its failure text, since `gradle -q` prints none (#2272); a test task Gradle skipped as up to date writes no report and adds nothing (#1489) |
| Scala (sbt) | `build.sbt` | `scalafmt`: `sbt -batch scalafmtCheckAll` (fixer `sbt -batch "scalafmtOnly <file>"`, one sbt command, so each task formats its own files) when `project/plugins.sbt` adds `sbt-scalafmt` and the project has a `.scalafmt.conf` (#1496), then `sbt-test`: `sbt -batch test`, or `sbt -batch testFull` when `project/build.properties` names sbt 2, whose `test` is `testQuick` and skips the tests its cache has seen pass, even in a fresh directory (#2440) |
| Swift | `Package.swift` | `swift-format`: `swift format lint --strict --recursive .` (the swift-format of the Swift 6 toolchain, fixer `swift format format --in-place`) when the package has a `.swift-format` file (#1492), then `swift-build`: `swift build`, `swift-test`: `swift test` (#1753) |
| Elixir | `mix.exs` | `mix-format`: `mix format --check-formatted` with the fixer `mix format`, only with a `.formatter.exs` (Mix stops with an error without one), `mix-compile`: `mix compile --warnings-as-errors`, `mix-test`: `mix test` |
| Dart, Flutter | `pubspec.yaml` | `dart-format`: `dart format --output=none --set-exit-if-changed .` with the fixer `dart format`, `dart-analyze`: `dart analyze`, `dart-test`: `dart test`; a Flutter package (`sdk: flutter`) gets `flutter-analyze` and `flutter-test` instead. The analyzer resolves every import, so it runs in the checks stage |
| Zig | `build.zig` | `zig-fmt`: `zig fmt --check .` with the fixer `zig fmt`, `zig-build-test`: `zig build test` |
| OCaml | `dune-project` | `dune-build`: `dune build`, `dune-test`: `dune test` |
| Ruby (Bundler) | `Gemfile`, and the `.gemspec` files a Gemfile with `gemspec` loads | `standardrb`: `bundle exec standardrb` (fixer `bundle exec standardrb --fix`) when the bundle names `standard`, else `rubocop`: `bundle exec rubocop` (fixer `bundle exec rubocop -a`, RuboCop's safe autocorrect) when it names `rubocop` or a `rubocop-*` plugin (#1493); both lint file by file, so each task runs them and the fixer gets the task's file. Then `rspec`: `bundle exec rspec` when the bundle names `rspec` or the project has `spec/` or `.rspec`; otherwise `rake-test`: `bundle exec rake test` when it has a Rakefile or the bundle names `rake`; otherwise no test gate (a Jekyll site), and the runner row below applies when nothing is found. A gem (`gem "x"`) or a gemspec dependency (`spec.add_development_dependency "x"`) names it. On Windows `bundle` is RubyInstaller's `bundle.bat`, which the check runs through `cmd.exe` like any batch file. `bundle exec` is an environment runner like `uv run`: the task-end test check, the affected tests and the fixers read the program behind it |
| PHP (Composer) | `composer.json` | Each tool runs as `php vendor/bin/NAME`, the same on Windows, so run `composer install` first (#1491). `pint`: `php vendor/bin/pint --test` with the fixer `php vendor/bin/pint` (applied per file), when the project requires `laravel/pint` or has a `pint.json`; otherwise `php-cs-fixer`: `php vendor/bin/php-cs-fixer fix --dry-run --diff` with the fixer `php vendor/bin/php-cs-fixer fix`, when it has `.php-cs-fixer.php` or `.php-cs-fixer.dist.php`. `phpstan`: `php vendor/bin/phpstan analyse --no-progress` when it has `phpstan.neon`, `phpstan.neon.dist` or `phpstan.dist.neon`, otherwise `psalm`: `php vendor/bin/psalm --no-progress` with `psalm.xml(.dist)`; both load every class a file uses, so they run after integration, not per task. `pest`: `php vendor/bin/pest` when the project requires `pestphp/pest`, otherwise `phpunit`: `php vendor/bin/phpunit` when it requires `phpunit/phpunit` or has `phpunit.xml(.dist)` (`php vendor/bin/phpunit tests` without a `phpunit.xml`). PHPUnit's and Pest's summaries give the test count, and a task's written `*Test.php` files run on their own |
| Lua (LuaRocks) | a `.rockspec`, `.busted`, `.luacheckrc`, `selene.toml` or `stylua.toml` (`.stylua.toml`) at the root | `stylua`: `stylua --check .` with the fixer `stylua` (applied per file), when the project has `stylua.toml` or `.stylua.toml` (#1495). `luacheck`: `luacheck .` with a `.luacheckrc`, otherwise `selene`: `selene .` with a `selene.toml`; both lint file by file without resolving `require`, so each task runs them. `busted`: `busted` when the project has a `.busted`, a rockspec that names `busted` or a `spec/` folder. busted's summary (`2 successes / 1 failure / 1 error / 1 pending`) gives the test count, its `Failure ->` and `Error ->` entries the failed tests, and a task's written `*_spec.lua` files run on their own. On Windows LuaRocks installs busted and luacheck as `.bat` wrappers, which the check runs through `cmd.exe` like any batch file |
| Shell | a `.shellcheckrc`, an `.editorconfig` section for shell files, or `.bats` files in `test/` or `tests/` | Added to the gates of the project's own toolchain or of the runner row below, since scripts sit beside both (#1497). `shellcheck`: `shellcheck` on the project's `.sh` and `.bash` scripts (three folders deep, without dot folders, `node_modules`, `vendor` and build output) when it has a `.shellcheckrc`; ShellCheck takes files, not folders, so the list is the one at detection, and a script added later is checked once the gates are detected again. `shfmt`: `shfmt -d .` with the fixer `shfmt -w` (applied per file) when `.editorconfig` has a section for `sh` or `bash` files. `bats`: `bats test` (or `tests`) when that folder holds `.bats` files; its TAP output gives the test count, and a task's written `.bats` files run on their own. On Windows the tools come with Git Bash or WSL, so these gates are offered there but not preselected |
| C, C++ (CMake) | `CMakeLists.txt` | Configure, build and test are separate gates, since a check runs without a shell, and they run in this order (#1490). `cmake-configure`: `cmake -S . -B build -DCMAKE_EXPORT_COMPILE_COMMANDS=ON`; `cmake-build`: `cmake --build build --config Debug`; `clang-tidy`: `clang-tidy -p build --quiet --warnings-as-errors=*` on the project's `.c`, `.cc`, `.cpp` and `.cxx` files when it has a `.clang-tidy` (the list is the one at detection, like ShellCheck's); it reads the compilation database the configure gate writes, which the Visual Studio generators do not, so it is not preselected on Windows, and it compiles each file with the project's includes, so it runs after integration, not per task. `ctest`: `ctest --test-dir build --output-on-failure -C Debug` when a `CMakeLists.txt` registers tests (`enable_testing()`, `add_test`, `include(CTest)`, `gtest_discover_tests` and the Catch2 and doctest equivalents); it runs the binaries the build gate built, so it is no test of its own. `--config Debug` and `-C Debug` are for the multi-config generators (Visual Studio, Xcode, Ninja Multi-Config); the others ignore them. CTest's summary (`67% tests passed, 1 tests failed out of 3`) gives the test count, skipped tests (`GTEST_SKIP()`) did not run, its `The following tests FAILED:` list the failed tests, and a test whose executable is missing (`Unable to find executable`) is a build failure; a GoogleTest binary run on its own is read from its `[==========] 3 tests from 1 test suite ran.` summary and `[  FAILED  ]` lines. A build tree, a folder holding `CMakeCache.txt`, is not part of the sources a check may change. `cmake-build`, `clang-tidy` and `ctest` read the build tree, which a fresh task worktree does not have, so when one of them runs where `build/CMakeCache.txt` is missing and the run's checks include `cmake-configure`, the host runs `cmake-configure` first and records it as its own result; a configure that does not pass is the gate's result, with a line naming it (`GATE_PREREQUISITES` in `src/core/workflows/gate-prerequisites.ts`, #2526). In R82 tinyflate `cmake-build` ran before `cmake-configure` 12 times, each a failed required check (`build is not a directory`). A Makefile project keeps the runner row below |
| C, C++ format | a `.clang-format` (`_clang-format`) at the root | Added to the gates of the CMake row or of the runner row below (#1490). `clang-format`: `clang-format --dry-run --Werror` on the project's C and C++ sources and headers, listed at detection, with the fixer `clang-format -i` (applied per file, and to the task's lines with `--lines=A:B`) |
| Any other toolchain | only when no row above matched: targets of a `Makefile` (`GNUmakefile`), `justfile` or `Taskfile.yml`, then single-command `run:` steps of `.github/workflows/*.yml` | `typecheck` (`typecheck`, `type-check`, `types`), `lint`, `format` (`fmt-check`, `format-check`, `check-fmt`, `check-format`, `fmtcheck`), `tests` (`test`, `tests`) and `check`, run as `make TARGET`, `just TARGET` or `task TARGET`. A `check` target is treated as a suite (autotools' `make check` runs the tests), not as a linter: it runs in the checks stage, not after every task that writes code. A kind no runner file has comes from the first CI step that runs a matching tool without a shell: no pipes, `&&`, redirects, globs, variables or `VAR=value` prefix, and not a setup step (`install`, `setup`, `cache` and the like). Examples are `luacheck src` as `lint` and `busted` or `phpunit` as `tests`. |

Workspace facts list the build and package manifests of every language in the language table among the manifests (`go.mod`, `Cargo.toml`, `pom.xml`, `global.json`, any `.csproj`, `.fsproj`, `.vbproj`, `.sln` or `.slnx` file, and also `pubspec.yaml`, `build.sbt`, `Package.swift`, `CMakeLists.txt`, a `.rockspec`, a `Makefile` or `justfile`), and the project line of the context names a .NET project.

**Languages.** One table, `src/core/intelligence/language-traits.ts`, keyed by the languages of the extension map, decides for every workflow rule what a file is:

- **Code** is a file of a programming language (TypeScript, JavaScript, Python, Go, Rust, Java, Kotlin, Scala, C#, C, C++, Ruby, PHP, Swift, Lua and Luau, Elixir, Dart, Zig, shell, OCaml, Objective-C, Vue, Svelte, ReScript, Solidity, SQL, PowerShell, R). A task that writes code gets the file-local static checks, and the duplication and harden checks read it.
- **Source** is code, plus CSS and HTML. A workspace with source is `source`, not `scaffold`.
- **Tests** are files under `test/`, `tests/`, `__tests__/` or `spec/` (RSpec, busted); `specs/` is not a test directory, since it holds the workflow's specifications. Outside those directories a test is a code file named `name.test.ext` or `name.spec.ext`, or one that follows its language's convention: `test_*.py`, `*_test.py`, `conftest.py`, Django's `tests.py`, `*_test.go`, Rust `tests.rs` modules and `*_tests.rs`, `*Test.java`, `*Spec.scala`, `*Tests.cs` and every file of a .NET test project (`App.Tests/`, `App.UnitTests/`), NestJS `*.e2e-spec.ts`, `*_spec.rb`, `*_spec.lua`, `*_test.dart`, `*Test.php`, `*Tests.swift`, `*_test.exs`, `test_*.c`, `*_test.c`, `*_test.cpp` and `*_unittest.cc` (#1490), `*_test.sh` and `.bats` files (#1497) and the like. Test integrity, sizing, the check baseline, duplication, review notes and `bake_find` all use this rule.
- **Manifests** are the build and package files listed above, for workspace facts and sizing.

The test-file conventions, the manifest names, the [dependency check](code-quality.md)'s manifest readers, the placeholder statements of the quality rules, the toolchain whose gates `detectGates` suggests, the test runners whose summaries the check baseline reads, the ecosystem mutation tool and the property-test library are records with a row for every supported language (`src/core/intelligence/language-records.ts`); a language without such a thing has a row that says why (Ruby has no placeholder statement the rule can tell from an abstract method, PHP and Lua have no registry check yet, Objective-C and Solidity have no gate detector). `tests/language-parity.test.ts` runs each record over a small fixture project per language in `tests/fixtures/languages/` and fails when a language lacks a fixture or a golden.

A language the table does not know is still read by the code graph. No workflow rule treats its files as code or tests, and its checks are the ones you configure.

**Language review.** When the policy names no `reviewProfiles.language` and the workspace root holds a Go, .NET, Rust, Java, Ruby, Dart, PHP, Lua, C, C++, Scala or Swift manifest (a `Gemfile` or a `.gemspec`, #1493; a `pubspec.yaml`, #1494; `composer.json`, #1491; a `.rockspec`, `.busted`, `.luacheckrc`, `selene.toml` or `stylua.toml`, #1495; `CMakeLists.txt`, `meson.build`, `vcpkg.json` or a `conanfile`, #1490; a `build.sbt`, #1496; a `Package.swift`, #1492), a `dbt_project.yml`, a `.sqlfluff` or a PowerShell `.psd1` (a module manifest or PSScriptAnalyzer settings), or an R `DESCRIPTION` or `renv.lock`, or a Gradle build script (`build.gradle(.kts)`, `settings.gradle(.kts)`: Kotlin when the script is Kotlin or applies the Kotlin plugin, else Java, #1489), the run starts with the `language-reviewer` profile as its language review dimension. It looks for platform and idiom pitfalls a general reviewer misses: platform newlines in output the spec fixes byte for byte (`Environment.NewLine` is CRLF on Windows, `PHP_EOL` likewise), hard-coded separators in tests, culture-dependent formatting, ignored errors, unreleased resources, PHP's loose comparisons and byte functions on multibyte text, Lua's one-based indexing, accidental globals and string patterns read as regular expressions, C's and C++'s undefined behavior, unbounded string functions and `long` that is 32-bit on 64-bit Windows, and code the language's formatter or linter rejects; for SQL, another dialect's syntax, migrations that cannot be reversed, schema changes that hold long locks and NULL compared with `=`; for PowerShell, `$null` on the right of a comparison, unrolled single results, stray output, case-insensitive operators, non-terminating errors and Windows PowerShell 5.1 differences; for R, `1:length(x)` on empty input, `sapply` result types, `if` on vectors or `NA`, `==` against `NA` or on doubles, silent recycling and `library()` in package code. This review runs only when the change touches a file of those languages (`.go`, `.cs`, `.fs`, `.vb`, project and solution files, `.rs`, `.java`, `.kt`, `.sql`, `.rb`, `.rake`, `.gemspec`, `.ps1`, `.psm1`, `.psd1`, `.dart`, `.php`, `.lua`, `.luau`, `.rockspec`, `.c`, `.h`, `.cc`, `.cpp`, `.cxx`, `.hh`, `.hpp`, `.hxx`, `.scala`, `.sc`, `.sbt`, `.swift`, `.R`, `go.mod`, `Cargo.toml`, `pom.xml`, `build.gradle`, `settings.gradle`, `gradle.properties`, `libs.versions.toml`, `dbt_project.yml`, `Gemfile`, `Gemfile.lock`, `Rakefile`, `pubspec.yaml`, `pubspec.lock`, `analysis_options.yaml`, `composer.json`, `composer.lock`, the Lua tools' config files and the C and C++ manifests above, `.scalafmt.conf`, `Package.resolved`, `.swift-format`, `DESCRIPTION`, `renv.lock`); a change to documentation or scripts alone skips it and acceptance does not require it. A policy that sets another profile keeps it, and that profile always runs. The language dimension, whichever profile runs it, sees only the request and the patches of the changed files (no spec, plan, task graph, check output or other reviews), about a third of the context the correctness review gets. It reports no criterion coverage, since correctness and convergence own that, and its verdict follows from its findings alone.

**Review rounds after a correction.** A repair or convergence correction keeps the round's reviews, with the source manifest and the snapshot they reviewed, as the run's `priorReviews`. The next review round treats each dimension in one of two ways:
- **Kept.** A dimension with no inconclusive criterion is kept, and runs no agent, in two cases. Its previous review passed with no finding and no changed file is in its scope: the language dimension's scope is its language files, the security dimension's scope is the security-sensitive paths, and every other dimension's scope is any changed file. Or its previous review only repeated notes: it raised findings, none of them started a correction, and each is a note that repeats a note of an earlier round (the run report's **Repeats** column), so the dimension already ran again after a correction and found nothing new; it is kept whatever changed, and its notes stay in the report. The converge stage still checks the whole change. The run records `reviews/carried-KEY-SNAPSHOT.json` with the files changed since.
- **Rerun.** A dimension that runs again gets `previousRound` in its context: its findings of the previous round, the files changed since, and a patch of those changes against what it reviewed (up to 24,000 characters). It checks those findings first and sets `repeatOf` on a finding that is the same defect.

**Portable tests.** Checks run only on the host's operating system, so the profiles guard the tests a run writes against the usual cross-platform failures: the spec author adds a criterion that the product's tests pass on Windows, macOS and Linux only when the request names CI or other operating systems (CI, GitHub Actions, Windows, macOS, Linux, cross-platform) or the project already runs CI (`.github/workflows/*.yml`, `.gitlab-ci.yml`, `azure-pipelines.yml`, `.circleci/config.yml`, `.travis.yml`); the host passes it as `extra.crossPlatform`, and otherwise the spec adds no CI, platform matrix or release automation the request does not ask for. A request that names other operating systems but turns CI down ("No CI workflow is wanted", "without GitHub Actions") gets a note to write only what this host can check instead. An automated criterion that still claims behavior on several operating systems, browsers or real devices does not send the spec back to its author: the host keeps the claim as a `post-release` criterion, adds an automated criterion for what this host proves (platform-neutral path, line-ending and process APIs; the tests pass here) to each requirement the claim leaves without a criterion this run can check, and records both in `spec-repairs/host-bound.json`; the implementer compares resolved paths (a macOS temp directory under `/var` is `/private/var`), builds paths with the path module, does not depend on one line ending and gives spawned processes generous timeouts; the correctness reviewer reports a test that would fail on another operating system as a correctness finding.

**Layout criterion.** When the request adds or changes pages of a graphical or web user interface (a new product with one, or an update whose work changes markup or styles), the spec author adds a criterion that those pages fit a 1280 px wide viewport without horizontal scrolling, or names the region whose scrolling the request makes deliberate; an update that changes no page gets none. The criterion states only that outcome: one that prescribes CSS the request never names (`max-width`, `padding`, `white-space`, a length such as `80rem`) goes back to the author. The sdd workflow does not render pages, so when no gate of the run drives a browser, the host moves a layout criterion that no repository browser test (Playwright, Cypress, Puppeteer, WebDriver) checks to `post-release`, adds an automated criterion the reviewer checks by reading the markup and styles (no fixed width, min-width or unwrapped content wider than that viewport outside the deliberate scrolling region) to each requirement it leaves without one, and records both in `spec-repairs/layout.json`. A layout criterion therefore never waits on a `manual_evidence` request in sdd. Measuring it in a browser is the v2 `ai-acceptance` gate with the browser driver, and a run with that gate keeps the criterion as written.

**Host-required criteria.** The layout criterion, and the cross-platform criterion when the request names CI or other operating systems or the project already runs CI, are criteria the host's own rules require. One table ([host-required-criteria](../../src/core/workflows/host-required-criteria.ts)) feeds both the spec author's rule and the analysis rule, and the analysis is told such a criterion is never a scope finding (#1765). When the analysis still raises a blocking scope finding whose cited ids are all host-required criteria or the requirements they serve, or that cites no id and describes one, the host keeps it as a note ("Objects to a criterion the host's own rules require") instead of returning the spec. A finding that also cites another id still blocks. md-site (bench round 52) ran specify three times and plan and analyze four times (740k tokens) over the layout criterion before this rule.

Each entry of that table states its condition through one predicate (`required`), which the host's filter reads and which also decides the `extra.crossPlatform` note: the spec author gets the note worded for writing, the spec reviewer the same decision worded for reviewing. The cross-platform criterion is required only when the request names CI or other operating systems, or the project runs CI, **and the request does not turn CI down**. When the request names other operating systems but wants no CI, the reviewer is told that platform-neutral code and tests that pass on this host settle the request's platform sentence, and a blocking scope or coverage finding that still asks for a criterion about Windows, macOS or Linux (or a CI matrix) is kept as a note ("Asks for a criterion about other operating systems, which the host does not require"). A deliverable that quotes the host's own words for a required criterion ("add a criterion that the product's tests pass on Windows, macOS and Linux") is an accepted deliverable quote. hdrlens (bench round 56) looped specify, plan and analyze in 3 of 8 features over the three-OS criterion its no-CI request did not need, 1.02M tokens (#1831).

The host writes the cross-platform criterion itself. When it is required and no post-release criterion of the spec names Windows, macOS and Linux (or their CI runners), the host adds, after specify, a requirement (`NFR-N`, verified post-release) and a post-release criterion: the delivered revision's tests pass on all three operating systems when the project's CI runs them. The addition is recorded in `spec-repairs/host-required.json`. The spec author is told not to write one, and not to write an automated criterion that needs CI job results; the reviewer is told that the host's criterion, a spec without it and its method are never a finding, and a blocking finding that cites only the ids the host wrote is kept as a note. An automated criterion that names the CI workflow file or its matrix but asks for what the CI jobs report ("each CI job provides evidence that the tests pass") is repaired to post-release like any other host-bound criterion; one that only reads the file stays automated. Before this, 3 of 3 sdd updates of bench rounds 60 and 61 paid an extra specify, plan and analyze round for the criterion, and taskplan's automated CI-results criterion ended convergence `no_progress` (#1943). When that repair leaves a requirement without an automated criterion, the fallback the host adds has the Given "the delivered tree on this host", not the original's, so a fallback for a criterion whose Given names the delivered revision and the CI matrix is not flagged host-bound again. The required predicate, both `extra.crossPlatform` notes and the analysis filter read the request with the clarification answers a person accepted, so an answer that adds the platforms gets the host's criterion and one that turns CI down drops it. A revised spec (`baked workflow revise`, or a package staged on the desktop) gets the same repairs before its hash is computed, since a revision restarts at analysis; the candidate records them under `repairs` (#1964). A fork keeps the parent's spec, which already went through them.

**Layout check.** The policy field `layoutCheck` (absent by default, so stored policies keep their hash) lets the host measure layout criteria itself instead of deferring them:

```json
"layoutCheck": {
  "start": { "executable": "bun", "args": ["run", "preview"], "cwd": ".", "envNames": [] },
  "url": "http://127.0.0.1:4173",
```
