```json
  "pages": ["/", "/zones"],
  "width": 1280,
  "height": 800,
  "timeoutMs": 60000
}
```

With it, the host keeps the spec's layout criteria `manual` (an automated or post-release one that no repository browser test checks becomes `manual`, recorded in `spec-repairs/layout.json` as `measured`).
- **When it runs.** After convergence and before it asks a person, the host runs the measurement on the current snapshot.
- **Start.** It starts `start` without a shell in a process group, with the safe environment plus `envNames` and `PORT` set to the URL's port, and waits until `url` answers.
- **Measure.** It opens each page in a headless Chromium at `width` × `height` and reads `document.documentElement.scrollWidth` against `clientWidth`. Content inside a scrolling container (`overflow-x` `auto`, `scroll`, `hidden` or `clip`) does not widen the page.
- **Network.** `url` must be a loopback address. The browser aborts every request that is not to loopback.
- **Browser.** The browser comes from Playwright as for the [browser driver](#browser-driver) (`BAKED_BROWSER_EXECUTABLE`, or the Chromium Playwright reports); BAKED never installs either.
- **Record.** The pages, their numbers, the ten widest elements past the right edge and the verdict are stored as `layout/<snapshot>.json`, with a PNG screenshot of the first page as `layout/<snapshot>.png.b64`.

When every page fits and answers below HTTP 400, that record is the criteria's evidence on this snapshot, so no `manual_evidence` request is raised. When a page is too wide or answers an error, or the app or the browser is not available, the request is raised as before and its body carries the record under `layoutCheck`, so the person sees the measurement.

**Documented examples.** Before the review, the host runs the command examples of the Markdown files and READMEs the run changed and hands the correctness reviewer the result as `extra.documentedExamples`, so the reviewer spends no model steps running them. An example is a fenced block whose `$ ` lines are commands followed by their output, or a shell block of one command followed (after at most two lines of prose) by a plain block of its output. A fenced diff shows a change and is neither an example nor a fixture, even where its context lines read ` $ tool --help`; it ends at its own fence, not at a code fence of the file it changes shown as a context line (#2018). The host runs a document's examples in order, in a copy of the workspace with its installed dependencies linked, so commands that write data leave the workspace as it was; each runs like a repro (one argv without a shell, a program the run's checks start, at most 30 seconds, the run's sandbox), and at most 8 run per review. The copy is removed afterwards with a few retries (`removeScratch`), and a copy a stopped Windows program still holds is left to the temp cleanup instead of replacing the example's result with the removal error. Each example has the status `match`, `mismatch` (with `firstDifference`: the first documented line that differs and the line printed in its place; a side with no line there is the empty string and `missing` names it, so a repro copied from it reproduces on a command that prints nothing) or `not_run` with the reason (a command that needs a shell, a program no check starts, the limit, a workspace over 64 MiB, `placeholder:`, or `needs build:`). An example that exits 0 and prints nothing where the document shows output is `not_run` ("launcher problem suspected"), not a mismatch, and the copy is made under the long form of the temp directory, since on Windows a Bun program run from the 8.3 short path skips its `import.meta.main` entry (#1999). A command with a placeholder argument (an all-caps word such as `FILE` or `--out=PATH`, `<file>`, `[options]`) is a usage line, so the host does not run it and does not compare it with the block after it. A command with an argument under a build output directory (`bin/`, `obj/`, `target/`, `dist/`, `build/`, `out/`) that does not exist, such as `dotnet src/App/bin/Release/net10.0/app.dll` after a documented `dotnet build`, is `not_run` with `needs build:`: the host copies the source files and does not build, and the reviewer reports no finding for it. When the workspace has that build output (a check built it), the copy links its deepest build directory (`src/App/bin`) and the example runs. A toolchain command that builds before it runs reuses the build the checks made (`EXAMPLE_BUILD` in `src/core/verification/example-build.ts`, #2505): the copy links the workspace's build directories of the toolchain wherever they are (`.build` for `swift run`, `bin` and `obj` for `dotnet run`, `_build` for `mix run` and `dune exec`, `target` for `cargo run`, `zig-out` and `.zig-cache` for `zig build run`, `.dart_tool` for `dart run`), and when one was linked the command runs with the toolchain's no-build flag (`swift run --skip-build`, `dotnet run --no-build`, `mix run --no-compile`, `dune exec --no-build`). A command that times out once (a program and its first argument, or a toolchain's build-and-run command) is not run again in the same review: its later examples are `not_run` with `an earlier example of ... did not finish`. In R81 hintsieve-update the README's eight `swift run` examples each compiled from nothing in a copy without `.build` and were killed at 30 s, 244 s of a 687 s run with no example compared. A mismatch is a correctness finding whose repro is the command; the reviewer runs a `not_run` example other than a placeholder or needs-build one, or a help-text example in source code, with `run_repro` when it can. The results are kept as the `diagnostic` artifact `reviews/examples-SNAPSHOT.json`, so a resumed review does not run them again. A review attempt has 16 model steps (at most the profile's and the policy's `maxAgentSteps`), and an attempt that ends unsubmitted gets one retry with 24. The examples are written from a real run in the first place: a task that writes a README, Markdown or reStructuredText file gets `run_probe` even when it is planned, and the implementer runs each documented command on the program in the workspace and copies what it prints instead of inventing output. An example whose output the request or the spec gives verbatim (a worked example, an output block) is copied as given, even when it uses files the workspace does not have, and the implementer names the copied examples in its summary. `run_probe`, `run_repro` and the review's example runs may start the programs the run's checks start and the runtime of each check's toolchain: `java` for `mvn`, `mvnw`, `gradle`, `gradlew` and `sbt`, `node` for `npm`, `npx`, `pnpm` and `yarn`, and `python` and `python3` for `pytest`, `python`, `uv`, `poetry` and `tox`. So a Maven README example such as `java -cp target/classes app.Main` runs once a check has built the classes (smoke caesar 21bc3a53 blocked with `mvn verify` green because `run_probe` refused `java`). Go, Cargo and .NET programs run through `go run`, `cargo run --` and `dotnet run --`. A command the project itself installs (`routeshadow tree app.txt`, `stampbook status`) runs through its manifest's entry point (`ENTRY_POINTS` in `src/core/verification/entry-points.ts`): a Cargo `[[bin]]` or package with `src/main.rs` as `cargo run --quiet --bin NAME --`, a Python `[project.scripts]`, Poetry script or `console_scripts` entry as a call of its function by the checks' interpreter, a Go `main` package at the module root or under `cmd/` as `go run`, an executable .NET project (by `ToolCommandName`, `AssemblyName` or file name) as `dotnet run --project`, a Maven main class or Gradle `application` as `java -cp` on the built classes (the copy links `target/` or `build/`), and a package.json `bin` as `bun FILE` or `node FILE`. The launcher must be a program the checks already start, so an entry point adds no program the approval did not cover. All-caps values such as HTTP methods (`GET`, `POST`), digest names (`SHA256`) and formats (`JSON`) are not placeholders, and a block that the prose before it introduces as a file (“Given this input `net.sp`:”) or that opens with a `//` comment is not output, so a command is not compared with the sample file after it (bench round 55, #1821). Such a block that the prose names (“save the following response fields as `resp.txt`:”, or a first line `# data/net.sp`) is the file the reader saves: the host writes it into the copy under that relative name before the examples after it, never over a file the workspace has (`extractFixtures`). An example that still names an input file the copy lacks, and whose output names it, is `not_run` with `input file X is not in the repository`, not a mismatch. An example may also be introduced in a sentence: one inline command, an output verb and a colon (“For example, `modeplan apply 0644 u+x,go-r` shows each step of the change:”), then a plain block of its output. Bench round 57 recorded both hdrlens-update examples as mismatches because the inputs its README saved were missing, and extracted none of modeplan's seven (#1849). An example whose program `run_probe` still refuses keeps its command without an output block, and the implementer names it in its summary as not run instead of reporting it unresolved.

**Documented prose and tables.** No example run reads a document's prose or table cells, so before the review the host also checks the changed Markdown files and READMEs for two defects a reader sees (`docTextProblems` in `src/core/verification/doc-examples.ts`, #2549) and hands them to the correctness reviewer as `extra.documentedText`: `escaped_literal`, a code span outside the fenced blocks that copies the body of an escaped string literal the spec's requirements or criteria quote (`\\{{` where the program reads `\{{`; a code span shows its backslashes as written), with the value decoded as `suggested`; and `table_pipe`, a code span in a table row with an unescaped `|`, which a renderer takes as a cell separator, with each bare pipe written `\|`. The decoding is the escapes the supported languages share (`\\`, `\"`, `\'`, `\$`, `\#`, a backslashed backtick); a raw literal (`r'..'`, `@".."`, `#".."#`, a triple quote) is not decoded, and a span that equals the decoded value is right even when it holds a backslash. Spec readers take table cells through the lenient `tableCells`, which keeps a pipe inside a code span; that reader is unchanged. The reviewer reports each entry as a wording finding (`noRepro`) whose fix replaces the span with `suggested`. The problems are kept as `reviews/doc-text-<snapshot>.json` when there are any. The implementer prompt tells a documentation task to describe behaviour in its own words rather than quote criterion or row wording, to decode the spec's literals and to escape pipes in table cells.

The same detection fills the checks written when E enables workflows on the runs screen; the build gate is not part of that set. Gates that are already configured (same ID or same command) are not offered again. Existing CI scripts are not read. On Windows, give the command name (`npm`, `pnpm`, `yarn`), not the shim file: Bun launches `npm` without a shell, but a name ending in `.cmd` fails with `EINVAL`. Only `npm` was tried on this host (npm 11.16); `pnpm` and `yarn` were not installed here.

### Token accounting and reservations

Before each implementation attempt calls its executor, the scheduler writes
`attempts/ID/context.json` as an `attempt-context` artifact. It records `schemaVersion: 1`,
the `taskId`, the task's `findingIds`, and the supplied `defectClasses` (null when shown nothing with the policy on), `recurringFindings`, `siblingFindings`,
`battery`, `correctionFocus`, `cutOffChecks`, `failedFixers`, `misquoteContinue`, `earlierEdits`, `earlierReads` and `previousAttempt` fields. `failedFixers` lists the host fixer runs on the files the attempt writes that failed without changing them (`fixers/failed.json`): each check ID, command line, exit code and output tail, with a note that the fixer cannot run as configured (#2436). These are the values sent to that
attempt, including notes, earlier finding IDs, symbols, repros, failed-check output and carried
patches. An absent field means the attempt did not receive it. A first finding has no
`recurringFindings`; a later finding on an already corrected symbol records the earlier findings
and their regression repros. Planned task attempts also get a record, so continuations keep their
check failures and earlier edits. Records remain available when an attempt fails or is cancelled,
and each retry gets its own record. Serial tasks and parallel task worktrees use the same rule.
Read the records with `baked workflow artifacts RUN_ID` and `baked workflow artifacts RUN_ID --file ARTIFACT_ID`,
or through the local API's run artifacts. TUI, headless CLI and desktop runs share these core artifacts.

An agent attempt records its spend as each model step finishes, not only when it returns. An attempt that fails, is cancelled or stops on a cap is charged the tokens of its finished steps, writes `attempts/ID/usage.json` with stop reason `error` or `aborted` where it threw, and releases its reservation; `baked workflow usage` therefore includes failed attempts. Only an attempt interrupted by a crashed process, whose executor left no record, keeps its reservation as unknown usage. An attempt whose model steps all reported zero input and zero output tokens is not counted as free either: Codex reports a response's usage only once its tool call is answered, so an attempt that ends at its only tool call (the submit) never hears its spend. Such an attempt that returns a result is charged an estimate from the sizes of what it sent and received, at about 4 bytes a token: the prompt once per model step as input, and its text and tool call arguments (the submitted result among them) as output. The estimate is its `usedTokens`, counts toward the run's tokens, budget and `estimatedCostUsd` (priced as uncached input and output) and releases the reservation; `usage.json` records it as `estimatedUsage`, the `attempt.finished` event has `usageComplete: false` with `usageEstimated: true`, and the Markdown of `baked workflow usage` counts these attempts under "Estimated usage". One that fails or is interrupted without a result is charged its recorded steps as above. A model attempt's `usage.json` also records the reasoning effort it sent to the provider (`effort`, the `effort` or `reasoningEffort` of its provider options; absent when none was sent), and its `attempt.finished` event carries its `spend`: input tokens (cache reads included), output, cache reads, cache writes, the effort and the estimated cost at the model's list price (null when the model has no price). Each priced attempt adds its cost to the run's `estimatedCostUsd`, so the run snapshot (`GET /v1/workflow/runs/RUN_ID`, the JSON of `baked workflow status`), the desktop run list and run screen and the run report show a cost, and a provider whose tokens are mostly cache traffic (Claude Code) can be compared with one whose are not. `baked workflow usage` returns the effort of every attempt, and its Markdown groups the **By model** table by model and effort. The Codex app-server provider also waits briefly for a usage report that arrives after the turn completed, and a report that arrives between two steps of a thread is counted by the next step. A model attempt's `usage.json` also records `calls`: for each model call, the provider, stage, effort, whether the call streams reasoning (`streamsReasoning`), the milliseconds to the first response part (`firstByteMs`, null when none came) and the call's length (`durationMs`), and for a call the provider refused, its HTTP `status` and `retryAfter` header. The bench-trace replay checks these against the current first-response deadline, so a change to that deadline is tested on recorded rounds before it merges (#1715).

Before each step the attempt reserves its spend so far plus an estimate of that step: the input tokens the provider reported for the previous step, adjusted by the bytes added or compacted since at three bytes per token, and an output of twice the largest output seen (at least 4096 tokens, at most the profile's output cap). Before any usage is known it counts the messages at three bytes per token plus 8192 tokens for the system prompt and tool definitions. A step is refused (`Next model step exceeds remaining token budget`, with the used, held and requested tokens) only when the spend plus this estimate would pass the run limit. The attempt cap is `maxAttemptTokens` or, when less, what the run limit still leaves after the other attempts, so an attempt that starts near the limit switches to its submit-only final step in time instead of failing mid-loop. Token caps come from history and defaults, but one step costs very different amounts on different providers, so the cap also has a step floor: once steps have run, the cap is at least 10 model requests (or the attempt's step limit, when lower) at the attempt's average request so far, up to what the run limit still leaves. A step whose provider reported no tokens is not a request: Codex reports a response's usage only once its tool call is answered, so every other step of a Codex attempt reads 0, and averaging over every step priced those as free. In bench round 63 a corrective made 7 requests of about 110k tokens in 10 steps, stopped at the floor of 10 steps at 76k, and its continuation re-read the project for 1.36M tokens (#1987). On the recorded rounds this raises the floor of 398 of 4,388 attempts and never lowers one; 10 of them had stopped at it. The cap counts each step's input less its cache reads, plus its output (`cappedTokens` in `usage.json`): every step replays the cached prefix of the earlier ones, which costs a fraction of new input, and counting it in full ended 37 of 85 codex task attempts at the cap in one bench round. A provider that reports no cache split is counted whole. All of an attempt's tokens, cache reads included, are capped too, at three times its cap and never below ten model requests at its average request: an attempt that loops read, edit and check cycles on a growing cached context never reaches its cap, so before this it ran to its step limit (one integration test file took 2.27M tokens in a single attempt). Reaching that cap switches the attempt to its final step like the cap itself (`finalizedBy: "tokens"`), and the task continues at the cap from a fresh context. What the run limit still leaves counts every token, cache reads included, so an attempt also switches to its final step before it would pass that. Because the attempt is admitted on its cap and the run limit counts cache reads, an attempt with a large replayed context could otherwise reach that point after one or two steps. Before it switches for this reason the run budget grows on the rules of growth while tasks finish ([plan-time sizing](#plan-time-sizing)), and the attempt switches only when the budget cannot grow. An implementer attempt that was switched this way before its first edit and then submits `failed` or `blocked` stops the run with `budget_exhausted` ("Task T012 was stopped by the run budget before its first edit"), which `workflow extend` continues, instead of recording the task as failed and ending the run `invalid_output`. The attempt's `usage.json` records the cap after the floor, the tokens it counted against it and all its tokens (`totalTokens`).

A task attempt that continues, restarts or retries a task gets `earlierReads` in its input: what the task's earlier attempts read (tool, paths or symbols, how often, bytes), up to 30 entries with the most repeated first, from their `read` evidence, and a note to read only the spans the fix still needs. md-site T075 (bench round 52) ended at its token cap without a submission, and the next session read the same project again for 1.24M tokens to submit a 710-byte fix (#1774).

A task attempt that reaches its step or token cap, submits an incomplete result and has edited files continues once: the task becomes ready again with both caps raised by half (`taskContinuations` in the run state records it), and the next attempt gets the files as the earlier attempt left them. In a run with several writers the task's worktree is discarded, but its edits are kept (`taskCarry` in the run state): the continuation's new worktree gets each edited file back, journaled as that attempt's own edit, unless the base changed the file meanwhile, and the prompt carries the same `earlierEdits` patch as in a single-writer run. A second incomplete attempt of the same task, an incomplete attempt that edited nothing, or one that did not reach a cap blocks the run as before. The run budget still bounds every attempt.

An attempt that submits a complete result after it was finalized by its step or token cap or by the progress watcher, with an edit after its last `request_check` (from the step tools in its usage record), is checked before its result is accepted: the host runs the task's required checks on its files, and when one fails the task continues once the same way, with both caps raised by half and the failing checks' output in the prompt (`cutOffChecks`, recorded in `tasks/TASK_ID/cut-off-checks.json`). In a run with several writers the result is not integrated, so a later task's worktree does not inherit the failure. Without this, a corrective cut off by the all-token cap submitted a regression test that contradicted the spec, its failed check opened another corrective, a sibling inherited the failing test and the run stopped `no_progress` (md-site 11b11f19, bench round 33, #1329). The finalize notice tells the attempt that the host runs the required checks on its files and sends a failure after an unchecked edit back to it.

A correction (a task the plan did not have) whose task checks fail after it submits goes back to the same correction once (a planned task that writes tests does the same on its own test files, see Written tests below), with the failures and their command output in `cutOffChecks` (`reason: "correction"` in `tasks/TASK_ID/cut-off-checks.json`). This applies when each failure is a check the correction was opened for, a test it broke (a check that passed before it), or output that names a file it changed. Its caps are not raised. Only a failure the continuation leaves opens a correction of the correction, and a failure on files another correction owns still goes there. Before this, a new correction started without the earlier one's context and from the union of the plan's files (url-shortener 651b2693, bench round 35: T007 broke a property test and T008 was opened for it with all 7 files, #1285).

A failed test check of a task starts its correction from the failing test files and the planned sources they import (`3 of 7 first` in the run report). When the output names no test file, as with a timeout or a bare assertion, the failing tests are the task's test files that the check's arguments name, or its only test file. A finding with one failing test file is placed on that file (`file`), not left at `null` (url-shortener 4c3137f0, bench round 36: a timed-out check of one property test file opened a correction of five files, #1285). These decisions, and the ones that make a correction wait for a sibling correction, hand a failure to a later task or continue a correction, read only the files the output reports failing: jest's `PASS` lines, vitest's `✓` lines, pytest's `PASSED` and all-dots progress lines and a bun file header with only `(pass)` tests under it name files that passed and do not count. Go's base-name assertion lines (`cli_test.go:42:`) and JVM and .NET class names (`com.acme.QueryTest`, `QueryTest.java:42`) count as the files they name. The planned sources a failing test reaches are found from its imports, including a Go test's own package directory and the packages its import block names, Rust `use crate::`, `use <crate>::`, `self::`, `super::` and `mod`, classes of a Java, Kotlin or Scala file's own package that it names, and Python relative imports (#1419, #1420).

A failed task check's finding carries the check's failing lines in its impact (`Failing: ...`). When a failing test's name runs another check of the policy (`bun run typecheck exits successfully` names the `typecheck` check whose command is `bun run typecheck`, or the test name contains the check's whole command), the host runs that check once more on its own and, when it fails, adds its evidence to the finding and the last 3000 characters of its output to the impact. A test that spawns the project's typecheck and asserts only its exit code otherwise shows the correction `Expected: 0, Received: 2` and none of the diagnostics. A correction of a correction whose finding has the same failing lines as the finding the earlier correction was opened for is told so in its suggested fix, with the note to isolate the cause before editing again (md-site d486645a, bench round 34: T008, T011 and T012 each failed on the same test without seeing the tsc output, #1285).

A step that stops at the profile's output cap (finish reason `length`) usually carried a tool call that was cut off and did not run. The next step gets a notice once, naming the cap and asking for smaller edits; like the finalize notice, it stays at the position where it first appeared so the replayed prefix is unchanged. The implementer's output cap is 16384 tokens.

### Cross-run defect classes

Each run used to start with nothing, so the bench control apps repeated one defect class every round: md-site's emphasis delimiters in R55 (T009 354k tokens), R56 (277k), R57 (506k), R59 (346k) and R62 (451k), manlens's trailing spaces (#1970). With the policy field `defectClasses: "on"` (setting `quality.defectClasses`, default `off`; absent means the list is neither read nor written) runs share a list of the classes they corrected, `defect-classes.json` in the BAKED data directory (`src/core/workflows/defect-classes.ts`):

- **Adding.** The `learn` stage takes the run's resolved correctness findings that carry a `family` (the reviewer's label of the defect class, see [Finding chains](#finding-chains)) and files each under one domain (`defectDomain`, #2140): the domain whose words (`DOMAINS`: Markdown, CSV, URLs and HTTP, decimal numbers and money, dates, text layout, JSON, command-line arguments) occur most often in its family, claim, symbol, file path and repro command, a tie going to a domain the run's request and spec name. A finding that names no domain goes to the run's domain when the run names exactly one, else to the `general` bucket; it is never filed under every domain the run names (R68 md-site's `output-symlink-resolution`, on `src/build.ts`, was filed under six). The decimal words are arithmetic words (`decimals`, `rounding`, `currency`, `money`, `amounts`), not an app's subject such as expenses or totals. A known class counts one more run and keeps its newest `CASES_PER_CLASS` (3) cases, each the repro's command and input, whole up to `CASE_TEXT_MAX` (2,000 characters), else a one-line summary (the executable and each argument's first 80 characters, marked `summarized: true`, to read and never to run) instead of a program cut mid-way, and its expected and printed values with their last 500 characters kept. Each domain keeps its `CLASSES_PER_DOMAIN` (8) most recently seen classes. The file is read back before each write, so other processes' classes are kept, and written through a temporary file and a rename. What a run added is recorded as `learning/defect-classes.json`.
- **Showing.** The planner and each planned implementer of a run whose request or spec names a domain with classes get `defectClasses` in their input: each domain's classes with their cases and a note that they are examples of what tends to go wrong, never requirements. A corrective does not get them; it has its own findings. A run whose request and spec name no listed domain gets the `general` bucket, if it holds classes, and nothing else. A planned implementer's `attempts/ID/context.json` records `defectClasses`: what it was shown, or null when the policy is on and the list had nothing for it (#2140). Existing entries filed under several domains before #2140 are not rewritten; each domain keeps only its 8 most recent classes, so they age out.

### Finding chains

A review that finds one defect class on one function one case at a time links each finding to the one before with `repeatOf`: a chain on one file and symbol (`Parser.findDelimitedInline()` and `findDelimitedInline` are one symbol). R62 md-site spent 8.41M of 11.6M tokens on a six-finding chain on `findDelimitedInline`, each corrective patching the reported input of emphasis delimiter runs and the next review finding the next nesting (#1959). With the policy field `chainRewrite: "on"` (setting `quality.chainRewrite`, default `off`; absent means off, so stored policies keep their hash) the host changes the task, not only its context:

- **The chain key.** A chain is keyed by its file and either the symbol or the defect family (#2048). Every reviewer and the convergence agent give every finding a `family`, a `missing` or `partial` finding as well as a correctness one (#2137): a short lower-case label of hyphenated words for its defect class, the rule the code breaks or the behavior left out rather than the input that shows it (`decimal-context-precision`, `nested-inline-delimiters`), the same label for every finding of that class and the one an earlier finding of the class has (`earlierFindings` carry their families), or null. The host compares labels folded (`familyKey`: lower case, each run of other characters one hyphen). A finding of a family on a file joins the latest earlier finding of that family and file that a correction other than its own already addressed, whatever its symbol and its `repeatOf`, and a `repeatOf` link counts when it points to a finding of the same file and either the same symbol or the same family; two cases found in one round are no chain. R66 expenses-py F2 (`build_report`) and F3 (`_share`), both Decimal context precision in `expenses/report.py`, were a chain of one each and F3's corrective cost 641k tokens; R65 md-site F1 (`findDestinationEnd`) and F3 (`renderInline`), nested inline constructs, the same at 1.09M. The family is read the same with the policy off, where a chain is only recorded. A reviewer's own `repeatOf` link also counts when both findings name the same file and either names no symbol (`explicitLink`, #2098): a finding on a test file rarely has a symbol, so R67 ampwhy F4 (a method of `ReaderNumericTest.java`, `repeatOf: F2`) and F2 (the same file, no symbol, no family) were a chain of one and F4's corrective (219k tokens) another patch. It also counts on one file when one symbol contains the other (`containsSymbol`, #2137): a class and its member, the shorter qualified name a run of the longer one's segments ending before its last (`Scanner` and `Scanner.IsEncodingToken`, `Reader` and `Reader::read`, `Reader` and `(*Reader).Read`), split by `symbolPath` at every supported language's separators (`.`, `::`, `#`, `:`, `->`, `\`, `/`, an Elixir arity, type arguments, a Go receiver and an Objective-C message dropped). R68 ewvet scanner F1 (`Scanner`) and F1-3..F1-6 (`Scanner.IsEncodingToken`, each `repeatOf: F1`) were seven chains of one, so no rewrite, no battery. The links the host infers (`familyLink`) keep the key, and a reviewer's link between two unrelated symbols without a shared family stays unjoined. A family also keys a chain across files (#2334): two findings of one family on two test files join as on one file, and a reviewer's `repeatOf` joins two findings of one family on any two files. A class of test code (inputs built by loops or interpolation where the request asks for literal examples) spreads one spec file per round: R74 callmend cmd F1 (`spec/cmd/stream_command_spec.rb`), F1-2 (`check_command_spec.rb`, `repeatOf: F1`) and F1-3 (`cli_spec.rb`, `repeatOf: F1-2`), all `literal-test-inputs`, were three chains of one with `chainRewrite` on, and the class cost 2.00M tokens in cmd and 4.25M over the run. The record of such a chain names its `files`, oldest first; its corrective may write each of them, a test-file chain's sweep lists them before the planned test files next to the reported one, a symbol rewrite's step, battery and sibling probe name them, and the round report (`bench-trace chains`) groups the chain under its oldest file. Two findings of one family on two source files without a reviewer's link stay unjoined.
- **A repeat after an empty corrective.** A review or convergence finding that repeats an earlier finding (its `repeatOf`, else the latest earlier finding on the same file with the same claim, case and spacing folded) whose corrective recorded changes and changed no file over all its sessions (`tasks/TASK/diff.json`, `taskChangedPaths`) records the `repeat_without_change` would-stop once per corrective (key `task-TASK`, `unchangedRepeats`, #2137), with the corrective, its changed paths, the two finding ids and the finding's chain: another session would start from the same files, and the next step is a decision on the finding's owner or scope. R68 ewvet scanner T010 reported "I made no edits in this task" after three sessions (252k tokens), the host marked it implemented and review found F1 again. Shadow: the corrective still opens.
- **A corrective that restores the previous one's removal.** A corrective (origin `repair` or `convergence`) whose patch puts back lines, compared trimmed and ignoring lines that are only punctuation, that the latest earlier corrective of the same file removed (and did not add elsewhere) records the `corrective_restores_removed` would-stop (`restoredRemovals`, #2277): two host gates ask for opposite changes, and the next step is a spec conflict naming both correctives' findings rather than a third corrective. Shadow: the run goes on as before. Both the in-place and the integrated (worktree) task diffs now keep their `patch`. In R72 evalpin foundation T008 removed `#[allow(unused_variables)]` from the prescribed cli stub for the integrity guard, T009 put it back for `cargo clippy -D warnings`, and five correctives ran before a person settled the conflict.
- **Rewrite at the second link.** When a review or convergence finding's `repeatOf` chain on one file and symbol reaches `CHAIN_REWRITE_AT` findings (2, in `src/core/workflows/finding-chain.ts`), its corrective is a rewrite of the symbol (title `Rewrite SYMBOL: ...`): its description asks to rewrite the symbol from the algorithm its spec rows or a named reference algorithm give (for Markdown inline emphasis, CommonMark's delimiter-run stack) instead of patching the case (for a family chain over several symbols: case N of the defect class FAMILY in FILE on those symbols, to rewrite every place of the file that handles the class), and its input carries `battery`: the symbol and file (with `family` and `symbols` for a family chain), the chain's finding ids, every repro of the chain and its side branches (findings that repeat a member) as one table (`command`, `stdin`, `files`, `expected`, `actual`, at most 16 cases, the newest kept), the requirements and criteria the findings name, and a note to make every case pass, not only the last. The host's repro check at `submit_result` holds the whole battery as its regression set. `battery` is recorded in the attempt's `context.json`.
- **A family's sites.** A finding whose family is a pattern of the code text (test inputs built by repetition, packing, interpolation or a loop where the request asks for literal examples) may carry `sites`, at most 32 `{ file, line, text }`: every place the reviewer saw the pattern, which `FAMILY_REVIEW_RULE` asks for (#2343). Before the corrective, the host checks each site ([family-sites](../../src/core/verification/family-sites.ts), `checkSites`): it keeps a site whose `text` occurs within two lines of `line` in the file with something other than blanks and quote delimiters left in its span after `maskNonCode` (so a comment or the inside of a string is no site, in every language `MASK_SYNTAX` has a row for), joins the kept site's planned file to the corrective's writes and lists every kept site under one "Sites of this defect class" step across files; a site in a missing file, outside the root, not on its line, only in a comment or a string, or in a file no planned task writes is dropped. Each check, with the raw and masked lines it read, is recorded under `chains/sites/<task>.json` (kind `finding-chain`, kept in bench traces with the sites' code) and replays in `replayFindingChains` (kind `site`); the next review's `earlierFindings` carry each finding's kept sites. R74 callmend's literal-input class, corrected one spec file per round, cost 4.25M tokens (14.6 percent of the run).
- **A chain on a test file sweeps the tests.** A rewrite decision whose finding is on a test file is no symbol rewrite (#2098): the chain record gets `corrective: "test-sweep"`, the corrective's last step is the test-style sweep of `classSweep` ([class-sweep](../../src/core/verification/class-sweep.ts), `testSweepStep`, over the planned test files of the same directory and extension the corrective may write) and it has no battery: no `battery` attempt-context key, no sibling probe and no battery run before the review (`rewritesSymbol` is false), since a test-file finding has no repro to run. A chain on a source file whose findings name no symbol still rewrites, named by its file (`chainSubject`).
- **Sibling probe.** With `chainProbe: "on"` as well (setting `quality.chainProbe`, default `off`, #1969), before a rewrite corrective's first attempt the `chain-prober` profile (output `chain-family`, model `$review`, read-only tools) is called once with the chain's battery table, its spec rows and the programs a repro may start, and proposes up to 16 more cases of the input class (the reported inputs varied at depth one to three, combined, the empty and a single-token input), each a repro without `actual`. The host drops the cases the battery already holds and runs the rest on the run's files as repros whose actual is their expected value: `reproduced` is a case the code already gets right, `refuted` (ran, printed something else) joins the battery (`familyDecision`), and `inconclusive` is left out. Each joined case becomes a finding (`F2-family-1`, severity high, the case's input and expected with its observed last line as `actual`, `repeatOf` the chain's finding, owned by the rewrite), so the rewrite's `battery`, its repro check and the battery before the next review hold it. R62 md-site found four more members of `findDelimitedInline` one review round at a time. The probe runs once per rewrite task; a failed call is recorded without cases and leaves the battery as it was.
- **Battery before the review.** Before each review round the host runs the battery of every chain a finished rewrite corrected, on the run's files, each case as a repro (`runRepro`). A case that shows its reported behavior again becomes a finding (`Battery case F1 of SYMBOL shows its reported behavior again after T010`, severity high, the case's repro, `repeatOf` the case's finding) that opens a correction at once, without a review call; it counts as a review round. That correction is the next link of the chain, so it is a rewrite with the battery again. When every case passes, the review runs as before.
- **Records and replay.** Each corrective whose findings form a chain of at least two records `chains/tasks/TASK.json` (kind `finding-chain`): the chain's ids and `repeatOf` links, the finding's `family` and, for a chain over several symbols, its `symbols`, which have repros, the length, the threshold, the policy and the decision (`rewrite` or `patch`), also when the policy is off. Each battery run records `chains/battery/ID.json`: each case's finding, task, symbol and outcome, and the decision (`reopen` or `pass`); it is the evidence of the findings it opens. Each sibling probe records `chains/family/TASK.json`: the chain's finding, the cases it was shown, and each probed case's outcome and decision (`join` or `skip`); it is the evidence of the findings it opens. Bench traces keep both kinds, and `replayFindingChains` in `src/core/workflows/trace.ts` decides each again from its recorded inputs, a probe per case (counter `newChainDecisions`). Traces also keep each finding's `repeatOf`, file, symbol, family (folded, not stood in) and repro (its prose stood in), so `replayChainEscalation` shows where the policy would have rewritten on a run recorded with it off, with each step's family as a column (`families` replays a recorded run with labels a reviewer would have given), and `bun scripts/bench-trace.ts chains` prints each chain's length, battery results and corrective tokens and cost for the round report (#1981), a family chain grouped by its file and family.

`run_repro`, the reviewer's repro tool, also takes `cases`: a list of up to 16 repros run one after the other in one call, returning `results` with each case's index and outcome and `reproduced`, the indexes that reproduced. A list counts as one of the review's 8 repro runs. R62 md-site called `run_repro` 28 times for 2.12M tokens, each call sending the context again. The single-repro form is unchanged.

### Edge-input probe

The costliest correctives of rounds 53 and 54 fixed inputs a criterion allows but no test tried: a non-ASCII redirect target that Bun's `Headers` rejects (R54 url-shortener, 2.43M tokens), an amount `1e4300` past Python's integer-string limit (R54 expenses-py, 586k), Decimal totals past 28 digits (R53, 295k). The final review found each after every test task had been written on top of the code (#1808). With the policy field `edgeProbe: "on"` (setting `quality.edgeProbe`, default `off`; absent means off, so stored policies keep their hash) the host probes each planned source task once, when it settles without failures and before any task that depends on it starts:

- **The call.** The `edge-prober` profile (output `edge-cases`, model slot `$review`, read-only) gets the task, its criteria, the source files it wrote, the programs the run's checks start, and the edge taxonomy of the task's languages (`edgeTaxonomy` in `src/core/verification/edge-inputs.ts`): input classes (huge and tiny numbers, long and non-ASCII text, empty values, boundary dates and sizes, the program's reserved words as data) and the runtime limits of each language (`NUMERIC_LIMITS`, a language-bound record with a row per supported language). It proposes up to 12 cases, each a repro without `actual` that names the criterion allowing its input. A task whose writes are all tests, a corrective, and a task without criteria are not probed.
- **The run.** The host runs each case naming one of the task's criteria through the project's entry points (`runRepro`), with `actual` set to its expected value. `edgeDecision` decides: `refuted` (the program ran and printed something else) is a finding; `reproduced` (it printed the expected value) and `inconclusive` (it crashed without output or could not start) pass, and are left to the tests and the review.
- **Crashes in the task's own code (shadow).** `edgeCrashDecision` decides each inconclusive case again on its output (#2136): a stack frame inside a file the probed task writes makes it a crash of the task's own code, with the first error line (or that frame's line) as its actual. A case whose command itself fails (a syntax error in the command, a module that does not exist, frames only outside the task's files) stays inconclusive. The frame table `CRASH_FRAMES` in `edge-inputs.ts` holds one spelling per runtime: Bun, Node and Deno, Python, Go, the JVM (base names), .NET, Rust, Ruby, PHP, Elixir, Dart, Swift, C and C++ sanitizers, Lua and Bash. The decision runs in shadow: each crash records a `decision.would_stop` of the `edge_crash_finding` reason, which names the task, the case and the actual, and nothing opens. Once the reason is promoted, the crash becomes a finding of the task, corrected at once. In R68 url-shortener, both AC-007 cases of T001 crashed in `src/storage.ts` (`Missing parameter "code"`) and passed, and the run blocked at 1.19M tokens. The chain battery and the sibling probe record the same decision against the chain's file. A reviewer's repro gets the same decision against the finding's file (#2152): when the review gate or the convergence gate keeps a finding a note (or an unreproduced limitation) because its repro was inconclusive, and the repro crashed inside the finding's own file, the gate records `crash: "finding"` on that finding and an `edge_crash_finding` would-stop that names it. A repro that fails on its own stays a note with `crash: "pass"`. Nothing changes until the reason is promoted, together with the probes'.
- **The correction.** Each failing case becomes a correctness finding of the task (severity high, basis spec, the case's criterion, its repro with the printed value as `actual`) and opens a task-level correction at once, so the tests are written against the corrected code. When the failing output's first workspace stack frame names a source a settled task of the run wrote, the finding is on that file and the correction goes to the task that wrote it, the probed task's first source otherwise (#2133: in R68 url-shortener the probe of T002's handler found a defect in T001's `storage.ts`); the case records the file as `file`.
- **Records and replay.** Each probe records `probes/edge/TASK.json` (kind `edge-probe`): the task's languages and source files, and each case's criterion, outcome, decision, the last 400 characters of its output (`outputTail`) its crash decision (`crash`) and, for a finding on another task's source, its `file`. A failed call records no cases and changes nothing. Bench traces keep the kind and the output tail as it is, and `replayEdgeProbes` in `src/core/workflows/trace.ts` decides each case and its crash again from the tail (counter `newEdgeDecisions`; a record from before #2136 has no output, so its crash decisions are null). The chain battery's and the sibling probe's cases keep `outputTail` and `crash` too, and `replayFindingChains` replays them as `crash` boundaries. The serial and the parallel implement paths both probe; `tests/workflow-scenario-edge-probe.test.ts` covers the scenario.

### Progress watcher

Every agent attempt that submits a result is watched for a stuck loop before each model step. The rules, checked in this order over the attempt's own steps:

- **Repeated reads**: the same `read`, `navigate`, `bake_find` or `bake_impact` call, with the same input, three times since the last edit.
- **Same failure**: the last three check results are the same: the same tool and check, the same status and the same output once `normalizeCheckOutput` has taken out what changes between two runs of one failure (timings, sbt's `elapsed time` line, temporary paths). The tools whose results count are `request_check`, `run_probe`, `run_repro` and `replay_rows` (`CHECK_TOOLS`), and `apply_fix`, whose `check` part is one more run of its check. A result is never compared whole: a `request_check` result carries an `evidenceId` and a `snapshotId` new for every run, so before [#2437](https://github.com/Gulliby-development/baked/issues/2437) two identical failures never compared equal, and R77 pairwick ran one failing `scalafmt` check up to 16 times in a task with no steering. After the steering message an `apply_fix` is not progress for this signal: a fixer that cannot change the failure is the loop itself. Rule: a tool whose result the watcher compares adds its real result shape (with the ids and timings it carries) to the fixtures of `tests/workflow-progress-watch.test.ts`, which fails when a `CHECK_TOOLS` entry has no real-shape case.
- **No edits**: ten steps with tool calls and no edit, for an implementer whose task writes files.
- **Unchecked spend** (`unchecked-spend`): half the attempt's token cap spent, for an implementer whose task writes files, with edits that no `request_check`, `run_probe` or `run_repro` call has run on since. Its steering message asks for a check of the edits and a submission now; only a check clears it, and three more steps without one stop the attempt. A convergence corrective spent its whole cap (1.22M tokens) on two unchecked edits without submitting, and its restart spent as much again for a 710-byte fix ([#1764](https://github.com/Gulliby-development/baked/issues/1764)).

Outside these rules, for an implementer whose task writes files: a step whose text says an edit tool is missing (the wording the restart rule for a lost tool matches, such as "exposes no edit_file or multi_edit tool") while the session has edit tools adds a message that names them (signal `no-edit-tool`). A second such step with no edit in between stops the attempt the same way, with the session's edit tools in the `attempt.advisory` detail, so the restart for a lost tool follows without spending the cap first. From an attempt's fourth `read` until its first edit, a file it already read with the same span and that has not changed since is answered with a line saying so, not with its content again. It gets the content when it asks for a fresh read (the history compaction stub tells it to), when more than three tool calls passed since that read (so compaction may have elided its output), and always in a role without edit tools, such as a reviewer or verifier. A restarted bench session spent 805k tokens on six reads, re-reading one test file five times, before it ended blocked saying it had no edit tool ([#1360](https://github.com/Gulliby-development/baked/issues/1360)).

The first signal adds one steering message that names the pattern and asks the agent to apply the change, change approach, or submit what blocks it as unresolved. The attempt is stopped only when the same pattern goes on after that message: the same read or the same check result again, three more steps without an edit, or three more steps without a check after unchecked spend. The watcher measures time in model steps, not seconds. The step sent with the steering message is a grace step (`STEER_GRACE_STEPS`, 1, in `src/core/workflows/progress-watch.ts`): an edit or a check in it still counts as progress, but nothing in it counts toward a stop. A repeated read or check result stops the attempt only in a later step, and a read first made in the grace step counts as seen. The `no-edit-tool` stop waits the same way. The stop therefore comes at the earliest one full model step after the step that answered the steering message. In md-site 54da0241 (bench round 63) the attempt was stopped 14 s after the steering message, by the step that answered it ([#1978](https://github.com/Gulliby-development/baked/issues/1978)). An edit clears the signal (a check clears unchecked spend), and an attempt is steered at most once. The stop switches the attempt to its submit-only final step (stop reason `watcher` in `attempts/ID/usage.json`), so the run gets a result with the blocker listed instead of spending the step or token cap; an attempt the watcher stopped does not get the continuation with raised caps. Both the steering message and the stop are recorded as an `attempt.advisory` event, shown in the activity feed of the TUI run view and the desktop run screen and in the `--events` stream of `baked workflow run` and `resume`.

With `restoreFile: "on"` in `workflows.policy` or a policy file (absent means `off`), an implementer gets `restore_file` (#1835). It takes a path whose current content has syntax errors the task's edits added (the check of [syntax errors after edits](#diagnostics-after-edits), #977) and writes back the newest pre-image of the task's own edits of that file that parses, across the task's attempts, so a restart that began from the broken file still finds the version before the bad edit; when none parses, the file as it was before the task's first edit. A file the task created with no version that parsed is refused with a request to write it again. A file the syntax check does not read (it parses Rust, Go, TypeScript, JavaScript, Python, C# and Java) is refused too, since no version of it can be told to parse. The restore is journaled as an edit, so it clears a watcher signal like any edit. While the attempt's last edit of a file left it not parsing, the steering message of every signal but unchecked spend names that file with up to three of its error lines and offers the tool. In bench round 56, hdrlens negotiate T004 and md-site T005 escaped a quote or a backtick one level too deep in a multi_edit of a test file, reread the broken lines until the watcher stopped them, reported that they had no edit tool, and their restarts began from the same file (up to 0.5M and 1.5M tokens after the bad edit).

**Host finish after a cap or watcher stop** ([#1978](https://github.com/Gulliby-development/baked/issues/1978)). Some correctives stop before they submit a complete result: at the token or step cap, or by the watcher. These include review-pass and convergence correctives, class sweeps (`sweepFiles`) and task-level repairs. If such an attempt changed files, the host checks the carried edits before it starts another model session. It reruns the repros of the task's findings. A task-level repair, whose finding names a failed required check (`Required check ID failed`, `Task T required check ID failed`), reruns that check. The host also runs every required task-end check. When no repro reproduces and every check passes, the host finishes the task. Its result becomes `implemented`, with those check runs as evidence, and the task goes on through its usual task checks and integration. `tasks/TASK_ID/host-finish.json` records how the attempt stopped (`stoppedBy`: `tokens`, `steps` or `watcher`), the repro verdict, the checks and their evidence IDs, and the changed files. A cap stop that would otherwise stop the run waits for this check and stops the run only when the check fails. In any other case, such as a repro that still reproduces, a failing check, a planned task, or a corrective with neither a repro nor a named check, the usual path runs: a widened correction, a continuation at the cap, a restart or a stop. The same rule covers the single-writer path, task worktrees with several writers, and every provider, Claude Code included. This happens in the scheduler, after the attempt ends. In md-site 54da0241 (bench round 63) T012's first session ended at its uncached cap with the repro already passing. Two more sessions, each re-sending a ~107k-token prompt, then spent 2.28M tokens to submit the same diff. Each attempt's `usage.json` also records its prompt size: `prompt.firstBytes` and `prompt.largestBytes` are the serialized messages its first step sent and the most any step sent, and each step has its own `sentBytes`. `baked workflow usage` returns the figures as each attempt's `prompt`. The first call in an implementer attempt's `calls` timing also records `promptParts` (#2470). It gives the size in characters of each part of the workflow context: the instructions, each run-level document (`request`, `spec`, `plan`, `graph`) and each key of the attempt's own input (`extra.task`, `extra.taskFiles`, `extra.evidence`, ...). R79 deltaglue's first-call prompts held 177,609 characters at the median, against 36,569 to 99,338 in the other apps of the round, and nothing recorded what they held.

**Green checkpoint of an attempt** ([#2470](https://github.com/Gulliby-development/baked/issues/2470), shadow). The host finish above looks only at the state an attempt ends in. An implementer attempt also keeps the latest state on which every required check of its task passed, as `attempts/ATTEMPT_ID/checkpoint.json`. The required checks are the ones the host finish would run (the checks the task's findings name and its gated task-end checks), limited to the ones the attempt can run itself with `request_check`. A check run counts for the journal position it ran at, which is the attempt's applied file edits so far. The checkpoint needs a passing run of every required check at the same position. It records that position, the checks and their evidence, the findings' repro result when `run_repro` ran there (`passed`, `reproduced` or `none`), and the attempt's tokens at that point. Later check runs update `after`: the latest position, and the required checks whose latest run failed on edits past the checkpoint. When the attempt ends unfinished and the host does not finish the task, its checkpoint is read before the task goes on or the run blocks. That covers a cap or watcher stop the host finish turned down, a cap stop that stops the run, and a stall. If the required checks failed after the checkpoint, the host records a `decision.would_finish` event (reason `attempt_green_checkpoint`), once per attempt. The event carries the stop (`stoppedBy`: `tokens`, `steps`, `watcher` or `stall`), the checkpoint's position and the position the checks ended at (`journalEnd`), the checks and their evidence, the repro result at the checkpoint, the checks that failed after it (`failedAfter`) and the tokens spent after it (`tokensAfter`). Nothing else changes: the task continues, restarts or blocks as before. Restoring the checkpoint's state from the journal's pre-images and finishing the task on it will be a pull request of its own, after a bench round with no false would-finish. `replayAttemptCheckpoints` in [trace](../../src/core/workflows/trace.ts) decides each recorded checkpoint again (`newWouldFinishes`). In R79 deltaglue chunks T004, attempt c8e5afe0 passed `tests-affected`, `tests`, `typecheck` and `lint` at 23:19:17. It then edited twice more, the checks failed, and at 23:19:40 it ended at its all-token cap (839,599 of 750,000 tokens). The run blocked `attempt_cap`.

The watcher only sees one attempt from inside its model loop. A provider call that hangs, or a host process that stops, is not detected here ([#604](https://github.com/Gulliby-development/baked/issues/604)).

### Run cost

A run's estimated cost in USD is `estimatedCostUsd` in its status (`baked workflow status RUN_ID`, `GET /v1/workflow/runs/RUN_ID`) and in `budget.updated` events. Each model attempt adds its cost when it finishes, priced from its `attempts/ATTEMPT/usage.json` with the model's price table: uncached input, cache reads, cache writes and output each at their own price, so a cache-heavy run (most of a claude-code run's input is cache traffic) costs far less than its token count at the input price. Host attempts, attempts without per-step usage and models without a price add nothing; the cost stays null until a priced attempt finishes. Runs recorded before the run kept its cost get it summed from their attempts' usage. The TUI shows it under the Tokens bar of a run and on the run list, the desktop app in the run header, run list and project home. The token limits still count every token alike; a run that should stop on dollars sets a cost cap.

### Cost cap

`maxCostUsd` caps a run's estimated cost in USD. It is opt-in: the default is `null`, the built-in policy and every budget preset keep only token, call and time limits, and a cap works beside them, never instead of them. A run takes the first cap that is set:

1. the one asked for at start: `baked workflow run --max-cost-usd N`, `maxCostUsd` in `POST /v1/workflow/runs`, or the Cost cap field of the TUI new-run form and the desktop start form;
2. `maxCostUsd` in the policy (`workflows.policy` in the config, or a `--policy` file);
3. the Cost cap setting, `runBudget.maxCostUsd` in the global or project config (Settings, Timeouts and limits in the desktop app; `/timeouts`, Workflow Cost Cap in the TUI). It is a key of its own because a project's `workflows` replaces the global one and must carry a whole policy.

The run freezes the cap in its policy at start, so a later change of the setting does not touch running runs.

- At start, and on every reroute, each model the run can call (the run model, every stage and profile route and their fallback models) must have a price in `src/core/llm/pricing.ts`, the one price table every provider shares. A model without one is refused with `maxCostUsd needs a price for every model the run can call; no price is known for MODEL`, so no model can spend outside the cap. Family guesses and Copilot request prices count as priced; they are approximate, like the cost itself.
- Before each model call, and before each step widens an attempt's reservation, the cost spent (`estimatedCostUsd`), the cost every other open attempt reserves and the call's own tokens must fit under the cap. Calls and reserved tokens use the price of finished attempts in their owning workflow stage, with planned and corrective tasks sharing task history. Matching models retain their paid cache discounts; a changed model uses the stage's measured token mix at its own prices. Without finished priced attempts in that stage, a priced model's call uses the stage's own token mix at the model's prices: 15% uncached input, 80% cache reads and 5% output for task calls, 60%, 30% and 10% for the others. The run's blended price no longer stands in: in bench round 51 (commitbump-update) planning on Opus in one-step uncached calls followed cache-heavy research, so its blended price put specify at a twentieth of what it paid and the first Sonnet task call at about three times (#1740). Child calls inherit their owning stage. Token affordability uses the same task price; before a task attempt is priced, it prices the remaining tokens on the model the plan sizing priced the tasks for (`sizing/plan-N.json`), at the task mix. Parallel attempts therefore reserve against what is left, and the last wave of a run does not start tasks the cap cannot pay for. A call that does not fit stops the run with `budget_exhausted` (`The next model call would pass the run's cost cap`), like the token limit.
- Before each attempt the run also forecasts its total: what it spent and reserved, plus each planned task still open at its sizing price (`sizing/plan-N.json`) times how far the finished tasks of the same kind went over their price, less what that task's attempts already used or hold in flight (both are in the spent and reserved dollars, so a continuation after a token cap is priced at only the rest of its task), plus the final review and convergence. A kind keeps its own sizing estimate unscaled (ratio 1) until three of its tasks have finished; the measured ratio then has one-third weight against the estimate, two-thirds at four and full weight at five, as the [initiative estimate](#initiatives) calibrates features. One test task that carried a shared fixture at 3x does not price every other test task at 3x (#1648). Each remaining stage is priced on its routed models. Matching stage and model attempts supply the measured price, including cache discounts and writes; a model without matching attempts uses that stage's measured token mix at its own prices. A stage with no measured mix uses the default estimate mix. Review child calls count toward their owning workflow stage. Planning stages often run on other models with little cache and cost about twice as much per token as the tasks, so the blended price alone forecast a bench feature at $11.88 that finished at $9.03 (#1505). Once at least 3 priced tasks have finished and the forecast passes the cap, the run stops with `budget_exhausted` and `reason: "cost-forecast"` while the money is still unspent, and records the forecast as `forecasts/cost-N.json`. The stop message gives the forecast and the cap to extend to; extend the run (with a higher cap) to finish it, or stop it. Each cap is asked about once: resuming at the same cap spends on to it, and a cap a person raised that the forecast still passes is asked about again. A cap the host grew on its own since the stop (`sizing.maxRunCostUsd`, see [Cost cap](#cost-cap)) counts as the cap already answered, so the run does not stop again after each automatic growth. The TUI names the forecast as what stopped the run (`the run is forecast to cost about $X, above its $Y cap`), and the desktop run screen and `workflow status` show the stop message; B in the TUI, Extend in the desktop app and `workflow extend` raise the cap as for any cap stop. Whether or not it stops the run, the forecast is kept once for each count of finished priced tasks as `forecasts/progress-NNN.json`, and the run report's Usage section gives the first and the last projection against the cap (or the cap and its tolerance) and whether the forecast stopped the run. `costForecast: "off"` in the policy turns the forecast off and records none (absent means `ask`); `advise` records it and grows a cap that may grow, but never stops the run on it, as an initiative's feature runs do until the initiative's forecast is measured; it is the forecast's [advisory mode](#stop-reasons-and-shadow-mode), so the stop it would have made is recorded as a `decision.would_stop` event once per cap. A bench run priced its test tasks at 250k tokens, saw them use 550k to 1.47M, and spent to $11.92 of its $12 cap with tasks unfinished and no review, although it could not finish from about $5.60 on.
- `costTolerance` turns the cap into a soft target with a hard ceiling above it. It is `off` by default, so a cap stays a promise unless the policy asks for a band. A number from 0 to 0.5 is the band as a share of the cap; `auto` takes it from the number of planned tasks in the latest `sizing/plan-N.json` (the planned task rows before the plan is priced): up to 8 tasks 10%, up to 16 15%, up to 24 20%, up to 32 25%, more 30%, because bigger plans miss their price by more and the plan's size means the same on every provider. With a band, every cost check above (each model call, the attempt check, the extension past the sizing ceiling and the forecast) holds the run to `maxCostUsd × (1 + tolerance)` instead of the cap, and a stop there says `cost ceiling`. Once the cost spent and reserved passes the cap, the run winds up: planned tasks and the final review still run, but only critical and high review and convergence findings start corrections; medium and low ones are kept as notes, as after the first repair round (a reproduced spec finding still starts one). Status carries `costCeilingUsd`, the budget view `cost.ceilingUsd` and `cost.wrapUp`; the TUI shows `up to $X to finish` beside the cap and says when the run is winding up, and the desktop run screen measures the Cost cap meter against the ceiling. A bench run priced 2 to 3 times too low spent $11.92 of its $12 cap and stopped with two of 26 tasks and the review left, where the whole run needed about $14; `auto` gives it 25%, a $15 ceiling. An unattended loop that sets a band keeps its own round budget as the hard stop and starts a run only while the round can pay for the run's ceiling.
- `baked workflow doctor` and `baked workflow estimate` take `--max-cost-usd N` too (`maxCostUsd` in `GET /v1/workflow/doctor` and `/estimate`). With a cap and a priced estimate, the estimate carries `cost: { maxCostUsd, fits }` and prints `Cost cap $N: fits | may be passed | will very likely be passed`; the doctor adds a `budget.cost` warning when the estimate's cost may pass the cap. The desktop start form shows the same under its Cost cap field.
- Status carries the cap as `maxCostUsd` (left out when the run has none). The TUI shows `about $X of the $Y cap` under the Tokens bar and names the cap when it stopped the run; the desktop run screen shows a Cost cap meter and `$X of $Y` in the header.
- Raising the limits of a capped run (B in the TUI, Extend in the desktop app, `workflow extend` with no values) doubles the cap when the spent and reserved cost reaches 80% of it, or when no token, call or time limit is 80% used, so the cap is what stopped the run. `--cost-usd N` (`costUsd` in `POST /v1/workflow/runs/RUN_ID/extend`) sets a cap of its own; it can only go up, and a run started without a cap cannot get one by extending. A run blocked for another reason (`invalid_output`, `no_progress`, ...) with its spending at 95% of its cap or more can be extended too, since its next step is a cost stop anyway; an `attempt_cap` stop still resumes instead. In bench round 35 a feature run blocked `invalid_output` at $1.45 of $1.46, extend was refused, and only a resume to the budget stop let the same extend through. The spending counted here is the cost spent plus what open attempts still reserve; the snapshot carries the reserved part as `reservedCostUsd`, so a run blocked at 90% spent with a 6% reservation can be extended. The TUI offers B, the desktop app Extend, by the same rule (`canExtendRun` in `src/core/workflows/extendable.ts`): the TUI status line of such a run names B, and the desktop run screen opens its Budget panel on its own, as for a budget stop, and its Cost cap meter shows the reserved dollars.
- A policy's `sizing.maxRunCostUsd` lets the cap grow with the token budget: every growth of the token budget the host makes itself (the sizing raise after plan sizing, `planning-floor`, `auto-growth`, `planning-growth`, `auto-extend`) raises `maxCostUsd` by the same factor as the tokens, rounded up to the cent, up to that amount, and its `policy-revisions/sizing-N.json` or `policy-revisions/auto-N.json` records the cap before and after. A model-call reservation or its expansion can also grow the cap without increasing tokens, to its projected cost plus tolerance headroom, rounded up to the cent and limited by the same bound. Growth is refused if even the bound and its tolerance cannot cover the projection. This records a `call-growth` policy revision with `approvedBy: "sizing"`. Calls beyond the bound still stop on the cost ceiling. Without it the cap stays where it started. An initiative sets it for each feature run under a dollar budget (see [Dollar budget](#initiatives)).
- Every cost check (the reservation before a model call, the forecast and the wrap-up band) holds spending to the larger of the cap the attempt started with and the run's stored cap, so a cap one attempt grew applies to the attempts already running.

Two test files check this accounting over generated runs rather than one scenario at a time. `tests/workflow-accounting-invariants.test.ts` holds [fast-check](https://fast-check.dev) properties of the forecast: it is never below what the run spent and reserved; finishing a task never raises what remains to be spent while its kind's ratio does not rise; an open task's attempt in flight is counted once; pricing twice, or from a connection without the cached price samples, gives the same forecast; splitting an open task into two whose prices add up to its own keeps the total within the tolerance; and a kind's own ratio prices its open tasks only from three finished tasks. `tests/workflow-scheduler-model.test.ts` drives the real service with the fixture executor through random sequences of start, approve, resume, extend, pause, cancel, fork, reroute, a worker that dies in its next implementation attempt and a host restart, and after each command compares the runs with a small reference model: run states and pending approvals, fork parents, attempt lineage (every attempt in a step of its run, numbered without gaps, none left running), reservations (never negative, held only by an attempt whose usage is unknown) and the run's tokens and calls (the sum of its attempts, never decreasing). A change that adds an accounting quantity or a scheduler command adds its property or its command there.

### Stop reasons and shadow mode

Every reason the host stops a run on, or declines an auto-approval for, is registered in `src/core/workflows/stop-reasons.ts` with a mode (#1714):
- `enforce` stops the run (`budget_exhausted` with the reason as `details.reason`) or declines the approval.
- `shadow` computes the same decision, records a `decision.would_stop` event and goes on.
- `advisory` does the same as `shadow`, and the run screens also show the record to the person, who is not asked to act.

The policy's `stopModes` sets a mode per reason, for example `"stopModes": { "cost-forecast": "shadow" }` under `workflows.policy` in the config file. Without an entry each reason keeps its default.

| Reason | Guards | Modes | Default |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `cost-forecast` | the [cost forecast](#cost-cap) passes the cap or its ceiling | all | `enforce`; `advisory` under `costForecast: "advise"`, as in an initiative's feature runs |
| `approval-scope` | the plan has more agent tasks or files than the [auto-approval](#auto-approval) limits | all | `enforce` |
| `initiative-forecast` | an [initiative's](#initiatives) binding dollar forecast passes its ceiling, or with `forecastStop` on its token forecast passes the shared budget | all | `enforce` |
| `initiative-dollar-share` | an [initiative](#initiatives) feature would start with less spare dollars than its priced low estimate | all | `shadow` |
| `initiative-dollar-cap` | no dollars left under an [initiative's](#initiatives) cap after its open runs' plan needs pay for a starting feature's own low estimate, so the initiative would pause (`dollar_cap`) with one extend remedy (#2218) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `initiative-row-owner` | an [initiative's](#initiatives) split gives a prescribed row file to a feature although one of its rows holds a literal that only a feature depending on it cites ([prescribed row files](#initiatives), #2487); enforced, the decomposer gets the problem back and a split that still has it is refused | all | `shadow` |
| `initiative-plan-charge` | an [initiative's](#initiatives) token pool holds an open feature run with a sized plan at its plan's need instead of its run limit (the plan charge, #2100), which can start a feature sooner or park one; promoted after bench rounds 70 and 71 (#2245) | all | `enforce` |
| `initiative-pool-growth` | an open feature run of an [initiative](#initiatives) holds a growth bound (its sizing ceilings, its dollar bound or an `auto-extend` hard ceiling) above what the token pool or the dollar ceiling holds for it with the other runs at their use (#2280); the run's own token growth is already held there, enforced (#2468) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `initiative-time-share` | an [initiative](#initiatives) feature would start although the shared active time left after the other waiting features' low estimates is below its own low estimate at the measured pace (#2413); stays shadow until the pace leaves out silent provider time (#2414) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `initiative-plan-share` | a started [initiative](#initiatives) feature run's own sized plan needs more than its ceiling (its share of the token pool) and the pool does not cover that need beside the waiting features, so the run would stop on its share in the middle of its tasks (#2488); the run still goes on under assume, and its promotion parks the feature before its tasks | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `repeated_failure` | a corrective's required checks fail exactly as the corrective's before it in the same chain (#1996), or a task ends unfinished with the same failing checks as its previous unfinished end (#2437) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `corrective_scope` | a task-level corrective cannot write a file its own failed check names in the failing task's files (#2094) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `check-config-approval` | a plan modifies or deletes a linter, formatter or type checker config other than `tsconfig*` and `biome*` (#2221; see [Check configs](#auto-approval)); enforced, the approval goes to a person | all | `shadow` |
| `corrective_unowned_file` | a corrective's evidence (its finding's file, the stack frames of its repro, its attempts' unresolved items) names a file it may not write (#2133); enforced, the host routes, widens, amends, parks or blocks (see below) | all | `shadow` |
| `upstream_stale_file` | the file a corrective would park on is the only one its evidence names, a test file of an upstream initiative feature whose run is completed (#2485); enforced, the host amends it in this feature instead of parking (see below) | all | `shadow` |
| `repeat_without_change` | a review or convergence finding repeats one whose corrective changed no file ([finding chains](#finding-chains), #2137) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `corrective_restores_removed` | a corrective puts back lines the previous corrective of the same file removed ([finding chains](#finding-chains), #2277) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `generated_case` | a task's test files that hold a case build inputs from generated values (repetition, formatting, packing, interpolation, a loop when banned) where the request or a criterion asks for literal examples (#2344); enforced, the walk refuses the submission naming each `path:line (kind)` (#2469) | `shadow`, `advisory`, `enforce` | `enforce` |
| `written_tests_unselected` | a task's written-tests check, selected by the test classes its files declare, exits 0 and runs no test (#2363): the host's filter, not the task's tests | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `edge_crash_finding` | a probed case (edge probe, chain battery, sibling probe) crashes inside a file the probed code's task writes, which would be a finding of that task (#2136), or a reviewer finding's inconclusive repro crashes inside the finding's file, which would start a correction instead of a note (#2152) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `repro_blind` | a corrective's repro prints the same output after the attempt changed the finding's file, and names a path outside the root it runs in, so it cannot see the correction (task-end repro check, #2287) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `upstream_symbol` | a released [planned-ahead](#initiatives) feature's spec names a symbol of an upstream feature that the accepted upstream files do not have (#2135) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `baseline_unrepairable` | a required check fails before any change on files no task of the plan writes, and the host fixer cannot repair it ([baseline comparison](#baseline-comparison-in-update-runs), #2215) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `upstream_baseline_debt` | a required check fails before any change on the base an [initiative](#initiatives) feature run starts its tasks from (its release, for a planned-ahead run), in files the boundary of an accepted upstream feature owns (#2228) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `baseline_signature` | a required check fails as it did before any change once durations, addresses, seeds and temporary paths are compared by shape, while the live signature tells the two runs apart ([baseline comparison](#baseline-comparison-in-update-runs), #2295) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `ci_check_drift` | a CI workflow (`.github/workflows/*.yml`) or root README the run changed runs a gate command that no configured check runs as written ([CI commands against the checks](#ci-commands-against-the-checks), #2284) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `host_content_conflict` | a task-level corrective the host writes itself (`hostWritten`) would write its files with the content they already hold, so it cannot change the failing check (#2456) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `fixer_unusable` | the fixer of a required feature check that passed before any change fails and changes nothing when the host runs it once, before the first task, on a tracked file of the plan's code kinds in a copy of the run (`fixers/preflight/<check>.json`, #2442) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
