| `worktree_environment` | the deepest worktree a task's checks run in is longer than a project language's test tool handles on this platform (`WORKTREE_PATH_LIMITS`: a project whose manifest it names; `dart test` over 88 characters on Windows), checked once before the first task (#2454) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `supplied_drift` | a file a task delivered leaves the block the request gives for it verbatim: a whole file, a Markdown section or an exact diff ([delivered files against the request's verbatim blocks](#delivered-files-against-the-requests-verbatim-blocks), #2332) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `row_block_drift` | a test file the run delivered lacks a quoted literal of a row the request prescribes for it, holds the row in its place without one of its tokens or out of order (#2521), counts a long byte run of it differently (#2515), or holds a row beyond them, added or duplicated (#2503) ([delivered test files against the request's prescribed rows](#delivered-test-files-against-the-requests-prescribed-rows), #2486) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `case_not_prescribed` | a criterion cites a literal that no row the request prescribes holds, for a task that writes only prescribed row files ([task-end case check](#diagnostics-after-edits), #2501) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `committed_tree` | a required file-local static check that passed on the working tree fails on a clean worktree of the feature's accepted commit ([worktree execution](#worktree-execution), #2225) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `test_cap` | a test file a task writes declares more tests than the brief's per-file or per-class cap ([test integrity](#test-integrity), #2368); enforced, the submission goes back to the task naming the file, its count and the cap | all | `shadow` |
| `silent_check_failure` | a required check exits nonzero with no failure line of any toolchain in its output ([check failures that print no failure line](#context-compaction), #2418); enforced, the checks stage runs it once more before opening a correction. It records a `decision.would_rerun` event instead of a would-stop | all | `shadow` |
| `attempt_green_checkpoint` | an implementer attempt ends unfinished (a cap, the watcher or a stall) after its required checks failed on edits past a state on which every one passed ([green checkpoint of an attempt](#progress-watcher), #2470); enforced, the host would finish the task from that state. It records a `decision.would_finish` event instead of a would-stop | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `host-resume-on-stored-limit` | a run blocked `budget_exhausted` on its own active time, tokens or calls whose stored limits now exceed its usage; enforced, the host would resume it once. It records a `decision.would_resume` event instead of a would-stop ([initiatives](#initiatives), #2226) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `host-settle-interrupted-check` | a check's command (its intent names a `checkId`) of unknown outcome whose process is gone after the host stopped; enforced, the host would settle it as not applied and continue, and the check runs again at its stage. It records a `decision.would_resume` event, and the recovery request stays with the person ([interrupted effects](#pause-crash-and-recovery), #2415) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `cost_refusal_wait` | a run past its tasks is refused a review or convergence call on its cost ceiling and its block names a priced `extend` remedy; enforced, the run would wait on that remedy instead of blocking ([a started run's review and convergence](#initiatives), #2249) | `shadow`, `advisory` | `shadow` |
| `cost` | spent and reserved dollars reach the cost cap, or a model has no known price | `enforce` | `enforce` |
| `run-budget` | the token, agent-call or active-time budget is used up | `enforce` | `enforce` |
| `budget_exhausted`, `attempt_cap`, `context_window` | a task stopped by the budget before its first edit, an attempt at its token cap, a context past the model's window | `enforce` | `enforce` |
| `deadline`, `evidence-size`, `walkthrough-steps`, `budget-share` | the active deadline, the 10 MiB artifact and 100 MiB evidence caps, the walk-through step limit, an initiative with no share left | `enforce` | `enforce` |

The hard limits on money, tokens, time and storage take only `enforce`, because a shadow mode would spend past a limit the person set. The policy schema rejects any other mode for them.

Under `approval-scope`, only the task and file counts can run in shadow mode. Paths outside `allowedPaths` and deletions always decline, because they guard what the run may touch.

A would-stop is recorded once per key:
- `cost-forecast`: once per cap (`cap-<USD>`), although the forecast is checked before every attempt.
- `approval-scope`: once per approval digest (`plan-<digest>`).
- `check-config-approval`: once per approval digest (`plan-<digest>`), with the writes it would keep with a person.
- `initiative-forecast`: on the initiative, once per count of measured features for the dollar forecast (`usd-m<N>`) and once per approved plan revision and accepted count for the token forecast (`tokens-r<revision>-a<N>`), when a feature starts that the forecast would have held. The mode comes from the initiative's base policy.
- `initiative-dollar-share`: on the initiative, once per feature (`share-<feature>`), when it starts short of dollars (counted to the tolerance ceiling until 3 features are measured, #2099). The mode comes from the initiative's base policy.
- `initiative-dollar-cap`: on the initiative, once per feature (`cap-<feature>`), when a feature starts whose priced low estimate is above the dollars left: what remains under the cap (to the tolerance ceiling until 3 features are measured, from the first measured feature on) with each open run at its dollar plan charge instead of its cap whatever the `initiative-plan-charge` mode, so an open run that still needs more than its cap holds that need and one that needs less frees the rest. Unlike `initiative-dollar-share`, the other waiting features' lows are not reserved. The event carries `parkReason: "dollar_cap"`, an `extend` remedy whose `suggestedUsd` is the larger of the cap that starts the feature at its low and the initiative's cost forecast (the expected remaining work), and its inputs (`leftUsd`, `lowUsd`, `openNeedUsd`, `remainingUsd`, `remainingTo`, `spentUsd`, `forecastUsd`, `measuredFeatures`). Bench round 70 (evalpin) started report-cli with $4.82 under the $30 cap against a $5.17 low while suite-runner still needed its review: report-cli spent $4.38 on planning and suite-runner stopped about $0.50 short. Each replayed start of the forecast replay corpus carries it as `capWouldStop` beside the final spend. Its promotion pauses the initiative instead of starting the feature.
- `initiative-row-owner`: on the initiative, once per proposed plan revision and row file (`row-owner-r<revision>-<path>`), when the decomposer's split (after the host's merges) is proposed: the file, the feature that owns it, the later feature that needs a row, the first such row, the literal it holds and how many rows do. The mode comes from the initiative's base policy.
- `initiative-plan-charge`: on the initiative, as a `decision.would_release` event instead of a would-stop: once per open run and plan sizing (`run-<run>-<plan high>`) with the tokens holding that run at its plan's need frees (`releasedTokens`, negative when the plan prices above the run's limit) and, with a dollar budget and a measured task-call rate, the dollars (`releasedUsd`, #2124), and once per feature when the charge would turn a share-gate verdict: a parked feature that would start (`start-<feature>`, `wouldStart`) or a starting one that would park (`park-<feature>`, `wouldPark`). The mode comes from the initiative's base policy; `enforce`, the default since #2245, records nothing and charges the pool for real, so only `stopModes: { "initiative-plan-charge": "shadow" }` (or `advisory`) records these events. A feature the charge parks waits with the share gate's own block and its `extend` remedy (#2042).
- `initiative-pool-growth`: on the feature run, once per run and set of bounds, when the initiative refreshes the run (before each drive) and one of its growth bounds (`sizing.maxRunTokens`, `sizing.extendToTokens`, `sizing.maxRunCostUsd`, or the `hardCeiling` of its latest `auto-extend` revision) is above what the initiative holds for it if every other run stopped now: the pool less what the other runs used, the dollar ceiling (the cap with its tolerance) less what they spent (`poolBoundDecision`). The record carries the bounds above them and its inputs (`limitTokens`, `othersUsedTokens`, `ceilingUsd`, `othersSpentUsd`, `holdTokens`, `holdUsd`). `replayPoolBounds` decides it again on a trace from each run's policy revisions against its start gate. In the corpus it records three runs: R73 flatelens' commands (#2280's case: its charge raised its 4.65M share to 8M and 12M and $3.82 to $19.30 with 5.66M tokens and $10.66 left), and two last features whose null token room took the base policy's ceilings, R71 xmlvet's cli-release (12M with 11.71M of the extended pool left, used 3.47M) and R73 evalpin's delivery (8M and 12M with 3.31M left, used 0.81M). Those three bounds are what the rounds recorded; with `chargeHold` (#2280) and a token room that is never null (#2294) the current code keeps each at what the pool left, so the records replay from the recorded revisions and stay listed in `accepted.json`. The `auto-extend` hard ceilings it recorded were true: R75 latcell's commands extended to hard ceilings of 19.47M and 22.32M with 14.78M held for it and used 14.82M, and R79 deltaglue's chunks extended to 12.0M with 10.14M held for it (hard ceiling 28.89M, from its $25.51 dollar bound) and took the initiative to 12.68M of its 12.0M pool. Since #2468 the run's own token growth stays under that hold, enforced, because the pool is a limit the person set: `growBudgetOnProgress` (`auto-growth`, `planning-growth`, `auto-extend`) and the planning floor read it through `WorkflowStore.tokenHold` (`InitiativeService.tokenHold`: the pool less what the decompose run and the other feature runs used, held and have in flight) and record it as `poolHoldTokens` on the revision; an `auto-extend`'s `hardCeiling` is the held one. A run of no initiative has no hold. An operator's `workflow extend` of a feature run is the person's own decision on that run and is not held. Its promotion holds the run at what the pool and the ceiling leave on the bounds it still records.
- `initiative-time-share`: on the initiative, once per feature (`time-<feature>`), when a feature starts whose low token estimate at the initiative's measured pace (`budget.time.pace`) is longer than the shared active time left after the other waiting features' lows at the same pace (`timeShortfall`). The share gate itself only refuses a time share below one second. The record carries the extend remedy with `suggestedTimeMs`: the time that starts the feature beside those lows, in 15-minute steps, at least the time notice's suggestion. R76 schemamask started three features on 2 to 4 minutes against lows of 9 to 30 and each waited 16 to 26 minutes for a person. The mode comes from the initiative's base policy.
- `initiative-plan-share`: on the initiative, once per run and ceiling (`plan-share-<run>-<ceiling>`), at the plan sizing of a started feature run that prices its own plan above its `sizing.maxRunTokens` (`split-feature`) when the plan-time share gate (`planShareDecision`, see [Extension inside the dollar cap](#initiatives)) does not grow the pool: under `extendWithinDollars: off`, or when the dollar cap cannot pay. The record carries the run, the plan's need (`needTokens`), the gate's `inputs` and the `extend` remedy with `suggestedTokens` (and `suggestedUsd` when the cap must grow too, `affordableTokens`); before the first feature is measured it records `hold: "no measurement"` (under `on` and `shadow`), and under `on` its summary ends with the #2471 text. A held run is asked again once per accepted feature, at the drive round after the acceptance and at each `feature.released` (`reaskPlanShare`, #2523), until a `plan-share` extension answers it: with the measured dollar forecast `on` grows the pool and the ceiling and `shadow` records its would-extend. R82 tinyflate's deflate held at 22:21 before core was accepted and was never asked again, and its would-stop was false. R80 deltaglue's cli sized 17.46M against its 3.38M start share and stopped `budget_exhausted` with 17 of 24 tasks left. The mode comes from the initiative's base policy.
- `repeated_failure`: once per corrective chain (`chain-<first task>`), at the first corrective whose failing checks equal the ones of the corrective before it; and once per task (`task-<id>`) at the first unfinished end whose failing checks equal the ones of the task's previous unfinished end.
- `corrective_scope`: once when the corrective is created (`<task>-create-0`) and once per session it opens (`<task>-start-<N>`), so a resume of a corrective that cannot write its file is recorded before it is paid.
- `corrective_unowned_file`: once when the corrective is created (`<task>-create-0`), once per session it opens (`<task>-start-<N>`) and once per blocked or failed result (`<task>-result-<N>`), with the path, its owner and the decision.
- `upstream_stale_file`: with the same keys as `corrective_unowned_file`, whenever the scope record's decision is `amend`, with the record and each path's `upstream` inputs (`closed`, `testFile`, `sole`); the enforced answer stays the park.
- `repeat_without_change`: once per corrective that changed no file (`task-<task>`), at the first finding that repeats its finding.
- `corrective_restores_removed`: once per corrective and file (`task-<task>-<path>`), with the restored lines, both correctives and the findings each corrected; the decision is also kept in `chains/restores/<task>.json` (kind `finding-chain`) for the replay (`replayRestoredRemovals`).
- `generated_case`: once per task (`task-<task>`), at its task-end case walk, with the path, line and kind of each generated value (`GENERATED_VALUE_SHAPES` over `maskNonCode` text, at most 20) in the test files that hold one of its cases, when `bannedGeneratedKinds` reads a ban in the request (`bansPropertyTests`, "no generated inputs", "no loops", "no interpolation") or a literal-example wording in the task's criteria; the walk record keeps `generated` and `generatedStop` and `replayCaseWalks` replays it. R74 callmend's specs built `"\\" * 3`, `pack`, interpolation and `each` inputs where the request asked for literal examples, and only reviews found them, one file per round (4.25M tokens). Enforced since #2469 (R77 and R79 records true; R79 deltaglue's corrective after review cost 8.28M tokens): the walk returns `generatedCaseRefusal` to the agent at submission, once for the same list, and records no would-stop; `stopModes: { generated_case: "shadow" }` keeps the record only. An interpolation or format built only from a path made at run time (a temporary folder or file, or a path joined from one, by the language's `RUNTIME_PATH_SOURCES` row) is not a generated value: no literal can stand there, and R78 deltaglue cli T008's record (expected stderr interpolating `mkdtemp` paths in an extra real-process test, every literal row held) was false.
- `written_tests_unselected`: once per check and command (`<check>-<hash of its arguments>`), with the check, its command and the declared classes; the trace replay decides it again from each recorded `checks/<check>-written/` artifact (`replayUnselectedTestRuns` in [trace](../../src/core/workflows/trace.ts)).
- `edge_crash_finding`: once per crashed case, keyed by its record's path and the case's number (`probes/edge/<task>.json#<N>`, `chains/battery/<id>.json#<N>`, `chains/family/<task>.json#<N>`), and once per such reviewer finding, keyed by its gate record's path and the finding's ID (`review-gate/<reviewer>-<id>.json#<finding>`, `convergence-gate/<id>.json#<finding>`).
- `repro_blind`: once per blind finding of a repro check, keyed by the check's record path and the finding's ID (`tasks/<task>/repro-check-<id>.json#<finding>`).
- `baseline_unrepairable`: once per check (`check-<check>`), at the baseline before the approval, or after a host repair that left the check failing, with the decision's record (and the repair's); once more per check (`release-check-<check>`) when a planned-ahead run is released onto a base where the check fails (#2228).
- `upstream_baseline_debt`: once per check and base (`start-<check>` at the baseline before the approval, `release-<check>` at a planned-ahead run's release), with the check, the base commit, the baseline failure decision and each upstream feature with the failing files its boundary owns (files the run's own boundary or plan also lists are left out). Not recorded while a planned-ahead run is held on its provisional base, whose upstream files are stubs. Its promotion asks the owner for an amendment (`amendmentAnswer`) instead of running tasks on a base that cannot pass.
- `baseline_signature`: once per check and path (`checks:check-<check>` for the checks stage's `baseline_failure` stop, `task:check-<check>` for the task-end excuse), with the comparison's record: both signatures before and after, by line, and the decision.
- `ci_check_drift`: once per changed file and command (`<file>#<command>`), at the checks stage, with the file, the command, its tool (through the package, Composer or Deno script it runs, which `script` names), the closest check that runs the tool (or none) and the words it lacks. A README drift says the documented command can fail; a workflow drift, the delivered CI.
- `host_content_conflict`: once per file set (`files-<paths>`), when the corrective is created, with the corrective, its files, the findings' first lines, the failed fixer runs on those files (`fixers/failed.json`) and the task's supplied-check record. Its promotion would block the run at once with the conflict between the request's content and the project's check, instead of opening host correctives until `maxTaskRepairs` ends the chain as `no_progress` (R78 pairwick cli).
- `fixer_unusable`: once per check (`check-<check>`), before the first task, with the probe's record: the file, the command line as run, the exit code or timeout, whether the file changed and the output's last 2,000 characters. Its promotion would stop the run there with that output, instead of letting implementers call a fixer that cannot run (R77 pairwick, #2436). A block's diagnostic also lists the fixer runs that failed without changing their files (`fixers/failed.json`), with each one's last output line.
- `worktree_environment`: once per language (`language-<language>`), before the first task, with the worktree path, its length, the limit, the tool and the platform. Its promotion would stop the run there and name the remedies (a shorter project path, or an inline run), instead of paying corrective sessions that cannot shorten a path (R78 latcell-update and latcellu: 2.1M tokens, #2454).
- `supplied_drift`: once per task and file (`<task>#<file>`), at the checks stage (or, with `source: "plan"`, before the host writes a host-written task's plan content, #2472), with the block kind (`file`, `section`, `diff` and its hunk), the problem (`differs`, `missing`, `moved`), the line the block gives with its text, and the file's closest line.
- `row_block_drift`: once per file and row (`<file>#row-<n>`), at the checks stage, with the file, the tasks that write it, the row's number and text as the request gives it, and each literal of the row the file lacks (its number in the row and its text), the first token the file row in its place lacks or holds out of order (#2521: the file line, the token's number of how many, its text, and, when it differs only by escape layers, both layer counts, #2545), and each long byte run of the row the file counts differently (#2515: the file line, the bytes it is off, the byte and both counts); and once per file row beyond the block (`<file>#added-line-<n>`, `<file>#duplicated-line-<n>`, #2503), with the file, the tasks, the kind, the line and its text, the prescribed row a duplicate repeats and how many rows the block prescribes. The comparison's counts are kept in `row-blocks/<file>.json` for the replay.
- `case_not_prescribed`: once per criterion at analysis (`analysis-<criterion>`, with the note's text) and once per task, criterion and literal at the task-end walk (`task-<task>-<criterion>-<literal>`), with the task, the criterion and the literal. A row count, a test file's name, a placeholder envelope or a described character is not recorded (#2524). The walk does not refuse the task for it; its promotion would make the analysis note blocking, so the spec author removes the example row from the criterion.
- `committed_tree`: once per commit and check (`<commit>#<check>`), after the accepted commit of a worktree run, with the commit, the check and the end of its output on the commit.
- `test_cap`: once per task and file over its cap (`test-cap/tasks/<task>.json#<path>`, with `#<class index>` for a per-class cap), at each task-end check, with the task, the path, the scope, the count and the cap.
- `silent_check_failure`: a `decision.would_rerun` event (same fields), once per failed run of a check (`<check>-<evidence id>`), at the checks stage, a v2 check stage or the reported-defect route (`stage`: `checks`, `check-stage`, `reported-defect`), with the check, its evidence and the last 600 characters of its output.
- `attempt_green_checkpoint`: a `decision.would_finish` event (same fields), once per attempt (`attempt-<attempt id>`), at its unfinished end, with the task, the attempt, `stoppedBy`, the checkpoint's `position`, `journalEnd`, the checks and their `evidenceIds`, the `repro` result at the checkpoint, `failedAfter` and `tokensAfter`.
- `host-resume-on-stored-limit`: a `decision.would_resume` event (same fields), once per block (`stop-<diagnostic id>`), when the drive has just blocked or an initiative's driver round looks at a blocked feature run (`at`: `drive` or `initiative-round`), with the limits it held (`held`: `active`, `tokens`, `calls`), the limits at the block (`atBlock`), the stored limits and the usage now, the tokens the refused call asked for and the time of the block (`blockedAt`). The block's inputs are kept as `limit-blocks/<diagnostic id>.json`.
- `host-settle-interrupted-check`: a `decision.would_resume` event, once per effect (`effect-<effect id>`), when the drive's recovery, a resume after the host died or acceptance opens the effect's recovery request (`at`: `drive`, `resume` or `acceptance`), with the effect, the check, its definition hash and root, the recorded pid, `processGone` and `cause: "host stopped"`.
- `cost_refusal_wait`: once per stop (`stop-<diagnostic id>`), when the run blocks, with the state it would take (`state: "waiting"`, `instead: "blocked"`), the stage it stopped in, the stages still to run, the remedy's `suggestedUsd` and the run's spent, requested and ceiling dollars.
- `upstream_symbol`: once per release of a planned-ahead feature (`release-<base commit>`), with each gap: the requirement or criterion, the symbol (`Reader.IsEncodingToken`, or a helper named only by its type, "Reader's public helper"), the upstream feature and its file. A member is read by the spellings of the upstream files' languages (`MEMBER_SYNTAX` in [upstream-symbols.ts](../../src/core/workflows/initiatives/upstream-symbols.ts), #2199): `Type.Member` in every language, `Type::member` in Rust, C++, PHP, R and Ruby (and Java and Kotlin method references), `Type#method` in Ruby, Scala and JavaScript docs, a Go package's `lines.Line`, a Python, Lua or Zig module function called or quoted (`reader.read(...)`, `` `reader.read` ``), Lua's `reader:read(...)`, Objective-C's `[Reader read]`, PowerShell's `[Reader]::Read` and R6's `Reader$read`; C, shell and SQL have no member syntax, and a file name such as `config.yaml`, a binary or package (`Maskback.dll`, `.so`, `.jar`, `.nupkg`, `.whl`) or a project file (`.csproj`, `.fsproj`, `.sln`, `.slnx`, `.props`) is no member. A type is matched to an upstream boundary file by its stem or its folder in any letter case (`Reader` to `Reader.cs`, `reader.go`, `reader/mod.rs`, a Go package `lines/parse.go`), and a member is looked up as a word in that file at the release base. A folder name is compared whole: `tests/Maskback.Tests/` is no folder `Maskback`, nor `maskback.egg-info/` a package `maskback`. A dotted name is a namespace, not a member, when the released feature's own boundary has it as folders (`Maskback.Core` for `src/Maskback/Core/Scanner.cs`, a Java or Kotlin package it writes) or when its head is the stem of a project file (`Maskback.csproj`, the root namespace) and of no other upstream file (#2365, R75 maskback: 3 false would-stops for `Maskback.Core`, `Maskback.Fake`, `Maskback.Cmd` and `Maskback.dll`). The upstream files are the boundary paths of the feature's dependencies that no other feature lists: a stub of a skeleton owner that the released feature, a feature planned after it or a sibling fills is that feature's code, not upstream code, and a path several dependencies list belongs to the one planned last (#2192, R69 ewvet: 6 false would-stops on the base's stub files).

The event carries the reason, the mode, the key, a summary in the words of the stop or decline that enforce would have made, and its inputs (the forecast, or the problems and limits).

Where the records show:
- `baked workflow status` and the run view's `wouldStop` list the records per reason: `reason`, `label`, `mode`, `count`, and `last` (`at` and `summary`).
- The TUI activity feed shows an advisory record as `Would stop (advisory): ...`, and so does the desktop run screen, which also lists the count per reason under **Would stop** in the run's facts.
- Shadow records stay out of the feeds.
- The events reach the `--events` stream and `/v1/workflow/runs/:id/events` like every run event.
- An initiative's records are `decision.would_stop` events of the initiative (`initiative events`, `/v1/initiatives/ID/events`), with the feature that started. `show` gives the mode as `budget.cost.forecastMode`; in advisory mode `budget.cost.forecastNotice` says the forecast is above the ceiling and the TUI and desktop cost lines mark the forecast `(advisory)`.

A new or changed reason ships in `shadow` and moves to `enforce` in a pull request of its own after two bench rounds with no false would-stop (see AGENTS.md).


A corrective whose required checks fail is recorded as `repeats/tasks/<task>.json` (#1996): for each failing check its id, its output normalized (terminal colors, line endings, path separators, timestamps, durations, rates, memory sizes, random seeds and run IDs, temporary directories and memory addresses taken out; file, line and column positions kept) and the files the output names failing, with a hash over all three. When the hash equals the one of the corrective it corrects, `repeated_failure` records a `decision.would_stop` once per chain, with both round ids (`inputs.rounds`), the checks, the files and `firstDifference: null`; a failure that comes back only after a different one in between is not a repeat. Durations are recognized by their shape, so every supported test runner's summary repeats (#2281): a decimal number with a unit (`0.015 seconds`, `0.01 sec`), a compound duration (`1m 3s`, `1 minute 2.5 seconds`), a whole number after a word that introduces a time (Gradle's `BUILD FAILED in 8s`, dotnet's `Duration: 12 ms`, sbt's `Total time: 3 s`), a clock (PHPUnit's `Time: 00:00.012`, Dart's `00:01 +3 -1:`, Maven's `01:05 min`), a rate (Minitest's `810.4 runs/s`), a seed (`--seed 12345`, `Randomized with seed 52731`) and a Lua address printed without `0x` (`table: 00000184D28BE670` on Windows); a whole number the test asserts (`expected 5s`) is kept. In bench round 64, cfgquill lint T005, T006 and T007 failed gofmt with the same `illegal byte order mark` at the same position, and only `maxTaskRepairs` ended the chain, after 4.8M tokens. The reason is not enforced yet: the correction still starts, and a later pull request promotes it to block the chain with the check, the position and the rounds before another model session.

A task that ends unfinished ("Task T001 did not complete its implementation", the run blocked `invalid_output`) opens no corrective, so the chain comparison never sees it (#2437). Each such end records the failing checks of its last attempt the same way, at the same path, with `end: "unfinished"` and `ends` (how many unfinished ends in a row failed alike); an end without a failing check records none and starts the count again. When an unfinished end's hash equals the one of the task's previous unfinished end (in the same drive or after a resume), `repeated_failure` records a `decision.would_stop` once per task (key `task-<id>`) with the checks, the files and the count, and the block message says "Task T001 ended unfinished N times in a row with the same failing output of CHECK". Such a block offers no `resume`, which would run the task into the same failure: its remedies are `reroute`, `revise`, `replan` and `cancel`, and the message names the input that must change (the stage's model, the plan or tests behind the check, or the initiative feature's plan). The record keeps the remedies, and the replay decides them again from `ends` (bench round 82 tinyflate deflate T007 was offered only `resume`, which spent 684k tokens on the same ctest failure, #2520). sbt 2's `elapsed time: 4 s, cache 50%, 106 disk cache hits` line is normalized as a whole. In bench round 77 pairwick's implement tasks failed one `scalafmt` check 121 times across 143 attempts and their resumes, for 17.13M tokens, with no record. `replayRepeatedFailures` decides each unfinished end against the end before it (root `task-<id>`).

A task-level corrective of a failed required check that is not a test check (a linter, formatter, type checker or build) writes the files that check names in the failing task's own files, also when a later planned slice of the same file is still to run (#2094); the slice depends on the failing task and reads the corrected file. The finding's suggested fix says which task runs after the correction and adds the rest of the file, and asks for no more than the failure needs there. A diagnostics-only corrective (a static check whose output names only the failing task's files) writes only the failing task's own files and none of its related tasks' sources, so the hold of task-level repairs (#1612) keeps back the later slice and leaves independent test tasks free to run. A failing test check keeps #1612: its corrective leaves a shared test file to the later slice and fixes the source the tests reach. In bench round 67, skillcomb T010 left seven ruff ISC004 hits in slice 1 of `tests/test_fields.py`; its corrective could not write the file, spent 1.89M tokens on three source files, held eight test tasks and blocked `out_of_scope_failure`. When a corrective is created and before each of its sessions the host records `scope/tasks/<task>.json` (kind `corrective-scope`, kept in bench traces): the failing tasks, the checks, the diagnosed files, the corrective's writes, the diagnosed files outside them and the decision `fits` or `refuse`. On `refuse`, `corrective_scope` records a `decision.would_stop`; the corrective still runs until a later pull request promotes the reason to refuse it at 0 tokens. A corrective is never deferred on a planned task that depends on a task it corrects (a later slice the corrective holds): `failureOwner` and the missing-artifact wait leave such tasks out, so the two cannot wait for each other. An `out_of_scope_failure` block names its remedy: fix the files and resume with `--accept-workspace`, or revise the plan; a plain resume would start the same correction again.

The hold of an open task-level corrective covers the files its findings point at, not its whole write scope (#2109). A test-check corrective may write the union of its related tasks' writes, and its hold kept every planned task depending on any of them waiting while the corrective was open: in skillcomb (bench round 67) eight independent test tasks never started while two of three writers were idle. `readyTasks` and `failureOwner` now read the corrective's narrowed writes, the files its first attempt may write once it started (`narrowedCorrections`), and before that the files its findings name with their tests (`correctionFocus`, `narrowCorrectionWrites`). A corrective whose narrowed attempt failed and that widened (`widenedCorrections`) holds its whole write scope again, and so does a corrective whose findings name no file.

A corrective that blocks (its row `blocked`, its stop the run's own: `out_of_scope_failure`, an unfinished correction, an unowned file it may not write) used to end the run at once, with ready tasks it does not hold never run; in skillcomb the resume after a person fixed the files still had eight test tasks to run (#2109). Each such block records `blocks/correctives/TASK_ID-N.json` (kind `corrective-defer`, kept in bench traces, no prose): the stop's code and reason, the ready tasks neither it nor a corrective blocked before it holds, those earlier correctives and the run's writers, and the decision `correctiveDeferDecision` in `src/core/workflows/corrective-defer.ts` takes (run them first when there are any). With the policy field `correctiveDefer: "on"` (setting `quality.correctiveDefer`, default `off`; absent means off, so stored policies keep their hash) the drive parks the corrective, on the single-writer and the batch path: it keeps its hold, so the tasks that read the files it would have fixed still wait, the independent ready tasks run, and once nothing else can run the run ends with the first parked corrective's stop and its remedies, as before. With the field off the record is shadow and the run ends at the block. `replayCorrectiveDefers` decides each record again (`newDeferDecisions`).

A corrective's evidence can name a file it may not write in other ways than a failed check (#2133): a review, edge-probe, sibling-probe or battery finding through its file and the stack frames of its repro's output, and every attempt through its unresolved items (a worker reports `blocked` with `unresolved: ["src/storage.ts: change ..."]`). In bench round 68 url-shortener T008 (1.09M tokens in 6 attempts) and ewvet scanner T003 (4.55M tokens after the finding) ended so: the host restarted the same session, threw `OUTPUT_INVALID` "did not complete its implementation", and every resume repeated it. The scope record (`scope/tasks/<task>.json`, written at creation, before each session and on each blocked or failed result) now also holds the origins, the named files (`evidencePaths` resolves a relative path, a frame under the run root, a frame from another machine that ends in a planned path, or a bare name one planned or boundary path has) and, for each named file outside the corrective's writes, its inputs and decision (`unownedRule`): `wait` when an unfinished task writes it (the existing wait for a missing artifact), `route` when a settled task of the run wrote it, `park` when another initiative feature's boundary holds it, `widen` when auto-approval's `allowedPaths` or the feature's own boundary hold it and no task writes it, `refuse` otherwise; `hostDecision` takes one act in that order. By default (`corrective_unowned_file` in `shadow`) the decision is a would-stop and the run goes on as before. With `"stopModes": { "corrective_unowned_file": "enforce" }` the host acts before any restart, on the single-writer and the batch-worker path alike, once per path (`scope/edits/<task>.json`):
- `route`: a correctness finding on the file, with the worker's unresolved item as its claim and the owner's criteria, opens a task-level correction of the owner (as `routeReportedDefect` does, #839); the corrective waits for it (`tasks/waits/<task>.json`) and runs again after it, and independent tasks go on.
- `widen`: the corrective's writes gain the path (one per finding) through a `host-widen` link of the correction chain, which acceptance checks (the frozen policy enforces the reason, no planned task writes the path, the approved paths hold it); the next session may write it.
- `park`: the run waits with a `manual_evidence` request (`upstream-<task>`) that names the path and the feature that owns it, and the corrective runs again once the person answers.
- `amend` (#2485): taken before `park` when the evidence names only one file outside the corrective's writes, that file is a test file (`isTestFileAt`) of another initiative feature, and that feature's current run is completed, so no run of it is open to change the file. The scope record keeps these inputs as `upstream: { closed, testFile, sole }` on the path. In bench round 80 ratdec (Elixir) representations' feature-scope `mix-test` failed seven times on one row of the accepted core's `test/stdin_test.exs`, which pinned `unsupported format` for the output representations implements; its corrective parked on `upstream-T007`, which only a hand edit of the run branch could answer, and the dependent `docs` feature never started. With `upstream_stale_file` in `shadow` (the default) the decision is a would-stop and the host parks as above. With `"stopModes": { "corrective_unowned_file": "enforce", "upstream_stale_file": "enforce" }` (or a person's `upstream` remedy with `upstream_stale_file` enforced) the host adds the file to this feature's boundary in `initiativeBounds` and to the corrective's writes through a `host-widen` link (`hostAmend`), journals it in `scope/edits/<task>.json` with its hash before the edit, and the corrective's next session updates the stale test. A source file of the upstream, a feature whose run is still open, or evidence that names more files keeps the park.
- `refuse`: the corrective blocks at once (`out_of_scope_failure`, remedies `--accept-workspace`, revise, cancel) instead of a restart, and a plain resume opens no session while the named file is unchanged since that result.

A task-level corrective of a failed test check may write the failing test file too while a later slice of it is still to run, when the failing task created the file, no task other than its later slices writes it, and the check's output locates no source (#2108): every case in the file is the failing task's own, so the defect can be in the test (a wrong expectation, a broken helper, a syntax error the runner reports). `applyTaskCheck` records the file in `slicedTests`, `diagnosedFiles` returns it for the finding, so `appendCorrections` keeps it in the corrective's writes and the `scope/tasks/` precondition requires it, and the suggested fix says which slice runs after the correction and adds the rest of the file. The sources the tests import stay in scope. A failure located in a source (a stack frame, a source path in the output) keeps #1612: the corrective fixes the source and leaves the shared file to the later slice. skillcomb T023 (bench round 67, `tests/test_body.py`) escaped only because its defect was in source.

In shadow (the default) a corrective that blocks on such a file still stops the run, and its block offers the host's act as a person's remedy (#2155): its last `result` record's `route`, `widen` or `park` decision becomes the remedy `route` (correct the owner first), `widen` (let the corrective write the path) or `upstream` (ask the owning initiative feature for the change), next to a plain retry, a revision and a cancel. `baked workflow resume RUN_ID --remedy route|widen|upstream` (T, W or Q on the run in the terminal, the matching button on the desktop run screen, `remedy` on `POST /v1/workflow/runs/RUN_ID/resume`) records `decisions/unowned-TASK-COMMAND.json` and resumes; the corrective's next session applies that one decision exactly as the enforced host would, its `scope/edits/` entry marked `approvedBy: "user"`, and a widened path is recorded in the run state so acceptance allows its `host-widen` link. A remedy the block does not offer is refused, and each remedy applies once.

#### CI commands against the checks

The checks of a run come only from the policy's `checks`, so a run can write a CI workflow whose commands no check ran (#2284). In bench round 73, evalpin's delivery feature wrote `.github/workflows/ci.yml` and its README with `cargo clippy --offline --all-targets -- -D warnings`, while the run's `clippy` check ran `cargo clippy -- -D warnings`: every feature was accepted, and the delivered CI would have failed on all three operating systems on a `clippy::type_complexity` warning in a test file.

At the checks stage (the schema-1 checks and the command-gate stage of v2 and quick runs alike), after the snapshot, the host reads every CI workflow (`.github/workflows/*.yml`) and root README the run changed since its baseline. It takes the single-line `run:` steps of a workflow (`workflowRunCommands`, the reader the gate detector uses) and the lines of a README's shell blocks (`sh`, `bash`, `shell`, `console`, `powershell` or no language, a leading `$ ` taken off), and compares each with the checks about to run (`ciCheckDrift` in `src/core/workflows/ci-check-drift.ts`):
- A command's tool is its program with its subcommand (`cargo clippy`, `go test`, `npm run lint`), read through wrappers (`npx`, `uv run`, `poetry run`, `bundle exec`, `python -m`), script paths (`./vendor/bin/phpstan`, `./gradlew`) and `.exe`/`.cmd` suffixes.
- When a check runs the same tool, every word of the command (a flag, a value, a scope such as `--all-targets`, `-race`, `./...`, `--strict`, `--max-warnings 0`) must be among the check's words, in any order. Words that change only output, network or lockfile use (`--offline`, `--locked`, `--quiet`, `--color`, `--message-format`) and a lone `.` do not count, and words the check adds are fine, since the check is then the stricter one. Otherwise the would-stop names the closest such check and the missing words.
- A command of a gate kind (a linter, type checker, formatter check or test runner, as the gate detector's `ciCommandKind` reads it) whose tool no check runs records a would-stop with no check. Setup steps and commands that need a shell are not read.
- Both sides are read through the scripts they run (#2416). In bench round 76, url-shortener's README said `bun run test`, its `package.json` had `"test": "bun test"` and the run's check was `bun test`: the same command, recorded as a gate no check ran. A package-manager script call (`npm run lint`, `npm test`, `pnpm run`/`yarn run`/`bun run <script>`, `pnpm <script>` and `yarn <script>` for a script the table names, `composer run-script`/`composer run`/`composer <script>`, `deno task`) becomes the script's own command line from `package.json` `scripts`, `composer.json` `scripts` (a one-entry list and an `@other` reference followed) or `deno.json` `tasks`, with the words after the script name (`npm run lint -- --max-warnings 0`) added to it; a file's commands use the workspace root's manifests, a check uses those of its `cwd`. The drift then names the resolved tool and the script (`"npm run lint" (script: "eslint . --max-warnings 0")`). `bun test` is Bun's own test runner, never the `test` script.
- A command the host cannot resolve records nothing unless a check runs the same command: a script the table does not name, a manifest that is missing or not JSON, a script of several commands (`tsc && vitest`), a Composer `@php` line or PHP callback, and the targets of `make`, `just`, `rake` and `task`, whose recipes are not command lines the host reads. A check that is such a command may run anything, so while one is configured a gate command no check runs records nothing either; a check that runs the same tool is still compared word by word.

The `ci_check_drift` reason records each as a `decision.would_stop` (shadow): nothing runs the CI command and the run goes on. Its promotion would make the drift a finding of the task that wrote the file. Running the CI command on the host in a workspace copy is not done yet.

#### Delivered files against the request's verbatim blocks

A request can give a file's content verbatim (a whole file, a Markdown section, an exact diff), and a model that retypes it can change a literal no check reads (#2332). In bench round 74, ewvet-update's request gave the README change as an exact diff; the implementer wrote its `printf` example with a doubled backslash (`...=\\n'` where the diff has `...=\n'`), the reviewer rated the deviation low, and the run completed with AC-005 and AC-006 failing. A low finding that names a failing criterion on such a file no longer passes the notes rule (#2349); this is the host's own comparison.

At the checks stage (the schema-1 checks and the command-gate stage of v2 and quick runs alike), after the snapshot, the host reads the run's [host apply records](#plan-time-sizing) (`host-apply/tasks/<task>.json`) and compares each write whose content the request fixes with its block (`suppliedDrifts` in `src/core/workflows/supplied-drift.ts`):
- A whole file (one fenced block that says it is the whole file, or any block for a new file) is compared line by line.
- A Markdown section is compared with the one section of its heading in the delivered file; a missing section is a drift, and a heading several sections share is not compared.
- A diff that applied to the task's base is compared hunk by hunk: each hunk's new lines (context and added lines) must be consecutive lines of the file, in the hunks' order. A drift names the first added line the file lacks, its line number from the hunk header and the file's closest line, or says the lines are there but not in the hunk's order.
- Line ends (CRLF or LF) and trailing blank lines are not compared. A fenced block that may be an excerpt, a diff that did not apply, a deleted file and a path another planned task also writes are left out; a corrective of the task that writes the file keeps the comparison.

The `supplied_drift` reason records each as a `decision.would_stop` (shadow): the run goes on. Its promotion would make the drift a finding of severity high on the task's file, which a repair task rewrites from the request's text. Under `hostApply: "on"` a task the host applied matches its blocks by construction, unless a later corrective changes the file.

A host-written task (every write carries the plan's `content`, kind `content-written` in `host-edits.json`) has no host apply record, so the checks stage never compared it (#2472). In bench round 79, trailspin-update's brief gave `internal/cli/summary_top_test.go` as a new file's `-U0` diff; the planner retyped it into T003's `content` with one extra blank line, and the host wrote that copy with no session. Before the host writes a planned host-written task, it now reads the task's apply inputs (`hostApplyInputs`) and compares each write's plan content with its block the same way (`planContentDrifts` in `src/core/workflows/supplied-drift.ts`: a new file's diff, a diff that applies to the base, a whole file, a Markdown section). Each drift is recorded in `host-apply/plan-content/<task>.json` (the drifts and the paths written from the request) and:
- in shadow, as a `supplied_drift` would-stop with `source: "plan"` (key `<task>#<file>`), and the host writes the plan's copy;
- under `hostApply: "on"`, the host writes the request's bytes for the drifting files instead (`hostApplyContents`) and records no would-stop. If the request's blocks cannot be applied, the record keeps `applyError`, the plan's copy is written and the would-stop is recorded.

Plan content that keeps its block (line ends and trailing blank lines aside) is written as planned and records nothing; correctives are not compared.

#### Delivered test files against the request's prescribed rows

A request can prescribe a test file as rows, one fenced block whose first line names the file and says it holds rows (`StdinTest in test/stdin_test.exs (16 rows, ...)`), read by `suppliedRows` in `src/core/workflows/sizing.ts` for pricing and row slices (#2455, #2466). The case walk demands only the literals a criterion cites, so a literal of a row no criterion cites (an input event, a stdin text, an expected line) could be changed or dropped and the task was accepted (#2486). In bench round 80, deltaglue's T010 transcribed row 2 of `tests/problems-choices-b.test.ts` without the input's final `data: [DONE]\n\n` event and then changed the expected output to match its program, and ratdec's core T014 rewrote a stdin row to expect an error the brief never names; both rows were delivered changed.

At the checks stage (the schema-1 checks and the command-gate stage of v2 and quick runs alike), after the snapshot, the host compares each test file the plan writes and the request prescribes rows for with those rows (`rowBlockDrifts` in `src/core/workflows/row-block-drift.ts`):
- Every quoted literal of a row (double-quoted, or single-quoted outside a word) is decoded until no escape layer is left and looked up in the delivered file decoded the same way (`verbatimHeld` in `src/core/verification/criteria-cases.ts`), so the test may spell it in its own string syntax, split it into adjacent strings of one quote style, or write a multi-line text as a heredoc's indented lines. Every character counts: a shortened input is not held.
- An empty literal says nothing; a `<name>` placeholder stands for what the test chooses, and the parts around it are looked up.
- A literal is looked up in the whole file, not in the row's own test, so a literal another row also holds is not missed. The comparison runs at the checks stage, not at task end, because a file split into row slices is whole only after its last slice.
- The whole-file lookup alone holds a row that lost arguments another row holds, and never reads a status code (#2521). In bench round 82 slotprompt's core delivered rows 5 and 13 of `test/text_test.dart` without the prescribed `'--var', 'a=1'` arguments, `row-blocks/test/text_test.dart.json` recorded `drifting: []` for all 17 rows, and the reviewer's two correctives (T009, T010) and the rounds after them cost about 0.9M tokens. So each prescribed row is also compared with the file row that takes its place (`rowPlaces`: the first file row matched to it, in file order as below, else the changed row that takes its place) as a token sequence (`rowTokens`): its quoted literals (and heredocs, dedented) and its bare values (numbers, a type suffix such as `0L` or `0i32` dropped, `true`, `false`, `nil`, `null`, `None`, `NULL`); names, keys and punctuation are not tokens, so the file may spell the row in its own notation (`assert_row(...)`, `{ args: ..., code: 0 }`, `{[]string{...}, ...}`, a C struct row). Every token must be in the row's own lines: the file row with its continued strings and heredoc, and the lines after it up to a blank line or the next file row (at most 8, a bats `@test`'s assertions). When the file row itself holds every token (one row per line), they must also come in the row's order, adjacent strings joining to spell one literal. The first token that fails is the row's `differs` (with the file `line` and the `tokens` compared), and the row drifts: a dropped argument, a changed status code and a swapped pair, in Dart, C, Elixir, TypeScript, Python, Go and the other one-per-line spellings below; in a bats file a dropped argument and a changed status, not a swapped pair. A row no file row takes the place of keeps only the whole-file lookup, and a row with a long byte run is left to its count (below).
- In that per-row order a quoted literal the file row quotes with the same character as the row is compared by its value with one escape layer decoded, the language's own string decoding (#2545); only when the quoting differs (a JSON string inside single quotes, #1997) are both sides decoded until no escape layer is left, as the whole-file lookup and the case walk decode them. In bench round 82 slotprompt's `dart-test-written` failed on a transcribed row that spelled `\\n` (a backslash and `n`) where the row gives `\n` (a line break); with every layer decoded the two were one literal, the row was held and the failing check read as a source failure. Such a row now drifts at that token, and the row's measure and the would-stop's `differs` carry `layers` (how many escape layers the row's literal and the file's decode through, `{ row: 1, file: 2 }`), in Dart, C, Elixir, TypeScript, Python, Go and the other languages whose strings decode `\n`; a raw string (`r'...'`) is not read, and a bats file, whose single quotes decode nothing, compares no order.
- A long byte run (16 bytes or more of one value) is compared by its count, in whatever notation each side spells it (#2515, `runLengthCompare` in `src/core/verification/byte-runs.ts`, the one comparison the literal lookup, the case walk's `testsHold` and the misquote reading `misquotation` share). In bench round 82, tinyflate's deflate T007 wrote `tests/deflate_match_test.c` with 303 bytes of `0x78` on line 11 where the brief prescribes 300, and 238 bytes of `0x71` on line 12 where it prescribes 259; 300 bytes read verbatim are held inside 303, nothing was recorded, the failing check read as a source failure and every corrective rewrote a correct `src/deflate.c` until the run ended `invalid_output`. Both sides are read as (byte, count) runs: hex strings, escaped byte strings (`\xHH`, a `b` prefix), lists of `0xHH`, and repeats (`"78" * 300`, `"78".repeat(300)`, `vec![0x78; 300]`, `strings.Repeat`, `bytes.Repeat`, `String.duplicate`, `:binary.copy`, `Buffer.alloc(300, 0x78)`, `memset(p, 0x78, 300)`, the prose `0x78 × 300` and `300 bytes of 0x78`), for C, C++, Python, TypeScript, JavaScript, Rust, Go, Elixir, Java and the other languages that spell bytes these ways. A row whose run the file counts differently drifts, and short runs around it must match inside the span.
- When a required check of the same checks stage fails, each of its findings first says so plainly, with the file line and the byte (`rowRunFindings`): "the test's input literal differs from the criterion row by 3 bytes (tests/deflate_match_test.c line 11: 303 bytes of 0x78 where row 2 of the request's row block gives 300)". This only adds what the host knows and is on by default. With the policy field `rowRunRoute: "on"` (setting `quality.rowRunRoute`, default off) the check's correction goes to the task that writes the test file only, and the source findings are dropped; without it, the route it would take is recorded in `row-runs/<check>.json` (the drifting test files, their tasks, the bytes off, the findings' files before and after; no text) and the findings keep their files. A spec conflict whose test misquotes a run is read the same way, and the host's exact misquote edit (`misquoteFix`, #2047) writes the row's count in the test's own notation (a repeat's count, a hex string or a byte list written out again).

The file's rows are also read back against the block (#2503), because a row the request does not prescribe left every prescribed row held: in bench round 81 ratdec's exact-cli T011 added `row(["eval", "--as", "frac", "7/3"], "", 0, "7/3\n", "")` to `test/cli_test.exs` as a 14th row of 13, nothing was recorded, and the review's corrective cost 368,154 tokens to remove the line (`measureFileRows` and `rowMoveDecision`):
- A row of the file is a line whose head (its first token up to its first bracket, `row(`, `assert_row(`, `{`, `(`, `Arguments.of(`, or its first word, `run_row`) is the head of a line that matches a prescribed row with two literals or more, joined with the lines after it that continue its strings (a line that starts with a quote, `+`, `<>` or `..`). One row per line in any notation the literal reader reads: Elixir, TypeScript, JavaScript, Python, Go, Rust, Java, Kotlin, Scala, C#, C, C++, Ruby, PHP, Swift, Lua, Dart, Zig, OCaml, ReScript, Objective-C, R, Solidity, and a bats `@test` per row through its `run` line.
- A file row matches a prescribed row when the row holds every literal of it (a `<name>` placeholder of the row standing for any text) and either the file row holds every literal of the row or each of its literals is one of the row's (a heredoc whose lines follow).
- Placed in file order: a file row that matches no prescribed row is `added`, unless it takes the place of a prescribed row nothing matches (the one it shares the most literals with: a row changed in place, which the missing literals already name); a second file row for a prescribed row is `duplicated`; a file row for a row before one an earlier file row holds is `reordered`.
- Rows that are one string each (Java `@CsvSource`) or a quoting the literal reader does not decode (PowerShell's backtick escapes, SQL `E'...'` strings) match no prescribed row, so no file row is read and nothing is recorded for them.

Each compared file records `row-blocks/<file>.json` (per row: its literal count and which literals the file lacks, and the file line in its place with its token count and first differing token, #2521, with both escape layer counts when the token differs only by them, #2545; per file row: its line, its literal count, the prescribed rows it matches and the one it takes the place of; the added, duplicated and reordered lines; no text), and the `row_block_drift` reason records each drifting row, and each added or duplicated file row, as a `decision.would_stop` (shadow): the run goes on. A reordered row is only recorded: the order is not a request a repair restores. The replay places the recorded file rows again (`newRowBlockMoves`); records before #2503 keep none. Its promotion would make the row a finding of severity high on the file, which a repair task restores from the request's row. How the host answers a spec conflict whose test side is a prescribed-row file is not changed by this check (#2493), nor which request text a conflict's request quote is checked against (#2492).

#### Restoring a changed prescribed row

The host holds every row it compares, so a row a delivered file changed is not something a model session needs to copy again (#2522). In bench round 82 transcribing prescribed row files was 5.1M of the two initiatives' 18.1M tokens and correcting their copies another 3.0M: tinyflate deflate T007 (2.43M) never got the long hex runs of `tests/deflate_match_test.c` right (303 bytes of `78` for the row's 300, 238 of `71` for 259), and slotprompt core T009 and T010 put back arguments a Dart row had lost. At each checks stage, before the snapshot and the comparison above, every prescribed row of a compared file that drifts, or that no file row holds while one takes its place (a row changed in place), is a site, at the file line in its place (#2521: a row that lost arguments is restored in place too) (`rowRestores` in `src/core/workflows/row-restore.ts`). The host restores a site when (`rowRestoreDecision`):

- a file line takes the row's place, and that file row is the one line (no line after it continues its strings);
- the line starts with the row's own head (`{`, `row(`, `INFLATE_ROW(`, `Arguments.of(`), so the request spells the row as the file does; a file in another notation (`assert_row(` for the request's `row(`) or with rows spread over lines (a bats `@test` per row, a formatter's wrapped row) is recorded and left alone;
- with the line replaced by the row as the request gives it (the line's indentation, trailing `,` or `;` and line ending kept), the file holds the row and no other row drifts that did not before.

Every comparison with a site records `row-restores/<file>.json` (kind `row-restore`: per site the row, the file line and the three flags, the rows to restore, the mode and the verdict; no text), which bench traces keep and `replayRowRestores` decides again (`newRowRestores`). With the policy field `rowRestore` absent (setting `quality.rowRestore`, default `off`; absent means off, so stored policies keep their hash) the decision is shadow: the verdict is `would_restore`, the file stays as written and `row_block_drift` records its would-stop as before. With `rowRestore: "on"` the host writes the restored lines as a journaled edit before the checks run and runs the writing task's required checks on it (its own checks, the written-tests check and the formatter on the file); when one fails the file is put back as it was (`put_back`), otherwise it is kept (`restored`) and the stage's checks run on the restored rows, so no corrective opens for them. Restoring at a row-file task's end, before its own checks, is #2529; writing a row file the task only transcribes, without a session, is #2528.

#### Restoring a miscounted row on resume and before a task

Both checks above run at the checks stage, which a run blocked at a task never reaches (#2543). In bench round 82 tinyflate deflate 82f19908 blocked `invalid_output` at T007, whose check reads `tests/deflate_match_test.c` (303 bytes of `78` for the row's 300, 238 of `71` for 259) and whose session would not change the test's literals; resumed after #2515 and #2522 it blocked again within a minute with the same diagnostic. Setting `quality.rowRunRoute` or `quality.rowRestore` afterwards does not reach such a run: a run's policy is frozen at its start, and neither runs before the blocked task's session.

The host now compares the delivered test files' rows run-length aware outside the checks stage too (`rowRunRestores` in `src/core/workflows/row-restore.ts`, the restore above for the rows whose long byte run the file counts differently, its decision unchanged):
- On resume of a run blocked `invalid_output`, before the repeat guard, each delivered file with such a row records `row-restores/resume/<file>.json` (`at: "resume"`, the tasks blocked then under `blocked`, the file's hash; no text). The file is not written there. A new record is an input of the invalid-output repeat key (`invalidOutputInputs`, `rows`), so the retry is no repeat; the same file content records nothing new, so a resume after the host put an edit back is still refused as a repeat.
- Before the task-graph stage drives its tasks, the files of the resume records (`at: "drive"`), and at the start of each task's session the test files the task writes and the files a resume record names for it (`at: "task"`), are restored: the restored lines are written as a journaled edit, the writing task's required checks run on it, and the edit is put back when one fails (`put_back`). Recorded under `row-restores/<file>.json` and replayed by `replayRowRestores` like the checks stage's.
- A miscount that stays (declined, put back, or with the restore off) is named in the task's brief (`rowRuns`, the words of `rowRunFindings`: "the test's input literal differs from the criterion row by 3 bytes (...)"), and a correction's first attempt may write the test file when the task writes it.

The row's line need not be in the request's notation (#2551). In bench round 82 tinyflate's request gives its rows as `run "deflate" "--level" "fixed" "--hex" stdin="7878…" => 0 "…\n" ""` and the test spells them `failed += row(ARGS("deflate", …), "7878…", 0, "…\n", "");`, so the whole-line restore above (which needs the row's own head) declined both rows, the resume recorded nothing and the third resume blocked again. For a miscounted run the host rewrites only the run's spelling on the line with the row's count, in the line's own notation (`respelledRunLine`: a hex string or a byte list written out again, a repeat's count in `"x" * 300`, `"78".repeat(300)`, `memset(p, 0x78, 300)`, `vec![0x78; 300]`, `strings.Repeat` rewritten), and keeps the rest of the line; the site records `respelled` when the file then holds the row and no other row drifts that did not before, and `rowRestoreDecision` restores it. The drive's restore compares every prescribed-row file the graph writes, not only the ones a resume recorded, so the workers of a parallel run (`maxConcurrentWriters` above 1), whose sessions start in their own worktrees, start from the restored file, and a worker's brief names a miscount that stays (`rowRuns`).

This restore is on by default: it only rewrites a row whose byte run the test miscounts, to the request's exact text, and keeps it only when the checks pass, and an opt-in would not reach a blocked run. The policy field `rowRunRestore: "off"` (setting `quality.rowRunRestore`, default `on`; absent means on) records the decision in shadow (`would_restore`) and leaves the file as written; the brief still names the miscount. 

### Raising a budget limit

A run blocked with `budget_exhausted` shows B (raise limits) next to fork and cancel. The confirmation names the new values: every limit that is used up, or at least 80% used, doubles, and the tokens double when nothing else explains the stop; calls stop at 1000, active time at 24 hours and tokens at 50 million. Y applies it and the run continues from the stage that stopped.

```sh
baked workflow extend RUN_ID --expected-revision N [--tokens N] [--calls N] [--time-ms N] [--cost-usd N]
baked workflow resume RUN_ID --expected-revision NEW_N
# or both in one command, with the exit codes of resume:
baked workflow extend RUN_ID --expected-revision N --tokens N --resume [--events]
```

When the workspace already executes `workflows.maxParallelRuns` runs, the resume after `--resume` has no slot. The extension is applied all the same, so the command answers with the run (`paused`) and a `queued` object instead of the `WORKSPACE_BUSY` refusal, and exits 0: `reason: "parallel_cap"`, the `message` ("The extension is applied; the run is paused and continues when a slot is free ..."), `maxParallelRuns`, the `executing` runs, the `initiativeId` whose drive starts the run once a slot frees (initiative feature runs), and `next`, the `baked workflow resume` command for any other run, which no drive picks up. The message is printed on stderr too. `revise --resume`, `reroute --resume` and every decision that continues the run (`approve`, `answer`, `evidence`, `accept`, `repair`, `recover`, `continue`) and `resume` with `--remedy` or `--accept-workspace` answer the same way (a plain `resume` applied nothing else, so it is still refused with `WORKSPACE_BUSY`, exit 5), and the terminal app shows the message with "the change is kept and the run starts when one of the executing runs ends" (#2415).

Limits can only go up, and `--tokens`, `--calls` and `--time-ms` are absolute values: a lower value is refused with the limit's current value and the one asked for (`Limits can only be raised: tokens 3,493,710 now, you asked 3,000,000`). The change is stored as the immutable artifact `policy-revisions/COMMAND_ID.json` (previous and new limits, both policy hashes, usage, the stage) and the run moves to `paused`. Scope, tools, checks, the stage, the evidence and the approval digest are untouched, so no new approval of the plan is needed and nothing is re-run. The user's command is the approval of the new limits. When `autoApproval.limits.maxTotalTokens` covered the old token limit, it is raised with it, so a later scope approval is not sent to a person only because of the larger budget; a bound set below the old limit is kept. For the same reason `autoApproval.renewal` accepts the extended limits: they are the policy the operator set (see [Auto approval](#auto-approval)).

Attempt-cap reports name the cap reached, uncached tokens used and their effective cap, total tokens used and their effective all-token cap, and the available run-budget ceiling. An implementer attempt that uses its token cap or its steps without a result being accepted after changing files continues once, also when its last step went past the cap before a final submit could be forced, like an attempt that submitted at the cap: its task runs again on those edits with both caps raised by half, and the other tasks of its batch are integrated. Any other agent attempt that uses its own token cap (the task's sized cap or `maxAttemptTokens`) without submitting a result, including that continuation, blocks the run with `attempt_cap`, not `budget_exhausted`, with one exception: a task that already used its continuation and its restart and changed files gets one more attempt with the same caps when its failing checks moved since its latest earlier attempt that ran checks (fewer tests fail, a failing check passes, or the first failure is a different one), compared on the last result of each check in each attempt, its `request_check` calls included. The attempt is recorded in `taskProgressAttempts` of the run state and in `tasks/progress-attempts/TASK_ID.json` with both attempts' check states; without movement, or after that attempt, the run blocks as before. A property test task of a bench run stopped the run on its third attempt while it failed fewer tests than on its first (#1351). In every other case it blocks the run with `attempt_cap`: the run budget is not used up, so there is nothing to extend, and `workflow extend` refuses such a run with that explanation. Resume it (R in the terminal UI, Resume in the desktop app, `baked workflow resume`): the task goes back to pending and continues on the edits already in its workspace. The tasks that finished next to it in its batch stay implemented, so the resume does not run them again.

The step cap works the same way (#1478). In bench round 41 a test task used 20 of its 20 steps at 37% of its token cap. Its last edit broke the syntax gate, which refused the final `submit_result`, and the run stopped `invalid_output`. Now such a task continues on its edits with both caps raised by half. An attempt that used its steps without changing a file (only reads, checks or a repro) is repeated once with half again as many steps. This applies to stage agents too, and their commands no longer rule the retry out. A step-capped attempt that cannot continue blocks the run with `step_cap`, which `workflow extend` refuses like `attempt_cap`; resume it to continue the task on its edits.

### Soft token checkpoints

The budget preset `checkpoints` removes the hard token cap (the limit becomes 1,000,000,000) and sets `checkpointTokens`, by default half the configured token limit (400k with the default policy). Calls and time are doubled like the extended preset. A run never reserves tokens past the next multiple of the interval: when the next model step could pass it, the step does not start, the run moves to `waiting` with reason `checkpoint` and a durable request of kind `checkpoint` records the usage. The same guard applies to every step of one agent loop, so a runaway loop stops at the checkpoint and not after it.

C (or Enter) continues up to the next checkpoint, one interval beyond the projected spend; X stops the run in `paused`, and resuming asks again instead of spending. From the CLI:

```sh
baked workflow continue RUN_ID --request REQUEST_ID --expected-revision N
baked workflow resume RUN_ID --expected-revision NEW_N
```

Set `workflows.policy.checkpointTokens` (at least 10000, or `null`) to choose the interval of the preset. A call interrupted by a crash, whose usage is unknown, keeps its reservation and counts toward the next checkpoint.

### Estimates

All numbers are estimates and are labeled as such. The new-run form shows a range before the start, and the run screen shows a re-estimate, the burn rate and a forecast.

- The range is built from the runs already in this project: per stage the middle half of the samples (the full spread with two or three samples, minus 30% to plus 40% around a single one), a typical number of tasks and the cost of one task. Without history it uses generic ranges and says so.
- The form compares the range with the chosen preset: it fits, the high end exceeds the budget, or even the low end does. The checkpoint preset has no cap, so it only reports the interval.
- After the plan exists, the remaining cost is the open tasks times the typical task cost plus the later stages, less what the running stage already spent. The burn rate is tokens per active minute, and the forecast names the stage where the budget runs out at the typical cost and at the high end.
- The range shown at creation is kept as `estimates/start.json`, with the approximate cost and the workflow ID. A completed run shows it next to the actual usage.

`baked workflow estimate [WORKFLOW]` prints the estimate before a run, stage by stage and model by model, with the options of `workflow run` that change it (`--model`, `--route`, `--policy`, `--worktree`, `--writers`, `--candidates`, `--json`):

- Only the definition's stages count. History is this project's runs plus those of the stores in `sizing.historyStores`, and the basis line says how many came from other projects. A stage uses earlier runs of the same workflow when they reached it, then runs of other workflows, then a generic range by stage type (tasks stages: task cost times task count; approval, check and learn stages without history: zero). Once the history holds at least 3 runs of the same workflow whose tokens went mostly to the model most planned stages run on, only runs of that model count, since providers spend very differently on the same work; with fewer, every run counts and a note says the history mixes models. Runs of other workflows on that model do not count toward the 3: in bench round 52 eight initiative decompose runs and two one-task feature runs keyed the history, and an initiative's planned features put a fifth of their tokens on their tasks where they spent four fifths, which priced the rest at planning rates (#1767).
- Each stage carries the models routing gives it: the stage route, else its profile's model; tasks stages use `implementer`, review stages their review profiles, and their tokens are split evenly.
- The cost in USD prices 60% uncached input, 30% cache reads and 10% output at the prices in `src/core/llm/pricing.ts`. The estimate leaves cache writes out, so Anthropic costs read low. Family guesses and Copilot request prices are marked approximate; a model without a price is left out and the cost is marked partial. The estimate is advice only; a run's [cost cap](#cost-cap) is enforced on its measured cost.

### Doctor

`baked doctor` (short for `baked workflow doctor [WORKFLOW]`, same options as `estimate`) checks a run before it starts and exits 1 when something must be fixed:

- Start refusals: workflows disabled, then everything `workflow run` would refuse (policy, definition, routing, gates and their confirmation, auto approval confirmation, profiles, skills, worktree prerequisites, writers). The doctor calls the same validation as the start.
- Credentials: every model the run can call (stage routes, stage profiles, review and skeptic profiles, and every profile that may implement a task) needs a known provider with a key. These are the same checks a start makes: a primary model without credentials fails; an unknown provider, an unanswered check, a fallback model or an unbound profile warns.
- Commands: every check command and AI walk-through start command resolves from its working directory. Missing required ones fail, optional ones warn. Nothing is executed.
- Sandbox: the run's OS sandbox mode against this machine's backend; `required` without a backend fails, `auto` without one warns.
- Warnings that do not block: review on the implementer's model, an MCP grant of a profile that no enabled server provides, uncommitted changes, stale run worktrees, task worktree paths longer than a project language's test tool handles (`WORKTREE_PATH_LIMITS`: `dart test` over 88 characters on Windows, #2361, #2454), no Git repository for an inline run, a run that exited without releasing the workspace, and an estimate above `maxTotalTokens`.
- A live run holding the workspace, or one that stopped with a command of unknown outcome, fails.

`--json` prints the report with every check's `id`, `status`, `title`, `detail` and `fix`, and the estimate.

### Context compaction

Every model step resends the whole conversation, so a stage that reads many files pays for each old file again on every later step. The step budget of an agent attempt (`maxAttemptTokens`, see [step-budget](../../src/core/workflows/step-budget.ts)) replaces older large results of `read`, `navigate`, `bake_find` and `bake_impact` with short stubs that keep every evidence ID; the last three tool results stay verbatim, and edits and check results are never compacted. Evidence is written by the tools into the run store, so nothing is lost; the model reads a file again if it needs the content.

An agent profile can change this for its own attempts with an optional `compaction` object (#334). `keepRecent` (1 to 20) sets how many tool results stay verbatim. `startAtTokens` (a token count) or `startAtShare` (a share of the attempt's token cap, above 0 and at most 1), never both, holds compaction off until the attempt has spent that much; from then on it stays on, so the cached prefix keeps matching. A profile without the object compacts from the first step and keeps three results, as above, and no built-in profile sets it. For example, a project copy of `baked/agents/implementer.json` with `"compaction": { "keepRecent": 2 }` compacts earlier on small tasks, and a reviewer with `"compaction": { "startAtShare": 0.6 }` keeps full context until 60% of its cap. This applies to agents run through BAKED's own model loop; the Claude Code and Codex CLI transports keep their own context.

The evidence handed to later agents is shaped the same way. Each implemented task stores a unified diff of its changes in its `diff` evidence (`patch`, from the content kept before each edit, cut at 48,000 characters), so reviewers and the convergence auditor see the change without reading whole files. Earlier `read` evidence reaches them as its tool, the paths it named and its size, not its content; implementers get no reads at all. An implementer of a planned task gets no check logs either (`command` evidence); a corrective task still gets the logs of the checks it fixes.

An implementer's prompt is trimmed to what writing its task needs, since every step of the attempt resends it: no research (the spec and plan carry what it found), the other tasks of the graph only by ID, title, dependencies and written paths (its own task arrives whole in its input), and only its own profile in the execution contract. The files the task modifies come preloaded in its input (`taskFiles`), as the read tool shows them: whole with line numbers up to 24 KB each and 64 KB in total, outlines above that ([read-outline](../../src/core/workflows/read-outline.ts)). Under an outline come the spans of the declarations whose names appear in the task's title or description (identifiers of four or more characters), as a range read shows them, up to 16 KB per file within the same total; a named class larger than that stays outlined. The implementer edits them on its first step instead of spending a step reading them. Files the task creates, missing files and binary files are not preloaded. A corrective task may write every file of the tasks it corrects, so the files its findings point at come first: the finding's `file`, the files a failing check's output names, and written paths named in the claim or the proposed fix, with the finding's `symbol` added to the names whose spans are preloaded. Its input lists them as `correctionFocus`.
