The correction's first attempt may write only those files and their planned tests (`correctionFocus.scope`: `writable`, with the rest of its scope as `later`); when the fix needs a file in `later`, the attempt reports it as unresolved, and a first attempt that does not finish is retried once with the full scope, keeping its edits. This holds for a correction that runs in its own task worktree beside others too. A correction whose findings name no file, or name all of its files, starts with the full scope. A failed linter, formatter or type checker (a static check, not a test check) names the files it rejects, including the failing task's own: its correction starts on those files only, without their planned tests, its proposed fix says to change only what the diagnostics require, with no behavior changes and no new tests, and `correctionFocus.diagnostics` tells the implementer not to probe the task's criteria (cfgtrace 424e275b spent 1.56M tokens and created two test files for three ruff rules). A failed test check of a task whose output names the task's own test files points the correction at those tests and the planned source files they import, directly or through one more planned file (relative imports and dotted module names, read from the run's files): its first attempt writes them and not every file of the related tasks, which in md-site 337d16db gave each of three correctives eight or nine files of a small project. When a finding carries a review repro, its correction's task description starts with that repro: the implementer runs it with `run_probe` before editing, sees the output the review saw, and runs it again after the fix. In a run with several writers, where planned tasks get no preloaded files, a corrective task gets these files preloaded from its own worktree.

The stages that judge a plan or code against the spec (analyze, the reviewers, the skeptic and convergence) get a digest of a large spec ([spec-digest](../../src/core/workflows/spec-digest.ts)). Above 24,000 bytes of spec JSON, each criterion's given, when and then is cut to its first line (at most 160 characters) and marked `full: false` when something was cut, such as an examples table; the rest of the spec is kept. The digest's note tells the agent to call `read_criterion` with up to 12 criterion IDs, which returns those criteria as `spec.json` holds them (unknown IDs come back in `unknown` with the list of known ones). `read_criterion` is a host tool, so it works with every provider; a profile gets the digest only when its `allowedTools` include it, which the built-in spec-reviewer, reviewer, security-reviewer, skeptic and spec-auditor do. The spec author, the planner, the implementers and the diff-only language reviewer always get the whole spec, and so does every stage of a spec under the threshold. A policy with `specDigest: "off"` turns the digest off. A 43-criterion spec of 53.6 KB, each criterion with the request's examples as a table, was resent whole on every step of these stages, and planning came to $5.88 of a $9.97 run.

The implementer's input also lists the existing tests that exercise the files the task changes (`affectedTests`), nearest first and at most 24 ([affected-tests](../../src/core/workflows/affected-tests.ts)). The list comes from the relative imports of the workspace's JavaScript, TypeScript and Python files, followed back from each written path for up to three hops, plus the naming conventions that pair a source with its test (`store.ts` with `store.test.ts`, `store.py` with `test_store.py`) and every `_test.go` file of a changed Go package. Dependency and build folders are skipped, and a workspace of more than 6000 source files gets no list. When a policy check at the project root runs a test runner that takes files (`bun test`, `vitest`, `jest`, `node --test`, `pytest`, `python -m pytest`, `go test`, `rspec`, `rake test`, also behind `bundle exec`, `phpunit` or `pest` through `php vendor/bin/`, `dart test` and `flutter test`, `mix test` (#2348), `busted`, `bats`, or `sbt test` and sbt 2's `sbt testFull`, which get `testOnly *CalcSuite *ParseSpec` by the test files' names, #1496, #2440), the implementer can also run `request_check <check>-affected`: the same runner with the check's flags on the listed tests only (packages for Go, `TEST=<file>` or `TEST={a,b}` for Rake, #1493). An output-format option such as `rspec --format documentation` keeps its value and does not make the check a formatter. Before the task's first attempt the host records the list and runs that check once as a baseline; the record (`affectedTests` in the run state: the paths, the check id, the baseline status and its evidence) is kept for the task and the corrective tasks that repair it, and a restart or replan clears it. When the baseline passed, the check is required after the task, next to the task's own checks, unless they already run the whole suite it narrows: a test the task broke fails the task even when that test is a file the task may not write, and the finding (`Task T001 broke existing tests: check test-affected passed before the task and fails after it`) starts a corrective task that fixes the change rather than stopping the run as out of scope. When the baseline failed or was inconclusive, the check stays optional and the implementer is told those tests failed before the task. A test file the task deleted is left out of the gate.

**Per-test timeouts.** At execution time the host supplies a 30-second per-test default for `bun test`, jest and vitest. The same default applies to the full suite, `<check>-affected`, `<check>-written`, mutation baseline and mutant runs, `baked workflow gate run`, and deterministic tests generated by an AI walk-through, before and after promotion. Simple package scripts such as `bun run test` and `npm test -- ...` receive the runner flag through the package manager. An explicit timeout flag, `bunfig.toml` `[test] timeout`, jest `testTimeout` in package.json or configuration, or vitest `testTimeout` in vitest/vite configuration takes precedence. The host reads configuration without executing it. Scripts with shell expressions are left unchanged because their argument forwarding is unknown. Each check keeps its own whole-process deadline. Stored check definitions and approval digests do not change. pytest has no per-test limit of its own and refuses `--timeout` without the pytest-timeout plugin, so a pytest check (`pytest`, `python -m pytest`, behind `uv run` and the other environment runners) gets `--timeout=30` only when the project declares `pytest-timeout` (in `pyproject.toml`, `setup.cfg`, `tox.ini`, `pytest.ini`, `Pipfile` or a `requirements*.txt` file) and sets no `timeout` in its pytest configuration. `go test -timeout` limits the whole test binary rather than one test, Maven and `dotnet test` take a limit only through their own configuration, `cargo test` has none, and `node --test` has no default per-test limit, so these checks run as written (#1655).

**Written formatting.** A task that creates code files gets the check `<gate>-written` after it when the project offers a formatter gate the run's checks leave out (a repository not formatter-clean, where the whole-tree gate is offered but not preselected or was dropped because its baseline fails): the same formatter in check mode on only the files of that language the task creates, required, with the formatter's write mode as its fixer, so a failure is repaired by the fixer on those files before it reaches anyone. Python gets `ruff format --check <files>` (fixer `ruff format`) or `black --check <files>`, the npm languages `biome format <files>`, `prettier --check <files>` or `dprint check <files>` through their package runner (only when the formatter is installed, so `npx` never downloads one) or `bun fmt --no-script --check <files>` (only when the Bun on the PATH lists `--range-start` in `bun fmt --no-script --help --all`, which Bun 1.4.4 added with `bun fmt`; an older Bun gets no check, #1920), Go `gofmt -l <files>` (fixer `gofmt -w`), C# `dotnet format --verify-no-changes --include <files>`, Dart `dart format --output=none --set-exit-if-changed <files>` (fixer `dart format`, also in a Flutter package, #1494), Swift `swift format lint --strict <files>` (fixer `swift format format --in-place`, #1492), Ruby `rubocop --only Layout <files>` through `bundle exec` (fixer `rubocop -a --only Layout`: RuboCop's layout cops are its formatter, and its lint cops stay with the `rubocop` gate, #1493; a Standard project gets none), PHP `php vendor/bin/pint --test <files>` or `php vendor/bin/php-cs-fixer fix --dry-run --diff <files>` (#1491), Lua `stylua --check <files>` (fixer `stylua`, #1495), shell `shfmt -d <files>` (fixer `shfmt -w`, #1497), Elixir `mix format --check-formatted <files>` (fixer `mix format`, only with a `.formatter.exs`, as the gate, #2490). Rust has none: `cargo fmt` and `rustfmt` follow `mod` declarations, so the checks stage runs them on the whole crate (#842); Java and Kotlin have no detected formatter gate, and Scala's `scalafmt` gate has no check mode for named files (`scalafmtOnly` only writes), so the gate itself formats each task's files. A file the task edits is checked only on the lines the run changed (`<gate>-lines`, against the file's content before the run's first write), because a whole-file run would reformat lines the request left alone, which a brownfield review rejects (#1582). A file whose content before the run holds no code (blank, or comments only, as the stubs an initiative's skeleton writes) is checked whole like a new file: its text is BAKED's own, and every later feature that fills a stub edits it, so a formatter with no range form (styler, Biome, dprint, shfmt) held none of it before (#2243). The range forms: `ruff format --check --range=<start>-<end+1>` (one check per changed block, every block however many, fixer `ruff format --range`), `black --check --line-ranges=<start>-<end> ...` (every block in one check), `prettier --check --range-start=<offset> --range-end=<offset>` and `bun fmt --no-script --check --range-start=<offset> --range-end=<offset>` (one check per block), and `stylua --check --range-start <byte> --range-end <byte>` (StyLua's offsets are bytes, one check per block). biome, dprint, gofmt, `dotnet format`, `dart format`, swift-format, RuboCop, Pint, PHP-CS-Fixer, shfmt and `mix format` have no line range, so a file they format that the task edits is not checked. A file the run itself created earlier is checked whole. Every format-written check fixes only the files it checks (`fixPaths`), never another file the task edits. The range checks of one file run from its last changed block up, so a fixer that changes a block's line count never moves a block fixed after it, and a fixed file's checks are built again from the file as it is before the re-check (#1898). A check runs in its gate's `cwd` and names its files from there; every fixer, of any check, is given the file path relative to its check's `cwd` (a fixer in `cwd: "py"` formats `tests/a.py`, not `py/tests/a.py`). Before it builds a ruff or black range check, the host reads the formatter's help (`ruff format --help`, `black --help`) once per command and directory for `--range` (ruff 0.2.1 and later) or `--line-ranges` (black 23.11 and later); a release without the flag gets no range check, as a formatter with no range form, because every range check and its fixer would fail (#1907). R57 zonevet-update appended a test to a file that was not formatted before the run and shipped it unformatted. A file the run's checks already format gets no extra check: a check of the language's formatter, not narrowed by `--include`, `--exclude` or a range, on the file or a directory above it from the check's `cwd` (no path means the whole `cwd`; a `dotnet format` project or solution file covers its directory), or a `format` script for the npm languages. A formatter check on other files does not hold it: `ruff format --check legacy/clean.py` in the run's checks leaves a new `tests/test_new.py` to the format-written check, and a path the check names that cannot be told apart from a flag's value counts as a path. Nor is a check added whose program does not resolve. The table is `FILE_FORMATTERS` in [format-written](../../src/core/workflows/format-written.ts). Two Python update runs (bench rounds 55 and 56) shipped a new test file `ruff format` would rewrite although the brief asked for it formatted, because the repository's older files were not formatted and the run had no format check (#1837). A created file whose content the request supplies whole (a create diff, or one fenced block for the path that is not a signature listing) keeps the request's bytes: it gets the advisory check `<gate>-supplied`, the same formatter in check mode with no fixer, so the formatter's verdict is recorded and nothing rewrites the file. In R81 seqmill-update the brief gave a 51-line test file as a create diff, the repository runs `ruff check` but no `ruff format`, and the fixer of `ruff-format-written` rewrapped it to 87 lines (#2504).

**Task-end Rust format check.** Rust has no `FILE_FORMATTERS` row: `cargo fmt` and rustfmt follow `mod` declarations, so the checks stage's `cargo fmt --check` is the only format check, and a file rustfmt rejects or never finishes on (evalpin R71, #2240) shows only after the task that wrote it has ended. With the policy field `rustFormatWritten: "on"` (setting `quality.rustFormatWritten`, default `off`; absent means off, so stored policies keep their hash) a task that creates Rust files gets `rustfmt-written` after it, even when the run's checks hold `cargo fmt`: `rustfmt --check --edition <crate edition> <files>` (one check per edition), required, with `rustfmt --edition <edition>` as the fixer on those files, run with the rustfmt next to the cargo of the project's `cargo fmt` gate (a cargo behind a launcher gets none). A file that declares a module out of line (`mod parser;`) is left to `cargo fmt`, since rustfmt would format the module's file too, which may be another task's or not written yet (#842); an edited file too, as rustfmt has no stable range form (#1582). A timeout of the check is classified like any formatter's: the file it hangs on is named (`timeout_slow`) and the task's correction repairs it (#2248).


**Written tests.** A task that writes test files gets the check `<check>-written` after it: the same runner on only those files, required, with the check's timeout capped at 60 seconds. Besides `bun test`, vitest, jest, `node --test`, pytest, `python -m unittest` (its value-free options kept, discovery options dropped), `go test`, RSpec and `rake test` (#1493), PHPUnit and Pest on the named `*Test.php` files (#1491) busted on the named `*_spec.lua` files (#1495) and bats on the named `.bats` files (#1497), also behind an environment runner (`uv run`, `poetry run`, `pipenv run`, `pdm run`) and in a check with its own `cwd` (the test files under that directory, by paths relative to it; #1655), it runs compiled stacks (#1396): `dotnet test --filter "FullyQualifiedName~<Namespace.Class>."` for a C# test file (`*Test(s).cs` or under a `Tests` or `*.Tests` directory), `cargo test --test <name>` (or `cargo nextest run --test <name>`) for a Rust file directly in a `tests/` directory, maven's `test` goal with `-Dtest=<package.Class>` and `-Dsurefire.failIfNoSpecifiedTests=false`, and gradle's `test --tests <package.Class>` for a Java or Kotlin file under `src/test/`. The classes are the ones the file declares, not its name (#2363): each top-level type whose code (comments and strings masked) holds a test attribute or annotation (`[Fact]`, `[Theory]`, `[Test]`, `[TestMethod]`, `@Test`, `@ParameterizedTest`, a Kotest spec), with its namespace or package, every such type of the file, and for Kotlin also the file's class (`<File>Kt`, or its `@file:JvmName`) when a top-level function holds a test. A partial class declared in two files is named once. Only a file that declares no such type is selected by its name, as before (`<Class>` from the file name for C#, `<package.Class>` from the path for Java and Kotlin). The reader per language is `TEST_CLASSES` in [test-classes](../../src/core/workflows/test-classes.ts). maskback (C#, bench round 75) wrote `FailureExceptionTests` into the scaffold's `UnitTest1.cs`; the filter `FullyQualifiedName~UnitTest1.` selected no test and the check failed on a correct implementation, and two correctives (1.31M tokens and a spec conflict a person answered) kept a `UnitTest1` class for the filter. Maven's `-Dsurefire.failIfNoSpecifiedTests=false` run of a class no file declares prints no count and passed vacuously. A written-tests check selected by declared classes carries them (`declaredTests`); when it exits 0 and runs no test, the host records a `written_tests_unselected` would-stop naming its command (shadow: the check still fails as before and the task is sent back; promoted, it fails as the host's selection instead of a corrective). CTest gets none: it runs the binaries the build gate built and selects tests by name, not by file, so a C or C++ task's written tests run with the `cmake-build` and `ctest` gates (#1490). A compiled test project builds every test file in it, so for these stacks another task's test file still to run also holds the check back. dieodds (C#, bench round 39) had no task check for its test tasks, and five correctives after the feature gate cost 1.76M tokens. It is added only when no other task of the plan that writes non-test files is still to run (the tests may import what that task writes); documentation (Markdown, reStructuredText, AsciiDoc, a README, CHANGELOG or LICENSE) does not count unless the test task reads it, so a README task after the tests no longer suppresses the check (#1408), when the task's own checks do not already run the whole suite, and not under tests first, where new tests are meant to fail. A failing or hanging file is then repaired by the task that wrote it, instead of a first execution in the checks stage. A planned task that writes test files and whose own checks do not run the suite is told the check in its input (`writtenTestsCheck`: the check ID, its test files and a note to run `request_check <check>-written` before submitting), and when the check still fails after it submits, the task goes back to itself once with the failures and their output (`reason: "written_tests"` in `tasks/TASK_ID/cut-off-checks.json`) before a correction is opened. This applies when each failure is the `-written` check and no named production file belongs to another finished task. A named source defect goes directly to a correction of those files; the test task keeps its required tests. In bench round 43 the bun plans gave their test tasks only typecheck and lint, the workers did not know they could run `tests-written`, and its failures became corrections that started without the task's context and from the union of the plan's files: 41 percent of url-shortener's and 23 percent of md-site's tokens (#1518). The implementer can run the same check with `request_check <check>-written`. A check that times out after its output names a failed test (a `(fail)`, `FAILED`, `--- FAIL`, `not ok` or `✗` line, cargo's and deno's `... FAILED`, dotnet's `Failed <test> [8 ms]`, Surefire's `<<< FAILURE!` or Gradle's `test() FAILED`; #1424) counts as failed with the reason `timeout_after_failure`, and the output gets a note to run the failing file alone and fix what keeps it from finishing. It then repairs like any failed check, in a task or in the checks stage, instead of ending the run inconclusive (`verification_failed`). The `<check>-written` check also counts a timeout without any failed test line as failed, with the reason `timeout_no_result` (its definition carries `onTimeout: "failed"`; absent, a timeout is inconclusive). The first run of such a check in a run also builds the toolchain's cache, so when it times out without a result and the check has no earlier result in the run, the host runs it once more and records that run, with a note on the first timeout when it then finishes. On a cold Windows runner the first `go test` of one new file took the whole 60 seconds to compile and the same check ran in 5.6 seconds after it, but the timeout sent the finished task back to its worker (#2162). A file that never ends times out on the second run too and fails as below. The tests are the task's own work, so a file that never finishes is a defect of that task: the output gets a note naming the files and asking for a representative few generated cases instead of the whole accepted domain, and a correction of the task repairs it. Before this, url-shortener (bench round 32) wrote a property test that ran about 10k synchronous file-database transactions, printed nothing within 60 seconds, and stopped the run with nothing to repair. When the task's title or description names a number of generated inputs ("at least 100 generated inputs per criterion", the largest such number), or it is a property test task that names none (then 100, the property gate's minimum), the check carries it as `minCases`. The note then keeps that number and asks for cheap cases instead: one store or temporary directory set up once and reused, no process, server or file per case. After a timeout, the correction's proposed fix repeats the number, so the correction's own run of the check carries it too; a plain assertion failure does not get that note, which would send the correction after speed instead of the defect. The earlier advice to keep to a few tens of cases made a correction of url-shortener (bench round 36) cut its property tests to 32 cases, and the review then asked for 100 again: three corrections and 3.14M tokens (#1367). The test runners whose own per-test limit is 5 s (`bun test`, jest, vitest) use the same 30 s default in `<check>-written` and the full suite, with project overrides as described above. A failed check whose output shows a test stopped at the runner's per-test limit gets a note that names the limit and points at the per-test argument of `test(name, fn, ms)` instead of cutting cases. md-site (bench round 38) hit bun's 5 s default in a property test while the check allowed 60 s, and the chain of tasks cost 2.71M tokens (#1385). A correction's `<check>-written` runs only the test files its findings point at (the failing tests of the check it was opened for, through the repair scope) and the test files it changed itself, not every test file in its write list. That list is the union of the related planned tasks' files, sibling tests included, and a sibling's test still in flight failed on its own defect: md-site (bench round 37) opened a correction of the correction that cost 4.31M tokens and blocked the run (#1373). The other tasks' tests run in their own task checks and in the checks stage. The implementer's `request_check <check>-written` uses the same files, together with the test files of the task the attempt has edited so far, which the host's check after the attempt runs too; when a correction points at no test file and has edited none, the request answers `deferred` with a note instead of being refused (#1421).

**Test support files.** The `<check>-written` check runs only the test files that declare a test, read in the task's worktree after its write, so a file the host wrote from the request (#2333) is read as written (`runnableTestFiles` in `src/core/workflows/affected-tests.ts`, #2439). A file declares a test when its language's `TEST_DECLARATIONS` row counts one in its code or its `spec` pattern matches (`declaresTest` in `src/core/intelligence/test-declarations.ts`; a language without that row asks its `TEST_MARKERS` row), when a runner that selects by class finds a test class in it (`TEST_CLASSES`), or when it holds the inline acceptance rows block. A test-tree file that declares none is support: Go fixtures in a `_test.go` file, Minitest's `test_helper.rb` (before, only that name was left out), a C# or Java helper class in the test project, a constants module named like a test. It is left out of the check, and a task whose test files are all support gets no `<check>-written` check; its other checks (build, vet, lint) still run, and the tests that use the file run in their own tasks' checks. trailspin (Go, bench round 77) wrote a constants-only `internal/cli/fixtures_test.go` that the brief gave verbatim; its check ran `go test ./internal/cli`, printed `[no tests to run]` and failed on `requireTests`, the correction added a test to the verbatim file, and the session and the convergence task that undid it cost 1.04M tokens. A Scala file under `src/test/` is a written test file too, run through sbt's `testOnly`.

**Check timeouts on the run's own work.** A formatter or linter is deterministic, so a required check that times out is not left inconclusive when the run can tell what it hangs on (#2240). When the check passed earlier in the same run, or when it runs a formatter with a file-scoped form (a `FILE_FORMATTERS` row, or rustfmt with the crate's edition for `cargo fmt`) and that formatter, run on each file the run wrote alone (the five largest of its language, up to 20 seconds each), does not finish on one of them, the check is failed with the reason `timeout_slow` and a note that names the file and what to change (restructure the code the tool hangs on, or put the tool's skip marker such as `#[rustfmt::skip]` on the item). It is then repaired by a correction like any failed check. Its fixer is not run, since the same tool would hang on the same file, and a v2 gate retries it only when its `retryOn` names `timeout`. A timeout without either signal (a cold toolchain, #1177) stays inconclusive. When an inconclusive required check timed out on the same workspace snapshot as its previous run, the block says it timed out twice on the same workspace, names the snapshot and both evidence IDs, and offers `revise` (change or drop the check) and `accept-limitation` besides `resume`; the run never skips a required check by itself. `accept-limitation` is a person's call (`baked workflow resume RUN --remedy accept-limitation`, `L` in `/workflow`, the Accept button in the desktop app, the API's resume `remedy`): the check is recorded as a known limitation of kind `check_not_run` (decided by the user, with both evidence IDs) that the run report lists, a decision artifact is written under `decisions/`, and the check no longer blocks the run while its last result is that timeout. A later run of the check that passes or fails counts as usual, and a revise or replan clears the acceptance (#2358). The host runs that end without a check result say what hung the same way: the baseline measurement (`measure`, which also lists the check under `timeouts` in `facts/check-baseline.json`), the edge-input probe and `run_repro` (both through `runRepro`), `run_probe` and documented examples name the command and the files it ran on (`` `bun src/cli.ts` on src/cli.ts did not finish within 30000 ms ``), and record it through `recordCommandTimeout` in `src/core/verification/command-timeouts.ts`, the host fact a later step reads with `commandTimeoutsUnder`. In R71 evalpin json, rustfmt never finished on a test file with a data literal nested 20 deep: `cargo fmt --check` ran into its 300-second timeout twice on one snapshot, and the initiative stopped with 7 of 10 features left.

**Check failures the machine causes.** A required check that exits nonzero with output matching a signature of its toolchain (`ENVIRONMENT_SIGNATURES` in `src/core/verification/environment-faults.ts`: for Gradle, Maven and sbt the JDK's "Unable to establish loopback connection", which a short 8.3 TEMP path on Windows triggers) is `inconclusive` with reason `environment_broken`, and its output gets a note with the fix (`JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS=-Djdk.net.unixdomain.tmpdir=<short directory>`, or TEMP and TMP at a short path). It opens no correction and no finding: the run blocks on the inconclusive required check, like any other, until the machine is fixed and the run resumed. `baked doctor` runs the toolchain's probe to report it before a run. R72 jvmsig hit it on every `gradle check` (#2273). For Swift the signature is SwiftPM's own crash, `error: Build failed` and `error: fatalError` with no compiler or XCTest `error:` line of a `.swift` file (R76 hintsieve, task worktrees building at once; its note says to run fewer Swift builds at once). SwiftPM's `warning: unable to create symbolic link ... encountered an I/O error (code: 512)` is not part of it: SwiftPM prints it on passing builds too (#2418). A toolchain without a signature has a `none` row that names the bench round and date through which nothing was observed.

**Check failures that print no failure line.** A required check that exits nonzero (reason `exit`) with output that holds no failure line of any toolchain (no failed test of `FAILED_TESTS`, no `BUILD_FAILURES` line, no linter or type checker count, and no word such as `error`, `failed`, `exception`, `warning` or `reformat`; `silentFailure` in `environment-faults.ts`) is a toolchain crash or kill, not a defect of the code: R76 hintsieve's `swift test` exited 4 with SwiftPM progress lines only and opened a 440k-token corrective whose diff was empty, and the next run of the same tree passed (#2418). The checks stage (schema-1 and a v2 check stage, for a gate its own retries ran once) and the reported-defect route run such a check once more before they open a correction, under the `silent_check_failure` [stop reason](#stop-reasons-and-shadow-mode). In `shadow` (the default) they record a `decision.would_rerun` event and correct as before. With `stopModes: { silent_check_failure: "enforce" }` they run it again and record `checks/<check>/rerun-<evidence>.json` (kind `check-rerun`): a pass makes the first run an inconclusive flake (the gate is `flaky`) and opens nothing; a second failure opens the correction as before.

**Gated and skipped test files.** A test file that runs only behind a build gate is run with that gate in its `<check>-written` check: a Rust file with a file-level `#![cfg(feature = "name")]` (also inside `all(...)`) gets `cargo test --features name --test <file>`, and a Go file whose `//go:build` line needs tags (`a`, `a && b`, apart from the operating system, architecture, `cgo` and version tags Go sets itself) gets `go test -tags a,b`; a value the check already gives is extended (`-tags x` becomes `-tags x,a`). The gate readers are the `TEST_GATES` record in `src/core/workflows/test-gates.ts`, with a `none(reason)` row for every language whose gate is not a flag of its test command. The `<check>-written` and `<check>-affected` checks also carry `requireTests: true`: a run that exits 0 but ran no test (cargo's "running 0 tests", go's `[no tests to run]` or `[no test files]`, bun's `0 pass` with only skips, a jest or vitest file of skipped tests, pytest's skipped- or deselected-only summary, a Maven run whose tests were all skipped, dotnet's "No test matches the given testcase filter", a TAP plan of `# skip` lines) fails with a note that the check verified nothing, and the task makes its tests run under the command. Skipped and ignored tests do not count as run. tonemath-update (Rust, bench round 56) wrote `tests/unicode_tests.rs` behind the `unicode` feature; its check ran without the feature, printed "running 0 tests" and passed, and the five tests first ran in the review (#1832). A check of the project's own (`requireTests` unset) keeps passing on 0 tests, so an empty suite at the baseline is not a failure.

**Checks behind a launcher.** A check, fixer or gate command that starts its program through a shell or batch launcher is routed by the program the launcher runs, not by the launcher (#1507): `cmd /c mvn -B -q verify` (also `cmd.exe /d /s /c "..."`), `sh -c "pytest -q"` (also `bash`, `zsh`, `dash`, `ksh`, with flags such as `-lc`), and `powershell` or `pwsh` with `-Command` (or `-c`). This covers the written tests and affected tests checks of every toolchain above, the [mutation gate](code-quality.md#mutation-gate)'s choice of test checks, the fixers (`cargo fmt`, `go fmt`, `dotnet format`), the listing check `gofmt -l`, and the check commands a criterion quotes. A derived check keeps the launcher: `cmd /c mvn -B -q verify` becomes `cmd /c mvn -B -q test -Dtest=com.example.SumTest -Dsurefire.failIfNoSpecifiedTests=false`, and a one-line form stays one line (`sh -c "pytest -q ./tests/test_sum.py"`, words quoted for the shell where needed). Up to two launchers are unwrapped (`cmd /c sh -c "..."`). A launched line with shell syntax is left as it is, since what it runs is up to the shell: `&&`, `;`, pipes, redirections, variables (`$X`, `%X%`), globs, or arguments after the `sh -c` line. A derived argument that `cmd.exe` cannot pass on unchanged (a space, a quote, `|`, `&` and the like, such as a `dotnet test --filter` of two classes) derives no check through `cmd`; the check itself still runs in the checks stage. In bench round 42 the Maven check of fecsmith (Java, Windows) was `cmd /c mvn -B -q verify`, and none of its 7 features got a `verify-written` check or a mutation score.

**Size targets.** A file size or length target in the request or the spec ("below about 400 lines") is advisory. The implementer is told to aim for it and never to block over it. An unresolved item of an `implemented` result that only reports a missed length (a line count or file size with a word such as target, guideline, limit, exceeding or above, and no word of a functional gap such as fails, missing or not implemented) does not leave the task unfinished: it moves to the result's summary, where reviewers read it, and to `tasks/size-targets/<task>.json`, and the task's checks still decide the task. The same holds for the integrator of a merge conflict: a merged file over its size target does not make the conflict unresolved. A `blocked` or `failed` result is not changed. Before this, ecfit (bench round 30) restarted a task whose checks passed only because its file had 543 lines against a 400-line hint, and the restart blocked the run on trimming lines it could not count.

A workflow agent's `read` of a whole file larger than 20 KB (24 KB for a file its task writes, whose exact text the edit needs) returns the file's outline instead: its classes, functions, methods, types and top-level variables with their line spans, from tree-sitter ([read-outline](../../src/core/workflows/read-outline.ts)). The agent then reads the spans it needs with `ranges` or a symbol with `target`. A file with no outline (an unsupported language or a parse failure) is read whole, and reads with ranges or a target are unchanged. A compacted `read` stub asks for `fresh: true`, because the read tool answers a repeated whole-file read with "already in your context". Reads of line ranges or symbols are never compacted and always run fresh: they are small and chosen for an edit, and an agent that lost them, or was refused a fourth range of the same file, read the same spans again instead of editing. An implementer that must edit and has only read for four steps in a row is told once to apply its edits. A retry of a task whose earlier attempt left edits in place gets those edits as a patch (`earlierEdits.patch`) and reads only the spans it still has to change.

The prompt of an agent attempt puts the run's documents (request, research, spec, plan and task graph) on one line before the attempt's own input (the snapshot hashes, the task and its evidence). Attempts of a run then share a byte-identical prefix, which providers serve from their prompt cache. On the Claude API an attempt marks its system prompt and, with the request-level automatic breakpoint, its newest message, so each step reads the earlier steps from the cache, and a breakpoint after the run's documents lets the next attempt read them too; on Claude Code those documents go into the cached system prompt ([providers](../providers.md#prompt-cache)). Attempt usage records cache reads and writes per step.

### Plan-time sizing

A policy with `sizing` prices the plan before it is approved ([sizing](../../src/core/workflows/sizing.ts)):

```json
"sizing": { "maxAttemptTokens": 600000, "maxRunTokens": 4000000, "extendToTokens": 8000000, "historyStores": ["../other-checkout"] }
```

- **Price.** A task costs, for each file it writes, the per-file rate of that file's kind and of its write action (`create` or `modify`). The kinds are docs (`*.md`, `*.mdx`, `*.markdown`, `*.rst`, `*.adoc` anywhere, and any other non-source file under `doc/` or `docs/`), test files (under `test/`, `tests/`, `__tests__/`, `spec/`, or `*.test.*`/`*.spec.*`), manifests (project manifests and solution files by name: `package.json`, `tsconfig*.json`, `go.mod`, `Cargo.toml`, `pom.xml`, Gradle build files, `pyproject.toml`, `requirements*.txt`, `*.csproj`, `*.sln`, `*.slnx` and similar) and code. A rate is the middle half of `tokens / files` over finished tasks of history that wrote only one kind, where a task's tokens include the corrective tasks of the same run that wrote the same files, because getting the task done took both. History is this store (this project's runs) plus `historyStores` (a `.sqlite` path or a project directory). With at least two of this project's tasks of the kind that only created (or only modified) their files, those tasks alone set the rate (`basis: "project"`). Otherwise every task of the kind counts, from any store and with either action, and the upper bound is kept at least at the default's (`basis: "history"`), since rates carried over from another project were off in both directions; with six or more such samples their own upper bound is used, so measured history can lower the default's. Rates are also kept per model: each sample names the model that spent most of its task's tokens, and once the model the tasks will run on (the routed model of their profile) ran at least two tasks of a kind, only those tasks price that kind; with fewer, every model's tasks count. The report names the model (`model`, and per rate the model that set it or `null`), since providers spend differently on the same task. A kind without history uses the defaults: code 30k–100k per created and 40k–130k per modified file, tests 60k–350k and 150k–350k, docs 40k–250k and 60k–250k, manifests 5k–40k either way. File size is not used: it said little about the size of a change. Test tasks use whole-task token costs instead of historical tokens per criterion or case. The report's `testTaskRates` records the median and 90th percentile as the lower and upper estimates. Legacy `criterionRate` and `caseRate` fields remain present as `null`. It prefers the planned model's history after two samples, then this project's history after two samples, otherwise all configured history. Case counts select small (1-5), medium (6-20) or large (21+) buckets when a bucket has at least two samples; otherwise the whole distribution applies. Equal case counts are valid samples. Without case-bearing history, tests keep their per-file allowance and a floor of 15k-75k tokens per literal case. Older samples without readable specs still supply file rates. The shared criterion-case collector counts quoted inputs, enumerated members and input table rows, removes duplicate literals and excludes check commands. `testTaskLimit` records the largest finished task's token cost and case and criterion counts. Proactive splitting uses the largest observed case count, the capacity: a test task above it stays whole up to four times the capacity (`CAPACITY_PROBE`) while its expected price fits one attempt's cap, and splits above that or above the cap (#1789). Splitting at the capacity itself kept every later task under it, so it never grew: bench round 53 cut url-shortener's 8 planned tasks into 20 at a capacity of 4 cases and spent 5.78M tokens against 3.42M, and in the bench-trace corpus a chain of slices of 9-16 cases cost a median 572k tokens against 349k for a whole task of the same size. A whole task that finishes raises the capacity for later runs. A file with five or fewer known cases stays whole. Without observed case capacity, larger or unknown tasks split only when their expected fallback price exceeds the attempt cap. Split tasks have distinct titles ending in `(part i of n)`, including file parts and sequential slices. Each task records `cases` and `sliceCount`; its attempt cap follows its upper price within the policy's floor and sizing ceiling. The attempt cap counts uncached tokens; the estimate and run budget count total tokens. Once test tasks finish their checks, serial and parallel dispatch refresh unstarted task caps and the remaining plan's budget from those measured task costs, recorded in `sizing/progress-<hash>.json`. Current-run samples take precedence; started attempts and their continuation caps stay unchanged. The approved graph stays fixed. The run price is what the run spent before approval, plus the tasks' price, plus the review and convergence ranges once more for a correction round. The tasks' price is the sum of their midpoints plus the square root of the summed squares of their half-ranges: one task is priced at its upper estimate, many independent tasks well below the sum of their upper estimates, which overstated round 01 runs 1.3-2.8 times. The report keeps the sums of the lower, middle and upper estimates in `taskTokens`. The report is stored as `sizing/plan-N.json` (`schemaVersion: 3`, rates per kind and action plus test-task distributions).
- **Supplied lines.** With `"suppliedDiff": true` in `sizing` (off by default), a task whose every written file the request supplies verbatim is priced from the supplied lines instead of the per-file rates (`suppliedTaskPrice`). A test task is no exception: the case rates assume the agent designs the cases, and a test file the request gives verbatim is only copied, so such a task is priced from its lines, split only on them (never on its case count or the observed case capacity), and a split never slices its files by criterion. In bench round 65 aclvet-update priced a 98-line supplied `summary_test.go` at 384k–893k and a 2-line `cli_test.go` diff at 426k–1.14M by cases; they cost 58k and 104k (#2019). A file is supplied by a fenced unified diff with `+++` headers (its added and removed lines count; a deleted file supplies none) or by a fenced block attributed to its path: named in the last line of prose before the block ("Replace `src/a.ts` with:") or in a comment on the block's first line (`// src/a.ts`), where every line of the block counts. A block named by two planned paths, or by none, supplies nothing. A fenced block ends at a closing fence of its own (the same character, at least as long, nothing after it), so a fence line with a language inside it is content; in a diff block (`diff` or `patch`, or a first line such as `--- a/x` or `@@`) a fence indented further than the opening one is a context line, the README's own code fence shown with the diff's leading space, and the hunk's lines after it still count. Inside a hunk, by its `@@` counts, a removed `---` line (a Markdown rule, front matter) and an added `+++` line count as changed lines, not file headers. In bench round 65 seqmill-update and aclvet-update lost their README hunks at such a context fence, so each mixed task fell back to history: seqmill 381k–1.06M for a 276k run, and aclvet a split-tasks replan of 81k tokens (#2018). The price is 250–1,000 tokens per supplied line, at least 34k–134k per file: 34k is the lowest measured exact-diff update (ecfit quick, bench round 64; sdd updates of round 63 cost 56k–79k per changed file), and 134k the highest outcome of a run that applied the supplied lines as given (spdxok); mdtoc's 320k came from another defect. Such a task is sent back to be split only when its supplied lines alone, at 1,000 tokens each, are above `maxAttemptTokens` and it writes several files, never on its file count. A task with any file the request does not supply keeps its history price. Each task in `sizing/plan-N.json` records `basis` (`supplied`, `history` or `default`, the defaults alone) and a supplied task its `suppliedLines`. When any task is supplied, the approval request carries `estimateSource`, which the TUI, the desktop run decision and the desktop Needs you detail show: "Task estimates: T001 (5 lines), T004 (4 lines) from the lines the request supplies; T002 from history". In bench round 64 spdxok-update supplied every hunk, yet two 2-file tasks were priced 307k–762k and 242k–683k from history, the plan was split, the replan cost 98k, and the three tasks it gave cost 59k, 72k and 105k (#2002).
- **Supplied exact text.** With `suppliedDiff` on, a test task with a criterion that compares a text exactly (`exact`, `exactly`, `verbatim`, `byte for byte`, `identical`) and names a Markdown section of the request by its heading title is priced at 650–1,300 tokens per character of that section when that is above its case price (`exactTextChars`). A section runs from its heading to the next heading of the same or a higher level, or to the close of the fence the request quotes it in (a `markdown` or bare fence); a heading inside a fenced block of the section (`# a shell comment`) ends nothing. The task records `exactTextChars` in `sizing/plan-N.json`, and its split is still decided on its case price, since a slice holds the same text. In bench round 67 srtvet-update T005 compared the 1,264-character README section "## Cleaning spaces" byte for byte in Go and cost 1.64M tokens, four check rounds over the string's quoting, against a price of 285k–834k from its 14 cases (#2101). The 1,300 is that run's rate; the low end is half of it.
- **Prescribed rows.** A request may give a test file as rows in a notation of its own: a fenced block whose first line names the file and says it holds rows (`ProblemsTests in tests/problems.test.ts (63 rows, ...)`, or the same line behind a comment marker), one row per following line. Sizing reads such a block for each test file a task writes (`suppliedRows`; a block that names two planned files, or none, is not read, and the block is never a supplied file, so host apply and supplied drift do not see it) and records its rows, characters, price and slice count as `rowVolume` on the task in `sizing/plan-N.json`. Off by default (`mode: "shadow"`), that record changes nothing. With `"rowVolume": true` in `sizing` (`mode: "on"`), the task is priced at the larger of its history price and 137–343 tokens per row character, and when the rows' expected price is above `maxAttemptTokens` the host splits the task before analysis into that many sequential row slices (at most one per row): each slice keeps the task's criteria and writes only rows *a* to *b* of the block, the first creates the file with the task's other writes, and each later one appends to it and depends on the one before; tasks that depended on the whole task depend on every slice, and each slice is priced at its share of the rows and never split again. Each slice carries its rows (`sizingSlice.rows`), and the task-end case walk asks a slice only for the criterion literals its rows hold (see the case ownership above, #2466). The planner is told the same before it plans and in a split note. A task the request supplies verbatim (`suppliedDiff`) keeps its supplied price, and a rows task (`rowsAsData`) is never priced or sliced by the block. In bench round 78 deltaglue's brief gave `tests/problems.test.ts` as 63 rows of 20,123 characters, the criterion cited "the 63 prescribed rows" and spelled out three literals, so T007 was priced at 2 cases (341k–863k) and kept whole; it cost 6.89M tokens over 7 attempts and never finished (#2455). The low end is the lowest rate of its attempts that ended with rows written (264k for 6 rows), the high end what the unfinished task spent per character.
- **Exact text as a string literal.** With the policy field `exactTextLiteral: "on"` (setting `quality.exactTextLiteral`, default `off`; absent means off, so stored policies keep their hash), a task that writes a test file gets, in its implementer context, each request section a criterion of the task compares exactly (the same reading as the pricing above, `exactTextSections` in [sizing](../../src/core/workflows/sizing.ts)) as a string literal of the test file's language, written once by the host (`exactText`: the section's title, its characters and the literal; `exactTextContext` in [supplied-content](../../src/core/workflows/supplied-content.ts), #2101). The literal is double-quoted with the language's own escapes (`STRING_LITERALS`, one row per supported language): the quote, the escape character, line ends and tabs by name, `$` in Kotlin, Dart and PHP and `#` in Ruby and Elixir against interpolation, the backtick, `$` and typographic quotes in PowerShell, `$'...'` in Bash, `@"..."` in Objective-C, other control characters by code (octal in Java, C and C++, where `\u` is read before the lexer), and every other character as it is. ReScript, Solidity and SQL have no row and get nothing. The implementer is told to paste the literal as the expected value, never to retype the text. R67 srtvet-update T005 compared a 1,264-character README section with backticks in Go, where a raw string cannot hold a backtick, and spent 1.64M tokens over four check rounds on the string's quoting.
- **Host apply (shadow).** Before a planned task's first implementer session, the host decides whether it would apply the request's verbatim content itself (`hostApplyDecision` in [host-apply](../../src/core/workflows/host-apply.ts), #2023). It reads each write's blocks with the same reader as supplied-lines pricing (`suppliedBlocks`), checks every diff with `git apply --check --recount -p1` (plus `--unidiff-zero` for a zero-context diff, see below) against the task's base in the run's root (worktree or working copy; no file changes), and records `host-apply/tasks/<task>.json` (kind `host-apply`, kept in bench traces): for each write its path and action, how many fenced files and diffs the request gives for it, whether a fenced file's prose says it is the whole file ("Replace `a.ts` with:", "the full contents of"), whether the path exists, and whether its hunks apply (with the last line of git's refusal; `eol: "crlf"` when they applied only with CRLF line ends, see below), then `wouldApply` and the reason when not. A task would be applied only when every write has exactly one kind of block, none deletes, a fenced file for an existing path is the whole file or a Markdown section, and every diff applies; a fenced block for an existing `.md`, `.mdx` or `.markdown` file whose first line is a heading, and whose prose does not say it is the whole file ("whole", "full", "entire", "complete", "overwrite", "replace README.md"; "exactly" and "verbatim" describe a section's text too), is that heading's section (#2101): the record keeps the heading's level and how many sections of the file share the heading (`region`, no title), and the host would write it in place of the one such section or append it, never when several share it (`applyRegion`); bench round 67 srtvet-update's README task (the section "## Cleaning spaces" given verbatim) cost an implementer 123k tokens; a task none of whose writes the request supplies records nothing. A fenced block next to a diff of the same file is context, not a fenced file, when every non-blank line of it is a line of that file's diff or the prose before it calls it the old or new line ("Old line:", "New line:", "the line", "Before:" / "After:"): the diff alone supplies the path, for the decision and for supplied-lines pricing alike (#2366); bench round 75 evalpin-update quoted the old and new line of `tests/cli.rs` after prose that named the file and said "Exactly", the host read the quote as the whole file and declined the task, and an implementer spent 49.7k tokens on a one-line edit. A block that is neither, such as a whole file, still conflicts with the diff and the task keeps its implementer. A fenced block named only by a path comment on its first line (`// internal/cli/cli.go`, `# tools/run.py`, `-- db/schema.sql`, `; src/core.lisp`), after prose that neither names the path nor says it is the whole file, is a listing of the file's shapes, not its content (#2438): the record marks the write `listing`, the host would not apply it ("may be a listing, not the file"), and supplied drift and the supplied test files leave it out; bench round 77 trailspin's brief listed each Go file's signatures that way under "Files (names and shapes are fixed; tests call them)", and the host wrote the six listings as the new files, which did not compile. The same block after "Write this file verbatim:" or "Create `internal/cli/cli.go` with:" is the file. A path comment on a block's first line names its file whatever the prose names (#2453): the block is that file's or, when the task does not write that file, nobody's, so the prose before it never moves it to another file; the comment may carry words after the path when the path has a directory or the words open with punctuation (`// src/frames.ts   (names and shapes are fixed)`, `# setup.py (entry point)`), and a bare file name (`# config.py`) still lets the prose say which directory ("Replace `src/config.py` with:"). Whether the prose says a block is the whole file is read from the clauses of its last line that name the block's path, or, when none does, from those that name no path, never from a clause about other files. Bench round 78 deltaglue's brief listed `src/cli.ts`'s shape (`// src/cli.ts`) after "Tests import `src/frames.ts`, `src/chunks.ts` and `src/assemble.ts` ... the whole stream": the host took the cli.ts listing as the whole of `src/frames.ts` and wrote it under `hostApply: on`, and lint failed on it. The plan approval, the record and the write read the blocks for the same paths, every write of the graph (`hostApplyPaths`), so a block the prose names with several planned files is no task's in all three, and the approval leaves a listing out as the decision does. This runs whether or not `suppliedDiff` prices the plan. It is shadow only: the implementer still runs, and `baked workflow status` lists the records under `hostApply`. The plan approval carries `hostApply` naming the tasks whose every write the request supplies ("Host-appliable from the request's verbatim content: T001, T002 (shadow: ...)"), shown by the TUI, the desktop run decision and the desktop Needs you detail; whether a diff applies is checked when the task starts. Bench round 65 spent 400k of eolscope-update's 580k tokens, 228k of seqmill-update's 276k and 170k plus an 81k replan of aclvet-update's 742k on such tasks. `replayHostApply` decides each record again on bench traces (counter `newApplyDecisions`). The record is made on every implementer path: one writer, a batch of several writers (in the task's worktree, on its base before an earlier attempt's edits are carried over) and contested candidates all run the pre-session shadow hooks through one helper, `beforeSession`, as does the corrective's `start` scope record (#2138; before it, bench round 68's three-writer runs recorded neither). `git apply --check` compares bytes, so an LF diff from a brief fails on a CRLF base git does not convert (a CRLF file committed as CRLF, or a working copy outside a repository whose files an editor on Windows wrote with CRLF); git already converts a `core.autocrlf=true` worktree to the repository's LF before it compares. When the base file has a CRLF line end and the LF check fails, the host checks the patch again with CRLF hunk lines (`crlfPatch`) and records `eol: "crlf"` when that applies (#2158). A diff with no context lines (`git diff -U0`, which update briefs use when a context line would carry a non-ASCII character) is applied with `--unidiff-zero` in the check, in host apply and in the supplied-content check (#2412; all three take their arguments from `applyArgs`): without it git pins a hunk with neither leading nor trailing context to both ends of the file, so a pure addition (`@@ -60,0 +61 @@`) landed after the file's last line while `--check` passed, and a replacing hunk was refused. Bench round 76 flatelens-update's two README additions were appended after the footer and reported as applied (a 96k-token corrective), and callmend-update's replacement fell back to an implementer (86k tokens). A diff whose every hunk has context keeps git's default arguments. A zero-context hunk is placed by its line numbers alone, so when the diff's `index <old>..<new>` line names a pre-image blob the base file must be that blob (a prefix of its SHA-1 or SHA-256 hash, of its bytes or of their LF form): otherwise the write records `applies: false` with a refusal naming both hashes and the implementer runs (`baseDrift`), and the supplied-content check is skipped with that reason. On a CRLF base a zero-context diff is tried in CRLF form first, since its pure additions compare no line and would otherwise add LF lines. With the policy field `hostApply: "on"` (setting `quality.hostApply`, default `off`; absent means shadow, so stored policies keep their hash) the host writes a planned task that `hostApplyDecision` would apply instead of starting its implementer (#2333): when the task starts (its first run; on the single-writer path, where such tasks are routed, never in a batch worker), it computes each write's final text with `hostApplyContents` (a whole file as given, a Markdown section through `applyRegion`, and the diffs applied with `git apply --recount -p1` to a scratch copy of the base file, CRLF hunk lines for a CRLF base, `core.autocrlf` off), writes the files through the run's journal with their pre-images as for a host-written task (`writeHostFiles`), and marks the task implemented: its task checks decide it, and a failing check opens the usual correction. The record then has `mode: "on"` and `applied` (`false` with `applyError` when the content could not be computed, and the implementer runs as before), `baked workflow status` shows both, and the plan approval says "Host-applied from the request's verbatim content". A task the decision keeps, or one whose request gives no verbatim block, runs its implementer. Bench rounds 73 and 74 recorded 12 `wouldApply` decisions whose delivered files matched the reference trees byte for byte and one correct refusal; the skipped sessions were 74 percent of idpeek-update's tokens, 42 percent of ewvet-update's and 29 percent of xmlvet-update's, and ewvet's README, where the model's text deviated from the request's (#2332), would have been exact.
- **Supplied content against the project's checks (shadow).** Right after the host apply record, before a planned task's first implementer session, the host runs the project's own static checks on the request's verbatim content for the task's code files (`suppliedCheckRecord` in [supplied-checks](../../src/core/workflows/supplied-checks.ts), #2275). It copies the task's base (the source manifest, as the documented-example runner does, `copyWorkspace`; a workspace over its copy limit is skipped), writes each supplied whole file in (a block for an existing file only when its prose says it is the whole file) and applies each supplied diff with `git apply --recount -p1` (host apply's `applyArgs`, so `--unidiff-zero` for a zero-context diff; with `core.autocrlf` off, so Git for Windows does not rewrite the copy's line ends; on a base with CRLF ends an LF diff that does not apply is tried again in host apply's CRLF form, `crlfPatch`, and line ends never count as changed lines), then runs each required feature-scope static check of the task (linters and formatters, never test checks) once in the copy. A whole-unit tool (`tsc`, `clippy`, `go vet`, ...) is named and not run, since it fails on files later tasks write. Only diagnostics on lines the supplied content sets count (any line of a whole file; for a diff, the lines it adds or changes), so debt the base already had elsewhere in the file is not the request's. It records `supplied-checks/tasks/<task>.json` (kind `supplied-check`, kept in bench traces): the supplied files with their line ranges, each check's status and its offenses as path, line and rule id (`Style/IfUnlessModifier`, `lint/style/useConst`, `F401`; no tool messages), why nothing ran when it did not, and `conflict` when any check reports an offense there: the request asks for both the verbatim content and a passing check and cannot get both. `suppliedCheckText` is the `spec_conflict` it would raise ("Supplied content fails the project's checks: rubocop at lib/group.rb:41 (Style/IfUnlessModifier). ..."). It is shadow only: the implementer still runs, and `baked workflow status` lists the records under `suppliedChecks` with that text. Bench round 72 namesound-update (Ruby) met the conflict inside T001: 858k tokens over 7 attempts and 24 rubocop runs, then a 103k review corrective; promoting the record to an analysis finding settled by the request conflict decision is a change of its own. A task that runs no session is checked the same way before the host writes or applies its files (#2456): a host-written task (every write carries the plan's content, `hostWritten`) with that content as whole files, and a task whose request blocks host apply writes with those blocks. A whole file in a folder the base does not have yet gets the folder in the copy. When a required check then fails on such a task and a task-level corrective would write its files with the content they already hold, the host records the `host_content_conflict` would-stop, naming the corrective, the files, the finding's first line and the fixer runs that failed on them: such a corrective cannot change the check (R78 pairwick cli: the plan's `Main.scala` failed scalafmt's parser, its fixer failed seven times, and host correctives T010 to T012 wrote it again until the feature blocked `no_progress`).
- **Planner guidance.** The planner gets `extra.sizing`: the per-file prices of code, documentation and manifests, the test-task quantiles and observed case capacity, the number of code files one attempt covers, and the instruction to put tests in tasks of their own and to give independent tasks disjoint writes and no dependencies (they run in parallel with `--worktree --writers N`). It also says how a rows task is sized (below). While a sizing re-plan is in flight, the analyzer gets the same `extra.sizing`, so a plan that quotes the note can be checked against it (#1930).
- **Test task order.** When the plan is accepted, a task that writes only test files gets a dependency on every code task that shares one of its criteria, unless one already reaches the other. A test task then runs after the code it tests instead of beside it.
- **Test split.** Before analysis, the runtime splits an oversized test task by file, then divides each oversized file into sequential slices of whole criteria. Test splitting and batching use observed case capacity, up to four times it while the price fits one attempt. A file with one to five known literal cases stays whole. Without observed capacity, splitting uses the fallback price midpoint; files without case data retain criterion-based grouping. This also applies to a task that writes only one test file. The first part keeps the original task ID and later parts get the next free IDs. File parts keep the criteria of their tested modules. An unmatched file keeps the criteria no matched file covers, or all criteria when none remain; when every file matched, unmatched criteria go to the first file. Within one file, the runtime groups criteria using their literal case counts, up to four times the observed case capacity within one attempt's price, or the fallback price midpoint against `sizing.maxAttemptTokens` when no capacity is known. The first slice keeps the original write action, later slices modify the same file and depend on the preceding slice. Every slice of a new test file waits for all slices updating existing test files from the same split, so suite checks see the updated expectations. New files remain independent of each other; a task that depended on the original waits for every part. Existing-test updates skip literal acceptance cases shared with any slice of a new file, while retaining cases assigned only to the existing file. Each slice retains the task's checks, names only its own criteria and preserves earlier tests. Its optional `sizingSlice` metadata records the original task ID, index, count and share of the per-file price. Each slice gets a whole-task quantile price when case-bearing history exists, otherwise its own default case allowance and share of the file allowance. If a criterion alone exceeds the capacity, it stays intact and the worker is told to write and check its cases in small batches inside the same attempt. Batching guidance names no extra check ID and uses the task's available checks. The analyzer receives `extra.hostAddedChecks`, which lists covering static checks attached after planning and derived `-written` and `-affected` test check IDs. Findings that only quote host batching sentences or object to these additions stay as notes, including when a plan decision declares empty `checkIds` or the derived check is absent from `executionContract.checks`. Reapplying the split replaces its previous host note instead of appending another copy. Planner-authored check requirements and findings about spec requirements or criteria remain blocking. This does not guarantee that an indivisible criterion finishes within one attempt. The split is recorded as `sizing/split-N.json`, and `sizing/plan-N.json` records each slice's count, price and cap. The TUI, headless CLI, local API and desktop use the same core tasks and sizing record.
- **Split.** A non-test task that writes several files and whose upper estimate is above `maxAttemptTokens`, or a test task above four times the observed case capacity, above it and priced over one attempt, or above its fallback allowance, or a run price above `maxRunTokens`, sends the plan back to the planner once with a split note. This holds for every workflow, v1 (`sdd`) and v2 (the quick, standard and other presets) alike; a v2 run whose `maxStageVisits` leaves no visit for the plan stage goes on with the plan as priced (#2036). A rows task (`rows` and `rowTest`) is never sliced, by the host or by the planner, and the per-task price is a finished task's tokens over all its continuations, so price alone never sends it back: it stays whole and its continuations finish it, and the report marks it with `rowsTask` and the criteria it holds without rows. Only above four times the observed case capacity (above five cases and the fallback price without one), and only when it has criteria without rows, does sizing send it back, and the reason names those criteria to move into a test task of their own that writes another test file and depends on it; the host then splits that task like any other. A host slice of one criterion is not sent back either: its worker writes and checks the cases in batches. Bench round 60, wheeltags explain: a 40-case rows task priced above the cap went between the planner and the analyzer five times, 1.03M tokens (#1930). Under `revision: "patch"` the split is a [revision by patch](#decisions-and-revisions): the planner gets the split note as a finding of kind `sizing` and splits the oversized tasks with edit operations on its last plan instead of writing a new one, and a patch that does not apply falls back to a new plan. A run price still above `maxRunTokens` after that continues within the ceiling under `clarification: "assume"`, recorded in `assumptions/sizing.json`; otherwise it waits for a clarification that suggests an initiative of smaller features; answering it lets the run go on within the ceiling, back at the approval step: the answer keeps the analysis verdict, because the question is about the plan size and not about the spec or the plan.
- **Steps and checks.** A task that writes only tests gets `sizing.testTaskSteps` steps (32 by default) per attempt instead of the profile's and the policy's `maxAgentSteps`; corrections use their base task's cap. With sizing on, a planned task that writes source code (`.js`, `.ts`, `.jsx`, `.tsx` and their module variants) must name the policy's task-scope typecheck check (the check `typecheck`, or one running `typecheck` or `tsc`) in its `checkIds`; otherwise the plan goes back to the planner at submission, so type errors show in the task that made them.
- **Caps and budget.** Each sized task's attempt cap is its upper estimate, at least the policy `maxAttemptTokens` (250,000 by default) and at most `sizing.maxAttemptTokens` (600,000 by default); a corrective task uses the cap of the task it corrects. A run price above `maxTotalTokens` raises the run budget to the price, at most `maxRunTokens`, with a `policy-revisions/sizing-N.json` record (`approvedBy: "sizing"`, not an operator decision). This happens before the approval request, so the approval and the auto-approval token bound see the sized budget, and also before a plan is sent back with a split note, so the re-plan does not run out of the base budget. Without `maxRunTokens` the budget is never raised automatically. The raised budget is the price plus a reserve for repair and convergence tasks, which the price does not include (`repairReserve` in `sizing/plan-N.json`); the reserve counts toward the budget, not toward the split decision. The reserve is a share of the priced work (planned tasks and verification). With at least three finished runs in this project's store and the `historyStores`, the share is the median of their loop costs: for each run, the tokens of corrective tasks and of every planned task, review or converge stage that ran again, divided by the tokens of their first pass; it stays between 30% and 300%. With fewer runs it is a flat 30%. `reserve` in `sizing/plan-N.json` gives the share, its basis (`history` or `default`) and the number of runs, and the budget reason names it.
- **Active time.** The active-time cap (`maxActiveMs`) grows together with the budget, under the same rule: only with `maxRunTokens`. Sizing estimates the active time the run needs as the tokens it expects to spend (the price plus the reserve, at most the sized budget) divided by the run's token rate: the median tokens per active minute of at least three earlier runs in this store and the `historyStores` (runs that are not created or running, with at least five active minutes and 100,000 tokens), or 60,000 tokens a minute until there are enough. A feature run of an initiative is sized at the initiative's own pace once two of its other feature runs have completed (with the same five-minute and 100,000-token floor): the median of their tokens per active minute, when that is slower than the history's (#2336). The history stays the floor a faster initiative does not go below, since a cap too short stops a run for a person's extend. Bench round 74 (ladlemath) ran at about 58k tokens a minute while the median of 262 runs of every project said 134k, and three feature runs stopped on their active deadline at half the time they needed. The record says `basis: "initiative"`, the rate and the number of runs, the initiative's own rates (`ownRates`, also when the history's pace was kept) and the history's pace it was preferred to (`history`); the approval names "the median of N finished runs of this initiative". The bench replay decides each recorded sizing report's cap again from these (`replayActiveTimes`). When the estimate plus a quarter is above `maxActiveMs`, the cap grows to it, in whole minutes and at most 24 hours, in the same `policy-revisions/sizing-N.json` record (`previous` and `next` name each limit that grew), and a run being driven gets the new deadline at once. `activeTime` in `sizing/plan-N.json` holds the rate, its basis and number of runs, the estimate and the cap before and after; the approval request carries it as `activeTime`, and the approval in the terminal and in the desktop app shows "Estimated active time at the observed rate: N min against maxActiveMs M min" with the raised cap. Without `maxRunTokens` the cap is not raised, and the approval says when the estimate is above it.
- **Planning cost.** The plan approval request carries `planCost` ([plan-cost](../../src/core/workflows/plan-cost.ts)): what the run spent so far by model, with the stages each model ran, and the planned tasks priced from the latest `sizing/plan-N.json` (its `taskTokens` low and high times a dollar rate per token). The tasks are priced like the cost forecast prices the task stage: at what this run's task attempts on the sized model paid per token so far (`rate: "run"`); without such an attempt, at what finished task attempts on the sized model paid per token in this project's earlier runs and the stores of `sizing.historyStores` (`history`), the history plan sizing reads its token rates from; without those, at the sized model's prices and the mix of uncached input, cache reads, cache writes and output that this run's task attempts of any model measured (`mix`); before any task ran, at a task loop's typical mix of 15 percent uncached input, 80 percent cache reads and 5 percent output (`default`), which the cost forecast also uses for a task stage without samples. Planning attempts never price the tasks: in bench round 51 Opus planned in one-step calls that never read the prompt cache, and their mix priced the Sonnet tasks, which read 91 percent of their input from it, at up to $16 for a run that cost $2.72 (#1712). In bench round 38 codex attempts read 70 to 80 percent of their tokens from the cache, and the estimate's default mix priced four plans at $19.2 to $36.1 for features that cost $11.82 in all. A plan waits for approval before its first task runs, so the typical mix priced every codex plan: codex task attempts of bench rounds 45 to 52 paid $0.81 per million tokens, 85 percent of them cache reads and 2 percent output, where that mix said $1.33. Replayed on the recorded approvals of rounds 46 to 52 with the round before as history, that mix priced 65 of 109 plans above what their tasks cost and 17 below; at what the earlier tasks paid, 36 above and 33 below, and the middle of the range is off by a factor of 1.78 on average instead of 2.14 (#1792). When the sizing took some task kind's token rate from other models' tasks, because the sized model has too few of its own, `implementation.otherModels` is true and the line adds "(token range from other models' tasks: this model has too few of its own, so it may be far off)". The approval in the terminal and in the desktop app shows "Planning so far about $X (model $Y, ...); the planned tasks about $A–$B on M", followed by "at this run's dollars per token so far", "at what earlier runs' tasks on it paid per token" or "at this run's mix of cached and uncached tokens" when a measured rate priced them. An attempt on a model without a known price is left out and marks the sum partial; the tasks are left out when sizing named no model or its model has no price.
- **Plan above the ceiling.** When the plan and its repair reserve need more than the budget can reach at the ceiling, `sizing/plan-N.json` has `overCeiling`: `needTokens`, `ceilingTokens`, `expectedTasks` (the tasks, in plan order, whose midpoint estimates fit what the ceiling leaves after the run's spending and verification), `totalTasks`, `extendToTokens` (how far the run may grow past the ceiling, or null) and `warning`, one line that is also among the reasons: "Plan and reserve need X tokens, ceiling is Y; the run is expected to stop at about N of M tasks", with "unless it grows on progress, up to Z" when it may grow. The approval request body carries the same line as `budgetWarning`, and the TUI approval, the desktop run decision and the desktop Needs you detail show it above the assumptions, so the person approving knows the plan is not expected to finish within the ceiling.
- **Growth while tasks finish.** A run whose policy has `maxRunTokens` does not stop on its token budget while it is making progress: when a model call would pass `maxTotalTokens`, at least one task is implemented or verified and the budget is below the ceiling, the budget grows to cover the call, by at least half again, never above `maxRunTokens`. Each growth is a `policy-revisions/auto-N.json` record (`kind: "auto-growth"`, `approvedBy: "sizing"`, the previous and next budget, the projected tokens and the finished tasks), so it is never mistaken for an operator decision. Before the first task exists the budget starts at least at the high end of the start estimate (`estimates/start.json`), up to the ceiling (`kind: "planning-floor"`), so planning and specifying do not stop below the doctor's own estimate. Until the first task finishes the budget also grows the same way, by at least half again and never above `maxRunTokens`, when a model attempt completed since the previous such growth (`kind: "planning-growth"`, with the count of completed attempts), so repeated specify, plan and analyze rounds do not stop the run with its ceiling unused; a planning phase whose attempts keep failing does not grow it. The same growth applies when a revise or a rejected tests-first task has invalidated every finished task, so the re-planning that follows does not stop with its ceiling unused either. A `budget_exhausted` stop before an attempt names the budget left, the budget and the sizing ceiling. The same check runs before an attempt starts: when the budget left does not cover the attempt's cap, the budget grows first, and when it still does not (the run is at its ceiling), the run stops with `budget_exhausted` before the attempt makes its first model call, instead of spending part of the attempt and failing on the run cap mid-loop. Without `maxRunTokens` an attempt starts with whatever is left and its cap shrinks to it, so it finalizes in time. Under `clarification: "assume"` a run may also grow past the ceiling, up to `sizing.extendToTokens`, or 1.5 times `maxRunTokens` when the policy does not set it: at the ceiling, a call or attempt the budget does not cover extends it by the call's need and at least a quarter of `maxRunTokens` (about one review round), provided a task finished or a finding was resolved since the previous extension (or since the run started). Finished tasks are counted from task events, so a corrective that finishes and invalidates tasks depending on it still counts as progress. An attempt that stopped at its cap with edits the task's next attempt continues (an interruption with cause `continue_at_cap`) counts as progress too, as does a restart, a widened correction or a lost worker's restart that carried the attempt's edits (an interruption with `carried: true`), so a run's last corrective that needs several attempts is not stopped between them with the cost cap unspent (md-site, bench round 38, blocked with $5 of its $13.2 cap left); each task gets a bounded number of such continuations, and the extension record counts them as `continuedAttempts`. An initiative's feature run grows, by any of these, no further than its token pool leaves after the other runs' use (`poolHoldTokens` on the record, #2468). Each extension is a `policy-revisions/auto-N.json` record with `kind: "auto-extend"`, the hard ceiling and the progress counts it saw; a run with no progress, or one at `extendToTokens`, stops with `budget_exhausted` as before.
