The split itself is refused when a feature contract makes work across features conditional ("if encoding needs ...", "any needed ...", "as needed" in a sentence naming another feature by its id or a file stem): the decomposer is told to state it as a requirement of the one feature that owns it, with the symbol and when it is required, or to remove it. With `workflows.upstreamAmendments: true` as well (off by default), such a gap is a request for an amendment of the upstream feature (#2157), asked once per approved split and answered by `amendmentAnswer`: an owner whose run finished, in an active or paused initiative, runs again from research (`replan` origin) with the request in its prompt (the requesting feature, each symbol, its file and the clause), and the planned-ahead feature stays held until that run is accepted. It is then released onto the amended result, or rebased (its approval goes to a person) when the owner's contract changed. Each request is a `feature.amendment_requested` event and shows on the owner as `amendments` (initiative view in the TUI, `baked workflow initiative show`, `/v1/initiatives/:id`, the desktop feature board). With `workflows.upstreamAmendments: "write"` (#2183), a gap of exactly one symbol in one file, which no other unfinished feature owns, is answered `write` instead: the owner does not run again; the planned-ahead feature's held run is cancelled and a fork of it starts again at its plan stage on the accepted result, released, with the file added to its own boundary (`initiativeBounds`) and to tiered approval's allowed paths, so a task of its new plan declares the write. Its spec stays. The request is recorded with `answer: "write"`, and a later run of the feature under the same split keeps the file in its boundary. A larger gap is still answered `replan`. Any other answer (`none`) releases the feature as before. Promoting `upstream_symbol` to enforce is not part of this.
- `baked workflow doctor` reports worktree runs that execute as `ok` (the working copy is free) and only the inline run's lease as busy.

## Pause, crash and recovery

A workspace has one writing run in its working copy (which may run several task writers in its own worktrees, see [several writers](#several-writers)), and worktree runs beside it up to `workflows.maxParallelRuns` (see [parallel runs](#parallel-runs)). Ordinary BAKED mutations use the same mutex; read-only tools stay available. An external editor is not governed by the lease, so content hashes are compared before and after checks and before acceptance. The Git baseline records HEAD, tracked files and the initial user changes; filesystem-only workspaces are supported too.

The TUI runs the workflow and SQLite transactions in a separate worker. The panel receives a consistent snapshot through asynchronous RPC; the SQLite busy timeout does not block the main interface thread. The execution time limit comes from the run's stored policy, including its revisions. A local RPC timeout does not stop observing an ongoing write.

After the worker exit is confirmed, the application creates a new session for reading state and recovery. It stores a receipt with the exact owner ID and the host PID. A live host PID alone does not mean that the exited worker keeps running. The receipt does not release the lease and does not allow repeating an unknown command: child process checks and recovery effects stay in place.

If the run's owner process has exited, `pause` and `cancel` release that run's leases when the owner is proven gone and the run has no command effect of unknown outcome whose process may still run. These are the same conditions under which `resume` would take the lease over. The release is recorded as the artifact `released-leases/ID.json`; afterwards, ordinary BAKED edits in the workspace are allowed again. A live owner or such a command keeps the lease.

Ctrl+C stores a pause and stops only the processes of that run. A second Ctrl+C forcibly ends the CLI; the next start requires recovery of unfinished effects. When the run starts again, an agent tool's edit (`edit_file`, `apply_fix` and the other file tools) that never reported back is settled from the file: it has no expected after-hash, because the tool formats the file after writing it, so a file that differs from its pre-image is recorded as applied (`interrupted-edits/EFFECT_ID.json`, the decision an operator makes with `record-applied`) and one that still has its pre-image as not applied. The interrupted task runs again from the file as it is. Only commands, deliveries and publications whose file matches neither recorded hash wait for a person (a process that died mid-edit during a laptop sleep used to wait too). The drive's periodic active-time write retries once on a transient SQLite error (`disk I/O error`, a busy or locked database) and otherwise leaves the interval for the next write, so a wake from sleep does not end the command with an uncaught exception. The other readers of the store that run on a timer or a poll do the same: the 100 ms check for a pause or cancel from another process, the headless CLI's event replay, and the local API's event streams skip a tick on such an error and read again on the next one. The 5 second lease heartbeat skips a renewal that fails that way as long as the lease (15 seconds) has not expired since the last good renewal; only a failure that lasts past that, or any other error, counts as a lost lease and stops the drive. A lost heartbeat does not allow taking the lease from a live PID, except from an owner that stopped renewing because its run made no progress (see [stalls](#stalls-deadlines-and-automatic-resume)). With an unknown command outcome, repetition is forbidden:

```sh
baked workflow recover RUN_ID --request ID --effect EFFECT_ID --resolution record-applied --file observed-result.txt --expected-revision N
baked workflow recover RUN_ID --request ID --effect EFFECT_ID --resolution retry --expected-revision N [--no-resume | --events [--after SEQ]]
```

Resolutions: `record-applied`, `record-not-applied`, `retry`, `cancel`. `--file` (what you checked) is optional for every resolution: without it the decision records a one-line note such as "Retry chosen without evidence: the command runs again at its stage." The desktop's recovery card, the terminal's recovery prompt (Enter on an empty note) and `POST /v1/workflow/runs/RUN_ID/recover` (no `evidence`) take it the same way (#2415). Like the other decisions, `recover` continues the run in the same command unless `--no-resume` is given, and takes `--events` and `--after`; `cancel` ends the run, so nothing resumes. `record-applied`, `record-not-applied` and `retry` settle the one effect and the run goes on; `cancel` **cancels the whole run** (the headless command prints a warning, and the terminal and desktop label it "Cancel the whole run"). When the stop is an edit whose outcome nobody observed, choose `record-not-applied` after checking that the change is not in the file. The edit journal settles most of these without a person: a publish that fails after the effect was prepared re-reads the file and records `failed` when it still has the old content (the other outcomes stay `unknown` for `reconcile`), and at acceptance an unknown file effect of a task attempt is recorded as `failed` and listed in the `superseded-effects/ID.json` artifact when the task is implemented or verified and a completed attempt of it started at or after the effect's attempt; the checks and reviews ran on that result. An effect nothing supersedes (a command, a delivery step, an edit of another stage) makes acceptance open the recovery request itself: the run waits (`interrupted_effect`) with the request and a note naming `record-not-applied`, instead of blocking with `no_progress` and no request. For a command, the end of its process must be confirmed first. A check's command (its intent names a `checkId`: a feature check, a task-end written-tests check or a formatter gate) whose process is gone runs again at its stage anyway, and its evidence is bound to the snapshot hash. Its recovery request says so in its note ("retry is safe and needs no evidence file"), and the `host-settle-interrupted-check` reason records, in shadow, that the host would settle it as not applied and continue: a `decision.would_resume` event per effect (see [stop reasons](#stop-reasons-and-shadow-mode)). The request stays with the person until that reason is promoted. A delivery step is never settled that way: a push must not be repeated. `retry` allows the next attempt, not an immediate replay of the previous shell command. With PID reuse the decision is conservative: a live PID blocks takeover. Do not delete the lease or the database to bypass recovery.

Before recovery or a lease takeover, the runtime checks the recorded command process and its descendants: on Windows, live processes whose parent is the recorded PID and that started after the command; on POSIX, the command's process group, and on Linux and macOS also any process that inherited the command's `BAKED_PROCESS_SCOPE` marker (Linux reads `/proc/<pid>/environ`, macOS runs `ps -E`), which finds descendants that left the group with `setsid`. Cancel and timeout signal the same set. A process table that cannot be read counts as running. On Windows, a descendant whose intermediate parent also exited cannot be linked to the command; confirm such cases by hand. Recovery `cancel` requires confirmation that all processes of the run and their descendants stopped. A known live command PID blocks this decision. The runtime stores an immutable `abandoned-effects` artifact, keeps the effect's original unknown status in history and releases only the previous leases of that run. Cancelling does not claim that the command did not change files.

Workspace and evidence files are written to a unique temp file and renamed into place. A failed write, including ENOSPC part-way through, removes the partial temp file, leaves the target unchanged and keeps the effect unknown until reconcile compares hashes. Interrupted task checks repeat before dependent tasks open. After a successful check, the runtime stores a gate bound to the generation, the snapshot and the task definition. Nested corrective tasks restore the whole chain of predecessors only after the required checks pass. The final `verified` is set during final acceptance.

### Files changed outside the run

Before each step of an approved run the runtime compares the workspace with the files it expects. The files it expects are the approval baseline, taken after the baseline checks and quality measurements run, so a file a check creates on its first run (`Cargo.lock` of a fresh `cargo init` crate, a `__pycache__/` that `.gitignore` does not cover, .NET `obj/` and `bin/`, Maven `target/`, Gradle `.gradle/` and `build/`) is part of the base, not a change outside the run; the `baselines/SNAPSHOT.json` artifact lists those files as `createdByChecks` (#1815: hexview stopped `input_changed` on its own `Cargo.lock` before anyone saw the plan). Toolchain cache directories (`__pycache__`, `.pytest_cache`, `.mypy_cache`, `.ruff_cache`, `.gradle`, `.kotlin`) are never part of the files the run expects, so a later check that writes the `.pyc` of a module a task added is not an outside change either. A file that differs and that the run's own edit journal does not explain stops the run as `blocked` with reason `input_changed`; the stop detail names the files (the first 10, then a count). Three ways out:

- Keep the changes: `baked workflow resume RUN_ID --expected-revision N --accept-workspace` (A "accept changes" in the terminal run panel, after a Y/N confirmation listing the files; **Accept changes** in the desktop run controls; `acceptWorkspace: true` on `POST /v1/workflow/runs/RUN_ID/resume`). The run takes the current content of those files as its baseline, records an `operator/accepted-workspace-ID.json` artifact (kind `recovery`) with each path and its hash before and after, and continues from where it stopped. Accepting is refused with `STALE_DECISION` when no file differs any more (a plain resume is enough then) or when one of the files changed again since it was listed.
- Restore the files and resume as usual.
- Fork the run, or cancel it.

The instruction and configuration files a run froze when it started (the project instructions such as `AGENTS.md`, `baked/constitution.md`, and the manifests `package.json`, the lockfiles, `tsconfig.json` and `biome.json`) stop the run the same way when a person edits them: `input_changed` with "Accepted input changed: PATH", and recovery advice that names the three ways out (#1654). Keeping the edit with `--accept-workspace` (or A in the terminal panel, **Accept changes** on the desktop) makes the run adopt the current instruction and configuration files, as a revision does; the confirmation lists them with the other changed files, and the operator artifact records them under `frozen` with their hashes before and after. A frozen skill, agent, workflow or gate file is not accepted this way: its advice is to restore it or revise the run's definition with `baked workflow candidate RUN_ID --workflow FILE`.

An attempt whose edits of one file were refused three times (the file differs from what the run recorded for it; see the edit check above) stops the run the same way, and the stop names the file and both choices. While the run's root still holds the refused content, a plain resume is refused with `STALE_DECISION` instead of starting another attempt that would be refused again: keep the file with `--accept-workspace`, or put back the content the run wrote and resume. A refused file in a task worktree does not hold the resume, because the resumed task gets a worktree rebuilt from the run's content.

Accepting covers the workspace only. A changed instruction file, gate or skill the run froze still needs a revised plan (see [decisions and revisions](#decisions-and-revisions)).

### Stalls, deadlines and automatic resume

Run active time, including each initiative feature's share, excludes host suspend and offline gaps. The runtime samples its execution clock every second. A gap longer than 15 seconds credits at most one second, whether the operating system's monotonic clock includes suspend or stops during it. The same clock controls the active deadline, model silence, progress-based lease renewal, tool deadlines, check and scanner timeouts, and acceptance and layout waits. A callback delivered on wake checks the remaining execution time before expiring. A run continues after wake without a budget extension; a genuine execution-time limit still stops it. Time while the driver is not running is not added on resume. A model call that has not answered is held out of the run's active time until it does. A call that ends with no output and no tool call is held out for its whole interval, even if its adapter reported activity first (a Codex reasoning item, a Claude Code content block). Such a call can end at the active deadline, a recovery-ladder or watchdog abort, or a cancel. The time it was charged while it looked answered is taken back from the run's `active_ms` (#2414). Bench round 76 hintsieve `patterns` was charged 555 s for such a call, which the deadline cancelled with 0 tokens. A call that answers stays charged, and its wait before the first response is recorded as `silentMs` on its call timing and summed on the attempt's usage record. Wait-stage deadlines remain wall-clock deadlines and keep their saved expiry across resume.

A run can stay alive and renew its lease while one call inside it never returns (benchmark round 8: an `edit_file` whose formatter child kept its output pipe open froze runs for 20 to 60 minutes after the file was written). Three bounds keep that from holding a run:

- **Tool deadlines.** Every tool call of an agent attempt runs under a deadline: file tools (`read`, `edit_file`, `multi_edit`, `undo_edit`, `ast_edit`, `structural_edit`, `delete_file`, `list_dir`, `grep`, `glob`, `navigate`) 2 minutes, every other tool (commands, git, analysis, MCP) 30 minutes on top of the limits those tools carry. A call that does not return is abandoned. If it was an edit and the file differs from its pre-image, the edit is recorded as applied from the file hash (the decision an operator makes with `record-applied`) and the model is told to re-read the file; if the file is unchanged the effect is recorded as failed and the model gets the deadline as the tool error. After `maxToolTimeouts` (3) deadlines in one attempt the attempt is stopped as stalled. The post-edit formatter (`auto-format.ts`, `project.ts formatFile`) kills the whole process tree on its own timeout (`taskkill /T /F` on Windows, the process group elsewhere) and reads its output pipes against the timer, so a surviving child no longer holds the edit. A format that runs out of its 30 s (the pre-image probe or the write) is said in the edit result (`rustfmt did not finish within 30 s on tests/json.rs, so the file was not formatted`; rename and move tools list `not formatted: rustfmt did not finish within 30 s` with the file's fixes), and the host records the file; after the second timeout on one file the edit tools stop formatting it for the rest of the session and say so once, since each try costs the whole deadline. A required check that later times out reads that record: a file the run wrote that the edit tools' formatter timed out on is named in its `timeout_slow` note without running the formatter on each written file again (#2242). Checks, fixers and on-demand gates, and the Git calls of a run and of delivery, stop reading a command's output 2 seconds after the command exited: a process it started that inherited the output pipes (on Windows a timeout kill does not reach it once its parent is gone) would otherwise keep the run waiting for as long as it runs. A check then keeps the output read so far, ends with the note `a process it started kept its output open`, and the processes it left behind are stopped where the platform can find them (the process group, and on Linux and macOS the scope marker).
- **Journaled tool calls.** `tool.started` and `tool.finished` events (attempt, tool, status `ok`, `error` or `deadline`, duration, `resultBytes`, the size of the JSON result handed back to the model, and for a failed call `error`, its message cut to 1000 characters) are part of the run's event log, so a stall can be attributed to the call it is in. Between them, `tool.phase` events (attempt, tool, `phase`) name the steps of an edit: `pre_image` (the guard keeps the file's content for `workflow undo`), `write`, `format`, `diagnostics` and `clone_hints`, written by `edit_file`, `multi_edit`, `ast_edit` and `rename_file`; a call that never returns names the step it stopped in and a stage's cost to the calls that fed its context. Host tools are journaled the same way as registry tools: a reviewer's `run_repro`, a repair's `run_probe` and the acceptance walk-through's commands, each under the 30-minute command deadline. The terminal view and the desktop timeline show only calls that hit their deadline and repair probes (`Probe ran`).
- **Step watchdog.** An attempt that journals no model step and no tool call for `stepStallMs` (20 minutes by default; the longest healthy silent step measured was a 569 second review) is aborted through its signal. With `adaptiveSilence` (on by default) the limit follows the run's own model calls once five of them have finished: four times the longest silence of a finished call (from the call's start to its first tool call or the end of its step), at least 10 minutes (half of `stepStallMs` when that is set below 20 minutes) and at most `stepStallMs`. A Codex call that stayed silent for 17 minutes after the Codex app replaced its binary, in a run whose healthy calls answered within 143 seconds, is stopped after 10 minutes instead of 20. The fault message names the limit and says when it came from the run's own calls. `adaptiveSilence: false` keeps the fixed `stepStallMs`. The file edits it left unsettled are decided from their hashes, the attempt ends with a `step_stalled` fault and the run blocks with reason `step_stalled`. The service then resumes the run itself, at most `autoResumes` (3) times, counted in `stallResumes` of the run state; each resume records `stalled/resume-N.json`. After the last one the run stays blocked for a person.

The lease follows progress. While a run's owner shows progress (an event, a model step, a tool call in flight) its heartbeat renews the lease. When the run is silent for 1.5 times `stepStallMs` the owner stops renewing and marks its identity as stalled; once the lease has been expired for 30 seconds another process (`baked workflow resume`, a second TUI, the desktop app) may take it over even though the owner's PID is alive. The new owner's epoch fences the old one: its next lease assertion fails and it stops writing. A command effect whose process may still run still blocks a takeover, and a live owner that keeps making progress is never taken over.

Every one of these bounds is a timer on the driving process's main thread, and so is the lease heartbeat. A synchronous call that never returns (a file a scanner holds on Windows, a native step that hangs) or a suspended process stops all of them at once: benchmark round 44 had a run sit `running` for 31 minutes after `tool.started multi_edit`, until an operator killed the driver and resumed it. Headless commands that drive a run (`baked workflow run`, `quick`, `resume`, `approve`, `answer`, `evidence`, `continue`, `accept`, and `extend`, `reroute` and `revise` with `--resume`) and an initiative (`baked workflow initiative start`, `approve`, `drive`, `resume`, `reroute`) therefore run in a driver process watched by a supervisor, the process that was started. The supervisor passes the driver's output through and exits with its exit code. Every 5 seconds it reads the driver's leases; when one has stayed expired for `supervisorGraceMs` (2 minutes by default) of the supervisor's own running time, the driver's thread has stopped. A gap in the supervisor's own polls means the host slept, and the wait starts over. The supervisor then stops the driver and the commands it started, journals `stalled/takeover-N.json` and an `errors/` diagnostic, blocks the run `step_stalled` and, within `autoResumes` (counted in `stallResumes` with the watchdog's resumes), resumes it and starts a new driver: `workflow resume RUN` with the same `--events` (after the last event printed) or `--json`, or `workflow initiative drive ID` for an initiative. Past `autoResumes` the run stays blocked for a person and the command exits with 4. A driver whose supervisor is gone stops. `supervisorGraceMs: 0` runs the commands without a supervisor. Scanners that run while a lease is held (Semgrep, ast-grep) and the edit tools' renames and syntax baselines run off the main thread, so they no longer hold it for their whole timeout. The terminal UI drives runs in its workflow worker, a thread of its own: the UI thread reads the worker's leases the same way and, when they stay expired for `supervisorGraceMs`, stops the worker. The next worker takes its runs over and drives the resumed ones, or the initiative they belong to. `baked serve` drives runs on its main thread, the one that answers the API. The desktop app checks a running server's `GET /v1/health` every 5 seconds; a server that leaves it unanswered for its `supervisorGraceMs` (reported in the health answer) is stopped and started again with `--take-over PID`, and the new server takes over the runs whose leases the stopped one held, then drives them. Stopping a worker or server can leave its commands running. On Windows the desktop first tries to stop the process tree, then stops the server itself if tree termination fails. A resume finds surviving commands as effects of unknown outcome and asks a person first (`interrupted_effect`).

The desktop app supplies the watcher for `baked serve`. A standalone server needs an external watcher to restart it with `--take-over PID` after its process stops.

The headless coordinator (`baked session coordinator send`) uses the same `WorkflowClient` stall watch and `takeOverDriver` recovery as the terminal UI. The CLI thread watches the leases of its workflow worker. After the same grace period it terminates that exact worker, waits for its close event, records its lease owner as exited and replaces the worker. Every running run interrupted by that worker's exit goes through the same bounded takeover, including initiative runs. Unknown command outcomes still require recovery; launch commands are never replayed. The coordinator keeps using the replacement for later launches and controls. `supervisorGraceMs: 0` disables this watch too.

`workflow status` (headless JSON, TUI overview, desktop run header) shows `stalled` for a running run that has journaled no event for longer than `stepStallMs`.

A call that produces no model output before its adapter's first-response deadline, or fails to connect before any output, enters `waiting` with reason `provider`. The deadline is 60 seconds unless the call may reason silently. Codex app-server and exec, including native-to-exec fallback, and Claude Code always can: they buffer reasoning until the answer finishes. Any other model can too at `high`, `xhigh` or `max` effort or on a specification or planning profile (`spec`, `plan`, `task-graph`), unless its reasoning streams: Anthropic's thinking does, and so does OpenAI's when `openaiReasoningSummary` is set. An OpenAI, GitHub Models, Copilot or other OpenAI-compatible reasoning model at `xhigh` effort streams nothing while it thinks, so the flat minute stopped it as unreachable while the provider was healthy (bench round 50, #1652). A call that may reason silently gets five minutes, seven and a half minutes at `high` effort, and ten minutes at `xhigh` or `max` effort or for specification and planning profiles. These are ceilings. In a workflow attempt the deadline of a call that may reason silently adapts once three calls of the same model, stage and effort have answered, in the workspace's recorded attempts (their `usage.json` `calls`) or earlier in the same process: twice the 95th percentile of their first-response times, at least three minutes and at most the ceiling. In bench round 52 a specify call that never answered waited the flat ten minutes on an initiative's critical path while the round's other specify calls answered in 66 to 166 seconds (#1766); with those answers recorded it is stopped after about five and a half minutes and retried. A call larger than the ones its shape answered gets more: each answer keeps the size of its prompt (`promptChars` in its `calls` entry), and the deadline is at least twice the shape's 95th-percentile time per prompt character times the call's own size, still at most the ceiling. A call that ran out an adapted deadline unanswered gives the calls of its shape the ceiling, until a call with a prompt at least as large answers: in bench round 69 (skillplumb) each retry attempt of a 142k-character plan call opened with a short call of the same shape that answered in about 8 s, which had put the large call back on the 180 s floor three times before a lower-effort shape answered it at 205 s (#2193). In bench round 66 an analysis of 86 derivations met the three-minute floor set by analyses of 34 to 56 derivations (47 to 70 seconds), took 182 to 185 seconds and was cut off five times, each retry under the same deadline: 15 minutes on an initiative's critical path (#2041). A call whose deadline adapted records it in its `calls` entry as `limitMs`, with what it was computed from as `deadlineInputs`: how many answers it took (`samples`), how many of them came from the shared file below (`shared`), their 95th-percentile time (`p95Ms`) and time per prompt character (`paceSamples`, `p95PaceMsPerChar`), and `expired` when the shape had run out an adapted deadline. The replay of a recorded run decides the call again from these, because the shared answers and the history a process started with are not in the run's database (bench round 73, flatelens: a 194 s answer under a recorded 290,666 ms deadline replayed against 180,000 ms, #2283). A fresh workspace has none of its own answers, so its first calls of a shape get the ceiling. With the run policy `sharedFirstByteHistory: "on"`, a shape that has fewer than three answers in the workspace is completed from the answers of every workspace on this machine that ran with the setting on. Those answers are kept in `first-byte-history.json` in the BAKED data directory (`~/.baked` on macOS and Linux, `%LOCALAPPDATA%\baked` on Windows), up to 50 per shape, and each answered call of such a run is added to the file. Once the workspace has three answers of its own, only those count. In bench round 58 the first specify calls of a fresh workspace waited the ten-minute ceiling each, 33 minutes before the first block, while the round's other workspaces answered their spec calls in 4 to 126 seconds (#1880). Off by default. The deadline uses the effort sent to the provider, including the configured default. Health probes use the same adapter rule with no stage or effort override. Reasoning deltas and tool arguments count as model output; local stream startup and CLI handshakes do not. Buffered CLI adapters report model traffic when available before their final answer. Only unanswered calls that may reason silently bypass the stall watchdog until their first response. After model activity, or throughout calls to streaming adapters, the watchdog enforces the run's adaptive silence limit. Continuations, compacted retries, reviews and format correction share this guard.

Adapters that explicitly reject streaming use generation with the same deadline and abort signal. Without an earlier traffic notification, their completed response counts as the first output. Messages-compatible Anthropic endpoints may return a complete JSON message to a streaming request; the adapter reads that response without sending a second request. An incomplete event stream still fails the provider health check.

The unanswered interval is provisional. A response charges it as active work; a connection failure or first-response timeout excludes it. The active deadline and periodic `active_ms` writes use that same accounting, on top of the shared clock's host-suspend exclusions. Healthy work in a parallel attempt still counts. Existing edits and completed model steps retain their usage.

While waiting, the service sends a fresh health request with no tools or workflow context and an output cap of 16 tokens. A probe has a 60-second deadline. Failed probes back off for 1, 2, 4 seconds and so on, up to five minutes. They spend neither `stallResumes` nor `providerResumes`, and their wait is outside the active deadline. `provider/wait.json` records the model, probe count and next probe time; driving a saved provider wait continues those probes. A successful probe records `provider/healthy.json` and resumes the interrupted stage. A probe the provider refuses as a request (HTTP 400 or 422, an `invalid_request_error`) is a defect of the probe, not news about the provider: the stage resumes the same way and `provider/healthy.json` records the refusal as `probeRejected`, instead of the run blocking `provider_unavailable` ([#1889](https://github.com/Gulliby-development/baked/issues/1889)). Pause, cancel and shutdown stop probing. A final account refusal blocks with `provider_account`.

The run snapshot includes `providerWait: {modelId, probe, retryAt}` while it waits. `baked workflow status` prints it, `/v1/workflow/runs/ID` returns it, and the TUI and desktop run header show the provider wait and next health probe. Recovery is automatic until the same stage, profile, task and model fail three consecutive attempts with recorded usage of zero tokens in which no model call answered. An attempt whose usage `calls` show an answered call followed by one that got no response is a stalled follow-up, not an outage: Codex app-server reports a step's usage only once its tool call is answered, so such an attempt also has zero tokens. Three consecutive stalled follow-ups stop the run as well, and the diagnostic says what happened (`answered 1 call(s) of spec-author, then gave no response within 216 s on call 2, in 3 consecutive attempts`) instead of claiming that no tokens came back (bench round 58, #1875). Successful health probes do not reset either count. The run then blocks with `no_progress` and a diagnostic asking you to check the provider and reasoning effort, then resume or reroute. Its durable circuit marker prevents another driver from restarting it without an explicit resume. Resuming a run the circuit stopped starts every counter of the run from zero, so the resumed stage gets three attempts again; in round 58 each resume got one, since the counter stayed at three. The host's own resumes after a provider wait keep counting. A successful attempt, an attempt with token usage or an unrelated failure resets the counter. Unknown usage does not count as zero. Counters belong to the frozen inputs and survive driver restarts. Ordinary outages whose probes keep failing remain in provider wait without spending additional stage attempts.

A stalled call is different from an outage: the provider got the request and never answered it (no first byte before the call's deadline, or a stream that ended without a finish), while it may answer other requests. When an attempt that changed no files stalls, the stage does not wait for a health probe. It tries again at once, one rung further up a recovery ladder, and each rung keeps what the rungs before it changed:

1. `fresh-session`: every step of the attempt starts a new Codex thread with the whole conversation, instead of resuming a thread that waits at a tool call.
2. `other-transport`: the attempt runs on `codex exec` instead of the Codex app-server.
3. `lower-effort`: the route's reasoning effort one step lower.
4. `next-model`: the next model of the route, preferring one on another provider, at that model's own effort and transport.

A rung that changes nothing for the call is skipped: rungs 1 and 2 apply only to Codex over its app-server, rung 3 only to a route with an effort above `low`, rung 4 only to a route with a fallback model or a derived one.

A checking call (analyze, review, approval review, critic, refutation, convergence) whose route names no later model gets a derived one for rung 4: a model of the stalled model's provider that the run's routing sends another call to and that completed an attempt in this workspace in the last 10 minutes, the most recent first, never a model the implementation uses (a review moved there would not be independent). When such a sibling exists, rung 4 comes before rung 3, since a lower effort cannot help a model that does not answer. The model runs at the stage's route effort on its own transport. Each stalled checking call records the decision with its inputs as `provider/fallback/<attempt>.json` (`routed`, `excluded`, `answeredSecondsAgo`, `siblings`), and the stop text names a sibling that answered. Implementation and authoring calls keep their route's chain. In bench round 72 codex/gpt-6.1-sol, the analyzer and reviewer, stalled for 25 minutes in three runs at once while gpt-6-luna and gpt-6-astra answered; each run walked four rungs of sol over 19 minutes and stopped for a person, and the loop's plain resume later completed all three ([#2271](https://github.com/Gulliby-development/baked/issues/2271)). A route with no rung to try keeps the provider wait above. Each rung's call has its own first-response deadline, so the ladder costs a few deadlines instead of resumes. Every step up is an `attempt.recovery` event (`rung`, `modelId`, `transport`, `effort`, and a `detail` with the stall and the next rung), shown in the activity feed of the TUI run view and the desktop run screen and in the `--events` stream of `baked workflow run`, `resume` and `/v1/workflow/runs/ID/events`. The ladder's position is saved per stage, profile, task, run input and route (`recovery/ladder-<hash>.json`), so a driver that restarts mid-ladder continues on the rung it was on, and a completed attempt clears it. When the last rung stalls too, the run blocks with `no_progress` behind the same circuit marker as above, and the diagnostic lists every rung with its model and how long it waited, and the ladder's total time ("19 min in all"); a resume starts a new ladder, a reroute a new one on the new models. Such a stop is parked as `provider_stall` in shadow: `provider/stall-park.json` records the automatic resume the park would take (`decision.would_resume`, after 10, then 20, then 20 minutes, with `tries` counting parks in a row without a completed attempt; the fourth leaves the run to a person) while a person still resumes. `baked workflow status` and the run view of `/v1/workflow/runs/ID` return it as `stallPark` while the circuit holds the run, and `replayStallLadder` decides it again on the corpus. Its promotion to a real resume timer is a change of its own after bench rounds show the would-resumes were right. Bench round 58 (wheeltags) lost 97 minutes, 3 resumes and a reroute to repeated stalls of one specify call on Codex native, while `codex exec` finished it in 126 s ([#1877](https://github.com/Gulliby-development/baked/issues/1877)).

A turn that finishes without an answer BAKED can use climbs the same ladder: Codex exec ending with no final agent message, a `tool-calls` finish with no tool call from either CLI, or an app-server reply missing its thread. The provider answered and billed it, so it is not an outage to wait out, and the same input on the same settings ends the same way. Its usage is charged to the attempt (estimated from the prompt at about 4 bytes a token when the turn reported none), its time stays off the active deadline when nothing reached BAKED before the answer was lost, and the attempt's `usage.json` keeps a `lostAnswer` record: the error, the prompt's size, the event types the stream carried, the lines read and those that did not parse, with up to three samples. When no rung is left, also on a route that has none, the run blocks with `no_progress` behind the circuit marker instead of `provider_unavailable`, so its automatic resumes are not spent on the same input; the diagnostic lists the rungs, the attempt's input hash and any invisible or line-separator characters the stage input holds (U+2028, U+FEFF and the like). In bench round 62 one analyze stage lost six exec answers this way and was filed as a provider outage at 0 tokens for 71 minutes, with three resumes on the same input ([#1957](https://github.com/Gulliby-development/baked/issues/1957)).

Other transient provider failures get the existing bounded recovery. When a model call keeps failing through its in-call retries and the route's fallback models (for example, overload or capacity errors) and the attempt changed no files, the run blocks with reason `provider_unavailable`. The service then resumes it by itself after a wait of `providerWaitMs` (1 minute by default), then three times as long each time (1, 3 and 9 minutes), at most `providerResumes` (3) times per outage, counted in `providerResumes` of the run state. Once a model attempt has completed after the last resume, the provider came back, so the next outage starts again from the first, shortest wait with all its resumes. Each wait records `provider/resume-N.json` (`attempt`, `of`, `waitMs`, `retryAt`, `completedAttempts`) before it starts, and while it runs the snapshot carries `autoRetry`: `{at, attempt, of}`; `workflow status` prints it, the TUI shows "Retrying by itself at ..." and the desktop run header "retrying by itself at ...". A person may resume, pause or cancel the run during the wait; the automatic resume then does nothing. An account problem (`provider_account`: a missing key, a sign-in, a spent quota, a subscription usage limit, or a Codex or Claude Code CLI that is not found) is not retried. After the last automatic resume the run stays blocked for a person, who can retry or move it to another model.

Configuration (milliseconds, all optional):

```json
{ "workflows": { "stall": { "toolTimeoutMs": 120000, "commandToolMs": 1800000, "stepStallMs": 1200000, "adaptiveSilence": true, "maxToolTimeouts": 3, "autoResumes": 3, "providerResumes": 3, "providerWaitMs": 60000, "supervisorGraceMs": 120000 } } }
```

`autoResumes: 0` turns the automatic resume after a stall off, `providerResumes: 0` the bounded resume after other provider failures. Unreachable-provider health waits continue until recovery or an operator stops the run. Set `stepStallMs` above the longest model call of the models you route to.

### Stopped convergence

Convergence repairs the findings of its final review for at most `maxConvergenceRounds` rounds (and `maxCorrectiveTasks` corrective tasks). When it cannot start another round, the run no longer ends as `blocked` with `no_progress`: it moves to `waiting` (reason `no_progress`) with a durable request of kind `convergence`. The request body (`topic: "convergence"`) holds the `reason`, a one-line `summary`, `checksPassing` (every required check passes on the current snapshot), the `criteria` (total, passing, and the failing ones with their verdict), the open `findings` (ID, severity, kind, criteria, file, symbol, claim, impact, suggested fix) and the `contradictions` the findings ledger found. The reasons are:

- `rounds`: the convergence rounds of the policy are used;
- `tasks`: the corrective tasks of the policy are used;
- `repeat`: the last repair left the same findings against the same criteria;
- `contradiction`: a new finding repeats one that a correction of the run already addressed. The convergence agent is given those earlier findings (`earlierFindings`, with their file, symbol, criteria and claim) and sets `repeatOf` on every finding: the id of the earlier finding when it is the same defect, even if the file, symbol or wording changed or the earlier fix was partial, and `null` for a different defect, even in the same file or function. `repeatOf` is the key of the findings ledger. A finding without the field (a reviewer that did not say) falls back to the location: the same file, a symbol sharing a word with the earlier one (or none on both, with the same kind) and a shared criterion. The second occurrence of a defect is a residual: another repair would likely undo the first one or chase the same edge case again, so a decision is requested. This stop can come before the round limit; it is listed with the finding it repeats and how the repeat was recognized (`repeatOf` or `location`). A finding that carries a reproduction the host ran, with a command that differs from the earlier finding's reproduction (another command or input, such as `tag --help` after `add --help` was repaired), is a new case of the defect, not a repeat: it is repaired in the next round, even when it names the earlier finding. The corrective task of a review or convergence finding is also told to look for the same defect in sibling code paths (other commands, options, branches or callers with the same pattern) and fix every case it finds, so one repair covers them. A corrective task starts focused on the finding's file, the files its claim or suggested fix names and, for a repeat (`repeatOf`), the files the correction of the earlier finding changed, so it does not load every file it may write (md-site T075 corrected a repeat with no focus file, loaded all ten files it could write and spent 2.46M tokens on a 710-byte fix). The corrective of a repeat also gets the patch of the correction that fixed the earlier finding (`correction` under that finding in `recurringFindings`, up to 8,000 characters), and its last step names the repeat and asks for the class of input both findings belong to (for example every control or whitespace character before a URL scheme, not only a tab), fixed where the earlier correction changed the code, with one table-driven test row per case, instead of the general sibling step. With `chainRewrite: "on"` a repeat that makes a chain of two findings on one symbol gets a rewrite of the symbol against the chain's battery of repros instead ([Finding chains](#finding-chains)). With the policy field `classSweep: true` (absent means off, so stored policies keep their hash; set it in `workflows.policy` of `.baked/config.json` or a policy file) a review or convergence finding about input robustness (deep nesting, huge numbers, long input, invalid encoding or empty input, recognised from its claim, impact and reproduction) gets a class sweep instead ([class-sweep](../../src/core/verification/class-sweep.ts)): the robustness findings of one pass become one corrective task per profile, which may write the planned source files of its profile in the same top-level directory as a reported file that are tied to it: files that import a reported file, files a reported file imports, and files the text of the findings' criteria or requirements names (at most 5, tests excluded). It starts focused on those files and is told to run each reported shape against the public functions of every one of them with `run_probe` and the other shapes of the battery only against the reported file, fix each failing case and add a regression test per entry point it fixes. An earlier version listed every planned source file of the directory (up to 12) against the whole battery: cfgtrace's sweep came to about 60 probes in one task and cost 3.96M tokens over three attempts. A class a reviewer would otherwise find one site per round (cfgtrace: the CLI, the library API, the TOML parser, the JSON parser) is repaired in one round. The same field sweeps test style: a review or convergence finding on a test file whose claim or impact is about how the tests are written (inputs built by loops or generators instead of static fixtures, hard-coded values, cases the criteria name left out, an output not checked exactly) becomes one corrective task per profile that may write every planned test file of its profile in the same directory with the same extension, starts focused on them, and is told to fix the same defect in each and make every case the criteria name appear with its exact output and exit code (aclvet: the loop-built fixtures of `check_test.go` and then of `diff_test.go`).

The person decides, on every surface:

| Decision | TUI key | CLI | API | Effect |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Accept | `A` | `baked workflow accept RUN_ID --request ID --expected-revision N` | `POST .../accept` | The findings become known limitations: they are closed, the criteria they name may fail at acceptance, and the run goes on as if convergence had passed. The run report lists them under "Known limitations" and `completion.json` carries them (`limitations`), so the delivery pull request shows them. |
| One more round | `C` | `baked workflow continue RUN_ID --request ID --expected-revision N --resume` | `POST .../continue` | The limit that stopped the run is raised just enough for these findings and they are repaired now. The findings a person decided to repair are not asked about again. |
| Stop | `X` | `baked workflow reject RUN_ID --request ID --reason TEXT --expected-revision N` | `POST .../reject` | The run is `paused`; resuming reviews again and asks again if the findings remain. |

`accept` continues the run after the decision like `approve`, `answer` and `evidence`; pass `--no-resume` to only record it. `continue` on a stopped convergence resumes the same way (`--no-resume` only records it); `continue` on a token checkpoint resumes only with `--resume`. In the desktop app the run's decision card has "Accept as known limitations", "One more round" and "Stop here".

With `clarification: "assume"` the host applies a decision policy to a stopped convergence and shows it in the request body (`hostRule`: `decision` `accept`, `extend` or `ask`, the three `rules`, and `why` for this stop; the terminal and desktop decision views print it):

1. **Accept** when every required check passes, at most 2 distinct findings remain (findings that repeat one cause through `repeatOf` count once; the share of failing criteria is not the measure, because one defect in a shared function fails 2 or 3 criteria at once), none is critical, and none is high with a reproduction the host ran (a high finding that rests on a static trace is accepted). The request is recorded and accepted at once (decided by `auto-approval`), the findings are listed as known limitations decided by the assume rule, and the run goes on.
2. **Extend** on a `contradiction` stop, once per run (`autoExtensions` in the run state): the stop is recorded as an automatic "one more round", the limits that stopped it are raised just enough and the findings are repaired now, because a repeat is often an incomplete earlier fix. If the next stop is for the same or another reason, rule 1 decides.
3. **Ask** otherwise, with `why` naming what stopped the host (a failing check, the number of distinct findings, a critical or reproduced high finding).

A run with no required check still waits for a person.

## TUI

`/workflow` without arguments opens the panel with the project's run list. Runs waiting for a user decision come first, then active runs, then finished ones. ↑/↓ select a run, Enter opens it, N opens the new-run form, I opens the [initiatives](#initiatives-in-the-tui), G the gates window, R the [routing screen](#routing-screen), U the [usage screen](#usage-screen), W the [workflows screen](#workflows-screen) and Esc closes the panel. `/workflow new TEXT` opens the form with the description filled in; `/workflow quick TEXT` does the same with the quick workflow selected. `/workflow edit ID` opens the [workflow editor](#workflow-editor). `/workflow routing` opens the [routing screen](#routing-screen), `/workflow usage` the [usage screen](#usage-screen) and `/workflow definitions` the [workflows screen](#workflows-screen). `/workflow report RUN_ID` prints the [run report](#run-report) in the chat. `/workflow help` prints the full command list; the other CLI commands (`run`, `status`, `approve`, `recover` and so on) are still accepted, and their result is printed in the chat as a short text.

If a configuration turned workflows off (`workflows.enabled: false`), the panel shows what will be written, and E turns them back on: an existing project block only gets `enabled: true`; when the global config turned them off, a `workflows` block with the default policy and checks found in the `package.json` scripts (`typecheck`, `lint`, `test`) for the project's runner is added to `.baked/config.json`. While the project is off, the panel starts no worker and creates no database.

The new-run form contains the description, an optional GitHub issue ([starting from a GitHub issue](#starting-from-a-github-issue); with one, the description is optional and adds instructions, and an untouched feature ID is named after the issue), the feature ID (derived from the description; Cyrillic text is transliterated), the workflow (`sdd`, the nine presets and `baked/workflows/*.json`, with the stages of the chosen one and "no file will change" for a workflow that only reads), the budget `quick`/`standard`/`extended`/`checkpoints` (×0.5/×1/×2 of the configured policy's token, call and time limits, or of the built-in default policy when the config sets none; `checkpoints` has no token cap and asks every N tokens), an optional cost cap in US dollars (empty keeps the policy's or the setting's cap, shown as the placeholder; see [cost cap](#cost-cap)), an estimate of the spend labeled as an estimate, the execution mode, the writer count, what blocking questions do (Questions: `ask` waits for your answers, `assume` lets the agents settle them with recorded assumptions; ←/→ or Space, starting at the policy's `clarification`), what the [test-integrity guard](#test-integrity) does (Tests: `block` or `warn`, starting at the policy's `testIntegrity`), the current chat tab's model and the configured checks. Ctrl+S starts from any field.

Mode (←/→ or Space) switches between `inline` (the run edits the working copy) and `worktree` ([worktree execution](#worktree-execution)); it starts at the chosen workflow's own `execution`, otherwise the policy's `executionMode`, and switching the workflow resets it. Writers (←/→, 1..4) is available only in worktree mode and is skipped by Tab otherwise; it starts at the policy's `maxConcurrentWriters`. The form sends exactly what `baked workflow run --worktree --writers N` sends: worktree mode sends the mode and the writer count, inline mode sends only the mode, so the policy's writer count applies and the core refuses a policy with more than one writer for an inline run. A definition that refuses worktree execution (review only, whose input is the working copy's diff) shows the core's refusal on the Mode line and below the form, and the run does not start until Mode is back to inline. Other refusals (no Git repository, a policy problem) come from the core when the run starts and are shown under the form. The run executes in the worker in the background; the panel switches to it immediately.

The worker reports new events of its runs to the panel immediately; the panel reads changes from other processes and active time by polling every 150 ms, and a poll without changes does not re-read the view.

The status JSON of a run (`baked workflow status`, `start` and `resume` output, `GET /v1/workflow/runs/RUN_ID`) has `limitations: {count, ids}`, the findings the run is accepted with; a completed run with `count` above 0 keeps exit code 0, and the terminal run screen and the desktop run header show the count. The run screen shows what is happening and what can be done, the stage line (a glyph per stage, ×N for a stage on its Nth round, and the current stage in brackets with the contract it is making, such as `[▶ Plan → plan]`; the desktop shows the same line as a conveyor), under it the gates of the current stage (or, once the run has ended, of the last stage that has gates) from the run's own definition, one per line with a result glyph, whether it blocks or only advises, its last result in this run (from `gate.finished`, or the check of the same ID for an `sdd` check stage; `not run yet` before that, `after N attempts` and `flaky` when they apply), and on a second line what it does and where it stands (at this stage, or before the line moves on or goes back), budget consumption (tokens used, held by attempts with unknown usage and in flight), a summary of tasks/criteria/checks/findings, the last model, the last recorded error and the event feed. When a budget blocks the run, the panel names the limit and the remaining amount. The action line contains only the keys valid in the current state: R start/resume/retry, A accept the files changed outside a run blocked with `input_changed` ([files changed outside the run](#files-changed-outside-the-run)), P pause (also for a run marked running without a live executor), C cancel with confirmation, B raise the limits of a run blocked on its budget, M switch a stopped run (created, paused, blocked or waiting) to the chat tab's model when it differs from the run model (pick it with Ctrl+L first; after a Y/N confirmation the run is [rerouted](#switching-a-run-to-another-model) and a blocked or paused run resumes), F fork for blocked and finished runs (when the workflow has stages after the implementation, ←→ picks where the fork starts, from the beginning or one of `forkStages`, and Y forks; the panel then opens the new run), D discard the worktree of a finished run while it is still on disk (after a Y/N confirmation naming the worktree and branch; the same as `workflow discard-worktree`). U undoes the run's uncommitted task edits (the same as `workflow undo` without `--path`) after a Y/N confirmation; it is offered while the run is neither created nor running. The panel then shows the `workspace-rollback` report: each restored file (restored, removed when the run created it, recreated when the run deleted it) and each file left in place with the reason, or a note that there was nothing to undo. Esc hides the report. Tab/Shift+Tab switch between overview, decision, tasks, changes, spec, plan, checks, criteria and findings. Changes lists the files the run changed according to the effect journal: created/modified/deleted, the number of edits, an unfinished status and a mark when a file was changed outside the run afterwards. ↑/↓ and Enter show the diff of the selected file against Git HEAD; the diff is available only for files in this run's journal. If the file had uncommitted changes in the baseline, the diff includes them and the panel warns about it. Without Git only the facts of the changes are shown: the journal stores hashes, and the pre-images it keeps are used only by undo. Checks shows the latest result of each check: duration, exit code, number of runs, stale evidence and the last 40 output lines of a failing check, as well as configured checks that have not run yet. When a new request appears, the panel opens decision by itself. Approval shows the criteria, the assumptions the agents made instead of asking, the files in scope, the checks and deferred criteria: A accepts the digest, X requires a rejection reason. Clarification shows the current question in full with its answer field right below it, then the references and a compact list of all questions with blocking ones marked; ↑/↓ switch questions without losing answers, Enter saves and moves on, Ctrl+S sends, and sending with an unanswered blocking question asks for a second confirmation. Questions come from research unknowns, spec open questions or analysis findings; each answer is keyed by its question ID and text. For manual evidence, Ctrl+T toggles the verdict. Recovery chooses the outcome with the keys 1..4 and requires a description of the observation. A stopped convergence shows its findings, the failing criteria and any contradiction: A accepts the findings as known limitations, C repairs once more, X stops the run. Esc returns to the list. Shift+PageUp/PageDown scrolls the content.

The status JSON also has `reviewNotes: {count, ids}`, the run's review notes (the run screen shows `Review notes N`, the desktop header `N review notes`).

While the panel is closed, a line for the selected run is shown above the footer: stage and tokens, a pending decision or the outcome. The indicator polls only an already open worker and disappears after the outcome has been seen in the panel.

A new `/workflow run` uses the current chat tab's model selected through Ctrl+L, even if the worker was opened before the model was selected. An explicit `--model provider/model` sets the model for that run only and takes precedence over the chat selection. The CLI without `--model` uses the configured defaultModel. The models of existing runs are frozen in their routing record; switching the model in the chat does not change them on resume, but M in the run screen moves a stopped run to it.

Closing the panel keeps the run in the current application process. Pause the run before closing its chat tab. Shutdown stops the workflow before shared resources are released. Session restore restores the run ID and observation; the model is not started automatically.

### Initiatives in the TUI

I on the run list opens the project's [initiatives](#initiatives). Each row shows the initiative ID, its status, accepted mandatory features of all mandatory features, open decisions and blockers, and shared tokens used of the limit; initiatives that need a person (a decision, a blocker or a pause) come first, finished ones last. ↑/↓ and Enter open one, N opens the start form, and Esc returns to the run list.

The start form takes the large request, the initiative ID (filled from the request until you edit it; lower case letters, digits and `-`), the default workflow for features that name none (←/→ over the project's workflows), the shared budget as `TOKENS CALLS TIME` such as `8M 400 4h` (empty derives it from the policy and the split) with an optional dollar cap as `$N`, such as `8M 400 4h $40` or just `$40`, tiered approval (off, or specs up to 5 tasks and 10 files approved for you), Forecast (report, or stop while the forecast is over the budget) and Features (merge small siblings, or keep the split as proposed). Feature runs use the chat tab's model. Ctrl+S, or Enter on Start, starts it like `baked workflow initiative start` without `--plan`: the decomposer proposes the split in the background and the screen opens the new initiative.

The detail shows the title, the current plan revision and whether it is approved, the feature workflow, the shared budget (tokens, calls and active time used of the limits), the next action in words, a pending split approval with its plan revision and digest prefix, the initiative's blockers, and the features in dependency order with their state, workflow, run status, run ID and upstream features. The selected feature also shows its contract, its workflow, size and starting budget share, and either its pending decision or its blockers. Enter opens the selected feature's run in the run screen, O opens the decompose run, and Esc on that run returns to the initiative. T [estimates the split](#initiatives): a line under the budget gives its level, tasks, tokens and the figure to plan for, and the selected feature its own token range.

The keys follow the commands the core accepts in the current state: A approves the pending split (after a Y/N confirmation; it is bound to the revision and digest on screen, so a changed split is refused with the core's message), P pauses an active or decomposing initiative, R resumes a paused one, C continues (`drive`) an active or decomposing initiative that this process is not already driving, N rejects the pending split with a reason you type, X re-plans the selected feature (after a Y/N confirmation; it supersedes the feature's current run, and is not offered while that run executes), B raises the shared budget (type `TOKENS [CALLS [TIME]] [$DOLLARS]`, such as `8M 400 4h` or `$40`; `-` keeps a limit, and limits only go up), and M [switches the initiative](#initiatives) to the chat tab's model when it differs from the initiative's model and no feature run is executing (pick it with Ctrl+L first; after a Y/N confirmation the stopped runs move to it and the initiative continues, as `reroute --resume`). The budget line shows the initiative's dollars (spent of the cap, its ceiling with a tolerance, what is left and the forecast; only the spending without a cap), the selected feature its dollar share and spending, and the detail line shows the initiative's model next to its feature workflow, and "up to N features at once" when its features run in worktrees side by side. Approve, resume, continue and the model switch drive the initiative in the workflow worker in the background, like the CLI commands; the screen refreshes every second, shows "⟳ live" while the worker drives it, and shows the last drive error. Decisions of feature runs are made in the run screen.

While the split waits for approval, or the initiative is paused after its split was approved, the selected feature can be edited: W sets its workflow (type the workflow ID), Z its size (`small`, `medium` or `large`), E renames it (the current title is filled in), S splits it into parts typed as `id: title` separated by `;` (each part keeps the feature's scope, paths and contract, as `split` does with a parts file without them), G merges it with the features whose IDs you type (the merged feature keeps the selected ID), and D removes it after a Y/N confirmation. Each edit is a new plan revision with a new approval request; the core's refusal (fewer than 3 features, an unknown ID, a cycle) is shown as typed. Text prompts submit with Enter and cancel with Esc.

## Run report

```sh
baked workflow report RUN_ID                 # prints Markdown
baked workflow report RUN_ID --output FILE   # writes FILE (relative to the workspace), prints {runId, path, bytes}
baked workflow report RUN_ID --json          # {runId, markdown}
```

One Markdown file for a run, at any status, read from the run's database without changing it:

- the title, run and feature IDs, workflow, status with its reason, and verification state;
- a summary: for a run that did not complete, a first line with its status, reason, stage and how many tasks are done, saying the request is not done; the spec's outcome labelled `Goal:` (left out for the host-made spec of a workflow without a spec stage, whose outcome only restates the request); a report stage's summary and recommendation when the workflow has one; and the request (cut at 2,000 characters);
- changes: every file the run changed, from the effect journal and the task diffs (published spec files are marked `spec`; a file edited outside the run since is marked `changed since`);
- tasks with title, status, origin (`repair, fresh context` for a [fresh-context restart](#fresh-context-restart)) and how many files each may write; a correction whose first attempt was limited to where its findings point shows that number first (`3 of 7 first`, with `, then all` when a later attempt got the full scope; `narrowedCorrections` in the run state), and how many are done of those the run still owes: a task a plan revision cancelled after approval is listed but not counted, and a task a revision dropped before anything ran has no row; checks with their latest result, validity, runs and time, plus a Tests column when a passing run printed a test summary the [baseline comparison](#baseline-comparison-in-update-runs) reads: the tests the last passing run ran, shown as `before → after (+added)` when the run measured the same command before any change, with the total added under the table so an update's test growth can be compared with what the request needed; the [code quality](code-quality.md) deltas, what the duplication ratchet found, the anti-slop rule matches, the new dependencies with their registry verdicts, the Semgrep matches, the criteria marked for property tests and the mutation score with the surviving mutants; acceptance criteria with verdicts and the final review verdict;
- which models (and their vendors) reviewed the change and which implemented it (the models the task attempts ran on, so a `--route implement=MODEL` route or a fallback is named, with the implementer profile's own model as `configured` when it differs); the [critic](#critic)'s score of each task attempt with its weakest rubric item; the [best-of-N](#best-of-n-candidates) candidates of each task with the reason for the choice and the oracle and selected rates; findings with status, severity, kind, file and claim; known limitations (findings accepted instead of repaired: at a [stopped convergence](#stopped-convergence), as notes on a low partial finding behind verified behavior, or by the repro gate on a convergence claim it did not reproduce, with who accepted them); decisions (every request with its kind, decision and who made it) and the spec's assumptions;
- review notes: review and convergence findings that started no correction, with why and the earlier note each repeats;
- usage: tokens against the budget, agent calls, active time, the estimated cost when known, and tokens per stage (in the order stages first ran) and per model;
- artifacts: the latest revision of each artifact with its ID, read with `baked workflow artifacts RUN_ID --file ID`. Attempt outputs, internal state and baselines are counted, not listed.

The report is the pull request body of a [delivery](#delivery). In the TUI, `/workflow report RUN_ID` shows it in the chat (`--output FILE` writes it). The desktop app shows it in the run's Report tab with Copy and Save buttons, and `GET /v1/workflow/runs/RUN_ID/report` returns it.

## Comparing two runs

```sh
baked workflow run sdd --feature totals-a --prompt-file request.md --worktree --route implement=MODEL_A
baked workflow run sdd --feature totals-b --prompt-file request.md --worktree --route implement=MODEL_B
baked workflow compare RUN_A RUN_B                 # prints Markdown
baked workflow compare RUN_A RUN_B --output ab.md  # writes ab.md in the project
baked workflow compare RUN_A RUN_B --json          # the comparison and its markdown
```

An A/B of routing maps runs one request twice with different `--route` maps (or `--model`), each in its own worktree so the two runs do not touch the same files, then puts the two runs side by side. `compare` reads both runs from the database without changing them, at any status:

- the routing targets whose model differs between the runs (from each run's latest `routing.json`), or a note that both used the same models;
- status with its reason, tokens, estimated cost, active time and agent calls, each with B minus A; repair rounds and review rounds, the latest result of each check (passed and failed counts), review findings by severity with how many stayed open, and accepted limitations;
- tokens per stage on both sides, in the order the stages first ran.

A note says when the two runs are different features or ran different workflows. In the TUI, `/workflow compare RUN_A RUN_B` shows the Markdown in the chat. In the desktop run list, tick two runs and press Compare: the same tables, with the better side of each row marked and Copy as Markdown. The local API answers `GET /v1/workflow/compare?a=RUN_A&b=RUN_B` (capability `run-compare`).

## Usage reports

```sh
baked usage --days 30 --group-by stage,model --format md          # prints Markdown
baked usage --days 30 --group-by stage,model --output usage.md    # writes usage.md in the project
baked workflow usage RUN_ID --format md [--output FILE]           # one run
```

Token use and estimated cost can be shared as one Markdown document. The project document has the window (days and UTC dates), the grouping, the model and stage filters, a totals table (runs, attempts, tokens, input with cache reads, output, cache reads, cache writes, estimated cost and unpriced tokens) and one row per group with the same figures, followed by a note on how cost is estimated. A run's document has its totals and the same figures by stage, by task generation (**By task generation**: a row for each task's first implementation and one for each rerun after a repair invalidated it, so a rerun's cost reads on its own) and by model; `--top` does not shorten it. When agents called code intelligence tools, it also has a **Code intelligence** line with the calls per tool and the backend that answered them, with the language server for `lsp` (for example `navigate lsp (rust-analyzer) 12, navigate tree-sitter 3, bake_find repo-map 4`), so a run shows whether rust-analyzer, csharp-ls or jdtls answered or a fallback did. The same line is in the [run report](#run-report)'s usage section, each attempt's `attempts/ATTEMPT/usage.json` has the counts as `toolBackends` (`{tool, backend, server, calls}`), and `GET /v1/workflow/runs/RUN_ID/usage` returns the run's totals as `toolBackends`. A **By tool** table lists the tools the model called, with the calls and the tokens of the steps that made them; a step's tokens are split evenly between its calls, so `--stage review` shows how much of a review went to `run_repro`, `read` or `grep`; the run report has the same figures as a **Tool calls** line. Each step in `usage.json` names its calls as `tools`, and the usage answer has `tools` (`{tool, calls, tokens}`) for the run and for each attempt; attempts recorded before tool names were have none. Only model IDs, stage IDs, the run ID and figures go in: no paths, prompts or account names. `--output` paths are relative to the project, and `--output` implies `--format md`.

In the TUI, E on the [usage screen](#usage-screen) writes the shown period as `baked-usage-30d-YYYY-MM-DD.md` in the project, grouped as on the screen; `/workflow usage RUN_ID --format md` shows a run's document in the chat (`--output FILE` writes it). The desktop Usage screen has Copy as Markdown and Export as .md for the chosen range, run and filters; the local API answers `GET /v1/usage?format=md` and `GET /v1/workflow/runs/RUN_ID/usage?format=md` with `{markdown}` ([API](../desktop/api.md)).

## Record and replay of model calls

A run can record its model calls so a fix to BAKED can be checked against the same situation without spending model tokens. Both options are off by default and only apply to the native executor.

```sh
baked workflow run --record-model-calls "Add a health endpoint"
baked workflow recording RUN_ID --output recordings/health.json
baked workflow run --replay recordings/health.json "Add a health endpoint"
```

`--record-model-calls` stores each model call of the run as the artifact `recordings/PROFILE/TASK/ATTEMPT_ID/N.json` (`TASK` is `run` outside a task). A call holds a fingerprint of the request (a hash of each prompt message, the offered tool names and the tool choice) and the provider's response. UUIDs, ISO times and `durationMs` values are replaced by placeholders before hashing, so they do not change a fingerprint. `baked workflow recording RUN_ID` prints the run's calls as one recording (`schemaVersion: 1`), or writes it to `--output FILE`.

`--replay FILE` starts a run that answers each model call from the recording instead of the provider. Calls are matched by profile and task, by the attempt's order for that key, and by the call's number within the attempt. The run starts only when the file exists and is a valid recording. When a request's fingerprint differs from the recorded one, or the recording has no call at that point, the run blocks with reason `replay_diverged`, and the message names the key, attempt and call and the first prompt message that differs. A replayed run records the calls it served, so `workflow recording` of the replay can be compared with the original. Streaming calls are not replayed.

An initiative records the same way: `baked workflow initiative start ID ... --record-model-calls` puts the option in the initiative's base policy, so the decompose run and every feature run record their calls. The bench loop starts every run and initiative with it, and `bun scripts/bench-trace.ts recordings WORKSPACE OUT_DIR [--run RUN_ID ...]` writes the recording of each run that recorded calls as `OUT_DIR/RUN_ID.json.gz` (the format `--replay` reads, gzip-compressed) with a `manifest.json` of the runs: feature, status, reason, the Git commit of the run's first workspace snapshot and the call count (#1715). Only runs of public bench apps are exported, because a recording holds the prompts and the model's answers.

## Import, export and retention

```sh
baked workflow import --source spec-kit --path reviewed-checkout/specs/feature --feature imported
baked workflow import --source ecc --path reviewed-checkout/agents --feature imported
baked workflow export RUN_ID --destination exports/run-001
baked workflow export-progress RUN_ID
baked workflow gc --before 2026-09-01 --dry-run
baked workflow worktrees --clean --dry-run
baked workflow redact RUN_ID --file ARTIFACT_ID --reason "Remove sensitive content"
```

For import, enable `importsEnabled` separately and point to a local source directory. The adapter does not download a repository, does not run scripts/hooks and does not activate imported agents. `.baked/workflow-imports/HASH` stores the candidate, hashes, the source commit when present, the license and an unsupported report. Spec Kit Markdown stays a draft; schema-compatible spec.json/tasks.json are validated with their IDs kept. An ECC prompt candidate requires native role/tool/output mapping and enabling through a reviewed profile. `markdown-edit` uses the same inert directory reader.

To accept a schema-compatible imported package, pass its spec/plan/tasks to `candidate`; the import itself does not change accepted data. Profiles are loaded from the built-in definitions, `~/.baked/agents/*.json`, then `baked/agents/*.json`. The source and hash are visible in `workflow profiles`. A project workflow is stored in `baked/workflows/ID.json`; the mandatory SDD stages cannot be removed or reordered. Skills live in `baked/skills/ID/SKILL.md` and are frozen by hash.

For a review by another model, route it with `modelRouting` (see [Model routing](#model-routing)) rather than copying a profile. A copied profile in `baked/agents/ID.json` can still name a specific `provider/model`, but a project file then carries one person's model choice. Reviewer context separation is mandatory regardless of the model choice; a separate profile name alone does not mean a different model. The model ID of every attempt is stored in the journal.

A CLI-backed provider (Claude Code, Codex) can pass the signed-in account to the model through the CLI's own context. The host reads that account's e-mail address from `~/.claude.json` (or `$CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR/.claude.json`) and `~/.codex/auth.json` (or `$CODEX_HOME/auth.json`), and refuses an edit or a submitted result that contains the address or its local part (five characters or longer, as a whole word), telling the agent to take the owner from the project manifest or write `<project name> contributors`. An identity the workspace already names in `package.json`, `pyproject.toml`, `Cargo.toml`, `composer.json`, `LICENSE*` or `AUTHORS` is the project's own and is allowed.

### Worktree disk usage

A worktree run keeps `.baked/worktrees/RUN/main` (and a directory per task with several writers) until delivery or `discard-worktree` removes it, so benchmark rounds and failed runs leave worktrees behind. `baked workflow worktrees` lists each run's directory, largest first, with its size, the size of each worktree in it, the run's status and feature, and whether a cleanup removes it. `baked workflow worktrees --clean --dry-run` shows what a cleanup removes and how many bytes it frees; `--clean` removes the worktrees of completed, failed and cancelled runs and of directories whose run the project no longer has, and empties `.baked/trash` (locked worktree directories moved aside). Running, waiting, paused and blocked runs, and runs with an unsettled delivery step, are kept. Unlike `discard-worktree`, the cleanup keeps the run branch `baked/wf/RUN_ID`, which carries the accepted commit, and records `worktree/cleaned.json` in the run. In the TUI it is the typed command `/workflow worktrees [--clean]`; the desktop run list has **Worktrees…** under the same capability, and the local API answers `GET /v1/workflow/worktrees` and `POST /v1/workflow/worktrees/clean` ([API](../desktop/api.md)).

Export creates a new directory inside the workspace. The host API allows a separate outside-path approval callback; the CLI does not provide one automatically. Raw provider messages and command output are not part of the portable report; known credentials are masked. Redaction removes the selected artifact content, keeps the original hash and a tombstone and invalidates evidence. It does not promise to remove every possible copy of a string from other artifacts.

In the desktop app, a run's screen has Export (into `.baked/exports/RUN_ID-STAMP`) and Redact…, and the run list has Import… and Clean up old runs…, which previews the runs it would remove before removing them. The local API has the same as `POST /v1/workflow/runs/RUN_ID/export` and `/redact`, `POST /v1/workflow/import` and `POST /v1/workflow/gc` ([desktop API](../desktop/api.md)).
