GC removes only terminal runs older than 30 days and the given cutoff when no child run needs them and they hold no lease. Active/waiting/blocked data and the shared `specs/` are kept. For a backup, stop the writers and save `.baked/workflows.sqlite` together with `.baked/workflow-runs/`. Copying a live SQLite database also needs the WAL/SHM files or an SQLite backup; an arbitrary copy of one file during writes is not a consistent backup.

## Model routing

Profiles name a model class: built-in ones use `$active` (the run model: the tab model or `--model`), `$fast` (the quick planner), `$coding` and `$review`. The `modelRouting` section of the global or project configuration turns classes, roles, profiles and workflow stages into concrete models:

```jsonc
"modelRouting": {
  "classes": { "fast": "anthropic/claude-haiku-5-5", "review": "openai/gpt-5.1" },
  "roles": { "researcher": "$fast", "reporter": "$fast" },
  "profiles": { "security-reviewer": "anthropic/claude-opus-5-5" },
  "workflows": { "sdd": { "stages": { "research": "$fast" } } }
}
```

A value is a `provider/model` ID or `$class`. A class names a model, not another class. A project layer is merged over the global one section by section, and stage routes workflow by workflow.

The model of an agent is chosen in this order, the first match winning:

1. `--route STAGE=MODEL,...` on `workflow run`;
2. `modelRouting.workflows.<workflow>.stages.<stage>`;
3. `modelRouting.profiles.<profile>`;
4. `modelRouting.roles.<role>`;
5. the profile's class through `modelRouting.classes`; `$coding` and `$review` then fall back to the `/router` Code (`taskRouter.ember`) and Review (`taskRouter.verify`) slots, and every built-in class to the run model.

`modelRouting.specifyOnSonnet` (on by default, #1173; `false` turns it off) adds one route after step 2: when the run model is a Claude Code Opus model (`claude-code/opus`, `claude-code/claude-opus-5-5`, ...), the `specify` stage runs on `claude-code/sonnet` (source `modelRouting.specifyOnSonnet`) and every other stage keeps Opus. A `--route specify=...` or a configured `specify` stage route still wins, `--model-strict` ignores it whether it is set or unset (the start and a reroute of a strict run pass `strict` to the stage routes, #2217), and runs on other models or providers are not affected. It became the default after the bench loop's measurements: in 2 of 2 routed runs (R27 ll1check, #1027) the Sonnet spec passed analyze on its first return with no patch round and every task passed, while specify on Opus cost 11 to 15 percent of a claude-code run (R55 to R61). Set it on the routing screens, with `baked workflow routing specify-sonnet on|off|unset` or with `PATCH /v1/workflow/routing`.

**Code web search.** `modelRouting.codeWebSearch: true` (off by default, #1239) lets implementer and acceptance-writer attempts on a Codex model use Codex's own web search, for current library documentation and API signatures. A run start copies it into the run policy as `codeWebSearch: true` (a policy file can set it directly), so the approval digest covers it. The attempt's provider options carry `codex.webSearch`, and the Codex adapter starts its thread (or `codex exec`) with `web_search = "live"` instead of `"disabled"`; every other attempt (planner, reviewers, critic, approval reviewer) keeps it off, and checks, tests, linters and probes run outside the model with the policy's `network` setting as before. The search runs on the provider's side, not on this machine. Each attempt's `attempts/ID/usage.json` records `webSearch: true` when it was on, `webSearches` (the count over its steps, also per step) and `webSearchQueries` (the queries in order, at most 50 of 200 characters each), so a round report can count the searches of the code stage. Models of other providers are not affected. Set it on the routing screens, with `baked workflow routing web-search on|off|unset` or with `PATCH /v1/workflow/routing`.

A stage route applies to every agent of that stage, including its tasks, review dimensions and skeptics; gates on transitions use their profile's model. A route naming a stage the workflow does not have, or an unknown class, is refused when the run starts.

A run freezes its routing at start in the `routing.json` artifact (each profile's and each routed stage's model with the source of the choice); resume, revise and fork use it, whatever the configuration says later, until a [reroute](#switching-a-run-to-another-model) writes a new revision. `baked workflow route [WORKFLOW] [--model ID] [--route ...] [--json]` prints the same table without starting a run, and `workflow profiles [--model ID]` shows each profile's resolved model and `modelSource`.

`--model` (and the chat tab's model in the terminal UI and desktop app) is the run model. An explicit `--model` serves the `$active` class even when `modelRouting.classes.active` names another model; that class keeps its effort and fallback models, the routing record keeps `explicitModel: true`, and a reroute of the run keeps it. Without `--model` the run model is `defaultModel` and a configured `classes.active` applies as written. Only the `$active` class follows `--model`, so a configured `modelRouting` class, role, profile or stage route, or a legacy `taskRouter` slot, still wins. When one wins, the start records a warning in `routing.json` and prints it as `warning: ...` on stderr of `workflow run` and `quick`: "Model X applies to the active class only; implementer uses Y (modelRouting.classes.coding)". `--model-strict` (API: `modelStrict: true`; `doctor` and `estimate` take it too) ignores every configured route and every default-on route (`specifyOnSonnet`) for this run, so the run model serves every class, role and stage; only `--route` still overrides it. The record keeps `strict: true`, and a reroute of the run stays strict: it builds the new routes and checks review independence on the same strict configuration the start used, so `modelRouting.independentReview` neither refuses nor warns about reviewers that share the run model ([#2230](https://github.com/Gulliby-development/baked/issues/2230)). `baked workflow status RUN_ID` returns `routing: {runModel, strict, warnings, effective: [{target, model, source}]}` (the effective model of each role and stage, from the routing record); the run view of `baked serve` carries the same `routing` object, and the terminal run overview and the desktop run's Model panel show the models by role, the warnings and whether the run is strict. The terminal New run form has a Routes field (‹ configured › or ‹ run model only ›, toggled with ←/→) and the desktop New run form a "Run model overrides configured routes" checkbox under Oven settings; both start the run with `modelStrict`.

### Usage screen

`/workflow usage`, or U on the run list of `/workflow`, opens the usage screen of the TUI: the tokens and estimated cost of every workflow attempt that started in the last 7, 30 or 90 days, across the project's runs. The first line sums runs, attempts, tokens, cost and the tokens no model price covers (`unpriced`, left out of the cost); the table below groups them by model (`(host)` for host attempts), stage or UTC day. ←→ changes the period, Tab the grouping, E writes the period and grouping shown as a Markdown file in the project ([usage reports](#usage-reports)), Esc returns to the run list. The numbers come from the same code as [`baked usage`](../headless.md) and `GET /v1/usage`, so they match the CLI and the desktop Usage screen. With a run ID, `/workflow usage RUN_ID` still prints that run's per-stage usage in the chat.

### Workflows screen

`/workflow definitions`, or W on the run list of `/workflow`, opens the workflows screen of the TUI. Each workflow a run can start from is one row: its ID, its family (spec-driven full, spec-driven, quick, repair or read only, from what it writes and the stations on its line) and its line of stations, such as `plan → approve → implement → checks ⟲ → review ⟲ → learn`, where `⟲` marks a station a repair loop leaves from. A definition that does not validate shows its first problem instead. The selected workflow's description is under the list. Enter steps through a run along the selected line: ← and → move one step, S switches between "everything passes", "a check fails once" and "review asks for changes" (offered when the line has a loop for that outcome), R starts over, and the text under the line says what the current station does. A station that works again after a loop shows how many times it ran (`×2`). E opens the [workflow editor](#workflow-editor) on the selected definition, and Esc goes back. The screen is static text, like `/graph`; the desktop Workflows screen plays the same walkthrough, and both use `src/core/workflows/v2/walkthrough.ts`. Outside the TUI, `baked workflow definitions` still prints the listing as JSON.

### Routing screen

`/workflow routing`, or R on the run list of `/workflow`, opens the routing screen of the TUI. It lists the model classes (the built-in `$active`, `$fast`, `$coding`, `$review` and every configured one), the routed roles, the stages of one workflow, every profile, `specifyOnSonnet` (under the stages), `codeWebSearch` and `context` (under the profiles), `independentReview`, `reviewMinConfidence` and `critic`. Each row shows what the selected configuration layer sets, a value only the other layer sets (prefixed with that layer's name), or what the row resolves to without a route of its own (`= model`). The lines under the list name the selected entry's path in `modelRouting`, what each layer sets, and the model, effort and fallback models it resolves to with the source of the choice, as `baked workflow route` would print them with the chat tab's model as the run model. Independence warnings of the shown workflow follow.

Keys: ↑/↓ select, Tab switches between the global (`~/.baked/config.json`) and project (`.baked/config.json`) layer, W switches the workflow whose stages are listed, Enter edits the selected route, D removes it from the selected layer, ←/→ on `independentReview` cycle unset, `off`, `warn`, `require` and `provider`, ←/→ on `reviewMinConfidence` change it by 0.05 (D clears it), ←/→ on `critic` cycle unset, `off`, `advise`, `select` and `earlyStop`, ←/→ or Space on `specify on Sonnet` cycle unset, on and off (D clears it), and Esc returns to the run list. "+ new class" adds a class.

The edit form has Model (a `provider/model` ID, or `$class` outside a class), Effort (←/→: unset, `default`, `low`, `medium`, `high`, `xhigh`, `max`) and Fallback (up to three model IDs separated by commas). Enter on Model or Fallback opens the list of available models: the model names (`$active`, `$fast`, ... with the model each resolves to) first, for a route's Model only, then the models of each provider with a key or login, then the providers without one, greyed out with the reason. Typing filters the list, ↑/↓ and Enter choose, Esc returns to the form. A chosen model fills Model, or is added to Fallback (the oldest is dropped past three). When the text matches no listed model, "Use … as typed" puts it in the field as it is, and both fields can also be typed directly. Ctrl+S saves from any field, and Enter saves on Name and Effort; a route without effort or fallback models is written as a plain string. Before writing, the screen checks the route and the whole layer as a run start would: an empty model, a class naming another class, a class reference that neither layer defines (including removing a class that a route still uses), fallback models that are classes, repeat the primary model or exceed three, and anything the `modelRouting` schema rejects. A refused change is not written and the form lists why. Saving writes only the `modelRouting` section of the selected layer, and an emptied section is removed. New runs use the saved routing (an idle workflow worker restarts to read it); started runs keep the routing frozen in their `routing.json` until a [reroute](#switching-a-run-to-another-model).

The desktop app shows and edits the same entries in Settings > Model routing, through `GET` and `PATCH /v1/workflow/routing` of the [local API](../desktop/api.md), with the same checks. Its Model and Fallback models fields offer the same list from `GET /v1/models`.

Scripts and CI change the same entries with `baked workflow routing`, which runs the same checks and writes only the `modelRouting` section of the project config (or the global one with `--scope global`):

```sh
baked workflow routing set role:reviewer openai/gpt-5 --effort high --fallback anthropic/claude-sonnet-4-6
baked workflow routing set '$coding' anthropic/claude-sonnet-4-6 --scope global
baked workflow routing set stage:sdd/plan '$review'
baked workflow routing clear role:reviewer
baked workflow routing review provider     # independentReview; unset removes it
baked workflow routing confidence 0.7      # reviewMinConfidence; unset removes it
baked workflow routing critic advise       # critic; unset removes it
baked workflow routing specify-sonnet off  # specifyOnSonnet; on by default, unset removes it
baked workflow routing web-search on       # codeWebSearch; off by default, unset removes it
baked workflow routing context auto        # context of every route without its own; unset removes it
baked workflow routing set stage:sdd/review '$review' --context max   # one route's context window
baked workflow routing show --json         # modelRouting of both files
```

A target is a class (`$name` or `class:name`), a role (`role:NAME`), a profile (`profile:ID`) or a stage (`stage:WORKFLOW/STAGE`). A refused change is not written; the reasons go to stderr (or `{saved: false, problems}` with `--json`) and the command exits 1. `--json` prints `{saved: true, file, modelRouting}` for a saved change.

`baked workflow models [--model ID] [--provider ID] [--json]` prints what a route, `--route`, `--model`, a reroute or a fallback can name: the model names with the model each resolves to for the run model (`--model`, else `defaultModel`), then the models of each provider, and the providers without a key or login with that reason. `--json` prints `{runModel, classes: [{ref, model, source}], providers}`, where `providers` has the shape of `GET /v1/models`.

### Effort and fallback models

Any route, and any class, can be an object instead of a model ID:

```jsonc
"modelRouting": {
  "classes": { "deep": { "model": "anthropic/claude-opus-5-5", "effort": "high", "fallback": ["openai/gpt-5.1"] } },
  "roles": { "researcher": { "model": "$deep", "effort": "low" } },
  "independentReview": "warn"
}
```

- `effort` is `default`, `low`, `medium`, `high`, `xhigh` or `max`. It replaces `performance.effort` and `performance.openaiReasoningEffort` for that agent's calls only; an OpenAI level the model does not support is lowered to the nearest one it does. `default` sends no effort setting, so the provider's default applies. Models configured with a fixed effort in a compatible provider's own settings keep it.
- An agent profile may set its own `effort` with the same values (#334). It applies when no route of the agent sets one: a role, profile, class or stage route with `effort` wins, and a stage route without it keeps the profile's. No built-in profile sets it, so it is opt-in through a profile in `baked/agents/` or `~/.baked/agents/`. `workflow profiles` and `workflow route` show it as the profile's route effort.
- `fallback` lists up to three model IDs (not classes) tried in order. A route without its own `fallback` takes the model's chain from `modelFallback` ([providers](../providers.md#model-fallback)), frozen into the run's `routing.json` at start. A class reference brings the class's effort and fallback models; the object route's own fields override them.
- `context` picks the context window for models that offer more than one ([providers](../providers.md#context-size-per-chat); today Claude Code's `opus` and `sonnet` with 200k or 1M through the `[1m]` model ID, #1256): `default` runs the model as named, `max` its largest window, a number (`1000000`) the window of that many tokens (the largest one below it when the model has no such window), and `auto` the model as named until a step's context does not fit. `modelRouting.context` sets it for every route without its own; a class reference brings the class's context. A model with one window runs as named whatever the setting says.

**Context window.** `max` and a token count are frozen into `routing.json` as the model IDs that ask for that window, for the route's fallback models too (`claude-code/opus` becomes `claude-code/opus[1m]`), so resume, fork and revise keep them, and the entry records `context`. With `auto` the entry keeps the models as named and records `context: "auto"`. When the step's context does not fit the window of its model (the check before the call, or the provider's size refusal) and that model offers a larger window, the step is tried once on the model's largest window, before the route's fallback models; if that does not fit either, the step moves on as below. The larger attempt runs, and is reported by `workflow usage`, under the `[1m]` model ID. A step that changed files does not move. A larger window costs more per call (it uses a Claude Code plan's limits faster; API prompts over 200k tokens are priced higher), so `auto` pays for it only on a step that would otherwise stop or leave its model. `workflow route` shows a CONTEXT column when any route sets one. Set it per route in the route form of the routing screens, with `baked workflow routing set TARGET MODEL --context max|auto|1m` or `PATCH /v1/workflow/routing` (`route.context`), and for every route with `baked workflow routing context default|max|auto|SIZE|unset` or the Context window row.

The primary model gets two retries on a transient provider error (timeouts, 408, 429, 5xx, 529, network errors); each fallback model gets one. A connection that closes after the answer started is a network error too: the SDK reports it as "Failed to process successful response" with the reset as its cause, and a Codex or Claude Code CLI that a signal ended mid-turn ("exited with signal") is treated the same way. Both were final failures before the provider fault matrix found them on every provider (#1719). A rate limit (a 429, or Codex's "429 Too Many Requests" after its own quick retries) waits about 5, 15 and 45 seconds, three retries on any model, or longer when the provider's `retry-after` asks for it, instead of retrying within seconds. A failed attempt records why in its `attempt.finished` event (`error`: `code` and the first 500 characters of the message). A provider that answers "Selected model is at capacity" or "try a different model" is treated the same way, but waits longer (about 5, 15, 45 and 90 seconds, four retries, on the primary model too) and hands the step to the next model of the route at once when there is one, instead of blocking the run as `provider_unavailable`. When a route has another model and the provider asks for a `retry-after` over 30 seconds, the step moves on instead of waiting, and a fallback model whose provider asks for such a wait is not waited for. A step also moves to the next model when its prompt does not fit the model's context window, or when the provider refuses the request for its size. The window is the one BAKED knows for the routed model (the `contextWindowOverrides` setting, the provider's model list, or its built-in table); for a model none of them names, BAKED does not guess a window and leaves the size to the provider's own refusal. When no larger window or model is left, the step runs once more with its run-level documents shortened before the run stops: no research, the task graph listed by what each task writes, and, for the stages that may call `read_criterion`, the spec digest at any spec size. The fresh session after a provider's size refusal gets the same shortened documents. If the shortened context still does not fit, the run blocks with `budget_exhausted`, and the message names the largest part of the context (the request, the spec, the plan, the graph or the attempt's own input) (the Codex app-server's `input_too_large`, a context-length error or HTTP 413). Without another model, a size refusal is not a provider outage: the step is tried once more in a fresh session, which does not carry the history that overflowed, and if that fails too, or the attempt had changed files, the run blocks with `budget_exhausted` and a message naming the limit, instead of restarting the task as a lost worker on the same history. A response the provider ends for running past its output cap and reports as an error instead of a `length` finish (Codex's "Incomplete response returned, reason: max_output_tokens", Claude Code's "Claude's response exceeded the 32000 output token maximum") is not a provider outage either: unless the attempt changed files, the step is tried again at once, one effort step lower (`max`, `xhigh`, `high`, `medium`, `low`) and with a note that its previous response ran past the output limit, at most twice; a route without effort gets one retry with the note. Only after those retries does the failure take the usual path. The cut-off call reports no usage, so the attempt is charged an estimate (the messages it was sent at about 4 bytes a token, and a full response at the profile's `maxOutputTokens`), marked as estimated usage, instead of 0 tokens. In bench round 41 such a 10 minute response was recorded as 0 tokens, blocked the run as `provider_unavailable` for a minute, and ran again with the same prompt and effort. A step never moves to another model after its attempt changed files, after a cancel, or on a non-transient error (401, 403, a refused request, invalid output). The Claude Code message that another Claude Code process is refreshing the OAuth token is transient too, but it passes in about a minute: it is retried up to three times, each after 30 to 60 seconds. When every model fails, the step fails with `provider_unavailable`, naming the primary's failure and the last model's. A refusal of the account is not retried and not handed to a fallback model on the same provider, which shares the account; the step goes to the next model of the chain on another provider instead, unless the attempt changed files. Without such a model, spent credits or quota (HTTP 402, or a 429 whose body says the quota or credits are used up, as OpenAI reports it) and a rejected API key (401, or a 403 about the key) block the run at once with `provider_account` and a message that says to add credits or fix the key, then resume. A usage limit of the account is refused the same way: Codex's "You've hit your usage limit ... try again at Oct 22nd, 2026 10:55 AM", Claude Code's "usage limit reached", "5-hour limit reached" or "Weekly limit reached ... resets 3pm", the Anthropic API's spend limit ("You will regain access on ..."), a GitHub Copilot monthly premium request or chat quota, and any 429 that asks to wait over an hour (GitHub Models' daily request limit). A rate limit with a short wait stays transient. A usage limit lifts at a reset hours or days away, so it is not retried on the provider-outage resumes, and the message names the reset time the provider gave and says to add credits or wait for the reset. An initiative whose feature run stopped with `provider_account` shows that message in the feature's blocker and in its next action, not only the reason. In a run with several writers this stops the batch instead of being reported as a lost task worker, and a lost worker's message now always names a cause. A refusal of the request under the provider's usage policy is neither an account problem nor an outage (#1895): Codex's and the OpenAI API's "Invalid prompt: your prompt was flagged as potentially violating our usage policy", Claude Code's "appears to violate our Usage Policy", GitHub Copilot's "filtered by the Responsible AI Service", GitHub Models' "content management policy", and a response any provider ends with the `content-filter` finish reason (OpenAI's `content_filter`, Anthropic's `stop_reason: refusal`). Each refused attempt is recorded in `provider/policy-refusals/ATTEMPT.json` (task or stage profile, model, tokens and the provider's message), and the run report's Usage section lists them with their tokens. The step runs once more as it was, on the files the refused attempt already changed, instead of being restarted as a lost worker; a second refusal of the same task or stage on the same model blocks the run with `provider_policy` and a message that says to move the run to another model with `baked workflow reroute`. A plain resume on the same model gets one more attempt. In bench round 59 a Codex classifier false positive on a Python test task cost 335,981 tokens and was journaled as `worker_lost` with a stack of BAKED's own code.

`routing.json` records each entry's `effort` and `fallback`, and `workflow route` prints them in the EFFORT and FALLBACK columns. `workflow usage RUN_ID` reports each attempt's `model` and a `models` summary (attempts, tokens and steps per model, most tokens first; host attempts such as checks are grouped as `(host)`). Each attempt of a task step also has its `taskId` and `taskGeneration` (0 for the task's first implementation, one more for each repair that invalidated it; null for other steps), and `tasks` sums them per task and generation (`taskId`, `generation`, `attempts`, `tokens`, `costUsd`).

### Preflight and independent review

Before a run is created, every model it can call is checked the way `baked doctor` checks it, and the start reports the same lines. A model that a bound profile or a stage route uses as its primary, and whose known provider has no credentials, refuses the start with `CAPABILITY_DENIED`. Fallback models, profiles the workflow does not bind, unknown providers and a check that does not answer within 5 seconds only add a warning.

`independentReview` controls whether a reviewer may run on a model the implementation also uses, fallback models included: `warn` records a warning, `require` refuses the start, `off` skips the check, and `provider` asks for a reviewer of another vendor (Codex reviews Claude Code output and the other way round). Without the field, `provider` applies when the configuration names models of two known vendors (the run model, classes, roles, profiles, stage routes and the legacy router slots), else `warn` once `modelRouting` is configured. Review, converge and reflection stages and review gates count as the review side; task stages as the implementation side.

With `provider`, vendors are compared rather than models: `claude-code/*`, `anthropic/*` and Copilot or GitHub Models Claude models are `anthropic`, `codex/*`, `openai/*` and GPT models are `openai`. Every review-side route that shares a vendor with the implementation (its fallback models included) moves to the first model of another vendor, in this order: the `$review` class, any other model the configuration names, then the first model of each signed-in single-vendor provider (Claude Code, Codex, Anthropic, OpenAI). A moved route keeps its effort, drops its fallback models and has the source `independentReview: provider`; each move is listed in the run's warnings as `Cross-provider review: ...`. When no model of another vendor exists, the review stays where it is and the start records a warning instead of refusing. A reroute names its models itself, so under `provider` it only warns about a shared vendor. The [run report](#run-report) says which models (and vendors) reviewed the change and which implemented it.

Every review finding carries a `confidence` from 0 to 1 that it is real and caused by the change; reviewers leave out problems the change did not introduce, nits and style, and what the project's formatter or linter reports. A finding below `reviewMinConfidence` (0.8 by default) is a note: it stays in the review artifact and starts no correction, whatever its kind or severity. One exception is a `missing` or `correctness` finding of a dimension reviewer or the convergence walker on behavior the request or spec names (basis not `inferred`): a reviewer that reports one below the threshold without a repro is asked once to try it with `run_repro`, and when the host runs the attached repro and it prints `actual`, the finding starts a correction whatever its confidence, so a request clause the spec dropped is repaired instead of kept as a note. A finding without a confidence counts as 1. Set the threshold in `modelRouting.reviewMinConfidence` (global or project layer) or directly as `workflows.policy.reviewMinConfidence`; a start copies the routing value into the run's policy, so it is frozen with the approval digest, and a policy without it keeps its hash.

### Critic

The critic (`critic` profile, role `reviewer`, model `$review`) scores every task attempt that reaches its checks: it reads the task, the patch of the attempt and the checks that failed, and returns a `score` from 0 to 1 (its estimate that the attempt is correct and will be kept) with five rubric items, each scored with a one-sentence note: `intent`, `instructions`, `scope`, `testIntegrity` and `completeness`. The critique is stored as the artifact `tasks/TASK/critique.json` (one revision per attempt, with the attempt number, the diff it scored and the model) and listed in the run report under "Critic" with the weakest item. It is advice: it changes no status and starts no correction. A critic that fails is recorded with its error and the run goes on; only a spent budget or a checkpoint stops the run as for any agent call. An attempt already scored is not scored again on resume. The critic keeps its own profile route even when the task stage has a stage route, and `independentReview: provider` moves it to another vendor with the reviewers.

`critic` is `off` (never score), `advise` (always score, decide nothing), `select` or `earlyStop`. `select` and `earlyStop` score like `advise` and also let a [calibrated](#critic-calibration) critic decide: `select` picks the best-scored passing candidate of a [best-of-N](#best-of-n-candidates) task instead of the vote, and `earlyStop` ends a convergence loop as described below. Until the critic is calibrated both act like `advise`. Without a setting the critic runs only when its model is of another vendor than the implementation's, so a setup with one provider spends nothing on it. Set it in `modelRouting.critic` (global or project layer, on the routing screen, with `baked workflow routing critic off|advise|select|earlyStop|unset`, `PATCH /v1/workflow/routing` or the desktop's Model routing page) or directly as `workflows.policy.critic`; a start copies the routing value into the run's policy.

#### Critic outcome log

Every critic score is also kept in the project's `.baked/workflows.sqlite` (table `critic_outcomes`, one row per scored attempt), with the outcomes that later show whether the critic was right. The data stays local, like telemetry, and is what [calibration](#critic-calibration) learns from.

- `task_result` is `passed` when the attempt's task checks passed, or `repaired` when they failed and a repair task followed.
- `acceptance` is `accepted` when the run completed, or `rejected` when it failed or was cancelled.
- `delivery` applies to a run with a [delivery](#delivery). It is `merged` once the base branch contains the delivered commit, `reverted` once a commit on that branch reverts it, and otherwise `open`. It is read from the local Git repository, so fetch first to see merges made on the server.
- `survival` is the share of the non-blank lines the attempt added that the project's files still hold. It is updated until 30 days after the run completed and is then final.

The delivery and survival labels are refreshed before a run starts, at most once a day per process. `baked workflow outcomes [RUN_ID] [--refresh] [--json]` (or `/workflow outcomes` in the terminal UI) shows the log:
- a summary (attempts whose checks passed or were repaired, with the mean score of each; accepted and rejected runs; merged and reverted deliveries; mean final survival);
- one line per scored attempt.

`--refresh` updates the labels from Git first, and `--json` gives the rows for the bench loop to aggregate across projects. The run report's Critic section adds an Outcome column with the labels of each attempt.

#### Critic calibration

A critic's score only decides something once it has shown, on this project's own history, that it separates attempts that held up from those that did not. An attempt is labelled:
- **held up** when its task checks passed and nothing later undid it;
- **did not hold up** when a repair followed, its run failed or was cancelled, its delivery was reverted, or less than half of its added lines survived to the final survival label.

The critic is calibrated when the log has at least 20 labelled attempts, at least 5 of each label, and the scores rank them with an AUC (the chance that an attempt that held up scores above one that did not) of at least 0.65. The threshold is the score with the best Youden index (true positive rate minus false positive rate). `baked workflow outcomes` prints the calibration line, `--json` and `GET /v1/workflow/critic` return it as `calibration` (`{samples, good, bad, auc, threshold, calibrated, reason}`), and the desktop shows it under Critic on the Model routing page.

A run computes its calibration once, from earlier runs only, and freezes it as the artifact `critic/calibration.json`, so a resumed run decides the same way. The same artifact holds up to two recent attempts of each label with their score and weakest rubric item; the critic receives them as `earlierAttempts` to judge what this project keeps.

With `critic: earlyStop`, a convergence round whose findings would start another repair accepts them instead when:
- the critic is calibrated;
- every required check passes;
- no finding is critical, a security finding, or high with a reproduction the host ran;
- the latest attempt of every scored task scores at or above the threshold.

The findings become known limitations decided by `critic` ("critic early stop" in the run report), the reasons are stored as `critic/early-stop-N.json`, and the run goes on to acceptance. Otherwise the round continues as without the critic.

Warnings are stored in `routing.json` under `warnings` and printed by `workflow route` as `warning:` lines.

### Switching a run to another model

When a provider becomes unusable in the middle of a run (credits or quota spent, a revoked key, a retired model), move the run to another model instead of starting over:

```sh
baked workflow reroute RUN_ID --expected-revision N --model ID [--route STAGE=MODEL,...]
# or switch and continue in one command, with the exit codes of resume:
baked workflow reroute RUN_ID --expected-revision N --model ID --resume [--events]
```

The run must not be executing: `created`, `paused`, `blocked` or `waiting` (pause a running run first; a finished run is forked instead). Completed stages, tasks, approvals and evidence stay; only the next agent calls use the new models. The routes are resolved again through the current `modelRouting` and `modelFallback` with the new run model, so every profile and stage that followed the old run model (directly or through a built-in class without a configured model) follows the new one. A class configured to name a model keeps it; change the configuration first, or route its stages with `--route`. Stage routes given with `--route` at start are kept, except that one naming the old run model moves to the new one; `--route` here replaces them stage by stage. `--model` may be left out to change only stage routes.

The new record goes through the same checks as a start: a bound model without credentials refuses the switch with `CAPABILITY_DENIED`, and `independentReview: "require"` refuses a switch that puts a reviewer on an implementation model. It is written as a new revision of `routing.json` (warnings included), and a `run.rerouted` event names the old and new run model and every changed route (`stage ID` or `profile ID`, with its model and fallback chain before and after). The run keeps its status; `resume` (or `--resume`) continues it from where it stopped. A `provider_account` stop names this command in its message. So does the refusal of a resume after a repeated invalid output: a new routing revision makes the same diagnostic a new attempt (see "Resume after a repeated invalid output").

## Delivery

A completed run whose verification is `current` can be delivered through Git: one commit of the run's files on a new branch, a push of that branch and a pull request. Every step needs a person's approval of the exact plan; delivery never runs inside auto approval.

```sh
baked workflow deliver RUN_ID [--remote origin] [--branch NAME] [--draft] [--no-pr] [--with-specs|--no-specs]
baked workflow deliver RUN_ID --local [--branch NAME] [--with-specs|--no-specs]
baked workflow approve RUN_ID --request ID --digest SHA256 --expected-revision N
baked workflow deliver RUN_ID --execute --expected-revision N [--request ID]
```

`deliver` prepares a plan and changes nothing in Git. It is stored as the immutable artifact `delivery/PLAN_ID/plan.json` with the reviewable diff in `delivery/PLAN_ID/diff.patch`, and a pending request of kind `delivery` carries the plan's digest. The plan names the operations (`commit`, `push`, `pull_request`), the remote and its URL, the branch (default `baked/FEATURE-` followed by the first 8 characters of the run ID), the base commit and branch (the current HEAD), the commit author, every file with its action, content hash and mode, the excluded files with a reason, the user's other uncommitted changes, the commit message, the pull request title, body, base and draft flag, and the check, criterion and review results. The command prints the `approve` command for that digest. A new `deliver` supersedes an older pending plan. Approving or rejecting a delivery request does not change the run's status; the run stays `completed`.

Which files are delivered: the files of the run's effect journal (task edits and published spec artifacts) and the files that changed between the approval baseline snapshot and the final snapshot inside the tasks' declared writes. Other files in the workspace are never committed; they are listed under `userChanges` and left as they are. Excluded and reported: anything under `.baked/` or `.git/`, symbolic links, files ignored by Git, files with no net change from HEAD, and files that look like secrets (`.env*`, `.envrc`, `.netrc`, `.npmrc`, `.pypirc`, `.pgpass`, `.git-credentials`, `credentials.json`, SSH keys, `*.pem`, `*.key`, `*.crt`, `*.cer`, `*.der`, `*.p12`, `*.pfx`, `*.jks`, `*.keystore`, `*.kdbx`, `*.asc`, `*.gpg`, `*.ovpn`, or content with a private key header).

The run's own `specs/FEATURE/` documents (spec, plan and task graph) are committed only when the base commit already tracks a `specs/` directory, so a pull request to a repository that does not keep specs carries just the code; they are listed as excluded with reason `specs` and stay in the run's artifacts, and the pull request body still carries the summary and criteria. `--with-specs` delivers them anyway, `--no-specs` leaves them out of a repository that keeps specs.

Preparation refuses, with `details.reason` in the error:

| Reason | Cause |
| --- | --- |
| `git_required` | The workspace is not a Git repository, or not its top level. Delivery needs Git; a filesystem-only project keeps its changes in place |
| `run_not_completed`, `verification_not_current` | Only a completed and currently verified result is delivered |
| `dirty_files` | A delivered file differs from the run's verified snapshot |
| `base_mismatch` | A delivered file had uncommitted user changes before the run, or a commit since the run started changed it; the run's changes cannot be separated, so commit or revert those changes by hand |
| `missing_remote`, `remote_unreachable`, `missing_auth` | The remote is not configured, cannot be read, or credentials (Git or the pull request token) are missing |
| `branch_exists` | The branch exists locally or on the remote; delivery never overwrites a branch |
| `detached_head`, `no_commits`, `missing_identity` | No base branch, no base commit, or no `user.name`/`user.email` |
| `nothing_to_deliver` | Every changed file was excluded |
| `worktree_missing`, `branch_moved` | A worktree run's worktree was removed, or its run branch no longer points at the accepted commit |
| `local_with_remote` | `--local` with `--remote`, `--follow-up` or `--answer-threads`; a [local delivery](#local-delivery-without-a-remote) pushes nowhere |
| `invalid_follow_up`, `not_delivered` | `--follow-up` names the run itself, or a run with no pushed delivery |
| `answer_without_follow_up`, `no_pull_request`, `thread_not_open` | `--answer-threads` without `--follow-up`, a followed delivery that opened no pull request, or a thread that is not an unresolved review thread of that pull request |
| `review_replies_failed` | GitHub refused a reply or a resolution of an approved thread; the commit is pushed, and the next execute answers the threads not yet answered |
| `branch_missing`, `fetch_required`, `branch_checked_out` | The followed branch is gone from the remote, its tip is not in the local repository (`git fetch` it), or it is checked out in the working copy or a worktree |

A HEAD that moved since the run's baseline is accepted when the new commits do not touch the delivered files; the plan records `headMoved`.

A run executed in a worktree is delivered from its run branch: the files are read in its worktree, which must still exist and hold the branch at the accepted commit (`worktree_missing`, `branch_moved` otherwise), and the plan records them under `worktree` (path, branch, commit). The delivery commit is still built on the working copy's current HEAD in a temporary index, so the user's checkout is not touched; the user's own uncommitted changes are listed under `userChanges`. After the delivery completes the worktree is removed; the branch `baked/wf/RUN_ID` stays.

`deliver --execute` performs the latest approved plan (or `--request ID`). It refuses a plan approved by anyone but a person, a plan whose artifact no longer matches its digest, a changed remote URL, HEAD moved since the plan (`head_moved`) and files changed since the plan (`dirty_files`). The commit is built in a temporary index from the approved content; the user's checkout, index, HEAD and files are not touched, and the new branch is created only if it does not exist. The author and committer date is the plan's time, so the same plan always produces the same commit ID. Commit hooks do not run because no checkout is used; the push runs the local `pre-push` hook and the remote's hooks. The push names the branch without `+`, so Git only creates or fast-forwards it. Delivery never force-pushes, merges, resets, rebases or deletes anything.

Every operation is a `delivery` effect in the run's journal with its plan ID, branch and commit. A failure Git or the host reports for certain (non-fast-forward, hook rejection, authentication, unreachable remote, HTTP 401/403) marks the effect `failed` and stops with that reason; the next `--execute` retries only the failed step. When the outcome is not known (a crash, a dropped connection, a lost API response), the runtime checks before doing anything again: the local branch for the commit, `git ls-remote` for the push, the pull request by head branch for the pull request. A found result is recorded as `reconciled`; a confirmed absence as `failed`, which allows a retry. If the check itself cannot be answered, the effect stays `unknown` and a `recovery` request is created, as for v1 commands; it is answered with `recover` (`record-applied`, `record-not-applied`, `retry`, or `cancel`, which abandons the delivery). A later `--execute` that can check the remote settles the effect itself and supersedes the request. When everything is done, `delivery/PLAN_ID/result.json` links the commit ID, the branch, the remote and the pull request URL to the run, its revision at planning, the accepted snapshot and the approval root. Executing a delivered plan again returns that result and creates nothing.

The pull request body is the [run report](#run-report) followed by a `Delivery` section: the delivered files, the files left out with their reason, and the run, snapshot and base commit. A report longer than 60,000 characters is shortened (long tables keep their first rows, then the text is cut with a note) so GitHub accepts it; the Delivery section is always kept whole. The body is part of the plan, so its digest covers the exact text.

Pull requests use the GitHub REST API. The token is read from the environment variable named in the configuration (default `GITHUB_TOKEN`) and sent only to the configured API URL; github.com remotes map to `https://api.github.com`, other API URLs (GitHub Enterprise) serve remotes on their own host. Other hosts are delivered with `--no-pr` (commit and push only).

```json
{ "workflows": { "delivery": { "tokenEnv": "GITHUB_TOKEN", "apiUrl": "https://api.github.com" } } }
```

### Local delivery without a remote

`--local` delivers into the branch that is checked out, for a repository without a remote, such as a folder BAKED [initialized for worktree runs](#worktree-execution). The plan has the operations `commit` and `advance`, `remote: null` and no pull request; `--remote`, `--follow-up`, `--answer-threads`, `--draft` and `--no-pr` are refused with it. The same approval by digest applies.

- The commit is built as for any delivery, on HEAD in a temporary index, and recorded on the branch `baked/FEATURE-…` (or `--branch`). A repository without `user.name`/`user.email` gets the author `BAKED <noreply@baked.invalid>` instead of `missing_identity`.
- `advance` moves the checked-out branch (`base.branch`) from `base.commit` to that commit with a compare-and-swap `git update-ref`, then writes the delivered files into the working copy and stages exactly their new content (a path-limited `git reset`). The person's other uncommitted changes stay as they are.
- Planning refuses with `dirty_files` when a delivered file in the working copy is neither what HEAD holds nor what the delivery commits, so the person's own edit is never overwritten. A working-copy run whose files are already there is committed as is.
- `--execute` checks again before the commit, so a refusal leaves no record branch: `head_moved` when another branch is checked out or the branch moved since the plan, `dirty_files` for an edit made after approval. Prepare a new plan after either.
- An interrupted `advance` is checked against the branch: at the commit it is `reconciled` and the working copy is brought up to it (a file the person edited since is left alone), at the base it is retried.

A worktree run's worktree is removed after a local delivery as after any other; its `baked/wf/RUN_ID` branch stays. An initiative's features are delivered one at a time, upstream features first, since a dependent feature's files start from its upstream results.

### Follow-up commits to a delivered pull request

A later run can add its commit to the branch and pull request of a run that was already delivered, for example a `fix-ci` run for a check that failed on that pull request, or a run that answers its review:

```sh
baked workflow deliver RUN_ID --follow-up DELIVERED_RUN_ID
```

The plan takes the branch and remote of the followed run's latest pushed delivery and builds the commit on that branch's remote tip (`base.commit`) instead of HEAD; `base.branch` stays the pull request's base. Its operations are `commit` and `push`: no pull request is opened, and `followUp` names the followed run and its pull request, which shows the new commit. The usual checks apply against that tip instead of HEAD, so a file whose content when the run started differs from the branch is refused as `base_mismatch`. A run that works on the followed run's result from the working copy (its files are still there, uncommitted) therefore delivers just its own change. The local copy of the branch, if there is one, must point at the remote tip and not be checked out in the working copy or a worktree (`branch_moved`, `branch_checked_out`): execution fast-forwards it with the commit and never changes a checkout. The push is a fast-forward as always; when someone pushed to the branch after the plan, the push is refused as `non_fast_forward`, and the next plan needs that commit fetched. `--branch`, `--draft` and `--no-pr` plan a new branch and are refused with `--follow-up`. The TUI and desktop approval views show which branch and pull request the commit goes to.

### Following a delivered pull request's checks

```sh
baked workflow follow RUN_ID [--interval 60] [--timeout 60] [--once] [--fix] [--reviews]
```

`follow` reads the checks GitHub reports on the commit the run's latest delivery pushed: check runs (a completed run passes with `success`, `neutral` or `skipped`) and commit statuses. It is host code, with no model call and no tokens. It reads every `--interval` seconds (5 to 3600, default 60) and ends at the first failed check, when every check passed, or after `--timeout` minutes (1 to 1440, default 60), with `outcome` `failed`, `passed` or `timeout`; no check reported yet counts as still running. `--once` reads once. It needs a GitHub remote and the delivery token (`workflows.delivery.tokenEnv`); a run without a pushed delivery is refused as `not_delivered`.

With a failed check the answer has `fix`: a `fix-ci` run for the failed checks, with the feature `FEATURE-ci` (`-2`, `-3` when taken) and a request that names the pull request, the commit and each failed check with GitHub's summary and link. `--fix` starts that run as `baked workflow run --workflow fix-ci` would; it reproduces the failure with the project's checks, plans, asks for approval and fixes. Its answer names the next step, `baked workflow deliver FIX_RUN_ID --follow-up RUN_ID`, which puts the fix on the same pull request ([follow-up commits](#follow-up-commits-to-a-delivered-pull-request)). Follow the fix run the same way until the checks pass. Every commit and push still waits for a person's approval of its plan.

The TUI run view offers K (PR checks) on a completed run: it reads the checks once and, when one failed, asks whether to start the fix run (Y). The desktop run screen shows a Pull request panel on a completed, delivered run with the checks, Check again, and Fix failed checks, which starts the fix run and opens it. `GET /v1/workflow/runs/RUN_ID/checks` is the same read over the [local API](../desktop/api.md).

### Repairing from review comments

```sh
baked workflow follow RUN_ID --once --reviews [--fix]
baked workflow deliver FIX_RUN_ID --follow-up RUN_ID --answer-threads THREAD_ID,THREAD_ID [--resolve]
```

Review comments are read only with `--reviews`. Once no check failed (a failed check is fixed first, as above), `follow` also reads the pull request's review threads and the latest review of each reviewer over GitHub's GraphQL API, again host code with no model call. The answer's `reviews` has `state` `changes` or `clear`, the unresolved `threads` (`id`, `path`, `line`, `outdated` and the comments with author, body and link) and `changesRequested`, the reviews whose latest state requests changes. Resolved threads are left out; comments from review bots count like any other thread. With changes asked for, `repair` is a `quick-fix` run with the feature `FEATURE-review` (`-2` and onward when taken) and a request that quotes each thread and review as data, with `threads`, the IDs its delivery answers. `--fix` starts that run, and the answer's `next` is the follow-up delivery that answers those threads.

`--answer-threads` adds the operation `review_replies` to a follow-up plan, after `commit` and `push`. When the plan is made, each thread must be an unresolved review thread of the followed run's pull request (`thread_not_open` otherwise). The plan lists the threads and the reply, `Addressed in {commit}.`, where `{commit}` is the pushed commit. `--resolve` also resolves them. The person who approves the plan's digest therefore approves the replies too, and nothing is posted before that approval. Each reply carries the plan's marker (`<!-- baked-delivery:PLAN_ID -->`), so a retry after a lost response finds the reply already there and does not post it again. The token needs permission to write pull request comments.

The TUI run view offers V (PR reviews) on a completed run: it reads the checks and reviews once and asks whether to start the repair run (Y), naming the delivery flags. The desktop Pull request panel has Read review comments, which lists the unresolved threads, and Fix review comments, which starts the repair run and shows the delivery command. The approval view of a follow-up plan lists the threads it answers. `GET /v1/workflow/runs/RUN_ID/checks?reviews=true` is the same read over the API, and `POST .../deliver` takes `answerThreads` and `resolve`.

## Initiatives

Each feature must have a writable target for its deliverables. The host checks test ownership after accounting for its automatic sibling merges, so a merged feature may supply its own test paths. With `--keep-features`, or when different dependents prevent a merge, the features keep separate boundaries. A proposed feature with source files and no path that can hold tests must set `testedBy` to the ID of a feature with test paths that depends on it. The host returns a split without that owner to the decomposer. `testedBy` is only for tests that need the later feature's code: a feature that builds on no other feature's code (none of its `dependsOn` features writes more than host-written skeleton stubs) keeps its own tests, and the owner must write code the tests need: a feature of tests only (test files, test-support files such as a test project's `.csproj`, `conftest.py` or Go `testdata`, and docs) runs after the code it tests and makes the longest chain longer, and it stays one after the host merges it with a small docs sibling (wheeltags, bench round 58: base, names and targets gave their tests to two check-only features after the front end, base's `tests` check collected nothing and the initiative waited, #1873). The host returns such a split to the decomposer with one problem per feature. A test file the request prescribes rows for (a fenced block whose first line names the file and says it holds rows, read by `suppliedRows`) belongs to a feature that can pass every row: a row that holds a literal quoted by the contract or scope of a feature that depends on the file's owner, and by no other feature, needs that later feature (ratdec, bench round 80: core owned all 16 rows of `test/stdin_test.exs`, one of which needed the mixed output of representations; a core task rewrote the row to pass and representations then failed on it, #2487). The rule is the `initiative-row-owner` [stop reason](#stop-reasons-and-shadow-mode), shadow by default: the initiative records a would-stop naming the file, the row and the later feature when the split is proposed. With `stopModes: { "initiative-row-owner": "enforce" }` the decomposer gets the sentence back ("give the file to representations (it depends on core), or ask for the row in the brief to be split into a file of representations"), and a split that still has it after the host's merges is refused. A split a person supplies (`--plan`) is not checked for it. The spec validator also returns automated criteria whose test or documentation evidence can only live outside the current boundary. A feature with no path that can hold tests runs with property-based tests off, and when its spec author still marks a criterion `property`, the host clears the flag and keeps the criterion automated instead of returning the spec: its behavior is this feature's, only its tests are not. `spec-repairs/boundary-property.json` lists the cleared criteria (wheeltags, bench round 59: names and targets rewrote their specs two and one more times for it, #1891). Shared paths and broad boundaries that can hold tests keep their own verification. Missing criterion coverage in a convergence report asks for implementation or evidence within the approved scope; it does not itself require a test-file target.

Review and convergence findings use paths in the file, claim, suggested fix and repro to identify sibling ownership. A pathless request for tests or documentation can use the designated test owner, or the sole sibling whose boundary can hold that deliverable. Mixed findings that also name a current path remain actionable here. Sibling findings become review notes naming the owner, without corrective tasks or known limitations. Criteria that fail only for sibling-owned findings are deferred to that owner, and the final review report says so. A finding without an owner or an approved target of the required kind stops for replanning before starting a corrective attempt. These rules apply to the TUI, headless CLI and desktop through the core workflow service and run report.

When tests belong to a later feature, the mutation gate keeps the source changes in `quality/mutation-deferred.json` instead of scoring them before tests exist. The test owner's mutation gate includes those source changes after its tests land, with results in that owner's `quality/mutation.json`. This also remeasures source from completed runs made by an older binary that already recorded a premature score.

An initiative is one big idea split into 3 to 12 features, each with a boundary, a contract it provides to other features, the features it depends on, the workflow its runs use and its size. The split is approved once, as a whole; then the initiative runs one ordinary workflow run per feature in dependency order, in the same workspace: one at a time inline, and independent features side by side when every feature's workflow runs in worktrees. It is stored next to the runs in `.baked/workflows.sqlite` (migration 004): the initiative, its immutable plan revisions, the split approval requests, the links from features to run IDs, an append-only event log and idempotent commands.

```sh
baked workflow initiative start ID (--prompt TEXT | --prompt-file FILE) [--plan FILE] [--workflow sdd] [--worktree]
    [--budget-usd N [--cost-tolerance auto|off|0.2]]
baked workflow initiative show ID
baked workflow initiative rename|remove|merge|split ID ... --expected-revision N
baked workflow initiative set-workflow ID --feature F --workflow W --expected-revision N
baked workflow initiative set-size ID --feature F --size small|medium|large --expected-revision N
baked workflow initiative approve ID --request ID --digest SHA256 --expected-revision N
baked workflow initiative drive ID
baked workflow initiative pause|resume ID --expected-revision N
baked workflow initiative replan ID --feature F --expected-revision N
baked workflow initiative extend ID --expected-revision N [--tokens N] [--calls N] [--time-ms N] [--budget-usd N]
baked workflow initiative reroute ID --model ID --expected-revision N [--resume]
```

Initiative IDs and feature IDs are lower case slugs. A feature's runs use the workflow feature ID `INITIATIVE_FEATURE`, so its accepted spec is `specs/INITIATIVE_FEATURE/spec.json`. A run owns its feature ID: a spec whose `featureId` names another feature is stored with the run's ID instead of failing the specify stage.

**Split.** Without `--plan`, `start` runs the built-in `initiative-decompose` workflow: one agent stage with the `decomposer` profile, which reads the code and returns an `initiativePlan` (title, goals, completion criteria, and features with `id`, `title`, `boundary.scope`, `boundary.paths`, `contract`, `dependsOn`, `mandatory`, and optionally `workflow` and `size`). It changes no file. Every feature is a run of its own with a fixed cost besides its tasks (research, specification, planning, analysis and review), so the decomposer gives each feature at least three files or a group of at least three related behaviors and merges a smaller one into a sibling with the same dependencies; it keeps one apart only when no sibling shares its dependencies, and then gives it a light workflow unless other features depend on it. In bench round 32 the notesift split had six one-file features that each paid 0.25M to 0.34M tokens of planning and 0.11M to 0.34M of review for 0.48M to 0.78M of task work. The host enforces the floor on the decomposer's split: features with one or two boundary paths, the same dependencies and the same `mandatory` are merged into one feature of at most 8 paths (scopes and contracts joined, as `merge --features`, except a skeleton-only part's, below), as long as the split keeps three features. Paths are counted only when every one names a file: a feature without paths, or with a glob such as `src/parser/**` (one entry and any number of files), is left as it is. With the critical-path setting below, siblings also need the same dependents to merge, since a merged feature makes every dependent of each part wait for all parts (bench round 45, grundykit: four analysis siblings of `rules` became one feature, and `solve`, which needs only one of them, waited for the other three). Dependents of a merged feature depend on the merged one. The merge is recorded in a `split.merged` event, and the split approval shows each merged feature with `mergedFrom`, the IDs the decomposer gave (TUI "Merged from", desktop "merged from"). When the request fixes a package skeleton up front (module files that hold only a comment until their feature fills them, and the `lib.rs`, `mod.rs`, `__init__.py` or index file that declares them), the decomposer lists those files with their whole content as the `skeleton` of the first feature that owns them (`[{path, content}]`, each a literal path of that feature's boundary, in one feature only; merging features keeps every file, and splitting one gives each file to the first part whose paths name it). That feature's run is started with the files as `hostFiles`: its planner gets them as `extra.hostFiles` and must write all of them in one [host-written task](#plan-time-sizing) with exactly that content, so no agent writes them, and a plan that does not is returned at submission. Later features replace the stubs through their own boundary. The run's request quotes each stub's content (as a JSON string) and tells every stage to quote it exactly, names the stubs the feature implements (`implements`, below), and names each stub another feature's boundary covers, literally or through a glob, with that feature, so the run leaves those as written. A spec whose requirement or criterion quotes a comment as a stub's content (a sentence that names a host-written file, a stub, placeholder or skeleton) that differs from every line of the fixed content goes back to the spec author with the real content before planning. In bench round 57 (modeplan) the spec author, told only that the split fixes the content, asked what it is and wrote another wording into AC-001; the plan used the real text and analysis sent the feature through a second specify, plan and analyze round (407k tokens, 8.8 minutes at the head of the critical path, #1847). The skeleton is part of the feature's digest, and the estimate does not count its files as paths to price. `show` and `GET /v1/initiatives/ID` give each feature's `skeleton`; the split approval lists it (TUI "Skeleton written by the host", desktop "skeleton written by the host"). In bench round 40 a 20-module skeleton cost 2.58M tokens as agent tasks. The host-written task's content may differ from the skeleton in whitespace only, so a planner that lays the stubs out for the project's formatter check is not refused. A formatter can also reorder a skeleton: rustfmt sorts consecutive `mod` lines and consecutive `use` lines, and gofmt sorts the lines of an import block, so a `lib.rs` whose request fixes the layer order of its `pub mod` lines was rewritten by the required `cargo fmt` check's fixer and the spec's exact-content criterion failed (bench round 70, evalpin: three review rounds and a converge finding, USD 0.60, #2222). These formatters sort only within a group of consecutive lines, so with `skeletonLayout: "on"` in the initiative's base policy the host puts a blank line before each skeleton line the language's formatter would move, before the split is proposed ([skeleton-layout.ts](../../src/core/workflows/initiatives/skeleton-layout.ts)). The change is whitespace only, so the request's order, the spec's quotes and every host-file check still match. Without the setting the layout is shadow: the content stays as the decomposer wrote it and the lines it would change are recorded in a `split.skeleton_layout` event (`mode`, and per file `featureId`, `path`, `formatter`, `lines`, and `marker` for the layout below). A split given with `--plan` is not laid out. Formatters that sort imports across blank lines keep an order only at their own split marker, so when the project's configuration turns such sorting on, every entry of the list after the first gets the marker before it (#2233): `# isort: split` after a blank line for isort and ruff's I001 (a `select` or `extend-select` with `I` or `ALL` in `pyproject.toml`, `ruff.toml` or `.ruff.toml`, or an isort config), and a detached `// order as written` comment between blank lines for Biome's organizeImports (a `biome.json` and a `package.json` script that runs `biome check` or `biome ci`, organizeImports not turned off; the gates' `biome format` keeps the order) and for clang-format's include sorting (a `.clang-format` without `SortIncludes: Never`; any non-include line ends a block, IncludeBlocks: Regroup included). The marker adds a comment line, which the spec author and the reviewers see in the skeleton like the rest of the host content. google-java-format, ktlint, `dotnet format`, swift-format and `ordered_imports` have no marker the layout knows and stay unlaid. A feature whose boundary is only its skeleton has nothing for an agent to do, but its run would still pay research, specification, planning, analysis and review (bench round 50, selwhy: 298k tokens and 280 s for 24 stubs). Unless `--keep-features` is set, its skeleton moves to the dependent that every other dependent already waits for, and the other dependents depend on that feature, so no chain gets longer. The fold is recorded in the `split.merged` event like a merge of small siblings. Whenever a feature that is only skeleton is merged with a feature that fills some of its stubs (the fold, a small-sibling merge or `merge --features`), its scope and contract are not joined as written but restated: the host writes the stubs, and the run's request says which of them the feature implements. The merge records those as the feature's `implements`: the stubs another part's boundary names, literally or through a glob, plus the parts' own `implements`, so a second merge or a split keeps them right (a split keeps the ones of the skeleton files each part takes). In bench round 56 (hdrlens) the joined scope read "Create all 25 internal stubs; do not implement them" next to base's own text, options and IO stubs, and specify, plan and analyze looped four times on the contradiction (503k tokens, 15.1 minutes at the head of the critical path, #1829). Such a feature stays when its dependents run side by side or when the fold would leave fewer than three features. In bench round 35 six one-file narrowfit features spent 67 to 80 percent of their 3.13M tokens on fixed stages. A small leaf of the decomposer's split (nothing depends on it, it has one or two literal boundary paths or size `small`, never size `large` or a glob boundary) that runs a workflow writing a spec runs `quick` instead, when `quick` can run a feature here and none of its paths is risky (migrations, auth, secrets, credentials, `.github`, deployment, Dockerfile, schemas, databases, payments, billing, permissions): nobody pins a leaf's spec, and in bench round 36 seqmill's documentation leaf cost $1.42 under `sdd` against about $0.5 for a quick run. The paths decide, not the words: bench round 37's `release` leaf (README, LICENSE, a test) reads as risky by its name. The change is recorded in a `split.lightened` event and shows as the feature's workflow; a leaf the rule fits that keeps its workflow because `quick` cannot run here is listed under `unavailable` in the same event; `set-workflow` changes it back before approval. `--keep-features` (TUI Features "keep", desktop "Keep small features apart", `keepFeatures` in `POST /v1/initiatives`) keeps the decomposer's split and workflows as they are; a split given with `--plan` is never merged or changed. `split` undoes a merge before approval. Goals and completion criteria are outcomes of the product; a goal that is a rule for writing specs (a count of acceptance criteria) reaches each feature run under "Rules for writing this feature's specification" instead of the initiative goals, so no feature turns it into a criterion it cannot meet. Duplicate IDs, unknown or repeated dependencies and cycles go back to the decomposer for correction. A split of more than 12 features is not corrected: the initiative becomes `refused` with the message to split the request into stages and start one initiative per stage. With `--plan FILE` the split is given by the user and validated the same way, including the bound.

**Critical path.** Features on the longest chain of dependencies run one after another however many feature runs may start at once, so one large feature there sets the wall time (bench round 43, intsmith: one feature of five independent modules took 38% of the wall time, and the parallel speedup was 1.04x). `--split-critical-path` (TUI Path "split", desktop "Split the critical path", `splitCriticalPath` in `POST /v1/initiatives`, or `criticalPath: "split"` in the run policy) has the decomposer look at it when it submits the split ([critical-path](../../src/core/workflows/initiatives/critical-path.ts)). The host finds the chain with the most work. A feature counts its size weight (small 1, medium 2, large 4) or half its boundary files, whichever is more, where the files are counted as for the estimate, without the files of its own skeleton; a feature that is only skeleton counts nothing, since the host writes it and folds it into a dependent (bench round 56, hdrlens: its 25 stubs made the skeleton feature 48% of the path and sent the decomposer's first split back, #1829). A feature on that chain that holds more than 35% of its work and at least six files goes back to the decomposer inside `submit_result`. The decomposer then splits it into features with the same dependencies, each with at least three files, so they run side by side, and makes the features that depended on it depend on every part. Or it sets the feature's `serial` to why its parts must stay in one feature. The reason shows on the feature in the split approval ("Kept whole on the critical path" in the TUI, "kept whole on the critical path" on the desktop, `serial` in the feature view). A split of 12 features gets no such request. The setting also asks about a long chain (bench round 45, grundykit: 8 of 9 features ran in series, the largest 16% of the path, and the speedup was 1.08x): when the longest chain has at least five features and at least 80% of the split's work, the decomposer is told to drop the dependency edges a feature's own tasks do not use and to split a feature by the parts its dependents need, or to set the plan's `chain` to why each feature on it needs the one before. The chain check still runs at 12 features because dropping edges needs no extra feature slot; a split above 12 is refused without correction. Each extra feature pays its own research, specification, planning, analysis and review (0.25M to 0.45M tokens per feature in round 43), so the setting is off by default. Whatever the setting, `show` reports the latest plan's `criticalPath`: its features, `workShare` (the path's share of the work by size and files), `speedup` (the most the split can gain over one feature at a time with `parallelRuns` runs at once: the total work over the longer of the path and an even spread) and `chain`. The TUI and the desktop show it as a "Critical path" line under the initiative's plan and budget, so a split edit shows its effect before approval.

**Light contracts.** A small feature runs the light workflow (`quick`) only when nothing depends on it, because its dependents pin an accepted spec and `quick` has no spec stage. In bench round 50 (selwhy) four command features of one to three files each had a dependent, so they ran `sdd`, and their fixed stages cost 1.1x to 4.4x their task tokens (a 156k-token plan stage for a one-task graph). `--light-contracts` (TUI Contracts "light", desktop "Light runs for small contracts", `lightContracts` in `POST /v1/initiatives`, or `lightContracts: "on"` in the run policy) counts `quick` as a workflow that provides a contract, and the split moves a small feature (one or two literal paths or size small, not large, no glob, no risky path) to `quick` whether or not other features depend on it. Its run starts with the feature's contract: the host-made spec states it in scope and in its one requirement, so the review checks it, and the run records that spec as its `spec.json` artifact. Every light feature run of such an initiative records it, whether or not anything depends on the feature yet, because status reads a contract for every feature of a contract workflow and a replan or an extension can add a dependent later; a light run that recorded none counts as a result without a contract, accepted once its verification is current. A dependent pins its contract hash like any accepted spec, and its prompt quotes the contract text instead of a published `specs/` file, since `quick` publishes none. Off by default: the dependent then builds on the contract text and the code, not on a spec with several requirements.

**Feature workflows and sizes.** Each feature runs the workflow that fits it ([workflows.ts](../../src/core/workflows/initiatives/workflows.ts)). The decomposer gets the project's workflows that can run a feature as `extra.featureWorkflows` (each `id`, `description` and whether it writes a spec) and picks one per feature, with a size: `small`, `medium` or `large`. A feature can run under a workflow that starts without problems and writes files; with tiered approval only schema 1 workflows and the shipped `quick` preset qualify. A `quick` feature run under tiered approval approves its plan within the feature's tiered limits, as `sdd` approves its spec: the run's `approve` stage becomes an auto approval stage bound to the boundary limits, and a plan outside them still waits for a person (bench round 37: without it, quick was never offered under tiered approval and a release leaf ran `sdd` for 1.65M tokens). A project's own v2 workflow keeps its own auto approval and does not qualify. A feature other features depend on needs a workflow that writes a spec (such as `sdd`), because its accepted spec is the contract they pin; a feature nothing depends on may run under one without a spec (such as `quick`), and is accepted when its run completes with current verification. A feature without a workflow, from a user's or the coordinator's split, gets the one [`proposeWorkflow`](../../src/core/workflows/preset-router.ts) proposes for its title, scope and contract among the workflows that fit it, except that a change [`assessRequest`](../../src/core/workflows/preset-router.ts) rates as high risk keeps the initiative's default workflow (`--workflow`); a feature without a size gets the size the assessment gives. A decomposer's workflow that cannot run its feature is replaced the same way; a user's is refused with the reason. `set-workflow` and `set-size` change one feature before approval or while paused, each as a new plan revision; changing the workflow supersedes the feature's run on approval, changing the size does not. Plans stored before features had a workflow run every feature under the initiative's workflow.

**Approval of the split** is mandatory. Each plan revision has a digest over the initiative ID, the revision number, the plan (with each feature's workflow and size), the shared budget and the settings fixed at start (default workflow, base policy hash, tiered approval, model). The pending `split_approval` request names that digest, and `approve` must repeat it. Before approval, `rename` (title only), `remove` (dependents lose the edge), `merge --features A,B` (one feature with the union of boundaries, contracts and external dependencies) and `split --feature F --parts FILE` (dependents depend on every part) each produce a new revision, a new digest and a new request; the older request is superseded. An edit that leaves fewer than 3 features, an unknown ID or a cycle is refused.

**Execution.** `drive` (also run by `start`, `approve` and `resume`) advances the initiative until a person is needed, it is blocked or paused, or it completes. When any feature's workflow runs inline, one feature run is open at a time: the runtime guards each run's source snapshot, so a second open run would block the first. `--worktree` (and every initiative the coordinator starts from a split) runs feature runs in worktrees, so independent features run side by side. A feature starts when every feature it depends on is accepted: its run completed with current verification and its spec is the newest approved spec of that feature. The new run is started with those upstream contract hashes pinned as its spec dependencies (the spec author's own dependencies are replaced), so its readiness uses the existing `contractHash` check. Decisions inside a feature run use the run commands (`baked workflow approve RUN_ID ...`); `show` lists them under `pendingDecisions` with the run and request IDs. Under `baked serve` (the desktop app), a feature run resumed after such a decision continues its active initiative once it stops, so the next features start without a separate `drive`.

**Features in worktrees.** When the feature workflow runs in worktrees (a v2 definition with `execution: worktree`, or a base policy with `executionMode: "worktree"`), `show` reports `parallelRuns`, the workspace's [`workflows.maxParallelRuns`](#parallel-runs) (1 inline). With `workflows.parallelRunsFromPlan: true` (off by default) it is the plan's width instead, the most features none of which depends on another directly or through others, up to `maxParallelRuns` (3 when unset): a plan with six independent features after a serial head starts three at once, a plan two wide starts two whatever the cap (bench round 55, routeshadow: six such features ran two at a time, and a replay of the measured durations took 12 percent less wall time on 3 slots; R56 measured 1.51x on 3 slots against 1.33x on 2, #1822). The critical path line of the split approval names both, `with 3 at once (plan width 6)`, and `criticalPath.width` carries the width. Each drive round starts ready features while fewer than `parallelRuns` feature runs can execute and drives them at the same time. Whenever one of them stops, the freed slot goes at once to the next ready feature, so a feature whose dependencies are accepted does not wait for its running siblings (bench round 44, cayleykit: features started in waves, and slots stayed idle for 8.3 minutes while the slowest feature of each wave ran).
