With more than one slot, the ready feature with the most work left on its longest chain of dependents starts first (the same work measure as for the critical path above), and plan order breaks ties; with one slot, features start in plan order; the start checks, the budget share and the cost forecast look at that feature too. A feature that waits for a decision or is blocked stops the drive only when no other feature can move. A feature's worktree starts from HEAD merged with the accepted result of each feature it depends on (the commit on that run's `baked/wf/RUN_ID` branch), so its agents see the code they build on; a feature without dependencies starts from HEAD. The merge is made with `git merge-tree` and `git commit-tree` without touching the working copy, with a fixed author and date, so the same results give the same base commit. Features whose boundary paths name a common file run one after the other: every proposed split (from the decomposer or an edit) gives the later of two such features in plan order a dependency on the nearest earlier one, unless a dependency path already joins them, and records the added edges in a `split.sequenced` event. When the earlier one runs a workflow without a spec (such as a `quick` leaf) and the later one writes a spec, the edge goes the other way, since a feature without a spec cannot be built on. A light feature that still gains a dependent runs the initiative's default workflow (or the first one that writes a spec) instead, listed under `restored` in the same event; before, the dependent stopped at `ARTIFACT_MISSING` on every drive. A path is common when both list it, or when one lists it literally and the other's glob matches it; two different globs are not compared. A documentation feature, one whose every boundary path is documentation or examples (Markdown, reStructuredText or AsciiDoc files, `docs/`, `examples/` or `samples/` folders, a README or changelog), runs after the code it describes: it gets a dependency on the last feature of every chain of the other features, unless a dependency path already joins them, listed under `documented` in the same event (bench round 52, srcwhere: a docs feature without dependencies started first and documented code that did not exist yet). A `quick` feature whose every dependent is a documentation feature keeps `quick` instead of being restored to a spec workflow, and its run records its host-made spec as the contract the docs feature pins, as under `lightContracts`; a docs feature reads the code, not a spec (#1768: a small entry feature at the end of a chain otherwise paid the fixed stages of `sdd`). A code feature that builds on it still restores the spec workflow. A license or CI configuration does not make a feature documentation, and a feature with code or tests beside its docs is ordered only by its own dependencies. A feature that already has a run keeps its dependencies. Siblings that each add a line to one registration file (`lib.rs`, `mod.rs`, an index) otherwise produce results that cannot be merged (bench round 34, sketchfold: four features added a `pub mod` line to `src/lib.rs`). When two upstream results still conflict, the feature is `blocked` with the conflicting files and the repair (merge the upstream features that wrote them with `merge --features`, or re-plan one of them), recorded in a `feature.upstream_conflict` event, and the drive stops with that reason; a new split approval clears it. A rebased run starts from the new upstream results. Each feature's delivery carries its upstream commits; deliver the upstream features first. Features started in the same round take equal shares of the remaining budget, as below.

**Forecast stop** (`--forecast-stop`, TUI Forecast, desktop "Stop when the forecast is over the budget", `forecastStop` in `POST /v1/initiatives`; off by default) holds the next feature start while the low end of the [estimate](#initiatives) is above the shared budget: `next` is `blocked` with "The forecast is over the shared budget", and features already running go on. Raise the budget with `extend` or trim the split to go on. Without it, features start until their budget share is too small. With a dollar budget it also makes the dollar forecast stop binding from the first accepted feature (see [Dollar budget](#initiatives)), and feature runs keep the base policy's forecast stop. The setting is fixed at start and, when on, is part of the split approval digest.

**Tiered approval** (`--tiered-approval [--max-tasks 5] [--max-files 10]`, off by default) gives each feature run auto approval whose allowed paths are the feature's approved `boundary.paths`, without deletes or new dependencies, using the same auto approval check and the actor `auto-approval` as v2 definitions. `--max-tasks` and `--max-files` are a floor: a feature whose boundary names more files gets a file limit of that count and a task limit of that count plus 2, because the person approved those paths with the split and a planner often writes one task per file. A boundary with a glob (`commands/*Command.java`) stands for any number of files the person approved with it, so it gets the host's ceiling of 500 tasks and files; its allowed paths still send a plan that writes outside the boundary to a person (bench round 42: a 19-file plan inside two globs and three files waited for a person under 8-task, 14-file limits). A package manifest the boundary names (a `.csproj` the split gives a feature to set its `AssemblyName`, say) was approved with the split, so the feature run edits it under `requestedManifestEdits` without a person; deleting it, a lockfile (other than a named `Cargo.lock`, `package-lock.json`, `uv.lock` or `Gemfile.lock`, bounded to the project's own entry), a tool config, a manifest outside the boundary and a new dependency still go to a person. These host-set limits are not held to the caps of a definition's own `autoApproval` (20 paths and 20 tasks); they go up to 500 tasks and files, like a run policy's. A feature whose boundary names 22 files gets auto approval for a plan of up to 24 tasks; before, more than 20 boundary paths dropped it without a decline reason. The flags themselves accept up to 500. A plan that writes outside the boundary, or a feature without boundary paths, goes to a person. When the host builds no auto approval for a feature run (no boundary paths, or a rebased run, below), the run keeps the reason and its approval request shows it as `autoApproval.declined`, as every other declined auto approval is shown in `show`, the TUI and the desktop approval card (#1467). It requires a schema 1 feature workflow such as `sdd`; a v2 definition takes auto approval only from the definition, so the combination is refused at start. A v2 feature workflow with its own auto approval is started with `--confirm-auto-approval HASH`, which is passed to every feature run.

**Contract changes.** When an upstream feature is accepted again with a different contract hash (after `replan` or a split re-plan), each dependent whose run is pinned to the old hash becomes `stale`. The next drive cancels its open run if any, marks that run's evidence `input_changed`, and starts a rebased run that keeps the dependent's spec, pins the new upstream hashes and starts again at the plan stage, so the plan is made, analyzed and approved against the new contract. A rebased run never gets auto approval: a person always re-approves it, and under tiered approval its approval request says so in `autoApproval.declined`. A dependent that already finished keeps its result on record and is shown `waiting` while the upstream runs again.

**Idle initiatives.** A decision on a feature run (`baked workflow approve`, `answer`, `evidence`) continues only that run; the initiative advances inside a drive. So `show` (and `GET /v1/initiatives/ID`, `GET /v1/initiatives`) reports `drive`: `{state: "driving"}` while a drive of the process, or a live process executing one of its runs, advances it; `{state: "idle", text: "idle: waiting for a drive", command: "baked workflow initiative drive ID"}` when its next step is one a drive takes by itself (start, continue, rebase or complete a feature, or split) and nothing advances it; null when it waits for a person or is finished. The answer of a decision on a feature run carries the initiative's step as `initiative` (`initiativeId`, `status`, `next`, `drive`) and, when it is idle, `next` with the drive command, which the CLI also prints on stderr as `next: baked workflow initiative drive ID` (uritpl R65: a run answered from the CLI completed and the initiative sat idle for 18.4 minutes, #2024). `workflow answer` does not drive the initiative itself: its process did not start that drive and could run for hours. The terminal UI shows `idle: waiting for a drive` in the initiative's status with C to continue, and goes on with the initiative by itself after a feature run decided or resumed in its run screen stops idle; the desktop shows the same status with Continue (`POST /v1/initiatives/ID/drive`), and `baked serve` already continues an initiative after a feature run resumed through it stops. One rule decides the state: `initiativeDriveState` in [drive-state.ts](../../src/core/workflows/initiatives/drive-state.ts).

**Progress.** `show` returns the status, the next action and its reason, the plan revision and approval digest, and per feature: its state (`proposed`, `waiting`, `ready`, `in_progress`, `needs_decision`, `paused`, `blocked`, `failed`, `stale`, `accepted`), its blockers in words (for example `waits for core's contract (core is waiting for a decision)`), its pinned upstream hashes, its accepted contract hash, its pending decisions and its usage over all its runs. The initiative completes only when every mandatory feature is accepted; `initiative.completed` records each feature's run and contract hash.

**Shared budget.** Set it with `--budget-tokens N --budget-calls N --budget-time-ms N`; otherwise it is the base policy's per-run limits for every medium feature (half for a small one, double for a large one), plus one more. Each feature run starts with a share of what remains, rounded down: remaining = limit - (usage of finished runs + the limits of open runs, decompose and superseded runs included), times the feature's weight, divided by the weights of the features of the approved plan without a run. Tokens are weighted by each feature's estimated need, the high end of its range in the [estimate](#initiatives), when this project's run history, or the initiative's own accepted features, price every waiting feature; otherwise, and for calls and active time, by size (small 1, medium 2, large 4). Size says little about cost: in one bench initiative a small feature used 3.57M tokens and a large one 4.11M. `show` gives each such feature's `share` and its `need` (the estimated token range, null while only generic ranges exist). A rebased run shares what remains with the features still waiting as if it were one of them, by its estimated need, and grows only as a starting feature does (below). Agent calls per run are capped at 1000. When a share would fall below 1000 tokens, 1 call or 1 second, the initiative is blocked with the budget named; a feature starts if the remaining pool minus the other waiting features' low estimates covers its own low estimate. When the pool covers every low estimate, token shares reserve any lows that a weighted split would underfund, then split the rest by the same weights. If the pool cannot cover those reserves, the feature waits before its run starts ("The shared budget is short"). In both cases `extend` raises the shared limits (only upward). A feature run whose base policy lets it grow (`sizing.maxRunTokens`) grows only up to what remains less the low ends of the estimates of the other features still waiting, so an early feature cannot hold what the later ones need; its `sizing.maxRunTokens` and `sizing.extendToTokens` are lowered to that. When no waiting feature has an estimate, each other waiting feature reserves its whole token share instead, and with none waiting, as for an initiative's last open feature, the bound is what remains of the pool. That room is never empty: read as no limit, it took the base policy's ceilings, and bench round 73 evalpin's last feature, delivery, grew to 8M and 12M with 3.31M of its 23.9M pool left (#2294). Starts, rebases and the growth refresh below read the same room. A starting feature's agent calls and active time are what remains of the shared budget less a floor for each other waiting feature, never below its own share; a floor is the base policy's per-run limit scaled by size (half for a small feature, double for a large one), at most that feature's share. Both are stuck-run guards rather than costs, and a finished run holds only what it used: split by size alone, the first medium feature of a 6 hour, 7 feature initiative got 38 minutes, needed 40 and blocked with most of the time unused. A feature run that exhausts its own share blocks like any run and is extended with `baked workflow extend RUN_ID`.

**Dollar budget.** Without one, each feature run keeps its base policy's own `maxCostUsd`, and nothing adds the dollars up across features (one bench initiative spent $29.15 of a $32 limit with no signal from the host). `--budget-usd N` gives the initiative a dollar cap its runs share, and `--cost-tolerance` a band past it like a run's [cost tolerance](#cost-cap): a share of the cap (0 to 0.5), `off` (the default), or `auto`, which takes 10% for up to 3 features, 15% up to 6, 20% up to 9, 25% up to 12 and 30% beyond. The dollars use the token weights without low-estimate reserves: remaining = cap - (the spending of finished runs + the caps of open runs), times the feature's token weight, divided by the token weights of the features without a run. Each feature run starts with its share as its `maxCostUsd`, with the initiative's `costTolerance`. The base policy's own cap applies only to runs outside a dollar budget: under one, a feature's dollars come from the shared budget like its tokens, so a feature with a share above the base cap gets its share (bench round 42: a feature with a $9.80 share was held to the $8 base cap and stopped with $21 of the initiative's $30 unspent). Its cap can grow independently of its token budget (`sizing.maxRunCostUsd`), up to the remaining dollar budget less every other waiting feature's priced low estimate. Those estimates use the selected workflow stages, configured model routes and measured stage prices, with the default token mix for stages without samples. They do not derive a price from the allocated dollars per token. An unpriced feature reserves its whole dollar share. Once a feature on the same workflow is accepted (not a host-written skeleton), each stage's tokens are weighted by what the accepted features' runs took on it instead of the history's stage estimates, so a feature is priced at the mix the initiative actually spends. Bench round 59 (wheeltags) priced features at $0.97 per million tokens at the split, from long cache-heavy history loops; its accepted features, with short task loops and uncached planning calls, paid $1.95, and their own stage rates at the history's weights said $2.67 per million at pick-command's start, so the waiting features reserved too much and pick-command could grow its cap only to $2.87. Their own mix says $1.97, and lets it grow to $4.51 (#1890). That bound, the run's token ceiling (`sizing.maxRunTokens`, within the base policy's) and, for a run that has used 80 percent of them, its calls and active time are refreshed before each drive of an open feature run, and at every release of a planned-ahead run: to what the run holds plus what the initiative can spare at that moment, recorded as a policy revision (`policy-revisions/initiative-N.json`, `kind: "initiative-growth"`, `approvedBy: "sizing"`, with the inputs). Bounds only rise. Calls and active time are stuck-run guards rather than costs: their room is the initiative's limit less what every other run used (an open run counts at its use, not its limit) and the floors of the features still waiting, and a run that used 80 percent of a limit rises by at most a quarter per refresh, with no revision for a raise under 1 percent. A feature run's active-time deadline also asks the initiative for that growth before it stops, so a run that is the only one left, with no drive between, re-arms its deadline instead of blocking. When the deadline fires, the drive adopts the stored limits and re-arms on whatever time is left, whether or not that last look raised them: a refresh between model calls had already been adopted into the drive's copy in bench round 70 soundex, the guard refused a further step at 79.77 percent of the raised 1,852,623 ms, and the run stopped at its old 1,482,098 ms share and waited 30 minutes for a plain resume (#2216). A deadline that does run out says how much active time the run used and the stored limit it ran out against. A limit can also grow after the block: in R70 soundex the stored limit was raised to 2,315,779 ms 6 s after the deadline stopped the attempt at 1,477,761 ms used, and the run waited 29 minutes 52 seconds for an owner's plain resume, as a block on the run's own tokens or calls would (#2226). The run's block keeps the limits it decided on and its usage (`limit-blocks/`), and `storedLimitResume` (`stop-reasons.ts`) decides whether every stored limit now exceeds the usage (and the token limit fits the refused call): the drive asks right after it blocks, and an initiative's driver round asks for each blocked feature run. The `host-resume-on-stored-limit` reason ships in shadow: it records one `decision.would_resume` per block and the run stays blocked; resuming for real is a later change after two bench rounds with no false would-resume. In bench round 62 stepvet, the open runs counted at their limits left lines a room of 0 to 7 ms over 24 revisions, and lines and tagexpr stopped on their shares with a third of the initiative's 6 hours unused (#1958). The workflow service holds this growth itself, so a feature run driven from any entry point grows the same way, and every drive (`baked workflow resume`, `repair`, `extend --resume`, `reroute --resume`, `continue`, `accept`, `revise`) first grows the run's share to what the initiative can spare. In bench round 68 ewvet scanner stopped "Active deadline exhausted" 33 s after a headless `reroute --resume` with 37 percent of the initiative's time used, then over its cost cap with $12 of $30 spare: that process had made no initiative service before it drove the run, so the deadline asked nobody (#2134). A run's room to grow used to be fixed when it started: bench round 60's wheeltags explain started planning 98 minutes before its tasks ran (`planAhead`), stopped on a $3.41 bound when about $6 was spare, and an owner extension was approved and never used (#1931). Split approval warns when a feature's weighted dollar share is below its priced low estimate, even when the token budget fits. Beside those warnings it states what the split is expected to cost in all (what is spent plus each waiting feature's expected tokens at the dollars per token of its priced range, held inside that range) against the sum of their lows, and how many waiting features, in plan order, the dollars left under the cap pay for at that cost (#2218): the lows alone read as the split's cost, and bench round 70 (evalpin) extended to their $30.11 sum for seven features expected to cost about $36.5. The suggested `extend --budget-usd` amount still lifts the warned shares to their lows; the expected total is the budget that covers the whole split. Start, resume, replan and rebase use the same reserves. A model call grows the cap to its projected cost plus tolerance headroom, rounded up to the cent and limited by that bound, including before there are enough completed tasks for a cost forecast. This also covers correctives opened after a failing lint or type check, even when plan sizing has no price for them and no cost forecast is available. Reservations use the owning stage's measured rate for the selected model, falling back to the stage's token mix at the model's prices when the stage has no samples, and record `kind: "call-growth"`. If an attempt also needs more tokens, its affordability check first tries the approved dollar growth before extending the token budget. When the run's [cost forecast](#cost-cap) passes its ceiling and that bound can cover the forecast, the host grows the cap to the forecast plus tolerance headroom, rounded up to the cent and limited by that bound, instead of stopping, and records it as a policy revision with `kind: "forecast-growth"` and `approvedBy: "sizing"`; a forecast the bound cannot cover still stops, and the message says how much the initiative can spare for the run. A plan approval warns when the plan's priced high end (planning so far plus the planned tasks) passes the cap, or what the cap may grow to, with its tolerance. In bench round 35 a feature's tokens grew from 1.53M to 2.29M while its cap stayed $1.46, and it stopped on the cap with 62% of its tokens used and $14 of the initiative unspent. A feature does not start on less than $0.01 ("The shared dollar budget is exhausted"), and when what its cap may grow to (the remaining dollar budget less the other waiting features' priced low estimates) is below its own priced low estimate ("The shared dollar budget is short ... the dollar budget needs at least $X more"), the `initiative-dollar-share` stop reason records it: in `shadow`, its default, the feature starts and a `decision.would_stop` event keeps the message; with `stopModes: { "initiative-dollar-share": "enforce" }` the feature waits for `extend --budget-usd N`, as the token budget's "The shared budget is short" waits for tokens. The cap these blocks and the `initiative-dollar-cap` park name on their `extend` remedy (`suggestedUsd`) is counted from what the runs committed (spent plus what open runs hold), never from the remaining dollars, which stop at $0: once the tolerance is spent the committed dollars are above the cap, and bench round 73 (flatelens) was told a $33.96 cap with $34.64 spent, which would have left docs at $0 again (#2282). Applied, the named cap gives the feature at least its priced low estimate. In R59, priced at the initiative's own mix, dead-command and expand-command were short at their starts (#1890). The check waits for the first measured feature (`measuredFeatures` above 0): before it the waiting features' lows are only the plan's guess, and bench round 62 stepvet recorded two false would-stops at its first starts, lines and stepexpr, which started anyway and finished with $3.67 of the $30 unspent (#1714). Until 3 features are measured (`MIN_CALIBRATION_FEATURES`, the count the re-forecast's measured ratio waits for) the dollars per token are a guess too, so the check counts the remaining dollars up to the tolerance ceiling instead of the cap, and its message says the spare is "within the ceiling"; from 3 on the cap bounds it, and the bound a feature run's cap grows to is the cap-based one throughout. Bench round 67 ampwhy priced its waiting features at $1.83 and $1.50 per million at one measured feature, against the $1.33 the initiative paid, and recorded would-stops for reader and scanning, which started anyway; the initiative spent $16.24 of its $30 cap (#2099). The would-stop's inputs carry `remainingTo` (`ceiling` or `cap`), and the replay of each start (`replayInitiativeStarts`) keeps the would-stop the current code records there as `shareWouldStop`, with the initiative's final spend, so the corpus shows whether the dollars were ever short. Once a feature is accepted, the initiative forecasts its total from the [re-forecast](#initiatives): what was spent, plus each unaccepted feature's point forecast less what its runs already used, at the prices of the workflow stages and models still to run. The point tokens are divided by the workflow's stage estimates, with its task stages scaled by size separately from planning and review. Completed stages and tokens already used by an active stage are removed before dividing the remaining tokens. Existing runs use their frozen definitions and current routing, including reroutes; features without a run use their selected workflow and configured routes. Matching stage and model usage supplies the paid price, including cache discounts and writes. A new model uses the same stage's measured token mix at its own prices; a stage without samples uses the default estimate mix. An unknown future model price leaves the dollar forecast unavailable. Until three non-skeleton features are accepted, unaccepted features keep their planned estimates at those stage and model prices. Features with a host-written skeleton never supply a measured-to-planned calibration ratio. At three non-skeleton acceptances the ratio has one-third weight against the planned estimate, at four it has two-thirds weight, and at five it has full weight. The same minimum and blend apply separately per size; a size without three samples uses the overall blend. With enough samples, calibration can fall below 25% of the prior estimate. The ceiling gate, estimate dollar ranges and remaining dollar forecast all use this rule. The point forecast, reported as `expectedTokens` on each estimated feature, respects its criteria, boundary-path and sized-plan floors independently. It does not take the midpoint after those floors widen the token range, which would overprice equal remaining features even at a steady spend rate. The token budget recommendation adds its estimate margin once to work still to run; the dollar projection adds no estimate margin, and the dollar tolerance applies once to the cap's ceiling. For example, two of twelve equal features costing $4.54 project $27.22, within a $30 cap and its $37.50 ceiling with `auto` tolerance. The decompose run is paid once and does not repeat, and held or in-flight tokens carry no dollars yet, so neither enters that price (#1505). Once 5 accepted features that are not a host-written skeleton have measured the forecast, or with the forecast stop on, no feature starts while that forecast is above the cap and its tolerance ("The initiative's cost forecast is $X, above its $Y cap"), and `extend --budget-usd N` raises the cap; the base policy's `costForecast: "off"` turns this stop off, and `stopModes: { "initiative-forecast": "shadow" }` (or `"advisory"`) makes it record a would-stop instead (see [Stop reasons and shadow mode](#stop-reasons-and-shadow-mode)). Before that the forecast advises: `budget.cost.forecastNotice` says it is above the ceiling and how many features are measured, the TUI and desktop cost lines mark it `(advisory: N measured features)`, and the first feature started at each measured count records a `cost.forecast_advisory` event (`initiative events`, `/v1/initiatives/ID/events`). The forecast and its stop read the re-forecast's blended ratio (`progress.ratio` of the estimate); the notice also reads the measured-to-planned ratio itself (`progress.rawRatio`, capped at 5x like the blend), as the token notice does (#2062): from three measured features, `budget.cost.advice` is the same forecast with each feature still to run at that ratio instead of the blend that priced it (a feature its criteria, paths, plan or test cases floor keeps its point forecast), and it is set while it is above `forecastUsd` (#2186). While the blend fits the ceiling and the advice does not, `forecastNotice` says so, leading with the measured ratio and naming the blended one: "At the measured 1.6x of the estimates the cost forecast is $13.03 ($11.34 at the blended 1.2x that the forecast stop uses; $6.00 spent), above the $12.50 ceiling. The advice changes no decision: features keep starting while the blended forecast and their spent and reserved dollars fit the budget. A cap of $14.00 covers it (baked workflow initiative extend values --budget-usd 14)." A notice above the ceiling names both forecasts the same way, the TUI and desktop cost lines add the advice beside the forecast ("forecast $11.34, $13.03 at the measured 1.6x") and mark it advisory only while the blend itself is above the ceiling, and the `cost.forecast_advisory` event carries `advice`. At three features the blend weighs the measured ratio a third, so the notice used to lag it as the token notice did; on the R66 nodepluck trace the advice says $24.25 at syntax's acceptance and $26.15 at lint's, against $23.41 and $25.83 from the blend and a final $28.45, and neither passes the $37.50 ceiling. Every feature start, and the first park of a feature on its share, records a `feature.share_gate` event with what the share gate read (the remaining budget, the dollar view, the waiting features' shares and estimates) and its verdict, so a bench trace replays the gate (#1707). The replay also rewinds each recorded initiative to every feature start, with each run's tokens, dollars, agent calls, active time (interpolated between its `budget.updated` events, #2417) and policy and the initiative's budget as they stood then (#2061), and computes the view again with the current code, so a forecast regression shows on real rounds before it merges: at R50 selwhy's second start (1 of 12 accepted) the code before #1643's fix forecasts $71.49 there, and main $24.56, against the $23.63 it finally cost. Features keep starting while the dollars spent and held by open runs leave a share, and each feature run's own forecast only advises too (`costForecast: "advise"` in its policy): it still grows the run's cap into the initiative's spare dollars, and the cap on spent and reserved dollars still stops the run. In bench rounds 48 to 50 every forecast stop came after one or two accepted features and was 1.3 to 3.1 times what the initiative then cost; R50 selwhy was held 2 h 24 min at $44.69 and $72.98 against a $37.50 ceiling and finished at $23.53 (#1659). `show` gives `budget.cost` (`capUsd`, `tolerance`, `ceilingUsd`, `spentUsd`, `reservedUsd`, `remainingUsd`, `forecastUsd`, `advice`, `measuredFeatures`, `forecastBinding`, `forecastMode`, `shareMode`, `forecastNotice`) and each waiting feature's `shareUsd`, `needUsd` low and high dollar estimates, and `spentUsd`; the estimate command reports each feature's dollar range as `costUsd` when a dollar budget is set; `spentUsd` is reported without a cap too. The forecast and the estimate's dollar ranges price the same central stage weights, the accepted features' measured stage mix once there is one. Finished runs in this project and `sizing.historyStores` supply measured stage prices, including cache reads and writes, when the current initiative has not measured that stage and model. Current initiative measurements take precedence. The split approval digest covers the dollar budget when one is set.

**Token pool forecast and blocks with amounts.** The shared token pool is a hard limit with the split rule above; what surrounds it says how far it is from the work (#2042). `show` gives `budget.pool`: `limitTokens`, `usedTokens`, `committedTokens` (used plus what open runs may still use), `forecastTokens`, `ratio`, `advice`, `coverTokens` (the pool that starts every waiting feature at its high estimate beside what open runs hold, rounded up to 100k), `suggestedTokens`, `usdPerMillion` (what the initiative paid per million tokens so far) and `capTokens` (the tokens the dollar cap pays for at that rate). Once three features that are not a host-written skeleton are accepted, the forecast is what was used plus each unfinished feature's low estimate (the one the share gate gave it at its start, or its current one while it has no run) times how far the accepted features landed above theirs, blended like the dollar re-forecast (a third of the measured ratio at three, two thirds at four, all of it at five), never below what a running feature used; before that it is the estimate's point forecast (`expectedTokens`). That blended `forecastTokens` and `ratio` are what budget decisions read (`decisionRatio`); `advice` is the same forecast at the measured ratio itself from three features (`adviceRatio`, capped at 5x), which only the notice reads (#2062): a notice changes nothing, so it need not wait for the blend. While the higher of the two is above the pool, `budget.forecastNotice` (also `budget.pool.notice`) says so with the command, leading with the measured ratio and naming the blended one when it rounds differently: "At the measured 2.2x of the estimates the 10 features need about 33.2M tokens (31.6M at the blended 1.8x that budget decisions use; 26.7M used); the shared pool is 30.0M. Extend to 36.0M, what the $30.00 cap pays for at $0.83 per million, so the pool stops no feature before the dollar cap does (baked workflow initiative extend nodepluck --tokens 36000000)." With a dollar cap that pays for more tokens than the pool at the measured rate, the notice and `suggestedTokens` advise those tokens (`capTokens` rounded down to 100k) whatever the ratio says, because the ratio swings with the heavy features while the cap is a limit the person already set: bench round 71 (xmlvet) advised 31.6M at 1.7x and 50.9M at 3.1x for a round that used 35.8M, while its $30 paid for about 35M (#2244). Without a cap, or with one that pays for no more than the pool, the notice advises the forecast, and no less than what starts every waiting feature at its low estimate, rounded up, naming what the cap would pay for. The advice changes no decision. On the R66 trace the notice now comes at syntax's acceptance (14:27, 31.8M at 2.36x while the blend said 25.1M), 13 minutes before select parked, where the blend alone warned 41 seconds before. The TUI shows it as a Token forecast line, the desktop initiative screen beside the shared budget, every `feature.share_gate` event records the pool, and the first drive step that sees it for a pool size records a `budget.forecast_notice` event. Every block on the shared budget names the amounts that answer it and puts them on its `extend` remedy (`next.recovery.remedies[0]`): "The shared budget is short" says how many more tokens start the parked feature, the pool that covers the waiting features' high estimates and the dollar room ("select needs 181277 more tokens to start; a pool of 33.1M (33100000 tokens, 3100000 more than 30.0M) covers the high estimates of select, cli, docs. The dollar cap has $4.55 left ($23.06 of $30.00 spent, forecast $26.82) and would pay for about 35.5M tokens at $0.85 per million."), with `suggestedTokens` on the remedy and `--tokens N` in the command; an exhausted token share suggests the same cover. The cover is held to what the dollar cap pays for at the measured rate (`affordableTokens`, rounded down to 100k), as the notice, the split approval and the host's extension hold it, and the message says so ("... would pay for about 32.5M tokens at $0.92 per million, so extend to 32.5M (32500000 tokens)."); when the cap pays for too few tokens to start every waiting feature at its low estimate, the remedy keeps the cover and also names the cap that pays for it ("too few to start the waiting features: a pool of 33.1M also needs a dollar cap of $38.17.", with `--budget-usd N` and `suggestedUsd`). Bench round 75 (maskback) suggested 31.5M while its message said the $30 cap paid for about 26.4M, and the loop extended to 26.4M by hand (#2367); the dollar blocks (the forecast above the ceiling, an exhausted or short dollar share) put the cap that answers them in `--budget-usd N` and `suggestedUsd`, `forecastStop`'s token block the estimate with its margin, and the warnings before split approval the estimate with its margin or the cap that lifts the warned dollar shares to their low estimates. The TUI's B prefills the suggested amounts, so Enter applies them, and the desktop offers one button, "Extend to 33.1M tokens", beside Extend budget. In bench round 66 (nodepluck) the block named no amount, and the person extended from 30M to 38M 3.8 minutes after the drive exited, 4.75M more than the initiative used.

**Time forecast and blocks with a time amount.** The shared active time (`--budget-time-ms`) is the sum of the feature runs' active time, so with three runs at once it runs out three times as fast as the wall clock. `show` gives `budget.time` (#2336): `limitMs`, `usedMs`, `committedMs` (used plus what open runs may still use under their limits), `pace` (`{tokensPerMinute, basis, runs}`: the median of the initiative's own completed feature runs once there are two, `basis: "initiative"`, else the active milliseconds per token the estimate measured on other initiatives' feature runs, `basis: "history"`; null without either), `forecastMs` (used time plus the tokens the token forecast still has to run, the higher of its two forecasts, at that pace), `wallMs` (that remaining time spread over the run slots), `coverMs` (what starts every waiting feature at its high estimate beside what open runs hold), `suggestedMs` (the forecast plus a quarter, rounded up to 15 minutes; null while it fits) and `notice`. While the forecast is above the limit, `budget.timeNotice` says so with the command: "At 58k tokens per active minute (the median of 3 finished runs of this initiative) the 12 features need about 598 min of summed active time (400 min used, about 66 min of wall time still to run with 3 at once); the shared time is 360 min. Extend to 750 min (baked workflow initiative extend ladle --time-ms 45000000)." The TUI shows it as a Time forecast line and the desktop beside the Token forecast; the first drive step that sees it for a limit records a `budget.time_notice` event. The advice changes no decision. The exhausted-share block puts the time that answers it on its `extend` remedy as `suggestedTimeMs` and `--time-ms N` (the forecast's suggestion, else the cover), and the TUI's B and the desktop's Extend prefill it. A feature run blocked `budget_exhausted` on its active deadline offers `extend` first, with the run's `suggestedTimeMs` (what it used plus the tokens its plan still has at the initiative's pace, else its own, with the quarter margin, at least a quarter above its limit) and the command `baked workflow extend RUN --expected-revision N --time-ms MS --resume`, then `resume`: a plain resume stops again at once (#2226). That amount is held to what the shared time can still grant: the run's committed time plus the room the limit has above everything committed (`heldRunTime`, #2414). A larger amount is offered as the most the room grants, followed by a second `extend` remedy for the initiative, with the shared time that grants the whole amount (`initiativeId`, `suggestedTimeMs`). The message says so: "... the shared time grants it 25 min: the rest needs the shared time extended to 750 min first". With no room left, the run's `extend` is dropped and the shared time's comes first. In bench round 76, schemamask `io` was told to extend to 21.18M ms, from its own stalled pace, while the whole shared time was 18.0M ms. `budget.time.cleanPace` records, in shadow, the pace without the finished runs' `silentMs` (the median of their clean rates from four runs, else the history's), with its inputs (`rates`, `silentMs`) and the `forecastMs` and `suggestedMs` it would give. The time notice event carries the same record, and each sizing report records `cleanPace` with the active-time cap it would set. The deciding pace is unchanged until a later pull request switches it. In bench round 74 the time had no notice and the blocks no amount, so the person learned of it from three blocks and doubled the limit by hand. The host also decides whether it would extend the shared time itself (`workflows.timePoolGrowth`, `shadow` by default, `off` to record nothing, #2346): only while the time forecast passes the limit, the dollar forecast is within the cap's ceiling (when a dollar cap is set; measured, not before), and the token pool still has the tokens to run longer at the pace. The extension is the notice's `suggestedMs`, held to the time used plus what the pool's remaining tokens take at the pace, rounded down to 15 minutes. In `shadow` nothing changes: the first drive step that sees it records a `decision.would_extend` event, once per time and token limit, with `reason: "time-pool"`, `toMs`, `byMs`, `capMs`, a summary and its `inputs`. The replay corpus decides every recorded share gate's time again (`replayTimePoolGrowth`); in R75 the time forecast never passed the limit, so latcell and maskback replay without an extension. Growing the time for real is a later change.

**Calls forecast and blocks with a calls amount.** The shared agent calls (`--calls`) get the same advice (#2345). `show` gives `budget.calls`: `limitCalls`, `usedCalls`, `committedCalls`, `pace` (`{callsPerMillion, basis, runs}`: the median calls per token of the initiative's own completed feature runs of at least 100k tokens once there are two, `basis: "initiative"`, else the median the estimate measured on other initiatives' feature runs, `basis: "history"`; null without either), `forecastCalls` (used calls plus the tokens the token forecast still has to run, at that pace), `coverCalls` (what starts every waiting feature at its high estimate beside what open runs hold), `suggestedCalls` (the forecast plus a quarter, rounded up to 50; null while it fits) and `notice`. While the forecast is above the limit, `budget.callsNotice` says so with the command, `baked workflow initiative extend ID --calls N`; the TUI shows a Calls forecast line, the desktop shows it beside the Time forecast, and the first drive step that sees it for a limit records a `budget.calls_notice` event. The advice changes no decision. A share block whose calls are below the minimum puts the calls that answer it on its `extend` remedy as `suggestedCalls` and `--calls N` (the forecast's suggestion, else the cover), and the TUI's B and the desktop's Extend prefill it.

**Extension inside the dollar cap.** When the pool parks a feature on tokens while the person also set a dollar cap, the host decides whether it would extend the pool itself (`workflows.extendWithinDollars`, `shadow` by default, `on` to apply it, `off` to record nothing): only with a measured dollar forecast within the cap's ceiling, to the pool that covers the waiting features' high estimates, held to the tokens the cap pays for at the measured rate, and only when that still starts every waiting feature at its low estimate. It never raises a dollar, call or time limit, and a pool without a dollar cap is never grown. In `shadow` nothing changes: the park's `feature.share_gate` event records `poolGrowth` (`mode`, `wouldExtend`, `toTokens`, `byTokens`) and the pool's inputs (`limits`, `used`, `pool`), and a would-extend records a `decision.would_extend` initiative event, once per feature and pool size, with `reason: "extend-within-dollars"`, the amounts, a summary and its `inputs`. The replay corpus decides each recorded park again (`replayPoolGrowth`); R66 nodepluck's 14:40:20 park replays as an extension from 30M to 33.1M. With `on` (opt-in, #2060) the host applies the same decision: the park's `poolGrowth` records `mode: "on"`, the pool grows to `toTokens` with a `budget.extended` event (`by: "host"`, `reason`, `featureId`, `toTokens`, `byTokens`, a summary and the `inputs`; the person's extensions carry no `by`), and the parked feature starts in the same drive. It grows only the pool the decision read, never past the tokens the cap pays for nor with the dollar forecast above the ceiling, and the grown pool is never grown again by the same rule. `on` waits for the first accepted feature (#2471): before one is measured there is no dollar forecast (`budget.cost.forecastUsd` is null with `measuredFeatures: 0`), so a park in the first wave is not extended then. The park's `poolGrowth` then records `hold: "no measurement"` (in `shadow` too), and a drive that retries the parked feature decides it again once a feature is measured, the moment the hold lifts, instead of returning on the earlier park (#2523); that record carries no hold, so it is decided once, and with `on` the park message (`next.message`, its recovery summary) and the forecast notice end with "extendWithinDollars: on waits for the first accepted feature's measured dollar forecast before the host grows the pool, so extend by hand with the command named here." The TUI, the desktop and `initiative show` read the same text. To exercise `on` itself, set a pool at least the sum of the waiting features' low estimates (a feature starts only while the pool left less the other waiting features' lows covers its own low, so a smaller pool parks at the first gate) and below what the first wave actually uses, so the park comes after a feature is accepted. The same setting acts at a second place, the plan-time share gate (#2488): when a started feature run's own plan sizing needs more than its ceiling (`split-feature`), the scheduler asks the initiative before the run's first task, before the split note sends the plan back to the planner (`WorkflowStore.planShareGate`, `InitiativeService.planShareGate`, `planShareDecision`, the `plan-share` row of `HOST_POOL_GROWTHS`). The run's need is its plan charge (`planChargeTokens`, on `ownPlanNeed` at the measured plan-to-actual ratio); the part the pool does not count yet joins the waiting features, and the pool's extension is decided by the same `poolExtensionDecision`, so the same bounds and the same wait for a measured dollar forecast apply; a run held for `no measurement` is asked again when a feature is accepted (#2523). With it the run's ceiling (`sizing.maxRunTokens`) grows to the need, held to the base policy's ceiling and to the grown pool less what the other runs used (the #2468 hold). In `shadow` a `decision.would_extend` initiative event of `reason: "plan-share"` records the pool and ceiling it would grow to (`toTokens`, `byTokens`, `ceilingTokens`, `needTokens`) and its `inputs`, once per run and ceiling. With `on` the pool grows as a `budget.extended` event (`by: "host"`, `reason: "extend-within-dollars"`, `gate: "plan-share"`, `runId`, `ceilingTokens`, the `inputs`) and the run's ceiling as a host policy revision (`policy-revisions/initiative-N.json`, `kind: "plan-share"`) together, and the plan is sized again on the grown ceiling, so the run starts its tasks within its plan instead of going on under assume. Under `off`, or when the cap cannot pay, nothing changes: the run goes on under assume as before, and an `initiative-plan-share` would-stop records that the feature would park before its tasks with the `extend` remedy and its amounts. The replay corpus decides each such run again (`replayPlanShare`): R80 deltaglue's cli replays as an extension of the pool from 7.1M to 20.2M and of its ceiling from 3.38M to 12.0M. The shared time's growth (`workflows.timePoolGrowth`) waits for the same measured dollar forecast when a cap is set, but it is shadow only and records nothing while it holds, so it has no such message. Making `on` the default is a pull request of its own.

**A plan charge above the pool (#2280).** In `enforce` a run's growth refresh counts the run at its plan charge, and the charge is also what its bounds start from (`held`). A charge priced above what is left would make the bound permission to take it: R73 flatelens' commands started with 28.54M of the 34.2M pool used and $25.34 of the $30 cap spent, was charged 31.20M tokens and $24.08, its bounds rose to them, its auto-extends took hard ceilings of 33.56M to 44.07M tokens from that dollar bound, and it used 12.92M: the initiative ended at 41.45M, 21 percent past its pool, and past its dollar cap, with nothing recorded. The charge a bound starts from is therefore capped at what the initiative can hold for the run (`chargeHold`): in tokens the pool less what the other runs use or hold and the low estimates of the features still waiting, never below the run's own use; in dollars the cap with its tolerance less what the other runs spent or hold, never below the run's own spent and reserved dollars. A charge that fits is unchanged, the pool itself still counts the whole charge, and a refresh only raises bounds, so a bound already above stays (the `initiative-pool-growth` reason records it). An owner's `extend` is the way past the pool, as its block's remedy names (#2042). The cap holds in the policy revision's inputs (`charge`, `hold`).

**A started run's review and convergence (shadow).** A feature run's dollar bound grows only into what the initiative can spare after the low estimates of the features still waiting, so a run whose last task overran its price can be left with nothing for the review and convergence that follow: R71 xmlvet get-command stopped at its first review call with both tasks done, $1.29 spent, $28.87 of the $30 cap spent ($37.50 ceiling with the tolerance) and cli-release's $3.13 low reserving the $1.13 left (#2241). Before a model call of a feature run that finished every planned task stops on its cost ceiling, the host prices the stages after the tasks that have not completed, as the cost forecast prices the final review and convergence, and decides whether the run's bound (`sizing.maxRunCostUsd`) would grow to its spent and reserved dollars plus that reserve (or the call, when larger), as long as what the initiative spent and holds, with the run held at that amount, stays under the cap with its tolerance (`stageReserveDecision`). `workflows.stageReserve` sets what happens: `shadow` (the default) records a `decision.would_extend` initiative event with `reason: "stage-reserve"`, once per run and bound, with `toUsd`, `byUsd`, `suggestedUsd`, a summary and its `inputs` (the run's spent, reserved and requested dollars, the remaining stages and their reserve, its bound, what the initiative holds for it, its spare after the waiting lows, and the initiative's dollars, cap and ceiling), and the run still stops; `on` grows the bound as a host policy revision (`kind: "stage-reserve"`) and the call grows into it; `off` records nothing. Whatever the mode, a cost-ceiling block of a run past its tasks names the cap that finishes it on its `extend` remedy (`suggestedUsd`: spent plus reserved plus the stage reserve) and in its message (`baked workflow extend RUN --cost-usd N`). Making `on` the default is a later change, after bench rounds without a false would-extend. Such a block is a question with a one-command answer, yet the run blocks (`budget_exhausted`) and a planned-ahead dependant waited on it as on a stalled upstream: R71 cli-release waited the 24 minutes 46 seconds get-command was blocked (#2249). The scheduler's fault handler decides, for every stage at once, that a cost refusal of a run past its tasks whose block carries a priced `extend` remedy, in a stage the fault lists as still to run (a review dimension's call runs in the review stage), would leave the run `waiting` on that remedy instead (`costRefusalWait` in `stop-reasons.ts`), and records it as the `cost_refusal_wait` would-stop (shadow): the run still blocks. The initiative view reads the record of a blocked feature's current stop as its `costWait` (the state, the stage and `suggestedUsd`), and a dependant lists in `stalledOn` only the unaccepted upstream features it is stalled behind: failed ones and blocked ones without a `costWait` (`stalledUpstream`). Promoting the reason leaves the run waiting on a request the person answers with the extend.

**Plan charge.** An open feature run counts against the pool at its token limit, its start share, for its whole run, although a planned-ahead run is priced as soon as its plan is sized. Bench round 67 (ampwhy, 11 features, plan ahead) held 30.5M at cli's gate for runs that had used 0.05M to 6.6M each, while their own plans expected 0.37M to 2.1M: under the 30M pool the gate would have parked cli for about 4 minutes, and the split's warning had the loop extend to 38.6M for an initiative that used 12.2M (#2100). The other way, a planned-ahead run whose plan prices above its limit is under-reserved (#2056). The plan charge is one rule for both: an open feature run whose latest plan sizing priced it is held at the high end of its plan's need at the measured plan-to-actual ratio (`ownPlanNeed`, the [estimate's](#initiatives) own-plan floor; times 1 until three accepted features with a plan measure it), never below what it used. While the ratio is unmeasured, a plan whose low end is already above the run's limit is held at its expected need instead (still above the limit, #2253): such a plan contradicts the size class that set the limit, and R60 wheeltags' names, sized at 10.3M-14.1M against 8.0M, used 4.57M, so its high end parked dead at 19:31:58 in the replay. It is the only one of the corpus's 24 unmeasured plans priced above their limits whose low end was above it; a plan that straddles its limit keeps its high end (R71 xmlvet's scanner, 4.26M-11.75M against 8.0M, used 12.10M, and canonical's would-park stays). The dollar charge prices the same tokens. `show` gives `budget.planCharge`: `mode`, `ratio`, `runs` (each with `featureId`, `runId`, `usedTokens`, `limitTokens`, `plan` and `chargeTokens`), `limitCommittedTokens` (what all runs hold with open runs at their limits), `planCommittedTokens` (the same with the charges) and `releasedTokens` (the difference: positive frees tokens, negative adds them); null without such a run. The `initiative-plan-charge` [stop reason](#stop-reasons-and-shadow-mode) decides which one the pool holds: in `shadow` `budget.pool.committedTokens`, `reserved` and `remaining` stay at the limits and a `decision.would_release` event records each run's charge once per plan and, at a share gate the charge would turn, whether the feature would have started (`wouldStart`) or parked (`wouldPark`); every `feature.share_gate` event records `planCharge`, and `planChargedGate` replays the gate with it. In `enforce`, the default since #2245 (an initiative's base policy without a `stopModes` entry for it), the pool holds the charges, the waiting features split what that leaves, and a run's growth refresh counts the run at its charge; the run's own limit is not lowered, so a run that passes its plan counts at its use from then on. Calls and active time still count an open run at its limits: a plan sizes tokens only, and calls and time already count other runs at their use when a run grows (`openGuardRoom`). With a dollar budget the same rule holds dollars (#2124): an open run counts at the larger of what it spent and reserved and its `maxCostUsd`, which is its dollar share, so the dollar side held each run's cap. A run whose plan is sized is held at its charge's tokens times the dollars per token the initiative's own task calls paid (`taskCallRate`, #2124), never below what it spent and reserved. There is no measured plan-to-actual dollar ratio. Until a task call of the initiative is priced, the run keeps its cap. `budget.planCharge.usd` gives `taskRate`, `limitCommittedUsd`, `planCommittedUsd` and `releasedUsd`, and each run gives `usedUsd`, `limitUsd` and `chargeUsd`. In `shadow` the dollar budget's `remainingUsd` stays at the caps, and the `decision.would_release` event adds `releasedUsd` and the dollar amounts to its summary; `planChargedGate` moves the remaining dollars too. In `enforce` the dollar budget holds the charges, and a run's growth refresh counts the run at its dollar charge. In R62 stepvet the runs held $21.11 of the $30 cap at stepscmd's start (rewound to that moment, as its share gate recorded), the initiative ended at $26.33, and the four open runs' plans at the task-call rate free $4.86 (lines' $2.41 cap is held at $2.22). No replayed start moves. The replay corpus reports the charge at every rewound start (`planCharge` on each start): in R66 nodepluck, a round short for use, no start would park with it, base's plan added 0.56M at json's start, and select still parks at its 14:40:20 park. The split's token warning says that its extension is for those reservations and what the estimate expects to be used. The charge shipped in shadow and was promoted to `enforce` after two bench rounds (#2245): R70 (namesound, evalpin) and R71 (xmlvet, evalpin) recorded 39 `decision.would_release` records and one would-park, R71 xmlvet's canonical at 04:48, where holding the open runs at their plans left 4.7M of the 30.0M pool (scanner's plan priced its run at 11.75M against an 8.0M limit and it used 12.10M). It was true: the pool ran short 65 minutes later with the tree idle, and enforced the extension is asked for at canonical's start. The replay corpus parks canonical there with the extend remedy and parks no other start of the two rounds. Enforced, the corpus parks no start of an older round either: R60 wheeltags' dead, which names' overpriced plan parked before #2253, starts.

**Estimate.** `estimate ID` (TUI T, desktop Estimate, `GET /v1/initiatives/ID/estimate`) forecasts the split, approved or not, without changing anything ([estimate.ts](../../src/core/workflows/initiatives/estimate.ts)). For each feature it gives a token range and a planned task count. Once this project has finished feature runs of earlier initiatives of the same size, it measures the feature from them (the initiative's own runs are not samples) (from those of the same workflow when there are two). A feature whose workflow has fewer than two of them, among features of other workflows, is priced as a run of its own workflow instead when this project has runs of that workflow: a quick leaf skips the research, spec, analysis and reviews of an sdd feature, and the small sdd features priced the light leaves of bench rounds 54 and 55 at 0.80M to 1.38M where they used 0.33M to 0.47M (#1813). With `"recencyHalfLife": N` in the initiative's `sizing` (off by default), the size pool weighs each finished feature by how recent its initiative is: its weight halves for every N initiatives started after its own (a feature without a start time counts as newest), and the pool's middle half is taken at the weighted quartiles, for its tokens and its task counts (`recencyWeights`, `weightedRangeOf`). In bench rounds 63 to 68, 24 of 60 finished features used less than the low end of their range (40%, against 24% in rounds 45 to 61), 20 of them priced by the size pool; at a half-life of 2 the replay puts 20 of 60 below and keeps the whole corpus within its bounds (#2150). With round 69 the last six rounds (64 to 69) are within the bounds without it (16 of 59 below, 11 above), so it stays off by default. The range backtest bounds the last six rounds as well as the whole corpus. A feature that names paths is placed among them by its path weight: a least-squares fit of their tokens on their paths gives its point, and their spread around the fit gives its range (`pathFit`: `base` plus `perPath` per path). Its planned task count is placed the same way, by a fit of their task counts on their paths under the same conditions. The fit needs four of them that name paths, and their tokens must follow their paths (a correlation of at least 0.5); otherwise the feature takes their middle half. In bench rounds 45 to 52 the tokens of medium features followed their paths (0.27M plus 0.38M per path) and those of large ones hardly did; R52 srcwhere's two-path medium modules had been priced like six-path ones (#1767). Otherwise it prices a run of the feature's workflow as `baked workflow estimate` does ([Estimates](#estimates)): runs of that workflow, then any run, then generic ranges. It then scales the tasks by size (small half, large double) and the stages around the tasks less steeply (small 0.6, large 1.6 times), since a larger feature also writes a longer spec and has more to review: in bench round 34 a large feature was forecast at 2.4 times a small one and cost 3.4 to 5.5 times as much. The total adds the features and is planned with a 25% margin on the high end, compared with the shared budget (`fits`: `yes`, `maybe` or `no`). The API, TUI and desktop use the same label: it gives the expected tokens (`expectedTokens`, what is used so far plus each feature's point forecast), says whether the low end and the expected value fit the shared budget, and warns when the high end exceeds the shared budget, with the measured spread behind that high end and a suggested extension including margin. At split approval, `next.message` warns by the expected value and the measured spread ("The estimate expects about 16.4M tokens, within the shared budget (30M); its high end (...; finished features used 1.00-1.28x their low end ...) exceeds the shared budget"); until three non-skeleton features are accepted it adds that the measured calibration is unmeasured and how many features still to run their own plans price (bench round 66, nodepluck: its first features ran in parallel and four were measured when half the budget was spent, #2043), and it offers one extension, with an `extend` remedy in `next.recovery`: the budget preview's `suggestedTokens` below (`splitExtension`, #2525), which the preview's notice, the message's command and the remedy all name. It is the high end plus margin, held to what the dollar cap pays for (the tokens used so far plus what the dollars left pay for at the estimate's price for the waiting features, rounded down to 100k), and nothing when the pool already covers the high end. When the cap's tokens are the smaller, the message says so, since the pool would otherwise stop features while the dollars the person set have room: "The $30.00 cap pays for about 34.4M tokens at $0.87 per million, less than the high end with margin and more than the shared pool (30.0M): the pool, not the dollar cap, would stop features first." (bench round 71, xmlvet: the approval said nothing, and the 30.0M pool stopped every feature for 19.6 minutes with dollars left, #2244). When the cap pays for no more than the pool, the message names no pool and says a larger pool would go unpaid at that price. Bench round 82, tinyflate, extended to the preview's 19.6M, what its $25 cap paid for, and the message then offered 29.6M "including margin", 50 percent above what the unchanged cap paid for; slotprompt's 14.2M pool covered its 12.5M of high estimates and was still told to extend to the cap's 23.5M. The CLI prints the extension command; the TUI offers B and the desktop offers Extend budget. While the initiative awaits the split approval, `budget.preview` (#2502) estimates the proposed revision as approval would start it: each waiting feature's low, expected and high tokens and their sums, what the dollar cap pays for at the same price, and the share gate the first ready feature would meet at the current pool (`firstGate`, `pass` or `park` with its share and need). Its `suggestedTokens` is the split approval's one amount (`splitExtension`): the high end plus margin, held to what the cap pays for, never the lows alone (R81 ratdec's lows were 1.8x over and 2.7x under what its features used), and null when the pool covers the high end. When it suggests an extension, the split approval's `next.message` ends with the forecast notice's own sentence and its one-line command ("At the estimates so far the 3 features expect about 10.4M tokens and up to 14.0M at the high end (43k used); the shared pool is 4.0M. Extend to 11.8M, what the $25.00 cap pays for ... (baked workflow initiative extend ID --tokens 11800000)."; an amount below the cap's reads "the high end with margin"), and the `request.created` event carries the preview (`budgetPreview`) and that sentence (`text`). The TUI and the desktop show it as a Budget before approval line. It is display only: the share gate and `extendWithinDollars: on` still act only after approval, and `on` still waits for a measured feature (#2471). In bench rounds 79 to 81 the pool was picked from a guess before approval and the first share gate parked the first feature seconds after it, costing a second hand extend each time. The `/v1/initiatives/ID` response carries the same warning and remedy. Approval can proceed with the current budget. The level runs from 1 to 5 by the expected task count, on the bench difficulty ladder's typical counts: about 6, 10, 15, 22 and 30 tasks. The estimate re-forecasts as the initiative runs. An accepted feature counts at the tokens its runs used (`basis: measured`), a feature still running at no less than it used so far, and the features still to run keep their planned estimates until three non-skeleton features are accepted. After that they blend the planned estimate with the accepted non-skeleton features' measured-to-planned ratio, capped at 5 times with no lower bound (`calibrated`). The measured ratio contributes one third at three samples, two thirds at four, and all at five. A size with at least three accepted non-skeleton features uses its own blend; otherwise it uses the overall blend. Skeleton acceptances still count at their actual usage, but never set either ratio. Notes report the applied blend. Bench round 50, selwhy, stopped after one accepted skeleton feature because its ratio projected $44.69 against a $37.50 ceiling; that feature is no longer a calibration sample. The split's decomposition run counts as used too, and the margin applies only to what is still to run, so with every feature accepted the estimate is what the initiative used. `progress` gives the tokens used, the accepted features, the blended ratio and `bySize`, the blend per size with at least three accepted non-skeleton features; the TUI and desktop show a measured feature as `used` and the tokens used next to the total. In bench round 32 the split's forecast was 10.6M to 19.0M and said it fits a 30M budget; the initiative used 32.7M, and its first features had shown the gap long before. Once a feature's run has written its spec, the feature is also forecast at no less than its acceptance criteria times the tokens per criterion measured on finished feature runs and this initiative's accepted features (`perCriterion`, from the lowest of those rates up to the upper end of their range, the third quartile from four rates on; it needs at least two measured features and is `null` before), since a feature's work grows with its criteria. In the same way a feature still to run whose spec is not written yet is forecast at no less than the paths of its boundary times the tokens per path measured on finished feature runs and accepted features (`perPath`: from the lowest measured rate up to their median, null before two are measured. The low end is the lowest rate however many are measured: from four rates on it had been their first quartile, and once other projects' features counted it put the low end of 11 of the 12 bench round 54 lanewalk features above what they used, at 365k per path over 91 features where lanewalk's Java features used 165k to 308k (#1806); the same holds for `perCriterion`; once the spec is written its criteria replace its paths, since a file dense in rules costs several times a plain one: in bench round 36 the rates ran from 0.29M to 1.28M per path and from 155k to 320k per criterion), and a feature whose run has sized its plan at no less than what that sizing expects the whole run to use (`planned`): what the run spent before its plan was sized, the plan's tasks, review and convergence, and its repair reserve at the high end, since the tasks alone left a run that had spent 2M tokens before its plan forecast at its 3M to 5M of tasks. Its low end is the plan's expected tokens (the same run around the plan's expected tasks) times how far this initiative's accepted features with a plan landed from what their own plans expected (`planCalibration`: none until three of them, then blended like the re-forecast), never below the plan's own low end, with the plan's high end above it; a feature without a plan keeps its class need, scaled as above. That need is what the share gate, the growth limits and the split approval reserve for the feature: in bench round 66 (nodepluck) six planned-ahead features' own plans expected 1.95M to 3.96M while the pool kept their class values (1.12M-2.29M medium) until they finished, and the initiative used 2.54 times its summed low ends (#2043). A feature the request demands a test volume of (the decomposer sets `testCases` on each feature that delivers stated cases, so one stated total adds up across them) is forecast at no less than those cases times the tokens a case cost in finished runs' test tasks (`testVolume`: `cases`, `features`, `perCase`, `samples` and `basis`; `floor: "tests"`). Each run's test tasks are one rate, their tokens over their cases, from the lowest run rate up to their median: this project's runs once two have finished test tasks with cases (`basis: "project"`), every store's before (`history`), and 15k to 75k a case with fewer than two (`defaults`). An accepted feature keeps what it used. The split approval message opens with the volume ("The request demands 150 test cases of 3 features (...); each feature is forecast at no less than 21k-26k tokens per case, ..."). Bench round 66 (nodepluck) demanded at least 150 cases, and its test tasks were 17.26M tokens, 51.9 percent of the initiative, while the split priced its features by size class (#2055). Single tasks are no floor: across the bench-trace corpus they ran from 1,354 tokens a case, while nodepluck's features ran from 21k to 41k. In bench round 35 the four large narrowfit features named 4 to 11 paths and used 1.8M to 7.9M tokens, at 0.36M to 0.92M per path, while their size gave them one range; the TUI and desktop show each feature's path count next to its range. Only files count: a boundary that names a glob (`src/format/**`) counts its literal paths and the files the initiative's request names that the glob matches (a bare name such as `AddCommand.java` matches a glob's last segment when that segment names more than an extension), in the features to estimate and in the finished features the rate is measured on; a glob that matches no named file leaves the feature without a path count, since one glob stands for any number of files (bench round 38: with glob boundaries the floor never applied). The decomposer is told to list each file the request or the code names by its path and to keep globs for open sets of files. A literal path that several features list (a module stub the first feature creates and a later one fills) counts in full only in the last feature in plan order that lists it; in the earlier ones it is a placeholder (`placeholders`) and counts as a third of a path, for the features to estimate and in the finished features the rate is measured on, and the TUI and desktop show it next to the paths (`7 paths + 15 placeholders`). In bench round 40 (tonemath) the five boundaries listed 48 paths for 33 files, the first feature's floor counted its 15 stubs as full paths and came out 1.54 times what it used, and the stubs cost about a third of a full path each. A file of the feature's own skeleton, which the host writes with no agent, counts neither as a path nor as a placeholder (`skeleton`, shown as `+22 host-written`), in the features to estimate and in the finished features the rate is measured on; a host-written task is not counted in a run's task count either, so it does not dilute the tokens per task. In bench round 45 (grundykit) the first feature's 22 skeleton stubs were priced at a third of a path each, 2.1M to 3.1M tokens it never used. Finished feature runs of the initiatives in the stores of [`sizing.historyStores`](#plan-time-sizing) given as a project directory (or its `.baked/workflows.sqlite`) count as samples too, both for a size and for these rates (`perPathSamples` gives the features measured and how many are other projects'), so a project's first initiative has a path rate at the split approval, before any of its features ran: in bench round 37 the ranktally split was 6.1M to 10.8M by size class, with the same range for large features of 9 and 10 paths, and used 18.98M. The high end is bounded by how far finished features actually landed above the low end the estimate gives them (`highCalibration`): each finished feature of an initiative of this project or of `sizing.historyStores` is priced from the other initiatives' features alone by the rules above (its size class, the path fit, the path floor at their lowest rate; no stage history), and compared with what it used; each accepted feature of this initiative is compared with the low end the samples gave it. With at least 6 such ratios from at least 2 initiatives, a feature still to run whose spec and plan do not price it, and that no re-forecast scales up, has its high end at its low end times their 90th percentile and its expected tokens at its low end times their median, where that is below the high end the rules above give (`highBasis: "ratios"`; TUI "high by measured ratios", desktop "high by ratios"). The total's high end is bounded the same way by whole initiatives (`highCalibration.total`: what an initiative's features used against their summed low ends), applied to what is still to run, since one feature far above its low end is common and a whole initiative less so; `expectedTokens` never exceeds it. With fewer ratios, or when the measured spread is as wide as the range already is, the range stays where the rules above put it, and the budget text says the high end is uncalibrated or names the spread. Replayed at R62 stepvet's split, the corpus history measures 0.43x to 2.8x per feature (median 1.31x, where stepvet used 1.32x) and 0.93x to 2.48x per initiative, so the range keeps its width there and the warning names the expected value and that spread. In bench rounds 62 and 63, stepvet used 1.32x the low end of its split estimate and eolscope 1.05x while the high end was about 2.04x, and the warning made an operator extend a budget the run used 37% of (#1982). An initiative in a language new to the pool is priced from other languages' features, which ran on toolchains the host already handled, while it pays for gates, fixers and scaffolding the host meets for the first time (#2398). Its language is the one most code paths of its plan's boundaries are written in (`planLanguage`), and each finished feature carries its initiative's. While the pool has fewer than 3 finished features of that language, this initiative's accepted ones included, every feature still to run has its high end at no less than its low end times the 90th percentile of the actual-to-low ratios of first-language features (each feature of an initiative whose language had fewer than 3 finished features in earlier initiatives, priced from other languages' features alone; `newLanguage` on the estimate, `newLanguage: true` on a raised feature), and the total's high end at no less than their per-initiative spread; the low end and `expectedTokens` stay, so the dollar forecast does not move, while the share gate, the feature shares and the split approval read the wider high end. In the bench-trace corpus, R73 flatelens (the first Lua initiative) went above the high end of 6 of its 8 features and R74 ladlemath (the first PHP one) of 7 of 12, putting rounds 70 to 75 at 31 of 90 features above their range, beyond the bound of 30.7; replayed with the first-language ratios (3.67x at ladlemath's split, over 96 features of 10 initiatives) the window has 17 above. The dollar forecast prices the same `expectedTokens`, which the ratios change only where they narrow a feature's range. A raised feature is `calibrated`, has `criteria`, `paths` or `planned` set, and names the floor that raised it in `floor` (`criteria`, `paths` or `plan`); the TUI and desktop show it next to the range ("by paths"). `basis` names the weakest source among the features. Every figure comes from this project's runs, so the estimate holds for any provider. It is an estimate, never a limit. The estimate also gives minutes (`duration`, #1557): each feature's expected tokens it has not used yet times the median active milliseconds per token of finished feature runs (of its workflow when there are two, else of all; `minutes` on the feature, none for an accepted one), summed as `activeMinutes`, which is what the shared `--budget-time-ms` limits, and replayed through the plan's `dependsOn` on `parallelRuns` slots as `wallMinutes`, a ready feature with the longest remaining chain first; when the plan's widest group of independent features is wider than the slots, `widest` gives the wall time with that many at once. The label, the TUI estimate line and the desktop Estimate panel show both ("about 95 min of wall time with 2 at once ... (about 140 min of summed active time, which the shared time budget limits)"). With fewer than two timed feature runs `duration` is null and no minutes are shown. Bench round 48 (recmill, 2 runs at once) used 282 minutes of active time in about 168 of wall time, and round 55 (routeshadow) took 66 minutes of wall for 88 of summed feature time, while the split approval printed only a speedup bound; idle wall while a person answers a stop is not in the figure.

**Pause, resume and re-planning.** `pause` stops the initiative and pauses a running feature run; `resume` continues it. While paused, the split can be edited again: approving the new revision keeps every feature whose boundary, contract, dependencies and mandatory flag are unchanged (a rename keeps its run), and supersedes and cancels the runs of removed or changed features, which then run again under the new revision. `replan --feature F` supersedes one feature's current run so the next drive starts it again; other features' results stay. Superseded runs and their artifacts remain linked to the initiative and are not collected by `gc`.

**Switching the model.** `reroute --model ID` moves the whole initiative to another model, for example when its provider runs out of credits: feature runs it starts from now on use the new model, and its open runs that are not executing (the decompose run and each feature's current run) are [rerouted](#switching-a-run-to-another-model) with the same checks and a `run.rerouted` event each. Every run is checked before any is changed, so a model that cannot be called, or an `independentReview: "require"` conflict in any run, changes nothing. Runs that already use the model are left as they are, runs started before routing was recorded are skipped, and finished and superseded runs keep their models; a rebased run inherits its run's latest routing. A run that is executing refuses the switch: pause the initiative first. The `initiative.rerouted` event records the old and new model and what happened to each run (`rerouted`, `unchanged` or `skipped`), and `show` reports the model as `model` (null for the configured default). The approved split stays approved; a split still waiting for approval is proposed again as a new revision, because its digest covers the model. `--resume` also resumes the rerouted runs that are blocked and a paused initiative, then drives it, with the exit codes of `drive`. The TUI offers the same with M, the local API with `POST /v1/initiatives/ID/reroute` and the desktop in the initiative view.

Exit codes of `start`, `approve`, `drive`, `resume` and `reroute --resume`: 0 completed or still progressing, 1 refused or error, 3 waiting for a decision, 4 blocked, 5 revision conflict, 130 paused. The workflow panel and the desktop app start initiatives, edit, approve and reject the split, pause, resume, re-plan features, extend the budget and switch the model; see [Initiatives in the TUI](#initiatives-in-the-tui) and the [desktop app guide](../desktop/app.md).

## Local API (`baked serve`)
